# Sūrah al-Aʿrāf (Chapter 7) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:1

> Alif-Lãm-Mĩm-Ṣãd.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Alif Lām Mīm Ṣād**

Four Arabic letters stand at the head of this sūrah, and they are known as the separated letters, the letters that appear singly at the openings of twenty-nine chapters of the Book. The combination in front of us is used once and once only: no other sūrah begins with Alif Lām Mīm Ṣād, just as no other sūrah begins with the five letters of Maryam (19:1) or the two letters of Ṭā Hā (20:1). The letters are recited before a single word of the sūrah's message is given, and the reciter meets them as sound rather than as statement.

The collection of these openings is a fixed feature of the Book's arrangement. Alif Lām Mīm stands at the head of the Cow (2:1), the Family of Imran (3:1), the Spider (29:1), the Romans (30:1), Luqmān (31:1), and the Prostration (32:1); Alif Lām Rā appears at the head of Jonah (10:1), Hūd (11:1), Joseph (12:1), Abraham (14:1), and the Rocky Tract (15:1); Alif Lām Mīm Rā opens the Thunder (13:1); and Ḥā Mīm opens the seven sūrahs from the Forgiver (40:1) to the Sand Dunes (46:1). The letters of this sūrah belong to a family that runs through the whole Book.

**What the Letters Signal**

The letters are followed in almost every case by a reference to the Book, and the pattern tells the reader what the opening is for. The letters of the Cow are followed by the words, This is the Book, no doubt therein (2:2); the letters of the Family of Imran are followed by the statement that God has revealed the Book to the Prophet in truth (3:3); the letters of Ṣād are followed by an oath sworn by the Quran, full of reminders (38:1); and the letters of the Pen are followed by an oath sworn by the pen and by what people write (68:1). The sūrah in front of us continues the same pattern, since its letters are followed immediately by the mention of a Book sent down to the Prophet.

The sequence places the reader in front of the recitation itself before it places him in front of its content. The Book is presented as a text that is recited, memorised, and written down, and the letters at the head of a sūrah are the audible sign of that. The sūrah of the Cave states that God has made the Quran upright and has not placed any crookedness in it (18:1), and the sūrah of the Troops says that the Book was sent down in truth. The letters that open the chapter in front of us introduce a text of that kind.

**Interpretations of the Letters**

Commentators have not settled on a single meaning for the separated letters, and the range of their suggestions is part of the tradition. Some have held that the letters are a name of God, others that they stand for the greatest name of the Divine, and others that they are the name of the sūrah or of the Book itself. The Companion Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said that the letters are among the ambiguous verses of the Book, and that the believing response to them is to accept them and to leave their meaning to God.

The differences among the explanations have not produced a dispute about the text, because the letters are recited and preserved as they stand in all readings of the Book. The community receives them as it receives the rest of the revelation, and the discussion of their meaning belongs to the study of the Quran's language rather than to the determination of its commands. The letters in front of us are recited in every prayer that uses this sūrah, and they are written at the head of every copy of it.

**The Opening of a Sūrah That Argues**

The sūrah that follows these letters carries an argument, and its opening is built for the audience that the argument addresses. The sūrah's introduction states that it is a late Makkan revelation, and its content concerns the consequences of rejecting the signs and messengers of God. It recounts the fate of communities that denied their prophets, from the people of Noah to the people of Pharaoh, and it sets that history in front of an audience that refused to believe in the prophethood of Muhammad.

The letters at the head of such a sūrah place the revelation before the debate. The audience is presented with the recitation first, and the letters are the point at which the reciter's voice begins. The sūrah's first ten verses then state the program: the Book has been sent down, the community is to follow it, the communities that were destroyed are a warning, and the reckoning will be just.

**The Name of the Sūrah**

The chapter takes its name from the Heights mentioned in its middle section, the barrier that stands between the people of Paradise and the people of the Fire (7:46; 7:48). The name is a description of the sūrah's scenery, and it has been used by the community since the earliest copies of the Book. The letters at the head of the chapter are not part of its name, although they are inseparable from the opening of the recitation, and the sūrah is known both by the word for the Heights and by its place in the sequence of the Book.

The title the community uses describes a scene from the Hereafter, and the sūrah's first verses speak of the world that is passing. The Book's habit of naming its chapters after an image or a theme inside them means that the title is a signpost rather than a summary, and the name of this chapter directs the reader to the middle of a long argument about the last things.

**Letters, Recitation, and the Claim of the Book**

The letters stand at the head of a Book that challenges its audience on the ground of its own eloquence. The sūrah of the Cow closes its longest passage on the signs with the challenge to produce a single sūrah like the Book (2:23), and the sūrah of the Night Journey states that if humanity and the jinn were to gather together to produce the like of the Quran, they would not do it (17:88). An audience that prided itself on its poetry was addressed in the sounds with which its poetry began, and the letters that open this sūrah are that address in its plainest form.

The Book's use of letters as an opening has been read by some commentators as a statement that the materials of the recitation are the letters the audience already knew, arranged in a manner that none of them could produce. The claim is not that the letters are unfamiliar but that the arrangement is beyond them, and the sūrah that follows develops the claim at length.

**Where the Verse Stands**

The verse is the first element of the sūrah's opening section, and it stands before the mention of the Book that its letters introduce. The ten verses that follow state the themes of the chapter: the sending down of the Book, the warning to the community, the protection that belongs to God alone, the fate of the destroyed communities, the questioning of the recipients of the message, the weighing of deeds, and the provision that has been placed on the earth.

The reader who begins the sūrah with these four letters is given the recitation before he is given the argument. The Book is placed in his hands, the warning and the reminder are named, and the long account of the prophets and the peoples that rejected them begins from the next passage. The letters are the door through which the whole chapter is entered.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The verse that follows describes the Book as sent down to the Prophet, tells him to carry no constriction in his breast because of it, and states that he is to warn by means of it and that it is a reminder for the believers (7:2). The letters of the opening are thus tied to the message they precede, and the reader passes from sound to statement in the space of a single verse.

The arrangement gives the chapter its shape from the outset. The revelation is named, the messenger is addressed, the warning is stated, and the community of believers is placed alongside the audience that is warned. The four letters that begin the sūrah are followed by a Book that has been sent down, and everything that follows in the chapter is an unfolding of that sending.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:2

> ˹This is˺ a Book sent down to you ˹O Prophet˺— do not let anxiety into your heart regarding it—so with it you may warn ˹the disbelievers˺, and as a reminder to the believers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Book Sent Down to the Prophet**

The verse names the thing the letters introduced: a Book sent down to the Prophet with a purpose attached. The phrase sent down carries the Book's account of its own origin, and it is used of the whole Quran as well as of particular passages. The sūrah of the Cow describes the Book as sent down to the Prophet and states that it contains no doubt (2:2), and the sūrah of the Female Ant states that the Book was sent down from the Wise, the Praiseworthy.

The address is personal, and it places the messenger at the receiving end of the revelation. The Book is not presented as a body of teaching that the Prophet composed, and the sūrah's later passages return to the same point when they describe the revelation as what has been sent down to him from his Lord (7:3). The verse in front of us states the fact on which the whole chapter's argument rests.

**The Constriction in the Breast**

The verse tells the Prophet that he is to carry no constriction in his breast because of the Book. The phrase describes the tightness that accompanies anxiety, and the Book uses it of the messengers in their hardest moments. The sūrah of Hūd records the instruction given to the Prophet not to let his breast be straitened by the task of conveying what has been revealed to him (11:12), and the sūrah of the Poets asks whether he will destroy himself with grief because the people do not believe (26:3). The sūrah of the Cave asks whether he would kill himself with sorrow over their refusal. The verse in front of us addresses the same condition directly.

The Book's answer to the messenger's distress is not an assurance that his people will accept the message. It is the reminder that the Book has been sent down and that his work is to convey it. The constriction in the breast comes from concern about the response, and the verse treats that concern by naming the revelation and its purpose.

**So That You May Warn**

The purpose clause names the first function of the Book: warning. The Book places the warning of the messengers at the centre of their mission, and it states in several places that the duty of the Prophet is to deliver the message and that the response of the people is beyond his control. The sūrah of the Family of Imran states that the messenger's only duty is to convey the message (3:20), the sūrah of the Table repeats the statement twice in a single passage (5:92; 5:99), and the sūrah of the Thunder says that the messenger's duty is only to convey (13:40). The verse in front of us names the warning as the reason the Book was sent down.

What is warned against is the consequence of rejecting the message, and the sūrah develops that theme at length. The audience is warned by the account of the destroyed communities, by the description of the reckoning, and by the signs that are laid out in the created order. The warning is therefore not a threat in the abstract; it is the statement of what has happened to those who refused in the past and of what awaits those who refuse now.

**A Reminder for the Believers**

The verse's second description of the Book is that it is a reminder for the believers. The word used denotes a recalling to mind, and the Book applies it to itself in several passages. The sūrah of Ṭā Hā describes the revelation as sent down as a reminder for the one who fears God (20:3), the sūrah of Yā Sīn states that the Book is none other than a reminder and a clear recitation (36:69), and the sūrah of the Forgiver calls the scripture given to Moses a guidance and a reminder for people of understanding (40:54). Sūrat al-Ḥijr Tract states that God has sent down the Reminder and that He will preserve it (15:9).

The two descriptions in the verse divide the Book's audience according to their response. The warning is directed at those who turn away, and the reminder is for those who accept and remember. The Book's presentation of itself in the sūrah of the Cow uses the same division, since it describes the Book as guidance for the mindful at its opening and turns to the warning of the disbelievers in its following verses (2:2; 2:6). This verse gathers both functions into a single sentence.

**The Two Audiences Named Together**

The verse places the warning and the reminder in one breath, and that arrangement is characteristic of the sūrah's approach. The introduction to the chapter describes its method as subtler and less polemical than the approach of the sūrah of the Cattle, and it argues through the history of the prophets and their peoples rather than through direct confrontation. The presentation of the Book as a warning and a reminder matches that method: the message is offered to both audiences on the same terms.

The Book's address to the community of believers appears throughout the sūrah, and the reminder is for them from its opening words. The sūrah's later passages describe the community of the Prophet's followers as the people who receive the Book and act on it, and the verse in front of us names them at the head of the chapter.

**The Anxiety of the Messenger**

The Book's accounts of the Prophet's distress over his people recur, and the verse in front of us answers it with the statement of the Book's purpose. The chapter of the Cave describes the Prophet's grief over the refusal of his people and tells him that if God willed, He could have gathered them all to guidance (6:35). Sūrat al-Shuʿarāʾ asks whether he will destroy himself with grief. The instruction to carry no constriction is therefore part of a pattern of reassurance that runs through the revelation.

The reassurance is tied to the fact that the messenger is not responsible for the outcome. The sūrahs cited above state the same principle in their descriptions of the prophets' duty, and the verse in front of us applies it to the Prophet at the beginning of a chapter that will describe the destruction of the peoples who refused their messengers. The messenger delivers, and the audience answers for itself.

**Where the Verse Sits in the Sūrah's Opening**

The verse is the second element of the chapter's opening section, and it follows the letters of the first verse with the mention of the Book. The verses that follow state the command to follow what has been sent down, the warning against protectors other than God, the fate of the destroyed communities, the questioning of those who received the message, and the weighing of deeds. The passage in front of us supplies the ground on which all of those statements rest.

The Book is introduced, its messenger is addressed, its purposes are named, and its audience is divided. The reader who continues into the sūrah finds the warning carried out in the accounts of the prophets and the reminder carried out in the verses that instruct the community.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse turns from the Prophet to the people addressed by the message and commands them to follow what has been sent down from their Lord and not to take protectors besides Him, adding the rebuke that they are seldom mindful (7:3). The two verses form a pair: the message is sent down, and the community is told what to do with it.

The Book's warning and reminder are thus given a human response in the verse that follows. The messenger is freed from anxiety about the outcome, and the audience is given the command that the Book brings. The chapter's opening moves from the page to the people who hear it.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:3

> Follow what has been sent down to you from your Lord, and do not take others as guardians besides Him. How seldom are you mindful!

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Follow**

The verse turns from the Prophet to the people who have received the message and commands them to follow what has been sent down to them from their Lord. The address is plural, and the commentary tradition takes it to be directed at the Prophet's community or at people in general. The command uses the same verb that the sūrah of the Cattle uses at its close when it describes the Book as blessed and commands the people to follow it (6:155), and the sūrah of the Cow places the Book's guidance first among the descriptions of the people who are mindful (2:2).

The phrase from your Lord attaches the revelation to the authority it carries. The audience is not told to follow a man's opinion or the arrangement of a tribe's customs; it is told to follow what has come down from the One who sustains it. The sūrah's audience traced its religious practice to its ancestors and its sanctuary to Abraham, and the verse directs it to the revelation that has arrived among them.

**The Protectors That Are Excluded**

The second half of the command forbids the taking of protectors besides God, and the Book uses the term throughout its warnings about alliance and reliance. The sūrah of the Cattle states that the believers have no protector besides God (6:51), the sūrah of the Thunder says that they have no protector other than Him (13:11), and the sūrah of the Cave gives the same statement (18:26). The sūrah of the Spider states that those who take protectors apart from God are like the spider that builds a house, and it adds that the frailest of houses is the spider's house (29:41).

The protectors the verse excludes are not only the idols of stone. The sūrah of the Bee describes the satans as the protectors of those who disbelieve, and the sūrah of the Cave names Iblīs among those taken as a protector in place of God (18:50). The passage later in this sūrah says that those who went astray took the devils as masters in place of God while thinking they were guided (7:30). The net cast by the command therefore covers every form of reliance that displaces the revelation.

**Relatives, Patrons, and the Pressure of the Community**

The commentary tradition notes that the protectors mentioned here may refer to the associates and friends who urged the first believers toward the religion of their tribe. The early Muslims were pressed by their families and their patrons to return to the worship of the idols, and the command in front of us answers that pressure by naming the revelation as the only thing to be followed. The Book's treatment of loyalty appears in its warnings about the alliances that pull a believer away from his religion, and the sūrah of the Table addresses the question of alliance directly.

The verse's phrasing also allows the reading that nothing is to be followed apart from what has been sent down, which makes the command a statement about the source of religious authority. The two readings come to the same point. Whether the audience is warned about patrons or about the teaching it follows, the command is that the revelation from God is the standard, and everything else that claims that place is excluded.

**How Seldom You Are Mindful**

The verse closes with a rebuke: how seldom are you mindful. The Book uses the same form of reproach in several places, addressing people who wonder at the message (27:62), who are given parables and fail to draw the lesson (40:58), and who attribute the recitation to something other than God (69:42). The rebuke in front of us is placed at the head of a sūrah that will display the evidence at length.

The complaint is not that the audience lacks information. The sūrah's accounts of the created order, of the prophets, and of the destruction of the earlier communities all supply material for reflection, and the verse states that the people seldom turn it over in their minds. The Book's use of the reproach in passages about the signs of the created order shows what the reflection is meant to cover: the rain, the harvest, the ordering of the heavens, and the record of the messengers.

**What the Audience Actually Followed**

The sūrah sets the following of the revelation against the following of the ancestors, and it names the practice explicitly later in the chapter when it reports the excuse of those who commit shameful deeds and claim that they found their forefathers doing it and that God commanded it (7:28). The Book rejects that excuse in the sūrah of the Cow, the sūrah of the Table, and the sūrah of Luqmān, where the disbelievers answer the call to what God has sent down with the claim that they follow what they found their fathers upon (2:170; 5:104; 31:21).

The protectors the verse excludes therefore have a concrete identity in the audience's life. They are the idols of the sanctuary, the customs of the tribe, and the powerful figures whose approval the audience sought. The command in front of us removes all of them from the place of authority and puts the revelation in that place alone.

**The Revelation as the Only Guardian**

The Book's own name for itself in such passages is the criterion that distinguishes the acceptable from the rejected. The sūrah of the Cow commands the community to judge by what God has sent down, and the sūrah of the Table states that whoever does not judge by what God has revealed, those are the disbelievers (5:44). The command in front of us belongs to the same family of statements.

The sūrah's audience is thereby given a rule for everything the chapter will discuss. The practices of the pilgrimage, the rules of food, the treatment of the orphan and the debtor, and the conduct of war are all to be settled by the revelation, and the verse in front of us states the principle before the chapter supplies the detail. The protector who owns nothing cannot be the authority; the Lord who owns everything has spoken.

**Where the Verse Sits in the Opening Section**

The verse stands third in the chapter's opening, after the letters and the description of the Book, and it begins the sequence of warnings and instructions that runs to the tenth verse. The verses that follow describe the destruction of the communities, the questioning of those who received the message, the weighing of deeds, and the establishment of people on the earth with their provision. The command in front of us is the response that the Book requires before the rest of the program is laid out.

The opening section of the sūrah thus moves from the letters to the Book, from the Book to its messenger, and from its messenger to the community that is commanded to follow it. The reader is given the revelation, the messenger, the command, and the warning against the alternative in four short verses.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse asks how many communities God has destroyed, and it states that the punishment came upon them at night or while they were resting at midday (7:4). The question follows the command to follow the revelation, and it supplies the consequence of the alternative that the previous verse forbade.

The two verses together state the choice and its cost. The community is told what to follow, and it is shown what happened to those who followed otherwise. The sūrah's warning then becomes specific in the verses that follow, where the punishment, the questioning, and the weighing are described in order.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:4

> ˹Imagine˺ how many societies We have destroyed! Our torment took them by surprise ˹while sleeping˺ at night or midday.

**Expanded Commentary**

**How Many Communities Were Destroyed**

The verse opens with a question about the number of communities that God has destroyed, and the question is put to the audience as a reminder of what lies in its own history. The Book uses the theme of the destroyed towns throughout its pages, and this sūrah contains the first of its long sequences on the subject, running from the account of Noah's people to the account of Pharaoh and his hosts, which occupies the middle of the chapter. The question form is deliberate, since it forces the hearer to reckon with a history he already knows.

The Book's other long treatments of the same theme appear in the sūrah of Hūd, where the sequence runs from Noah to the people of Shuʿayb (11:25–100), in the sūrah of the Poets, where the same sequence is repeated in shorter form (26:105–89), in the sūrah of the Ant, where the towns of Lot and Ṣāliḥ are described (27:45–58), and in the sūrah of the Moon, whose opening passages recount the fate of ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of Lot, and the people of Pharaoh (54:9–42). The verse in front of us states the conclusion of those accounts in a single question.

**The Timing of the Punishment**

The verse states that the punishment came upon the communities by night or while they were resting at midday. The two times describe the hours in which people are settled and unguarded, and the choice of such hours makes the point that the punishment can arrive without further warning. A person who is asleep at night or resting in the heat of noon is not prepared for a reckoning, and the Book's description of the earlier communities repeatedly presents them as secure in their homes and their livelihoods when the command arrives.

The Book's usage of this hour appears in its accounts of particular peoples, where the punishment is said to have come in the morning or at the end of the night, and in the warnings of the sūrah itself. The audience of the sūrah lived among the ruins of the towns of the earlier peoples and travelled past them on its trade routes, and the verse takes that knowledge as its starting point. The question is not whether such destructions happened but how many of them stand in the record.

**Security and the Illusion of Safety**

The verse's force comes from the false confidence of the destroyed. The Book describes the people of the towns as feeling secure against the arrival of punishment, and it answers that confidence with the reminder that God's command arrives at the hour He has set. The sūrah of the Cattle repeats the theme when it asks whether the people of the towns are safe from the arrival of punishment while they sleep or while they are in the open during the day.

The same illusion is addressed throughout the sūrah's later passages, where the prophets warn their peoples and the peoples answer with mockery. ʿĀd claims its strength, Thamūd points to its dwellings carved in the rock, and the people of Lot rely on the protection of their clan. The verse in front of us summarises the outcome of all of those encounters before the chapter has told them.

**What the Destroyed Towns Establish**

The destruction of the communities serves the sūrah's argument about the truth of the message. The peoples who were destroyed had received messengers, and their destruction followed their rejection of what had been sent to them. The Book's accounts of these peoples therefore provide evidence for the audience in Makkah: the pattern of warning, rejection, and ruin is the pattern in which the present audience now stands.

The Book's use of the destroyed towns as evidence appears in the sūrah of the Bee, where the audience is told to travel through the earth and observe the end of those who denied, and in the sūrah of the Romans, where the fate of the peoples before them is placed in front of the hearer. The verse in front of us belongs to that family, and it states the fact on which the sūrah's history of the prophets will rest.

**The Book's Restraint in Retelling**

The verse states the destruction without lingering on its details, and that restraint runs through the sūrah's treatment of the earlier peoples. The Book's accounts of ʿĀd, Thamūd, and the people of Lot are brief, and their function is to establish the pattern rather than to satisfy curiosity about the ruin. The verse in front of us keeps to the same method: the question is asked, the timing is named, and the hearer is left to draw the conclusion.

The Book's habit of leaving the ruins themselves unvisited and the lesson in place appears in its instructions to consider the end of those who denied. The sūrah's audience passes by the ruins on its journeys, and the verse makes the ruins speak without describing the stones.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fourth in the chapter's opening section, and it follows the command to follow what has been sent down and the prohibition against taking protectors besides God. It supplies the consequence of refusing that command, and it prepares the account of the peoples that the sūrah will give in its middle section.

The verse that follows supplies the words the destroyed communities spoke when the punishment arrived, and the pair together state both the fact and the response. The opening section of the sūrah therefore moves from the revelation to the command, from the command to the warning, and from the warning to the confession of those who were overcome.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the plea of the communities, when the punishment came upon them, was only to say that they had been wrongdoers (7:5). The confession follows the destruction and arrives too late to change its outcome, and the sūrah presents it as the last thing the condemned say in the world.

The two verses together establish the sequence that the sūrah's later narratives will repeat. The warning is given, the message is rejected, the punishment arrives without notice, and the confession is spoken when nothing can be done with it. The reader who has been told to follow the revelation is shown the end of the path that refuses it.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:5

> Their only cry—when overwhelmed by Our torment—was, “We have indeed been wrongdoers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Cry Left to Them**

The verse states that the communities had nothing to say when the punishment came upon them except to confess that they had been wrongdoers. The Book gives the condemned several such confessions, and it places them at the moment when the outcome is already settled. The sūrah of the Prophets describes the wrongdoers saying that they have been unjust when the punishment touches them (21:46), and the sūrah of the Pen reports the owners of the garden saying that they were transgressors once their harvest was destroyed (68:29).

The Book's account of the people of Lot at the end of their sūrah reports a similar admission, and the sūrah of the Believers describes the condemned confessing their misery before their Lord. The verse in front of us states the pattern in its plainest form: one sentence, spoken at the moment of ruin, and it is the admission of the guilt that the community had denied all its life.

**Why the Confession Arrives Late**

The confession is not accepted as repentance, and the Book's treatment of the matter distinguishes between turning to God in the time of action and admitting the truth in the time of punishment. The sūrah of the Women states that repentance is not accepted from those who persist in evil until death comes upon them (4:18), and the sūrah of Jonah describes Pharaoh's profession of faith as he was drowning and states that it came too late to profit him (10:90). The confession of the destroyed towns belongs to the same category.

The sūrah states the sequence plainly: the punishment arrives, the community admits its guilt, and the punishment proceeds. What the confession does in the narrative is to establish the justice of the outcome. The condemned confirm the truth of the charge against them, and the reader sees that the destruction was not arbitrary.

**The Meaning of the Word Wrongdoers**

The term the verse places in the mouths of the condemned describes those who have wronged themselves, and the Book uses it for the people who have brought harm upon their own souls. The sūrah of the Cattle states that no soul bears the burden of another and that the wrongdoers are those who have forfeited their own selves. The Book's repeated phrase that God does not wrong people in the least, but that people wrong themselves, runs through the sūrah of the Family of Imran and the sūrah of Yūnus, and the confession in front of us uses the same vocabulary.

The confession is therefore accurate, and it is the truth the audience's own traditions about the destroyed towns preserve. The ruins the people of Makkah passed on their journeys stood as testimony to communities that had denied their messengers, and the verse in front of us supplies the words those communities spoke when they understood what they had done.

**The Silence of the Ruins**

The Book's treatment of the ruins uses their silence to make the same point. The sūrah of the Bee instructs the audience to travel through the earth and observe the end of those who denied the messengers, and the sūrah of the Ant describes the dwellings of the destroyed peoples as empty. The verse in front of us adds the one utterance that the ruins cannot reproduce, and it places it in the narrative as the last word of the condemned.

The sūrah's audience lived among people who boasted of their descent from tribes whose cities had been destroyed, and the verse turns that history into a warning. The confession of the ancestors is reported to the descendants, and the descendants are placed in the position of choosing whether they will also need it.

**The Pattern the Sūrah Will Repeat**

The middle section of the sūrah recounts the destruction of the people of Noah, ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of Lot, and the people of Shuʿayb, and each account follows the sequence stated in the opening verses: the messenger is sent, the people deny him, the punishment arrives, and the denial is abandoned too late. The verse in front of us states the sequence before the narratives supply their detail.

The Book's arrangement of these accounts in other sūrahs follows the same pattern, and the sūrah of Hūd presents the sequence of prophets and peoples in a form that culminates in the destruction of Pharaoh's hosts. The verse in front of us supplies the closing sentence of each of those stories, and its brevity gives it the force of a formula.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fifth in the chapter's opening section, and it supplies the response that followed the destruction described in the verse before it. The pair of verses states the fact of the punishment and the words of those who suffered it, and the two together close the first movement of the sūrah's introduction.

The verses that follow turn to the questioning of those who received the message and of the messengers themselves, and to the weighing of deeds on the day of judgment. The theme of accountability is thus opened by the confession of the destroyed communities, and the reader passes from the ruin of the world to the reckoning of the Hereafter.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that God will question both those to whom the message was sent and the messengers themselves (7:6). The passage moves from the admission of guilt in this world to the formal accounting of the next, and it names both parties in the transaction of revelation.

The two verses together state the two sides of the account: the recipients who were warned and the messengers who warned them. The sūrah has already described the sending down of the Book and the duty of the messenger to warn, and the verse that follows completes the arrangement by stating who will be asked about the delivery.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:6

> We will surely question those who received messengers and We will question the messengers ˹themselves˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Recipients Are Questioned**

The verse states that God will question those to whom the message was sent, and the questioning is placed after the destruction of the communities in the preceding verses. The Book mentions the interrogation of the recipients of revelation in several places: the sūrah of the Bee says that they will be questioned about what they used to do, and repeats the statement later in the same sūrah (16:56; 16:93); the sūrah of the Night Journey states that every person will be questioned about what he heard and saw (17:36); the sūrah of the Spider says that those who were given the message will be asked (29:13); and the sūrah of the Ornament says that the recipients of the reminder will be questioned (43:44).

The questioning is not the gathering of information, and the verse that follows states the reason plainly when it says that God will recount the deeds with knowledge and that He was never absent (7:7). The interrogation serves the reckoning that the sūrah describes in its following verses, where the scales are set and the deeds are weighed.

**The Messengers Are Questioned**

The verse adds that the messengers themselves will be questioned, and the tradition of the commentary explains that their questioning concerns the delivery of the message rather than the response of the people. The Book states in several passages that the duty of the Prophet is only to convey: the sūrah of the Family of Imran says the messenger's duty is to deliver (3:20), the sūrah of the Table repeats it twice (5:92; 5:99), the sūrah of the Thunder says the messenger's duty is only to deliver the message (13:40), and the sūrah of the Bee makes the same statement twice in a single passage (16:35; 16:82).

The distinction is important for the sūrah's argument. The messenger is not answerable for the refusal of his people, and the people are not relieved of responsibility by the messenger's diligence. The verse in front of us places both parties in the same hearing, and the reckoning of each is conducted on the terms of what each was given.

**The Questioning in the Qur'an and the Question It Raises**

The Book's statements about the judgment include passages that appear to deny any questioning at all. The sūrah of the Merciful says that on that day neither human nor jinn will be questioned about his sin (55:39), and the Book elsewhere describes the interrogation as a confrontation with a record that leaves nothing out. The tradition of commentary addresses the apparent conflict by distinguishing between the interrogation that establishes facts and the questioning that serves as rebuke.

The resolution rests on the Book's own description of the day. The deeds of every person are recorded in a book that he will be given in the Hereafter, and the sūrah of the Night Journey states that every person will be called with his record (17:71); the sūrah of the Inevitable describes the one who is given his record in his right hand and the one who is given it behind his back (69:19). What the questioning adds is the confrontation with the record, and what it produces is the acknowledgment that the condemned themselves supply.

**The Rebuke That the Questioning Serves**

The tradition of commentary understands the questioning of the Hereafter as a means of rebuke, and the Book's accounts of the day support that reading. The condemned admit their guilt, the followers blame their leaders, and the leaders blame the followers, in a sequence the sūrah itself describes later in the chapter (7:38–39). The questioning that opens the reckoning sets that exchange in motion.

The Book's description of the final address to the condemned includes the reminder of what they were given, and the sūrah of the Cave reports the words spoken to those who are brought before their Lord after their deeds have been presented. The verse in front of us states the principle behind those scenes: the recipients of revelation are asked, and the messengers are asked, and the record decides between them.

**What the Verse Says About the Community**

The verse speaks to the sūrah's audience about the position it occupies in the history of revelation. The community in Makkah has received a messenger, and the verse states that the receipt of a message is a matter that will be examined. The Book's warnings about the earlier communities therefore apply to the present audience on the terms the verse states, since the question the earlier communities answered late is the question this audience is asked now.

The Book's treatment of the community of the Prophet's followers in the same terms appears in the passages that describe the covenant taken from the children of Israel and the covenant taken from the believers. The verse in front of us states the common feature of every generation that has received a message: each is questioned about what it did with what it heard.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixth in the chapter's opening section, and it introduces the theme of the final accounting that the two following verses develop. The verses before it have described the destruction of the communities and their confession; the verses after it describe the knowledge with which God recounts the deeds and the justice with which the scales are set.

The reader of the sūrah's opening is thus given the full arc of the warning in six verses: the Book is sent down, the community is commanded to follow it, the communities that refused were destroyed, they confessed too late, and the reckoning of both the warned and the warners follows.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that God will recount to them their deeds with knowledge and that He was never absent (7:7). It supplies the ground on which the questioning of the previous verse proceeds, and it names the attribute of knowledge that makes the account complete.

The two verses together place the day of judgment in the hands of a Lord who knows. The question is asked and the record is exact, because nothing that the recipients of the message did was hidden from the One who sent it.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:7

> Then We will give them a full account with sure knowledge—for We were never absent.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Account Given With Knowledge**

The verse states that God will recount to the people what they did with knowledge, and the phrase names the ground of the whole reckoning. The Book describes the day of judgment as the day on which every person is informed of what he did, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that people will return to their Lord, who will inform them of what they differed about (6:164). The informing in the verse in front of us is described as proceeding from knowledge, which means that the account is complete and accurate in every particular.

The Book's language about the day includes the giving of a written record to every person. The sūrah of the Night Journey states that every person will be called with his record, and the sūrah of the Cave describes the record that is placed open, from which nothing great or small is omitted (18:49). The sūrah of the Inevitable describes the one who receives his record in his right hand, and the one who is given his record from behind his back (69:19; 84:10). The account in front of us is the reading and the explanation of that record.

**The Deeds That Are Recounted**

What is recounted is the whole conduct of a life, and the Book's description of the book of deeds states that it leaves out nothing. The sūrah of the Cattle says that God does not forget the weight of an atom, and the sūrah of the Bee states that God knows what is in every breast. The chapter of the Emigration describes the day when people will be shown their deeds, and the Book's repeated statements that God knows the hidden and the open appear in the sūrah of the Cattle itself.

The tradition of commentary notes that the recounting serves to establish the justice of the outcome rather than to supply information to God. The deed is named, its circumstances are stated, and the one who did it is left with no ground for denial. The verse in front of us states the character of that recounting: it is made with knowledge, and it is therefore complete.

**The Direct Address in the Sound Reports**

The sound reports describe the reckoning as a private conversation between the Lord and His servant, without an intermediary. The Prophet is reported to have said that there will be none among you but his Lord will speak to him, and that there will be no interpreter between them and no screen to screen Him (al-Bukhārī 7443); and in another narration, that each person will meet God without needing an interpreter between him and his Lord, who will ask him whether a messenger was sent to teach him and whether he was given wealth and favours (al-Bukhārī 3595). The reports place the recounting of deeds in the context of a face-to-face accounting.

The same reports describe the person looking to his right and his left and seeing only his own deeds and the fire before him, and they end with the instruction to guard against the fire even by giving half a date in charity. The verse in front of us states the principle that these reports illustrate, since the account proceeds from a Lord who knows and who addresses each person with what he knows.

**Never Absent**

The verse's closing clause states that God was never absent, and the phrase asserts the presence of the One who recounts. The sūrah of Iron says that God is with people wherever they are and that He sees all that they do (57:4), and the sūrah of the Cattle states that no leaf falls without His knowledge. The Book's use of the attribute in its discussions of the reckoning ties the exactness of the account to the constant presence of the One who keeps it.

The commentary tradition reads the clause as naming both God's knowledge of all things and His presence with His creation. The verse that precedes it describes the recounting as proceeding from knowledge, and the clause in front of us names the presence from which nothing escapes. The two attributes together make the day of judgment a settling of accounts rather than a reconstruction of events.

**What the Verse Contributes to the Warning**

The verse follows the questioning of the recipients of the message and of the messengers, and it supplies the reason the questioning can be conclusive. The sūrah's opening has described the destruction of the communities in the world and the confession they made when it was too late; the verse in front of us moves the reckoning to the day when nothing can be concealed and no excuse can be invented.

The Book's use of the same theme appears in its warnings to the audience of the Prophet. The chapter of the Bee says that God will ask the recipients of the message about their response, and the sūrah of the Cattle says that the people who forget God will be informed of what they did. The verse in front of us states the basis of those warnings.

**The Reckoning and the Record**

The Book's presentation of the record gives weight to the sūrah's insistence on the revelation that has been sent down. The community is told to follow what has come to it, and the verse in front of us states that the same revelation's audience will have to give an account of what it did with the message. The knowledge of God and the record of deeds meet in the verse, and the audience is left with the plain statement that its conduct is written.

The Book's descriptions of the day include the testimony of the limbs and the tongues of the condemned, and the sūrah of the Heights itself will describe the dialogue between the people of Paradise and the people of the Fire. This verse supplies the foundation of those scenes, since a reckoning conducted with knowledge requires no witnesses beyond the Lord who speaks.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventh in the chapter's opening section, and it completes the pair formed with the verse before it. The questioning is stated in the sixth verse, the recounting in the seventh, and the scales are set in the eighth and ninth. The opening section of the sūrah thus presents the full sequence of the last judgment in four verses.

The reader who has been told to follow what has been sent down is now given the account he will face if he does not. The sūrah's warning does not rest on the ruins of the earlier cities alone; it rests on the knowledge of the Lord who has sent the message and who will recount what each community did with it.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the weighing on that day will be just, and that those whose scales are heavy will be the successful (7:8). The passage in front of us has stated that the deeds are recounted with knowledge; the verse that follows states the standard by which they are weighed.

The two verses together describe the reckoning in its parts: the account is complete and the measure is just. The sūrah then proceeds to the fate of the two groups, and the reader is left with the plain bookkeeping of the day on which no one is wronged.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:8

> The weighing on that Day will be just. As for those whose scale will be heavy ˹with good deeds˺, ˹only˺ they will be successful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Weighing Set on That Day**

The verse states that the weighing on that day is just, and the phrase identifies the judgment with a balance. The Book uses the image in several passages about the last things: the sūrah of the Prophets says that God will set up the scales of justice on the day of resurrection, so that no soul will be wronged in the least (21:47); the sūrah of the Believers states that those whose scales are heavy will prosper and those whose scales are light will lose their souls (23:102–3); and the sūrah of the Clatterer describes the same division between the one whose scale is heavy and the one whose scale is light (101:6–9).

The image gives the reckoning its public and measurable character. The deeds are not weighed by a standard that is chosen for the occasion; the verse states that the weighing itself is just, and it fixes the outcome to the balance that has been set. The commentary tradition reads the phrase as a statement of God's justice, since the measure is applied without partiality.

**Whose Scales Are Heavy**

The verse states that those whose balance is heavy are the ones who will prosper, and the Book's usage of the image indicates that the weight belongs to good deeds. The sūrah of the Cattle has stated the principle of the reckoning in the multiplication of rewards before this scene, and the sūrah of the Bee describes the reward of the believers in terms of what they have earned. The heavy scale is therefore the record of a life in which the good outweighed the evil.

The Book's statements about the weight of deeds include the report that the deeds of the believers are made heavy by what they have done in obedience, and the commentary tradition notes that the weight is not measured by quantity alone. The verse in front of us does not describe the deeds themselves but the outcome of the measuring, and it leaves the content of the scale to the rest of the revelation.

**The Sound Report on Good Character**

The Prophet is reported to have said that nothing placed on the scale is heavier than good character, and that the person of good character attains the rank of the one who fasts and prays (al-Tirmidhī 2003); another narration of the same Companion's report states that nothing is heavier upon the scale of a believer on the day of resurrection than his good character (al-Tirmidhī 2002). The reports identify the deeds that carry weight in the balance, and they place conduct toward people among the heaviest of them.

The Book's own emphasis on conduct alongside worship appears in its repeated pairing of faith with righteous action, and the sūrah of the Cave places the believers who do good in the same category. The verse in front of us states the outcome; the reports name one of the things that fills the scale.

**The Use of the Balance in the Book's Teaching**

The image of the balance runs through the Book's instructions about trade and measurement as well as its descriptions of the judgment. The sūrah of the Cattle commands the giving of full measure and weight with justice (6:152), the sūrah of the Heights itself commands Shuʿayb's people to give full measure and weight and not to defraud people of their goods (7:85), the sūrah of Hūd repeats the command (11:84–85), the sūrah of the Night Journey states that the measure should be given in full (17:35), the sūrah of the Poets repeats it (26:182–83), and the sūrah of the Merciful states that God has set up the balance and commands that it not be transgressed (55:7–9).

The connection between the two uses of the image gives the verse its force for its first audience. A community of traders that adjusted its scales for profit is told that its conduct is measured on a scale it did not control. The Book elsewhere states that God created all things in due measure and sent down the balance (42:17; 57:25), and the reckoning in front of us applies the same order to human acts.

**Why the Deeds Are Weighed**

The tradition of commentary explains that the balance does not supply God with information, since the deeds are already known and recorded. Its function is to confront the wrongdoer with the accuracy of the outcome, so that he acknowledges the justice of what has been decided and abandons the excuses he would otherwise offer. The Book's descriptions of the condemned include their admissions of guilt, and the verse in front of us supplies the ground of those admissions.

The image serves the same purpose for the reader of the sūrah. The verse that precedes this one has stated that the account is given with knowledge, and the verse in front of us adds that the account is measured with justice. Nothing in the arrangement leaves room for a complaint about the standard.

**The Heaviness and the Lightness**

The verse names only the heavy scale and its reward, and the verse that follows names the light scale and its loss. The Book's habit is to state the two outcomes together, as it does in the sūrah of the Believers and in the sūrah of the Clatterer, and the sūrah in front of us follows the same pattern. The reader is given the reward of the heavy scale in one verse and the ruin of the light scale in the next.

The light scale is not the absence of all good but the insufficiency of what was brought against what was earned. The Book's statements about the deeds of the deniers describe them as dust scattered and as a mirage in a plain, and the sūrah of the Light states that the deeds of those who disbelieve are like a mirage in a desert which the thirsty takes for water until he comes to it and finds nothing. The verse in front of us states the outcome for those who arrive with weight.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eighth in the chapter's opening, and it follows the description of the questioning and the recounting. The verses that come after it state the fate of those whose scales are light, and the passage then turns to the establishment of people on the earth and the provision placed in it. The balance in front of us is the hinge on which the opening section's account of the last day turns.

The reader who has been told to follow what has been sent down is now given the standard by which his following will be measured. The deeds are recorded, the account is exact, and the weight is set in the balance.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that those whose balance is light are the ones who have ruined their souls by treating the signs of God wrongfully (7:9). The verse names the conduct that produces the light scale, and it ties the loss to the denial of the signs rather than to any deficiency in the measure.

The two verses together present the division of humanity at the reckoning. One group prospers by the weight it brings, and the other loses itself by what it denied. The sūrah's opening then states the provision that has been placed on the earth and the gratitude that is owed for it.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:9

> But those whose scale is light, they have doomed themselves for wrongfully denying Our signs.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Light Scale**

The verse names the second half of the division that the previous verse opened, and it states that those whose balance is light are the ones who have lost their souls. The Book uses the light scale in the same way elsewhere: the sūrah of the Believers states that those whose scales are light are the ones who lose themselves and abide in Hell (23:102–3), and the sūrah of the Clatterer states that the one whose scale is light will have his refuge in a pit (101:8–9). The image of the balance is thus applied consistently to the two groups.

The lightness is described as the absence of weight, and the Book's presentation of the matter does not suggest that the measure failed. The balance is exact, and what the person brought to it was insufficient. The verse in front of us identifies the state of the one who arrives at the reckoning with a record that does not outweigh his evil.

**The Ruin That Is Named**

The verse states that they have ruined their souls, and the phrase describes a loss that the person has inflicted on himself. The Book uses the language of loss for the condemned throughout, and the sūrah of the Cave states that those whose efforts went astray in the life of the world will think they are doing well, and that they are the ones who reject the signs of their Lord and the meeting with Him, so their deeds are void (18:103–5). The ruin in front of us is of that kind.

The Book's treatment of the self as the subject of loss appears in its repeated statement that God does not wrong people, but that people wrong themselves. The verse in front of us states the outcome of that self-wronging: the soul is the thing that is forfeited, and no one else bears the cost.

**The Wrongful Denial of the Signs**

The verse names the cause of the loss as the wrongful treatment of God's signs, and the phrase covers the denial of the revelation and the rejection of the evidence that supports it. The Book uses the phrase the signs of God for the verses of the Quran, for the phenomena of the created order, and for the fate of the earlier communities. The sūrah of the Cattle describes the signs of the heavens and the earth that people pass by without attention, and it states that those who deny the signs of their Lord and the meeting with Him have wasted their works.

The denial is described as wrongful because the evidence for the signs was available. The sūrah in front of us presents the history of the communities, the created order, and the messenger's testimony as the material from which the audience could have drawn the right conclusion, and the verse states that the loss followed a denial that was not innocent.

**Where the Deeds of the Deniers Go**

The Book describes the deeds of those who deny as material that loses its substance, and its images for the loss are several. The sūrah of the Light states that the deeds of those who disbelieve are like a mirage in a desert which the thirsty takes for water until he reaches it and finds it to be nothing. The sūrah of the Cave states that the works of the deniers are scattered dust, and the sūrah of the Bee describes their deeds as ashes on which the wind blows on a stormy day.

The imagery explains the light scale without diminishing the seriousness of the reckoning. The deeds were performed, and the record contains them; what they lack is the weight that would have carried them through the measure. The verse in front of us states the outcome, and the Book's images supply the picture of what was placed on the scale.

**The Fairness of the Outcome**

The verse's description of the loss follows the previous verse's statement that the weighing is just, and the two verses are meant to be read together. The loss is not the product of an uneven balance but of a record that was light. The Book's statement in the sūrah of the Prophets that no soul will be wronged in the least on the day of the scales (21:47) establishes the same principle.

The tradition of commentary treats the balance as an assurance to human beings rather than a device for the divine judgment, and the verse in front of us supports that reading. The one who loses is told exactly what he lost and why, and the explanation removes any complaint about the standard.

**What the Audience Takes From the Verse**

The sūrah's audience has been warned about the destruction of the earlier communities and has been told to follow the revelation that has come to it. The verse in front of us states what the refusal of that revelation produces in the long run, and it places the outcome in the balance of the reckoning rather than in the ruins of the world. The two warnings together cover both the life of the world and the life to come.

The Book's presentation of the two outcomes as a division appears in the sūrahs that describe the two groups in adjacent verses, and the sūrah of the Heights places the same division at the head of the chapter. The reader who follows the argument to its end in the sūrah's later passages finds the same two groups described in the dialogue between the people of Paradise and the people of the Fire.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands ninth in the chapter's opening section, and it completes the pair that describes the weighing of deeds. The verses that follow turn to the establishment of people on the earth and the provision that has been placed there, and the account of Adam's creation begins with the eleventh verse. The balance in front of us closes the sequence of the judgment that the chapter's opening had set out.

The reader of the sūrah has now been given the letters, the Book, the command, the warning, the destruction of the communities, the questioning, the account, and the weighing. The chapter's program is stated, and the narratives that fill the rest of it are the working out of the terms.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that God has established people on the earth, provided them with means of livelihood, and that they seldom give thanks (7:10). The verse turns from the reckoning to the present life, and it names the provision that makes the denial of the signs a matter of ingratitude rather than ignorance.

The two verses together place the audience between two accounts: the record of what it does and the provision by which it lives. The sūrah's opening section then moves into the story of Adam, where the same themes of command, refusal, and consequence are presented in their first form.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:10

> We have indeed established you on earth and provided you with a means of livelihood. ˹Yet˺ you seldom give any thanks.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Established Upon the Earth**

The verse is addressed to people generally, and it states that God has established them on the earth. The phrase carries both a spatial and a political sense, since the establishment of a people can mean the provision of a dwelling and also the granting of a measure of authority in the world. The Book uses the language of establishment for the communities that were given a place and a term, and the sūrah of the Cattle states at its close that God has placed people as successors on the earth (6:165).

The Book's account of the establishment of the first human being in the Garden is followed by his descent to the earth, where the sūrah of the Cow reports the statement that he would find in it a residence and provision for a time (2:36). The verse in front of us states the same arrangement for the audience, and it places the provision of the earth in the same sentence as the establishment upon it.

**The Means of Livelihood Placed Therein**

The verse names the provision that has been placed in the earth for the people who live on it, and the Book's accounts of the created order describe that provision at length. The sūrah of the Cattle lists the produce of the earth, the livestock, and the fruits of the palm, and the sūrah of the Bee describes the rain that brings out every kind of crop. The verse in front of us summarises the arrangement in a single phrase, and the chapter's later passages on food and livestock fill it in.

The provision is described as something made ready rather than something achieved. The Book's passages about the harvest and the rain present them as gifts, and the sūrah of the Prophets states that God has made the earth a place of settlement and the sky a canopy. The verse in front of us places the audience in a world already supplied, and the rebuke that follows rests on that fact.

**An Introduction to the Story of Adam**

The verse serves as the introduction to the account of Adam's creation and fall that begins with the following verse, and the Book joins the two themes in its other accounts of the same story. The sūrah of the Cow reports the announcement of a deputy on the earth before it recounts the prostration of the angels, and the sūrah of Hūd states that God established people on the earth and gave them provision after the destruction of Noah's people. The place of the verse in the sūrah's arrangement is therefore deliberate.

The account of Adam that follows explains how the earth came to be inhabited by the audience that is addressed here. The command to the angels, the refusal of Iblīs, the temptation, and the descent are all presented as the opening of the history that the sūrah has already begun with the destroyed communities. This verse states the position in which that history leaves the present generation.

**Gratitude and Its Absence**

The verse closes with the rebuke that the people seldom give thanks, and the Book uses the complaint in the same contexts elsewhere. The sūrah of the Ant states that most people do not give thanks, the sūrah of the Sheba repeats the complaint in its account of David's family, and the sūrah of Yūnus states that God is full of grace toward people but that most of them do not give thanks.

The complaint is directed at the failure to draw the connection between the gift and the Giver. The Book's passages about the created order present the rain, the crops, and the livestock as evidence of the One who provides, and the verse in front of us states that the people seldom make the connection. The sūrah's later passages return to the same complaint in their treatment of the harvest and the herds.

**The Provision and the Signs**

The Book places the provision of the earth among its signs, and the sūrah of the Cattle describes the variety of the crops and the livestock as a sign for people who believe. The passage in front of us supplies the premise of that argument: the audience lives on an earth that has been prepared for it, and the preparation is the work of the Lord whose revelation it is being asked to follow.

The signs are therefore not confined to the sky. The Book's habit of drawing evidence from the crops, the herds, and the fruits places the argument in the audience's daily experience, and the sūrah of the Bee describes honey, oil, and the fruits of the palm as signs for people who reflect. The verse under discussion begins that sequence for this chapter.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands tenth in the chapter's opening, and it closes the introductory section that runs from the letters to the account of the reckoning. The following verse begins the narrative of Adam's creation and Iblīs's refusal, and the verses after that continue the story to the descent from the Garden and the mission of the messengers.

The reader has been given the Book, the command, the warning, the reckoning, and now the world he inhabits. The provision in front of him is stated as a gift, and the gratitude that it calls for is named as the thing that is missing.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that God created the people and shaped them, and then commanded the angels to prostrate before Adam, and that they all prostrated except Iblīs, who refused (7:11). The verse opens the account of the first human being, and it follows the mention of the earth and its provision.

The two verses together establish the position of the audience within the created order: the earth has been made habitable for them, and their first ancestor was honoured above the angels. The sūrah then recounts how the honour was tested, and the reader is given the story of the refusal that the audience is being warned against repeating.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:11

> Surely We created you, then shaped you, then said to the angels, “Prostrate before Adam,” so they all did—but not Iblîs, who refused to prostrate with the others.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Account of Adam**

The verse opens the sūrah's account of the creation of Adam, his temptation, and his descent from the Garden, and it is the second of the four extended versions of that story in the Book, following the account in the sūrah of the Cow and preceding the versions in the sūrah of the Night Journey and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā (2:30–39; 17:61–65; 20:115–24). The same narrative is told in shorter form in the sūrah of the Rocky Tract and the sūrah of Ṣād, where the creation of the first human being and the refusal of Iblīs are described without naming Adam in the opening lines (15:28–43; 38:71–85).

The four accounts differ in their details and agree in their structure: the creation, the command to the angels, the prostration, the refusal of Iblīs, his request for respite, and his vow to mislead. The account in front of us is the longest of them, and it continues into the following verses with the temptation of Adam and his wife, their exposure, their repentance, and their descent to the earth.

**Created and Shaped**

The verse addresses all human beings when it says that God created them and then shaped them. The commentary tradition reads the first verb as referring to the creation of humanity through the creation of Adam, and the second as referring to the formation of each individual, whether in the womb or in the loins of Adam. The two verbs together state the double claim that the Book makes on its audience: they were brought into being, and they were given their particular form by the same hand.

The Book's accounts of the creation of the first human being describe the stages by which he was formed, and the sūrah of Ṣād states that God created him from clay with His two hands and asked Iblīs why he had not prostrated before what God had made with His own hands (38:75). The sūrah of the Rocky Tract describes the creation from clay and the breathing of the divine spirit into the form (15:28–29), and the sūrah of the Prostration repeats the breathing of the spirit as the completion of the human being (32:9). The verse in front of us states the fact of the creation before the account of the honour that followed it.

**The Command to the Angels**

The verse reports the command given to the angels to prostrate before Adam, and the command is a key element in every account of the story in the Book. The sūrah of the Cow reports the same command and the same prostration (2:34), and the sūrah of the Night Journey, the sūrah of Ṭā Hā, and the sūrah of Ṣād repeat them. The tradition of commentary understands the prostration as a prostration of honour rather than of worship, since the Book forbids the worship of anything other than God.

The Book provides an illustration of prostration as honour in its account of Joseph's family, where his parents and brothers fall down before him in the fulfilment of his dream (12:100). The command to the angels therefore honours Adam without displacing the worship that belongs to God, and the sūrah's warning about taking protectors besides God is not compromised by the scene.

**The Refusal of Iblīs**

The verse states that all the angels prostrated except Iblīs, who was not among those who prostrated. The Book's phrasing here places Iblīs in the company of the angels, and the sūrah of the Cave, in an identical context, states that he was of the jinn (18:50). The two statements have produced a long discussion among the commentators about his classification, and the discussion is settled by naming him in the same sentence as the angels while also reporting his kind.

What the verse establishes is the fact of the refusal and its position at the head of the sūrah's narrative. The account that follows explains the reason Iblīs gave and the outcome of his pride, and the sūrah's later passages return to his vow to mislead the descendants of Adam. The verse in front of us states the refusal in its plainest form, and the reason is given in the next verse.

**Why the Scene Is Placed Here**

The scene of the prostration and the refusal follows the verses about the destroyed communities and the reckoning, and it supplies the sūrah's deeper explanation of the waywardness it spends the chapter describing. The Book's accounts of the refusal present it as the origin of the enmity between the human being and the tempter, and the sūrah's warnings about the satans as protectors in place of God return to the same event.

The sūrah's reader has been told to follow what has been sent down and not to take protectors besides God. The narrative that begins in front of us supplies the history of the protector who refused the command, and the sūrah's later passages identify him as the enemy whose promises are delusion.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eleventh in the chapter, and it begins the second movement of the sūrah after the opening section. The verses that follow continue the account through the questioning of Iblīs, his sentence, his request for respite, his vow, and the temptation of the first human pair, and the passage then turns to the instruction given to the children of Adam about clothing, food, and conduct.

The transition from the accounting of the last day to the story of the first human being is the sūrah's method. The warning is stated in the abstract, and then it is shown operating in the history of the beginning of the human race.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the question put to Iblīs about what prevented him from prostrating when he was commanded, and it reports his answer that he was better than Adam because he was created from fire and Adam from clay (7:12). The refusal stated in front of us is given its reason, and the reason is the pride that the sūrah will name in the verse after that.

The two verses together present the first act of disobedience and its justification. The reader who has been told to follow the revelation sees the alternative in its original form: a creature who measured himself against another and set his own reasoning above the command he had received.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:12

> Allah asked, “What prevented you from prostrating when I commanded you?” He replied, “I am better than he is: You created me from fire and him from clay.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Put to Iblīs**

The verse reports the question put to Iblīs about what prevented him from prostrating when he was commanded, and the question exposes the gap between the command and the answer. The Book puts the same question in its other accounts of the scene, and the sūrah of Ṣād records the words that God would ask him whether he was too proud or had become one of the exalted (38:75). The question in front of us is not an inquiry after information; it is the setting out of the charge.

The account's arrangement places the command first and the explanation second. The Book does not present Iblīs as ignorant of what was required of him, and his answer confirms that he understood the command exactly. What the verse establishes is that the refusal was deliberate, and that the reasoning behind it is offered as an excuse.

**I Am Better Than Him**

The answer Iblīs gives is a claim of superiority, and the Book's accounts of the scene present it as the source of the refusal. He measures himself against Adam by the substance from which each was created, and he draws the conclusion that the command does not apply to him. The Book's ethical teaching treats such self-measurement as a form of pride, and the sūrah states in the next verse that the Garden is no place for arrogance.

The claim is stated without any reference to the command that preceded it. The reasoning runs from his own assessment of the two natures to a conclusion about his obligation, and the Book's account gives the reader the anatomy of an excuse that many later people repeat. The sūrah's warnings about following the customs of the ancestors and about the protectors taken in place of God are versions of the same operation.

**Fire and Clay**

The verse states the comparison on which the claim rests: Iblīs was created from fire and Adam from clay. The Book's account of the creation of the jinn from smokeless fire appears in the sūrah of the Merciful (55:15), and the accounts of Adam's creation from clay, dust, and sounding mud appear in several sūrahs. The materials are named by Iblīs himself, and the tradition of commentary develops the contrast between them.

The qualities the commentators attach to the two substances turn Iblīs's argument against him. Fire is described as luminous and subtle but also as restless, fickle, and destructive; clay is described as low and heavy but also as patient, stable, and humble. The sūrah's narrative shows which of the two qualities leads to repentance: Adam admits his wrong and asks forgiveness, while Iblīs argues about his own merit. The verse in front of us supplies the reasoning that produced the difference.

**The Nobility That Iblīs Ignored**

The commentary tradition notes that Iblīs's argument fixed on one aspect of the matter and ignored the rest. The Book states that Adam was created with God's two hands (38:75), that the divine spirit was breathed into him (15:29; 32:9), and that he was taught the names of all things (2:31). Each of these is a mark of honour that has nothing to do with the substance of his body, and Iblīs's comparison takes account of none of them.

The Book's treatment of human nobility elsewhere rests on the same facts, and the sūrah of the Night Journey states that God has honoured the children of Adam and carried them by land and sea. The verse in front of us reports the reasoning that sets all of that aside, and the narrative then states the consequence.

**Reasoning Against a Command**

The Book's narrative shows Iblīs using an argument in the face of an explicit instruction, and the tradition of commentary notices the shape of the operation. The argument is drawn from analogy: fire is nobler than clay, and therefore the one made of fire should not bow to the one made of clay. Its defect lies not in analogical reasoning as such but in its use against a direct command, and the commentary treats the scene as a warning about the order in which arguments and commandments are ranked.

The lesson is drawn out by the sūrah's audience as well, since the people of Makkah had their own arguments for retaining their practices.
