The claim that the forefathers did a thing, the claim that God commanded it, and the claim that a practice is honourable in itself all appear in the sūrah's later passages, and each of them is answered on the same ground. The verse in front of us supplies the first and most famous example of the reasoning that sets itself above the revelation.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands twelfth in the chapter, and it follows the report of the prostration and the refusal. The verse that comes after it states the sentence passed on Iblīs, and the passage then continues with his request for respite and his vow to mislead. The exchange in front of us is the turning point of the narrative, since it converts a single act of disobedience into a declared position.

The reader of the sūrah is given the command, the refusal, and the excuse in three short verses. The pattern is then repeated in the sūrah's accounts of the prophets and their peoples, where the message is delivered, the people refuse, and the refusal is supported by an argument about ancestry, custom, or status.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the command given to Iblīs to descend from the Garden, and it states that arrogance has no place there and that he is among the humbled (7:13). The excuse offered in front of us is answered not by refuting the argument but by removing the one who made it.

The two verses together state the consequence of pride in the presence of a command. The reader who has heard the argument about fire and clay is shown where it leads, and the sūrah's account then proceeds to the request for delay that Iblīs makes next.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:13

> Allah said, “Then get down from Paradise! It is not for you to be arrogant here. So get out! You are truly one of the disgraced.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Descend**

The verse reports the sentence passed on Iblīs, and the command to descend is given in the same terms in the sūrah of the Cow, where the command is addressed to him and to Adam and his wife (2:36), and in the sūrah of Ṭā Hā, where it is given to the first human pair (20:123). The descent in front of us removes Iblīs from the Garden, and the sūrah later states that he retained his presence there long enough to tempt the first pair, since the temptation is recounted in the verses that follow this exchange.

The Book's accounts of the scene agree that the refusal was answered with expulsion. In the sūrah of Ṣād, the sentence includes the curse that rests on Iblīs until the day of judgment (38:78), and in the sūrah of the Rocky Tract the same elements appear. The verse in front of us states the removal and names the character that produced it.

**Arrogance Has No Place There**

The verse states that it is not for Iblīs to be arrogant in the Garden, and the phrase gives the reason for the sentence. The Garden is a place of obedience and humility, and the one who refuses a command out of pride cannot remain in it. The commentary tradition draws from the verse the conclusion that arrogance is a barrier to the paradisal state, and it cites the Book's repeated warnings against those who are too proud to worship.

The Book's descriptions of the condemned include the proud among them, and the sūrah of the Heights later states that those who deny the signs and are too proud to accept them will not enter the Garden (7:40). The verse in front of us names the pride that produced the first refusal, and the chapter's account then shows the same pride operating in the audience it addresses.

**The Humiliation Named**

The verse's final phrase states that Iblīs is among the humbled, and the Book uses the word for those who are brought low. The sūrah of the Cow records the curse that accompanies his expulsion, and the sūrah of Ṣād states that the curse will remain on him until the day of judgment (38:78). The humiliation is the direct consequence of the claim of superiority that the previous verse reports.

The contrast between the claim and the outcome gives the account its force. The one who argued that fire is nobler than clay is described as disgraced and expelled, and the Book later reports his vow to lead humanity astray as the activity of a creature whose own position is settled. The verse in front of us states the reversal in three words.

**The Pattern of Pride in the Book**

The Book's warnings against pride run through its accounts of the communities that rejected their messengers, and the sūrah of the Heights will describe those communities in its middle section. The people of ʿĀd refused the message and relied on their strength, the people of Thamūd relied on their carved dwellings, and the people of Pharaoh relied on their kingdom. The commentary tradition treats each of these as a version of Iblīs's reasoning, since each set a claim of status above the command of God.

The sūrah of the Bee states that God does not like the arrogant, and the sūrah of the Troops states that those who are too proud to worship will enter Hell in humiliation (16:23; 40:60). This verse supplies the first and clearest instance of the pattern, and the sūrah returns to it when it describes those who treat the signs with disdain.

**Grace and Repentance Contrasted**

The account in front of us is completed by the response that Adam and his wife give later in the same passage, when they admit their wrong and ask for forgiveness (7:23). The two responses to the same kind of failure produce the sūrah's teaching about repentance, and the commentary tradition sets the pride of Iblīs against the humility of Adam.

The Book's treatment of repentance in other passages rests on the same distinction. The sūrah of the Cave states that God is forgiving to the one who repents and believes and does right, and the sūrah of the Troops tells those who have exceeded all limits against their own souls not to despair of the mercy of God (39:53). The expulsion in front of us is the answer given to the one who preferred an argument to an apology.

**What the Verse Says to the Audience**

The audience of the sūrah lived in a society in which claims of lineage, wealth, and standing settled most questions, and the sūrah addresses those claims throughout its argument. The passage in front of us states that the first refusal of a divine command was made on exactly such grounds, and it shows where the reasoning led. The reader who recognizes the reasoning is given the warning in advance of the sūrah's own account of the arrogant communities.

The Book's practice of teaching through the fate of the earlier generations appears throughout the sūrah, and this scene is the first of its narratives. The status that Iblīs claimed was real in his own estimation and worthless in the scales of the command, and the chapter's remaining passages apply the same measure to the great ones of Makkah and to the peoples that preceded them.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands thirteenth in the chapter, and it concludes the exchange that began with the report of the prostration and the refusal. The verses that follow report the request of Iblīs for delay and the grant of it, and then his vow to lie in wait for the descendants of Adam. The scene in front of us is the moment at which the antagonist of the sūrah's human story is separated from the company of the obedient.

The reader has been given the command, the refusal, the excuse, and the sentence. The sūrah then turns to what Iblīs does with the time that remains to him, and the account of his activity explains the warnings that the sūrah has given about the protectors taken in place of God.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the request of Iblīs that his end be delayed until the day of the resurrection (7:14), and the verse after it reports the grant of that delay (7:15). The request is made by the one who has just been sentenced, and it is granted, and the consequence is that the enmity between him and the descendants of Adam has the whole span of the world in which to operate.

The two verses together state the arrangement under which the sūrah's later warnings make sense. The tempter is not destroyed at the outset; he is given time, and the human being is given the revelation and the reckoning. The sūrah's reader is placed in the middle of that arrangement, and the passage that follows states the tempter's plan in his own words.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:14

> He appealed, “Then delay my end until the Day of their resurrection.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Request for Delay**

The verse reports the request that Iblīs made after he was sentenced, that his end be delayed until the day on which the dead are raised. The request does not ask for forgiveness, and the Book's other accounts of the scene present the same petition: the sūrah of the Rocky Tract reports the request to be delayed until the day of the appointed time (15:36), the sūrah of the Night Journey reports it as a request to be delayed until the day of resurrection (17:62), and the sūrah of Ṣād reports the same request with the same answer (38:79).

The Book's narrative places the petition after the sentence and before the vow, and the arrangement tells the reader what the granted time will be used for. The one who asks for delay does not intend to change his position; the verse that follows his vow makes his purpose explicit. The request in front of us is the request of an enemy for the opportunity to continue.

**What the Request Reveals**

The request reveals that Iblīs has no hope of restoration. The confession he could have made is not made, and the pardon he could have sought is not sought. The Book's accounts of the condemned in the Hereafter include the same refusal to ask for what would help them, and the sūrah of the Troops and the sūrah of the Family of Imran describe the despair of those who meet their reckoning with excuses instead of repentance.

The commentary tradition notes the connection between the name of Iblīs and the sense of despair that it may carry, and the narrative in front of us supports the connection. The request for time is not a request for time to amend; it is a request for time to work against the creature whose honour he resented. The verse states that request without commentary, and the reader is left to draw the conclusion from what follows.

**Adam's Response Compared**

The Book's account of the same events contains a second response to the divine command, and the comparison gives the passage its teaching. When Adam and his wife disobey, they turn immediately to their Lord with the admission that they have wronged themselves and with the prayer for forgiveness and mercy (7:23). Iblīs, when he disobeys, argues that his substance is nobler than Adam's and then asks for time to continue.

The two responses divide the sūrah's characters, and the division recurs in the sūrah's accounts of the prophets and their peoples. The people of Noah, ʿĀd, and Thamūd are given messengers and respond with mockery and demands for proof; the prophets themselves respond to rejection with patience and with continued warning. The verse in front of us states the reaction of the one who has resolved to remain what he is.

**The Day Named in the Request**

The day to which the delay is asked to extend is the day of the raising of the dead, and the Book uses several names for it in its accounts of the last things. The sūrah of the Heights itself will describe the weighing, the questioning, and the dialogue between the people of Paradise and the people of the Fire; the sūrah of the Clatterer, the sūrah of the Inevitable, and the sūrah of the Resurrection give the day its names and its scenes.

The request places the whole history of humanity between the sentence passed on Iblīs and the day he named. The Book's account of the enmity between the tempter and the human being therefore occupies that interval, and the sūrah's address to its own audience falls inside it. The verse in front of us states the length of the interval in the words of the one who asked for it.

**The Silence of the Verse About the Reason**

The verse gives the request without stating the motive, and the omission is filled by the verses that follow. The vow to lie in wait and the approach from every direction make the purpose clear, and the sūrah's later passages describe the methods that the tempter uses. The verse in front of us keeps to the petition, and the reader who continues finds the answer and the plan in sequence.

The Book's narrative style in such passages leaves the motives of the wicked to be inferred from their words and acts. The same restraint appears in the accounts of the peoples who demanded signs from their prophets, where the demand itself exposes what is behind it. The verse in front of us applies the method to Iblīs.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fourteenth in the chapter, and it follows the sentence passed on Iblīs in the verse before it. The grant of the request is reported in the verse that follows, and the vow comes immediately after that. The passage in front of us is the hinge between the expulsion and the activity that the rest of the sūrah describes.

The reader who has followed the sūrah from its opening has been given the Book, the command, the warning, the reckoning, and the story of the first refusal. The scene in front of us adds the fact of the interval between the refusal and the end, and the chapter's warnings are addressed to the people who live inside that interval.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the answer given to Iblīs, that he is among those who are granted delay (7:15). The answer is short, and it establishes that the request was permitted and that the tempter's activity takes place within a term that has been set.

The two verses together state the terms on which the enmity operates. The petition is made and granted, and the reader of the sūrah knows from the verses that follow both the purpose of the one who asked and the end that awaits him. The sūrah then reports his plan in his own words, and the account of his approach from every direction follows.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:15

> Allah said, “You are delayed ˹until the appointed Day˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Given to Iblīs**

The verse reports the answer to the petition of the previous verse, and the answer grants what was asked. The Book's other accounts of the scene report the same grant with the addition of the term: the sūrah of the Rocky Tract states that Iblīs is of those granted respite until the day of the time appointed (15:37–38), the sūrah of Ṣād gives the same answer in the same words (38:80–81), and the sūrah of the Night Journey reports the request for delay without the answer. The sūrah in front of us states the grant in a single sentence.

The answer settles the terms of the interval. Iblīs is not destroyed at the moment of his refusal, and he is not free of the term that has been set for him. The verse in front of us states that the delay is granted and that its end is fixed, and the narrative then reports what he does with the time.

**The Appointed Day**

The day named in the answer is the day of the resurrection, and the Book's accounts of it supply the names and descriptions that the verse leaves out. The sūrah of the Clatterer opens with the day on which people will be like scattered moths (101:4–5), the sūrah of the Inevitable describes the falling of the event that has been denied (56:1–3), and the sūrah of the Overwhelming describes the faces of the day, some humbled and some blessed (88:2–9). The term in front of us ends at the day on which those scenes take place.

The Book's statements about the timing of the resurrection place it in the knowledge of God alone, and the sūrah of the Heights itself will state that no soul knows what it has been prepared for. The answered petition in front of us fixes the outer limit of the interval that the whole history of the human race occupies.

**Respite as a Fixed Practice**

The Book describes the delay of punishment as a settled practice of its Lord, and it applies the description to the communities that refused their prophets. The sūrah of the Thunder states that many messengers were mocked before the Prophet and that God granted respite to those who disbelieved and then took them, and it asks how the taking was (13:32). The sūrah of the Family of Imran warns the believers not to think that the respite given to the disbelievers is good for them, since it is granted so that they may increase in sin (3:178). Sūrat al-Aʿrāf itself will describe the communities that were taken by surprise after their respite had run its course.

The practice serves the sūrah's argument about the audience's security. The people of Makkah lived in the enjoyment of their prosperity and their position, and the verse in front of us states the principle that governs such enjoyment: it is a delay, and the delay has an end. The Book's warnings about the destroyed towns make the same point with the ruins that stood along the audience's trade routes.

**What the Respite Is For**

The Book presents the time between the refusal and the reckoning as an opportunity that can be used in either direction. The chapter of the Cow states that God gives the wrongdoers rope to lengthen, and the sūrah of the Pen describes the same extension as a gradual drawing on. The time is granted to the audience of the Prophet as it was granted to Iblīs, and the Book's descriptions of the repentant and the obstinate show the two uses to which it can be put.

The sūrah's later passages describe people who use the time to argue about the message, to demand signs, and to mock the messenger. The verse in front of us, with the vow that follows it, presents the alternative use in its most extreme form. The tempter has been given time and resolves to spend it on the deception of others.

**The Term and the Human Being**

The Book applies the language of the appointed term to individual human beings as well as to communities and to Iblīs. Sūrat al-Aʿrāf states in its later verses that every community has a term that can neither be advanced nor delayed (7:34), and the sūrah of the Believers and the sūrah of the Heights repeat the statement in different words. The verse in front of us applies the same language to the one who asked for delay.

The reader of the sūrah is therefore placed in a position where the terms of the answer are visible. The tempter has his term, the communities have theirs, and the individual has his own. The delay in the verse is not a privilege reserved for the wicked; it is the shape of the world in which the warning is delivered.

**Divine Forbearance and the Delay**

The Book's treatment of the divine forbearance accompanies its warnings about the delay. The sūrah of Fāṭir and the sūrah of the Troops describe God as the Forbearing and the Forgiving, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran places the delay of punishment in the context of an instruction to the Prophet not to be distressed by the wealth of the disbelievers. This verse states the grant of delay without commentary, and the sūrah's reader is left to connect it with the account of the reckoning that the opening section has recently given.

The connection is the point of the narrative's placement. The chapter has described the questioning, the recounting, and the weighing; it then shows the enemy of the human race receiving the time in which to operate before that reckoning arrives. The reader is told that the interval is not a gap in the accounting but a period within its scope.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fifteenth in the chapter, and it closes the exchange that began with the prostration. The verse that follows reports the vow of Iblīs to lie in wait for the descendants of Adam on the straight path, and the account then describes his approach from every direction. The grant in front of us is the condition under which the whole of that activity takes place.

The reader has been given the creation, the command, the refusal, the sentence, and the petition. The narrative now turns to the activity that follows from the sentence and the petition, and the sūrah's warnings about the protectors taken in place of God receive their explanation in the character of the one who waits on the path.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that Iblīs, claiming that God has caused him to err, vows to lie in wait for the descendants of Adam on the straight path (7:16). The passage in front of us has granted him the time; the verse that follows states how he intends to use it.

The two verses together move the narrative from the sentence to the campaign. The reader is shown an enemy with a fixed term and a declared intention, and the sūrah's later passages describe the human beings who are the object of that intention and the revelation that is their defence.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:16

> He said, “For leaving me to stray I will lie in ambush for them on Your Straight Path.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Claim Against God**

The verse reports the words of Iblīs that he has been caused to err, and the claim attributes his condition to the God who sentenced him. The commentary tradition records two readings of the statement. One holds that the claim describes what happened without excusing it, since the Book elsewhere states that God leads astray whom He wills; the other holds that God's role consisted in the command that exposed what was already in Iblīs, so that the testing brought his hidden pride into the open.

The Book's verses about God's guidance and misguidance appear throughout, and the sūrah of the Cow states that God misleads many by a parable while He guides many by it (2:26); the sūrah of the Troops reports the answer given to the condemned that they were misguided in the same way as those whom God abandoned; and the sūrah of the Cloaked One states that God leaves in error those whom He wills (74:31). The verse in front of us places one such statement in the mouth of Iblīs, and the narrative does not treat it as a valid excuse.

**The Questions the Statement Raised**

The passage produced extended discussion among the theologians, and the sūrah's own words supply the material for it. Those who held that human moral destiny is determined by human choice read the divine guidance and misguidance as rewards and punishments for what a person has already done, and they pointed to the sequence in which Iblīs refused and was then left to his error. Those who held that God's control extends over all outcomes read the verse as evidence of that control and answered that God is not answerable for what He does.

The debate concerns the reading of the whole revelation rather than the meaning of the single clause, and the Book's statements about the matter include both the divine will and the human responsibility. The sūrah of the Heights itself states in its third verse that the community is to follow what has been sent down and not to take protectors besides God, and it will state in its thirtieth verse that some have been guided while others are deserving of error because they took the devils as masters. The two statements stand together in the same chapter.

**The Vow to Lie in Wait**

The verse reports the vow of Iblīs to lie in wait for the descendants of Adam, and the image is that of a hunter or a bandit who takes his position where his quarry must pass. The Book uses the same image in its warnings about the enemy, and the sūrah of the Night Journey reports the vow in similar terms (17:62). The verse in front of us states the intention, and the verse after it describes the directions from which the attack will come.

The vow answers the question of what the granted time will be used for. The delay was requested in the previous verse, the reason is stated in this one, and the plan is described in the next. The narrative supplies the whole sequence in three verses, and the sūrah's warnings about the deception of the enemy are grounded in the declaration that the enemy himself makes.

**On the Straight Path**

The path on which Iblīs takes his position is named as God's straight path, and the Book uses the phrase for the religion that the prophets brought. The opening sūrah of the Book asks God to guide its reciter to the straight path (1:6), and the sūrah of the Cattle describes the covenant of commands as God's straight path and commands the Prophet to follow it (6:153). The Book's use of the image elsewhere identifies the path with the revelation and with the community that follows it.

The commentary tradition notes that the choice of the path as the place of ambush indicates the target of the attack. Iblīs does not wait in the byways but on the road itself, and his effort is directed at those who are travelling on it. The Book's statements about the limits of his power accompany the warning: the sūrah of the Rocky Tract states that Iblīs has no authority over the sincere servants of God (15:42), and the sūrah of the Bee states that he has no authority over those who believe and rely on their Lord (16:99).

**What the Vow Says About the Enemy**

The Book describes the methods of the enemy in many passages, and the commentary tradition gathers them into a list that includes whispering to the heart, commanding indecency, sowing fear, making false promises, inciting enmity between people, and making evil deeds appear fair. The verse in front of us states the general intention from which the methods follow, and the sūrah's later passages name several of them in the context of the audience's own practices.

The enemy's tactics are also described as subtle, and the sūrah's account of Adam's temptation shows the method at work: the false claim about the tree, the oath sworn to the pair, and the suggestion that the fruit would make them angels or immortal. The vow in front of us is the announcement of a campaign of that kind, and the verses that follow describe its reach.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixteenth in the chapter, and it follows the grant of respite in the verse before it. The verse that follows reports the approach from every direction and the prediction that most of the descendants of Adam will not be grateful, and the sūrah then reports the second sentence passed on Iblīs. The narrative in front of us is the point at which the adversary moves from argument to strategy.

The reader of the sūrah has been given the story of the first refusal and the terms of the delay. The verse in front of us states what the adversary intends to do with his time, and the chapter's later accounts of the prophets and their peoples show the intention being carried out among the communities that refused the messengers.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that Iblīs will approach the descendants of Adam from in front of them and from behind them and from their right and their left, and that God will find most of them ungrateful (7:17). The vow stated in front of us is expanded into a description of the attack's directions, and the verses that follow report the sentence passed on the one who made it.

The two verses together present the enemy's plan in his own words. The reader is given the position on the path and the directions of the approach, and the sūrah's warnings about the deception of the satans are thereby placed in the context of a declared intention rather than a general caution.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:17

> I will approach them from their front, their back, their right, their left, and then You will find most of them ungrateful.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Four Directions of the Approach**

The verse reports the plan of Iblīs in the words he used, and it describes an approach from the front, from behind, from the right, and from the left. The image is that of a force that surrounds its object on every side that a traveller might use, and the commentary tradition reads the directions as descriptions of the kinds of attack rather than of geography.

The tradition of commentary offers several readings of the four directions. The attack from the front is described as the planting of doubt about the resurrection and the judgment, together with the suggestion that the present life is all there is; the attack from behind is described as the diversion of the person's desire from the Hereafter to the goods of the world and the accumulation of what must be left behind; and the attacks from the right and the left are described as the corruption of religion through disbelief and through invented practices that have no warrant in the revelation. The four together cover the whole of a person's outlook and conduct.

**Why the Directions Are Named**

The verse names the directions for which the adversary claims responsibility, and the naming serves the sūrah's warning about the varieties of misguidance. The Book's accounts of the peoples who refused their messengers show the same varieties at work: some denied the resurrection outright, others accepted the message's language while retaining their old practices, and others resisted the messenger because his followers were drawn from the weak. The verse in front of us presents the plan in a form that covers each of them.

The approach from the left is sometimes identified with the corruption of good deeds through the introduction of practices that were never commanded, and the sūrah's audience had a large stock of such practices in its pilgrimage, its food rules, and its offerings. The chapter addresses those practices at length, and the verse in front of us states the strategy behind them.

**The Two Directions That Are Not Named**

The commentary tradition gives attention to the directions that the verse leaves out. When the adversary made his vow and the angels asked how the human being could escape an attack from every side, the answer is reported to be that two ways remain open, the direction from above and the direction from below: the human being can raise his hands in supplication and bow his head to the ground in humility, and the adversary has no claim on either.

The Book's language of supplication and prostration as the believer's refuge appears throughout, and the sūrah of the Cow ends its longest verse with the instruction to call upon God with fear and hope. The directions the adversary does not name are therefore the directions of prayer, and the reader of the sūrah is shown where the defence lies in the same verse that describes the attack.

**The Prediction About Gratitude**

The verse ends with the words of Iblīs about the descendants of Adam, that God will find most of them ungrateful. The Book's statements about the majority's response appear in several passages, and the sūrah of Sheba reports that Iblīs proved his opinion of them true and that they followed him except for a group of believers (34:20). The sūrah of Yūnus states that most of them do not give thanks, the sūrah of the Ant repeats the statement, and the sūrah of Sheba states that few of God's servants are grateful.

The prediction is part of the adversary's case, and the Book's account does not present it as a justification for his conduct. It states a fact about the human response that the sūrah addresses throughout, and the sūrah's own instruction to follow the revelation is the answer to it. The community that receives the Book is the group named in the sūrah of Sheba as the exception.

**What the Verse Establishes About the Enemy**

The verse states the reach of the adversary's ambition without stating the limits of his power. The Book supplies the limits elsewhere: the sūrah of the Rocky Tract states that he has no authority over the sincere servants of God (15:42), the sūrah of the Bee states that he has no authority over those who believe and rely upon their Lord (16:99), and the sūrah of the Night Journey states that he has no authority over the servants of God. The plan described in front of us therefore operates by suggestion rather than compulsion.

The distinction matters for the sūrah's teaching about responsibility. The human being who is misled has followed a suggestion, and the Book's repeated warnings about the enemy are addressed to people who are able to refuse him. The verse in front of us states the intention of the tempter, and the sūrah's later passages state the position of those who accept and those who reject.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventeenth in the chapter, and it follows the vow of the previous verse and precedes the second sentence passed on Iblīs. The account then turns to the command given to Adam and his wife to dwell in the Garden and to the temptation that follows. The verse in front of us supplies the shape of the enmity before the narrative shows it in operation.

The reader of the sūrah has been given the adversary's ambition, its directions, and its expectation. The chapter's remaining narratives and instructions are addressed to people who live inside that ambition, and the revelation that the sūrah commands them to follow is presented as the road the adversary occupies.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the sentence passed on Iblīs for the second time, that he should go forth disgraced and rejected, and that Hell will be filled with him and with those among the descendants of Adam who follow him (7:18). The vow is answered with the declaration of the outcome, and the narrative then moves to the first human pair.

The two verses together state the terms of the contest and its end. The adversary is given his time and his method, and the destination of those who follow him is named in the same breath as his own. The reader is left with the choice that the sūrah places before the children of Adam.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:18

> Allah said, “Get out of Paradise! You are disgraced and rejected! I will certainly fill up Hell with you and your followers all together.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Sentence**

The verse reports the answer given to the vow, and the answer repeats the expulsion in stronger terms and adds the destination of those who follow the tempter. The Book's other accounts of the scene contain the same elements: the sūrah of the Rocky Tract reports the command to leave in disgrace and the curse that rests on Iblīs until the day of judgment (15:34–35), and the sūrah of Ṣād reports the same sentence with the further statement that Hell will be filled with him and with those who follow him among the condemned (38:77–78; 38:85).

The narrative therefore answers the plan of the previous verse with the statement of its outcome. The adversary who has announced his campaign is told where the campaign ends, and the verse names both him and his followers in the destination. The sūrah's warnings about the protectors taken in place of God are given their final term in this sentence.

**Disgrace and Rejection**

The two terms of the sentence describe the condition of the one who is driven out. Disgrace covers the loss of standing, and rejection describes the separation from mercy and from the company of the obedient. The Book uses the language of expulsion and curse for the adversary in each of its accounts of the scene, and the sūrah of the Rocky Tract states that the curse will remain on him until the day of judgment (15:35).

The contrast with the honour that Adam received in the same narrative is the point of the arrangement. The angels were commanded to prostrate before the first human being, and the one who refused to bow was driven out. The text places the two outcomes side by side, and the sūrah's account of the temptation that follows shows the expelled one at work within the Garden.

**The Filling of Hell**

The verse states that Hell will be filled with Iblīs and with those who follow him, and the Book uses the same declaration in the accounts of the scene and in its descriptions of the judgment. The sūrah of Ṣād gives the statement as the conclusion of the account of Adam's creation (38:85), the sūrah of Hūd reports the word that Hell will be filled with the jinn and with humanity together (11:119), and the sūrah of the Prostration states that the word will be fulfilled and that Hell will be filled with the jinn and with people.

The declaration establishes the scale of the outcome, and it places the followers of the adversary inside the sentence that was passed on him. The Book's descriptions of the day include the gathering of the deniers with their leaders into the Fire, and the sūrah of the Heights will describe the entry of the communities into it one after another in its later verses.

**Following the Adversary**

The verse names those who follow Iblīs as the companions of his destination, and the Book's treatment of following is one of the sūrah's central themes. The command of the third verse is to follow what has been sent down and not to take protectors besides God, and the sūrah's later passages describe the people who followed their ancestors, their leaders, and their own desires. The verse in front of us states the end of one such following at the head of the chapter.

The Book's contrast between the two paths is developed throughout the sūrah's narratives. The prophets call their peoples to follow the revelation, and the peoples answer that they follow what they found their fathers upon. The verse in front of us names the consequence of that answer, and it places the consequence in the sentence passed on the one whom they followed.

**The Mercy That Remains**

The sūrah's narrative of the first human pair will end with their repentance and with the statement that God is forgiving and merciful (7:23), and the contrast between the two sentences is one of the chapter's lessons. The one who argued and asked for time is expelled; the pair who admit their wrong and ask forgiveness are received. The sūrah's later passages apply the same distinction to the communities and to the audience.

The Book's treatment of the adversary's despair appears in its accounts of the condemned who ask for another chance at the moment of punishment, and the sūrah of the Heights itself will describe that request in its later verses. The verse in front of us states the sentence as it was passed on the one who made no such request at the time when a request would have been of use.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eighteenth in the chapter, and it concludes the exchange that began with the prostration. The verses that follow begin the account of Adam and his wife in the Garden, their temptation by the adversary who has just been sentenced, and the exposure of their nakedness. The sentence in front of us states the position of the tempter at the moment the temptation begins.

The reader of the sūrah has been given the creation of the first human being, the honour conferred on him, the refusal of the adversary, his argument, his sentence, his petition, his vow, and his destination. The narrative then turns to the human pair and to the test that the sūrah's instruction to the children of Adam is designed to prepare them for.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the command given to Adam to dwell with his wife in the Garden, to eat from wherever they wish, and to avoid the tree, with the warning that approaching it would make them wrongdoers (7:19). The narrative in front of us has stated the enemy and his intention, and the verse that follows states the liberty and the single prohibition that the first pair were given.

The two verses together set the scene for the temptation. The adversary is sentenced and remains at work, and the human pair are given a wide permission with one boundary. The sūrah then recounts the temptation, the fall, and the repentance in sequence, and the chapter's teaching about the children of Adam begins from that account.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:19

> ˹Allah said,˺ “O Adam! Live with your wife in Paradise and eat from wherever you please, but do not approach this tree, or else you will be wrongdoers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Dwell in the Garden**

The verse reports the address to Adam to dwell with his wife in the Garden, and it belongs to the sequence of commands and permissions that the Book gives in its accounts of the first human pair. The sūrah of the Cow contains the parallel command, in which the pair are told to eat freely of the Garden and are warned against one tree (2:35). The two accounts agree on the liberty granted and on the single boundary.

The Book's presentation of the Garden in these passages is one of abundance and ease. The sūrah of Ṭā Hā states that the first human being was told that he would not go hungry or naked there, nor thirst nor suffer the heat (20:118–19). The verse in front of us states the same arrangement in its own words, and the single prohibition stands out against the extent of the permission.

**The Wife of Adam**

The verse names the wife of Adam, and the Book does not give her name in any of its accounts. The tradition of the community supplies the name Ḥawwāʾ, and the commentators discuss her creation on the basis of the sūrah of the Women, which states that God created people from a single soul and created from it its mate and spread abroad from the two a multitude of men and women (4:1). The verse in front of us presents the pair as the two human beings who receive the command.

The narrative's silence about her name is matched by its silence about any separate origin, and the Book's accounts of the creation and the prostration treat the two as the recipients of the same command and the same warning. The verses that follow the temptation state that both were deceived, both ate, both were exposed, and both repented. The sūrah's narration therefore keeps the pair together throughout the account.

**The Tree That Was Forbidden**

The verse forbids the approach to a particular tree, and the Book does not identify the tree by species. The sūrah of Ṭā Hā calls it the tree of everlastingness in the report of the tempter's words (20:120), and the commentary tradition offers identifications drawn from the reports and the earlier scriptures, including wheat, the fig, and the vine. The verse in front of us keeps to the prohibition, and the prohibition is what the narrative requires.

The wording forbids the approach rather than the eating alone, and the commentary tradition notes the wider boundary that the phrasing sets. What is forbidden is not only the act but the movement toward it, and the Book's instructions to the community elsewhere use the same manner of speaking when they forbid the paths that lead to a transgression. The temptation in the next verse is directed at that boundary.

**Permission and Boundary Together**

The verse combines a wide permission with a narrow prohibition, and the arrangement is characteristic of the Book's treatment of human conduct. The sūrah of the Cattle states the general principle when it says that God has not forbidden most of what the audience imagined to be forbidden, and its list of the actual prohibitions is short. This verse gives the first human pair the whole of the Garden except one tree.

The commentary tradition draws from the arrangement a lesson about the nature of the test. The prohibition is small in comparison with what is permitted, and the disobedience consisted in crossing a boundary that was plainly marked. The sūrah's later treatment of the audience's self-imposed food rules and its forbidden practices presents the reverse error, since the audience forbade what God had permitted while taking liberties that God had forbidden.

**The Warning of Wrongdoing**

The verse states that approaching the tree would make the pair wrongdoers, and the warning names the consequence in the same vocabulary the sūrah uses for the communities that were destroyed. The Book's description of wrongdoing as a harm that the person brings on himself appears in the sūrah of the Cow and the sūrah of Yūnus, and the warning in front of us states the outcome before the act. The pair's own confession after the fall uses the same word when they say that they have wronged themselves (7:23).

The choice of the word connects the first disobedience with the crimes of the nations. The sūrah's audience is warned about wrongdoing through the accounts of the destroyed communities, and the narrative of the Garden supplies the original instance. The wrong is committed first against the self, and the sūrah's teaching about the reckoning follows from that.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands nineteenth in the chapter, and it follows the sentence passed on the adversary and precedes his temptation of the pair. The narrative then reports the whisper, the false promise, the oath, the fall, the exposure, and the confession. The command in front of us establishes the conditions under which the temptation and the fall take place.

The reader who has followed the sūrah from its opening has been given the revelation, the command to follow it, the warning about protectors, the account of the reckoning, and the story of the adversary. The passage in front of us places the human pair inside the same narrative, and the sūrah's address to the children of Adam in the following passage is built on what happens to them.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that Satan whispered to the pair in order to expose what had been hidden of their nakedness, and that he told them their Lord had forbidden the tree only to prevent them from becoming angels or from living forever (7:20). The prohibition in front of us is answered by the whisper, and the response of the pair follows in the verses after that.

The two verses together show the method of the adversary at work. A boundary is set, the boundary is described as a deprivation, and the false promise is offered as the reason for crossing it. The sūrah then recounts the crossing and its consequences.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:20

> Then Satan tempted them in order to expose what was hidden of their nakedness. He said, “Your Lord has forbidden this tree to you only to prevent you from becoming angels or immortals.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Whisper**

The verse reports that Satan whispered to the pair, and the Book uses the language of whispering for the enemy's method throughout. The sūrah of the Heights itself states in its later instruction to the children of Adam that Satan is a manifest enemy, and the sūrah of the People uses the image of the whisperer who withdraws, who whispers into the hearts of people (114:4–5). The commentary tradition distinguishes between an audible low speech and a suggestion placed in the heart, and both readings describe an approach that does not announce itself.

The whisper follows the sentence passed on the adversary and the grant of his respite, and it shows the campaign of the previous verses beginning. The sūrah's warnings about the enemy's approach from every direction are given their first illustration here, and the temptation of the pair is presented as the model of what the children of Adam will face.

**The Exposure of What Was Hidden**

The verse states the purpose of the whisper, that the hidden nakedness of the pair might be exposed to them, and the Book repeats the description in the sūrah of Ṭā Hā, where their nakedness is disclosed to them after they taste the tree (20:121). The commentary tradition reports that the pair had been clothed in a covering that concealed their bodies, and that the disobedience removed it. The exposure is therefore presented as a consequence of the act rather than as an independent event.

The Book's treatment of nakedness in the sūrah's following passage turns the incident into teaching about clothing and modesty, and the verse that states that God has sent down clothing to cover the children of Adam follows shortly after the account of the fall (7:26). The exposure in front of us is the condition that the gift of clothing addresses, and the sūrah's instruction about dress at worship grows from the same root.

**The False Promise About the Tree**

The verse reports the claim that the tree was forbidden only to keep the pair from becoming angels or from living forever, and the claim is the first lie that the Book records the adversary telling to human beings. The sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the same approach in the form of a question about the tree of everlastingness and a kingdom that never decays (20:120). In both accounts the prohibition is recast as a deprivation, and the supposed benefit is offered as the reason to disobey.

The Book states elsewhere that the enemy's promises are delusion, and the sūrah of the Night Journey reports the promise he makes and the statement that he promises them nothing but delusion (17:64). The sūrah of the Women states that he makes promises and raises hopes in them and that he promises them nothing but deception (4:120). The verse in front of us records the original instance of the pattern that those verses describe.

**The Two Things Promised**

The promise names angels and immortality, and the two are joined in the narrative because both describe a condition above the human state as the pair then knew it. The commentary tradition notes that the promise of becoming angels works on the desire for a higher rank, and that the promise of abiding forever works on the fear of loss. The two together cover the ambitions that the Book elsewhere warns against: the wish to be other than what one was created to be, and the refusal to accept that the present life ends.

The Book's treatment of immortality as a claim made against God appears in its accounts of the people who denied the resurrection, and the sūrah of the Cave reports the man who entered his garden saying that he did not think the Hour would ever come. The verse in front of us shows the promise of immortality being used at the beginning of the human story, and the sūrah's teaching about the reckoning answers it.

**The Method and Its Repetition**

The temptation in front of us uses the elements that the sūrah's later passages identify in the audience's own practice: a claim about what God intends, a benefit offered for disobedience, and an oath to support it. The chapter goes on to describe the people who attribute to God what He has not commanded (7:28), who forbid what He has permitted (7:32), and who follow their ancestors on the strength of inherited claims (7:28). The first whisper in the Garden is the model of each of them.

The Book's instruction to the children of Adam in the following verses is therefore an instruction about a method as much as about an act. The verse in front of us states the method in its original form, and the reader who recognizes the shape of the argument is prepared for the sūrah's account of the same argument used against the prophets.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands twentieth in the chapter, and it follows the command given to the pair and precedes the oath that the adversary swears to them. The narrative then reports the fall, the exposure, the sewing of the leaves, and the divine question, and the pair's confession follows. The whisper in front of us is the opening move of the sequence.

The reader of the sūrah has been given the boundaries that were set, the enemy who was sentenced, and the first attempt on the boundary. The chapter's teaching about clothing, food, and worship follows the account of the fall, and the verses in front of us supply the narrative on which that teaching rests.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the adversary swore to the pair that he was their sincere adviser (7:21). The whisper of the previous verse is supported by an oath, and the narrative's next act is the fall. The verse in front of us has stated the false promise; the verse that follows states the false guarantee attached to it.

The two verses together present the technique of deception in its parts. A claim is made about God's intention, and an oath is sworn to make the claim credible, and the pair accept both. The sūrah then records what followed, and the instruction to their descendants is built on the record.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:21

> And he swore to them, “I am truly your sincere advisor.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Oath Sworn to the Pair**

The verse states that the adversary swore to the pair that he was their sincere adviser, and the oath is the element that distinguishes the temptation from a simple false statement. The commentary tradition reports that the oath was sworn in the name of God, and that the pair took it as evidence of sincerity because they could not imagine that anyone would swear by God in order to lie. The narrative presents the deception at its strongest, since the promise of the previous verse has now been guaranteed by an appeal to what is most sacred.

The Book's warnings about the oaths of the wicked appear in its accounts of the hypocrites and the deniers, and the sūrah of the Bee describes those who make God the subject of their oaths for a small price. The verse in front of us gives the first instance of the pattern, and the sūrah's instruction to the children of Adam about the enemy's deception is addressed to people who will meet the same kind of guarantee.

**What Adam Is Reported to Have Said**

The commentary tradition reports a defence offered by Adam after the fall: that the adversary swore to him by God, and that he could not have believed that any creature would swear by God in order to deceive him. The report is used to explain how the pair were taken in by an oath that they had no reason to doubt. The narrative in front of us supplies the oath, and the report supplies the answer to the question of why it worked.

The Book's teaching about the enemy's methods follows from the same observation. The enemy does not come with an open declaration of hostility; he comes with the appearance of good counsel, and the sūrah's later passages show the audience being deceived in the same manner by claims about the practice of their ancestors and about what God has commanded. The verse in front of us states the first and clearest case.

**The Sincere Adviser**

The word the verse puts in the adversary's mouth describes a counsellor who wants only the good of the one he advises, and the sūrah's later verses give the audience instructions about whom to take as a counsellor. The Book's descriptions of the guidance that comes through the messengers present them as the sincere advisers of their peoples, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the prophets addressing their peoples with the declaration that they are trustworthy messengers. The verse in front of us shows the title being claimed by the one who intends the opposite of what it means.

The Book's treatment of the counterfeit adviser appears in its warnings about the leaders of misguidance who will disown their followers at the reckoning, and the sūrah of the Heights will describe the followers blaming their leaders in the Fire (7:38–39). The oath in front of us is the beginning of the relationship whose end the sūrah describes in its later verses.

**The Oath as a Model of Deception**

The Book's language for the enemy's activity includes the promise, the whisper, and the oath, and the three are joined in the account of the first temptation. The sūrah of the Night Journey states that the adversary makes promises and raises hopes, and the sūrah of the Women states that his promises are deception (4:120). The oath in front of us adds the strongest available guarantee to the false promise, and the narrative shows that it was believed.

The sūrah's instruction to the children of Adam in its following verses is shaped by the account. The children are told not to let the enemy deceive them as he deceived their parents, and the instruction names the deception as the thing to be recognized. The oath is recorded so that the method can be identified when it is used again.

**The Pair and Their Descendants**

The account in front of us presents the first human pair as the recipients of a deception that their descendants will meet in other forms, and the sūrah addresses the descendants directly in the passage that follows. The verses that call on the children of Adam to take their adornment at every place of worship, to eat and drink without excess, and to avoid the forbidden things are addressed to people who have been told this story.

The Book's practice of making the story of the first pair a lesson for later generations appears in the sūrah of the Cow, where the descent from the Garden is followed by the promise of guidance to those who follow it (2:38). The verse in front of us supplies the point at which the deception succeeded, and the sūrah's later instructions supply the means by which the descendants can avoid it.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands twenty-first in the chapter, and it follows the false promise and precedes the fall. The narrative then records that the pair were brought down by the deception, that their nakedness was exposed, and that they began to cover themselves with the leaves of the Garden. The oath in front of us is the last element of the temptation before it takes effect.

The reader who has followed the sūrah from its opening has been given the revelation, the command, the warning, the account of the reckoning, the adversary's story, and now the first temptation. The verses that follow complete the narrative and bring the pair to the confession that the sūrah commends.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the adversary brought about the fall of the pair through deception, that they tasted the tree and their nakedness was exposed, and that they began to sew the leaves of the Garden together to cover themselves, and it reports the call of their Lord asking whether He had not forbidden them the tree and told them that Satan was a manifest enemy (7:22). The oath in front of us is followed by the act and by the question that names what the pair had been told.

The two verses together complete the account of the temptation. The guarantee is given, the boundary is crossed, the covering is lost, and the Lord who had warned them asks the question that establishes their responsibility. The sūrah then records their answer.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:22

> So he brought about their fall through deception. And when they tasted of the tree, their nakedness was exposed to them, prompting them to cover themselves with leaves from Paradise. Then their Lord called out to them, “Did I not forbid you from that tree and ˹did I not˺ tell you that Satan is your sworn enemy?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fall Through Deception**

The verse states that the adversary brought about the fall of the pair through deception, and the Book's accounts of the event present the act as the result of a lure rather than of a contest. The sūrah of the Cow reports the same sequence in which the enemy caused the pair to slip from the Garden (2:36), and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the temptation and the eating in the same order (20:120–21). The Book's descriptions of the enemy's methods elsewhere state that his promises are delusion, and the verse in front of us shows the first working of the method.

The phrase describes the manner of the fall, and the commentary tradition draws attention to the absence of any compulsion. The pair were not forced to eat; they were persuaded by a false description of the prohibition and by an oath. The Book's teaching about responsibility in the following verses rests on that fact, and the sūrah's warning to the children of Adam about the same deception treats them as free to accept or refuse.

**The Exposure of Nakedness**

The verse states that the nakedness of the pair was exposed to them when they tasted the tree, and the Book's account in the sūrah of Ṭā Hā states the same result (20:121). The exposure is described as a disclosure rather than a change in their bodies, and the tradition of commentary reports that a covering of light or of the Garden's clothing had concealed them until the act. The account therefore presents the loss as a consequence carried by the act itself.

The sūrah's following passage builds on the loss. The verse that tells the children of Adam that God has sent down clothing to cover their nakedness and to serve as adornment, and that the best clothing is the fear of God, follows the account of the pair's attempts to cover themselves (7:26). The sūrah's instructions about dress at worship and about modesty take the incident as their starting point.

**The Leaves of the Garden**

The verse reports that the pair began to sew together the leaves of the Garden to cover themselves, and the detail is one of the narrative's most concrete. The commentary tradition identifies the leaves with those of the fig tree, and it notes that the pair's own effort at covering is recorded alongside the divine gift of clothing that the sūrah announces shortly afterwards. The contrast between the improvised covering and the clothing that God provides is part of the passage's teaching.

The Book's presentation of clothing in the following verses includes both the covering of the body and the adornment that goes beyond it, and the sūrah's instruction to wear one's adornment at every place of worship is placed in that context. The leaves in front of us are the first clothing of the human race, and the sūrah's treatment of dress begins from them.

**The Divine Question**

The verse reports the call of the Lord to the pair asking whether He had not forbidden them the tree and told them that Satan was a manifest enemy. The question is not an inquiry after information, and the commentary tradition reads it as a rebuke that establishes the terms of the responsibility. The pair had been warned in the command itself, and the enemy had been named.

The form of the question appears elsewhere in the Book as a means of confronting those who have no answer, and the sūrah's later passages use the same device when they report the questions put to the condemned. The verse in front of us places the question at the moment of the fall, and the pair's answer in the following verse is the model of the response that the sūrah commends.

**The Enemy Named as a Manifest Enemy**

The verse states that the pair had been told that Satan was their manifest enemy, and the sūrah's warnings to the children of Adam repeat the description. The verse that addresses the children of Adam shortly afterwards says that he and his tribe see them from where they cannot see him (7:27), and the sūrah of the Cow instructs the community not to follow the footsteps of the enemy. The description in front of us is therefore given twice in the same narrative: once to the pair and once to their descendants.

The Book's treatment of the enemy's enmity runs through its accounts of the prophets and their peoples, and the sūrah's later passages describe the leaders of misguidance who will be gathered into the Fire with their followers. The verse in front of us states the warning that the pair received, and the sūrah's instruction to their children repeats it as a warning that remains in force.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands twenty-second in the chapter, and it completes the account of the temptation that began with the whisper. The pair's confession follows in the next verse, and the narrative then reports the command to descend to the earth and the provision that has been placed there. The verse in front of us is the point at which the account moves from the forbidden act to its consequence.

The reader of the sūrah has been given the boundary, the deception, the oath, the fall, the exposure, and the rebuke. The chapter's instruction to the children of Adam about clothing, food, and worship takes its force from this sequence, and the reader is placed among the descendants who are addressed.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the words of the pair, that they have wronged themselves and that if God does not forgive them and have mercy on them they will be among the losers (7:23). The rebuke in front of us receives the answer that the sūrah presents as the right response to a fall, and the narrative then states the descent and the terms of life on the earth.

The two verses together contrast the fall with the confession, and the sūrah's teaching about repentance is built on the contrast. The command was given, the enemy promised, the pair crossed the boundary, and they answered the question with an admission rather than an argument.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:23

> They replied, “Our Lord! We have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy on us, we will certainly be losers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Words of the Pair**

The verse reports the answer that Adam and his wife gave to the divine question, and their words combine an admission, an appeal, and a statement of what is at stake. They acknowledge that they have wronged themselves, they ask for forgiveness and mercy, and they state that without both they will be among the losers. The Book's account in the sūrah of the Cow reports that Adam received words from his Lord and that God turned to him in forgiveness (2:37), and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the same sequence of disobedience followed by forgiveness (20:122).

The confession in front of us is the model of the response that the sūrah commends, and the commentary tradition contrasts it with the answer that Iblīs gave to the same kind of questioning. The enemy argued about the comparative nobility of fire and clay and then asked for time; the pair admit their fault and ask for what will save them. The two answers stand at the head of the sūrah's teaching about repentance.

**The Admission of Wrong Against Oneself**

The pair's first words state that they have wronged themselves, and the phrase is the Book's standard description of the harm that a person brings on himself by disobedience. The sūrah of Yūnus states that God does not wrong people in the least but that people wrong themselves, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran repeats the statement in its accounts of the battles and the losses of the community. The confession in front of us applies the description to the first human act of disobedience.

The commentary tradition draws attention to the fact that the admission is made before any sentence is pronounced on the pair. They do not wait for the outcome to be announced; they name their own fault at the first question. The Book's treatment of the confession in the sūrah of the Women states that if the wrongdoers had come to the Prophet and sought forgiveness and he had asked it for them, they would have found God relenting and merciful (4:64), and the scene in front of us presents the original instance of that response.

**The Appeal for Forgiveness and Mercy**

The pair ask for two things, and the commentary tradition distinguishes them. Forgiveness covers the offence and removes its consequence, and mercy supplies what the offender needs beyond the removal of the penalty. The Book pairs the two terms in its accounts of divine response throughout, and the sūrah of the Troops tells those who have exceeded all limits against their own souls not to despair of the mercy of God, since He forgives all sins (39:53). The pair's prayer in front of us stands at the head of that tradition.

The Book's instructions to the community about seeking forgiveness often include the acknowledgment of the offence before the petition, and the sūrah of the Heights itself will present the community's confession in a later verse. The words in front of us supply the pattern for both parts of the prayer.

**What Is at Stake in the Appeal**

The pair state that without forgiveness and mercy they will be among the losers, and the term connects their situation with the account of the scales that the sūrah's opening has given. The loss they name is the loss of the self that the sūrah attributes to those whose balance is light (7:9), and their language ties the first fall to the general reckoning that the chapter describes. The pair know the consequence of the wrong they have done.

The Book's use of the word for loss appears in its descriptions of the condemned in the Hereafter, and the sūrah of the Believers states that those whose scales are light will lose themselves. The confession in front of us is therefore placed within the framework the sūrah has established, and the mercy they request is the means by which the loss is avoided.

**The Contrast With the Adversary**

The Book's narrative places the two answers to the divine questioning in the same passage, and the contrast is deliberate. The adversary claimed a reason for his act and asked for time; the pair admitted the act and asked for forgiveness. The sūrah's later accounts of the prophets and their peoples repeat the contrast, since the peoples who are destroyed refuse the message and answer the warnings with arguments while the prophets answer their own failures with repentance.

The commentary tradition reads the scene as the founding example of the difference between the proud and the humble, and the sūrah's opening verses on the weighing of deeds supply the framework in which the example is set. The pair's confession is brief, and its brevity is part of its force; the Book records no defence from them and no request that the consequence be lifted apart from the appeal to mercy.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands twenty-third in the chapter, and it answers the divine question of the previous verse. The narrative then reports the command to descend and the terms of life on the earth, and the sūrah's instruction to the children of Adam about clothing, food, and worship follows that. The confession in front of us closes the account of the fall and opens the account of life in the world.

The reader who has followed the sūrah from its opening has been given the revelation, the command, the warning, the reckoning, the adversary's story, and the story of the first pair. The verse in front of us records the answer that the chapter holds up to its own audience, and the instruction that follows is addressed to people who have heard both answers.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the command given to the pair and to the adversary to descend, with each as an enemy to the other, and it states that the earth will provide them with a dwelling and a provision for a time (7:24). The forgiveness requested in front of us is not presented as a return to the Garden, and the narrative proceeds to the conditions of life in the world.

The two verses together state what followed the fall. The confession was accepted, the descent was commanded, and the earth became the place of dwelling until the term appointed for it. The sūrah then states the cycle of life, death, and resurrection that the earth contains.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:24

> Allah said, “Descend as enemies to each other. You will find in the earth a residence and provision for your appointed stay.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Descend**

The verse reports the command given after the fall, that the pair and their adversary should descend with enmity between them. The sūrah of the Cow reports the same command with the same clause about mutual enmity, and it adds that the earth will be a dwelling and a provision for a time (2:36). The sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the descent and the statement that some of them will be enemies to others (20:123). The three accounts agree on the elements of the command.

The command is addressed to more than the two human beings in the reading the commentary tradition prefers, since the adversary is included in the descent and the enmity is placed between the parties. The verse in front of us therefore states the terms on which the whole history of the human race will proceed, and the sūrah's warnings about the enemy are grounded in the conditions of that history.

**Enmity as the Standing Condition**

The verse describes the descent as a departure into mutual enmity, and the Book treats the condition as the framework within which the human being lives. The sūrah's instruction to the children of Adam later in the chapter says that the enemy and his tribe see them from where they cannot see him, and the verse that warns them against being deceived as their parents were deceived follows the same line (7:27). The enmity is not a passing quarrel but the standing relation between the human being and the tempter.

The Book's descriptions of the enemy's activity elsewhere state its limits as well as its reach. The sūrah of the Rocky Tract states that he has no authority over the sincere servants of God (15:42), the sūrah of the Bee states that he has no authority over those who believe and rely on their Lord (16:99), and the sūrah of the Night Journey states that he has no authority over the servants of God. The enmity in front of us is therefore the setting of a test rather than a state of helplessness.

**The Earth as a Dwelling**

The verse names the earth as the place of dwelling for the pair and their descendants, and the commentary tradition explains the term as a settled and established place. The sūrah of the Cattle uses the same word in its description of the resting place of human beings, and the sūrah of the Cow states that the earth will be a dwelling and a provision for a time (2:36). The verse in front of us states the dwelling before it states the provision.

The Book's treatment of the earth as the place of human life appears throughout its accounts of the created order, and the sūrah of the Heights itself has already described the establishment of people upon the earth and the means of livelihood placed in it (7:10). The descent in front of us places the pair in the world that the opening verses described, and the narrative connects the two passages.

**Provision for a Time**

The verse describes the provision of the earth as belonging to a limited period, and the Book repeats the qualification in its accounts of the present life. The sūrah of the Cow gives the same phrase in its account of the descent (2:36), and the sūrah of the Heights will state later that every community has a term that can neither be advanced nor delayed (7:34). The enjoyment of the earth is real, and it is bounded.

The commentary tradition places the phrase alongside the Book's many comparisons between the present life and the life to come, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran describes the love of the present life in its list of the things that people desire before it states that the reward of God is better. The verse in front of us supplies the reason for the comparison, since the provision is granted for the interval that ends at the appointed term.

**The Reading of the Fall in the Tradition**

The commentary tradition contains a reading of the descent that emphasizes its place in the divine arrangement. The fall brought the pair to the earth, where the human being was to be the deputy of God, and the tradition notes that the drawing near to God that follows the experience of distance is a part of the human vocation. Ibn ʿAṭāʾ Allāh of Alexandria expressed the thought in his aphorisms, saying that a disobedience which bequeaths humility and need is better than an obedience which bequeaths might and pride.

The Book's own statements about the purpose of human life on the earth support the reading, and the sūrah of the Cave states that God has made what is on the earth an adornment for it in order to test which of them is best in deeds (18:7). The verse in front of us states the descent and the provision, and the sūrah's remaining instructions are addressed to people whose term on the earth is running.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands twenty-fourth in the chapter, and it follows the confession of the pair and precedes the statement of the cycle of life, death, and resurrection. The narrative then turns to the instruction of the children of Adam about clothing and conduct, and the account of the first pair gives way to the address to their descendants. The command to descend in front of us is the hinge between the two.

The reader of the sūrah has been given the Garden, the boundary, the deception, the fall, the confession, and the descent. The chapter's teaching about the present life, its provisions, and its term follows from the scene in front of us, and the warnings about the enemy continue to apply to the people who live on the earth.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the people will live on the earth, die there, and be brought forth from it (7:25). The dwelling and the provision named in front of us are given their completion in the cycle of life and death and in the raising that ends it.

The two verses together state the whole span of human existence on the earth. The descent is followed by the dwelling, the dwelling by the death, and the death by the bringing forth, and the sūrah then addresses the children of Adam who live between the descent and the raising.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:25

> He added, “There you will live, there you will die, and from there you will be resurrected.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Cycle Stated in Three Clauses**

The verse states that the people of the earth will live there, die there, and be brought forth from it, and the three clauses describe the whole course of human existence in the world. The sūrah of Ṭā Hā states the same arrangement in its account of the creation of the human being from the earth and the return to it and the bringing forth from it again (20:55), and the sūrah of Noah describes the growth of human beings from the earth and the return to it in its account of the resurrection.

The verse follows the command to descend and the statement of the provision that the earth contains, and it supplies the end of the arrangement. The dwelling is temporary, the provision is temporary, and the exit from the world is as certain as the entry into it. The Book's warnings about the reckoning rest on the sequence that the verse in front of us states in a single sentence.

**The Earth as the Place of Living and Dying**

The Book describes the earth as the source and the destination of the human body, and its accounts of creation state that the first human being was formed from what the earth contains. The sūrah of the Cave states that the human being is created from clay, and the sūrah of the Pilgrimage describes the stages of the creation of the human being from dust. The verse in front of us states the return to the same material, and it places the burial of every generation within the arrangement.

The Book's teaching about death as a transition rather than an end appears in the clause that follows the mention of dying.
