The bringing forth from the earth is the resurrection, and the sūrah of the Heights will describe the reckoning that follows it in the verses of its opening section and in its later descriptions of the day. The three clauses in front of us are therefore the frame of the whole chapter's teaching about human destiny.

**The Bringing Forth From the Earth**

The Book uses the image of the earth giving up its dead in its descriptions of the resurrection, and the sūrah of the Earthquake opens with the earth's convulsion and the casting out of its burdens (99:1–2). The sūrah of the Clatterer describes the day on which people will come out in scattered groups to be shown their deeds (99:6), and the sūrah of Qāf states that the earth will be split open and the dead will come forth quickly (50:44). The verse in front of us states the event in the plain language of the return from the ground.

The Book's argument for the resurrection often rests on the first creation, and the sūrah of the Romans states that the One who originated the creation will repeat it. The sūrah's audience knew the sequence of life and death from its own experience, and the verse in front of us states the third clause as the completion of a process that has already happened twice.

**The Verse and the Present Life**

The verse's description of the earth as the place of living and dying is accompanied in the Book by many statements about the brevity of the time spent there. The sūrah of the Heights will later state that the enjoyment of the world is little compared with the life to come, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran places the desires of the present life in a list whose conclusion is that the reward of God is better. The verse in front of us supplies the frame within which those statements make sense.

The Book's addresses to the community regularly use the term of the earth as a reminder, and the sūrah of the Cave states that the present life is only play and amusement while the home of the Hereafter is better for those who are mindful. This verse states the arrangement that the reminder describes, and it does so without leaving the narrative of the first pair.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands twenty-fifth in the chapter, and it closes the account of the fall and the descent. The verses that follow turn to the children of Adam with instructions about clothing, food, and worship, and the narrative of the first pair gives way to the address to the people who live on the earth. The passage in front of us completes the passage from the Garden to the world.

The reader of the sūrah has been given the creation, the honour, the refusal, the sentence, the temptation, the fall, the confession, and the descent. The verse under discussion adds the terms of life in the world, and the sūrah then turns to what the children of Adam are to do with the time between their birth and their return to the ground.

**The Terms of the Arrangement**

The Book's statements about death and resurrection appear in its arguments against those who denied the raising, and the sūrah of the Heights itself will address the audience's doubts about the day. The text in front of us states the arrangement without argument, and it places the audience inside it by addressing people who will live, die, and be raised like the generations before them.

The Book's practice of stating the resurrection as a fact rather than a proposition appears in the sūrahs of the last things, where the scenes of the day are described before they are defended. The verse before us applies the method to the life of the individual, and the sūrah then moves to the instructions that the children of Adam are given for the interval.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse addresses the children of Adam, states that God has sent down clothing to cover their nakedness and as an adornment, and adds that the clothing of reverence is better, and it closes with the observation that this is among the signs of God so that people may remember (7:26). The address to the descendants follows directly from the account of the first pair's exposure.

The two verses together move the sūrah from the story to the instruction. The first pair lived and died on the earth, and their descendants are told what to wear, what to eat, and how to worship, with the account of the fall as the background of the commands.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:26

> O children of Adam! We have provided for you clothing to cover your nakedness and as an adornment. However, the best clothing is righteousness. This is one of Allah’s bounties, so perhaps you will be mindful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address to the Children of Adam**

The verse turns from the narrative of the first pair to their descendants, and the address to the children of Adam governs the passage that follows. The sūrah's instructions about clothing, food, and worship are given to people who have just heard how their parents lost the covering they had, and the commands are presented as the provision that answers that loss. The Book's other addresses to the children of Adam in this sūrah follow at intervals, and each of them is attached to the same narrative background.

The verse states that God has sent down clothing for the children of Adam, and the phrase covers both the material provision that makes clothing possible and the guidance that prescribes its use. The commentary tradition notes that the covering of the body is described as a gift from God rather than as a human invention, and the narrative of the leaves sewn in the Garden supports the reading. The first attempt at covering was the pair's own; the verse in front of us announces the provision that was given afterwards.

**The Covering of Nakedness**

The verse names the covering of the nakedness as the first function of clothing, and the mention connects the instruction directly with the account of the fall. The sūrah has described the exposure that followed the eating of the tree, and the command in front of us supplies the remedy that the descendants of the pair are to use. The Book's instructions about modesty elsewhere in the sūrah develop the same theme in the context of worship and of conduct.

The commentary tradition reads the verse as an answer to the pre-Islamic practice of circumambulating the sanctuary without clothing, and it cites the account of the Quraysh's rules about the garments and the food that could be used in the sacred precinct. The provision of clothing in front of us stands against that practice, and the sūrah's instruction to wear the adornment at every place of worship restates the same point in its next verses.

**Adornment Beyond Covering**

The verse adds that God has provided adornment as well as covering, and the distinction is preserved in the commentary. Covering answers a need, and adornment answers the desire for beauty, and the Book treats both as legitimate. The sūrah's following verses state that the children of Adam are to take their adornment at every place of worship, and the instruction presents the beauty of dress as a fitting accompaniment to prayer.

The Book's teaching about the created order includes the beauty that has been placed in it, and the sūrah of the Cattle describes the livestock and the crops as objects of use and of beauty. The verse in front of us applies the same estimate to clothing, and it forbids neither beauty nor expense in itself. What the sūrah prohibits in its following verses is excess.

**The Clothing of Reverence**

The verse states that the clothing of reverence is better, and the commentary tradition gives two broad readings of the phrase. One reading treats it as the clothing worn for prayer or as the covering that modesty requires, which would make the phrase a preference for the modest garment over the merely ornamental. The other reading treats it as the quality of reverence itself, made up of faith, modesty, righteous conduct, and the fear of God, and reads the phrase as a preference for the inward garment over the outward.

The sūrah's teaching about the heart and the outward act supports the second reading, and the Book's statements about the fear of God as the beginning of right conduct appear throughout. Al-Rāzī expresses the thought by saying that the shame of the believers is always covered even when they are unclothed, and that the shame of the profligate is always exposed even when they are dressed.

**The Verse as a Sign**

The verse closes by describing the provision of clothing as one of God's signs, given so that people may remember. The Book attaches the same formula to the created order and to the revelation, and the sūrah of the Cattle uses it in its accounts of the rain and the harvest. The clothing in front of us is presented as a sign because it answers the condition that the fall produced, and the memory it is meant to trigger is the memory of what happened to the first pair.

The Book's signs are presented as material for reflection rather than as objects of veneration, and the sūrah's instruction about worship develops the point in its next verses. The reader who takes the sign as a sign remembers the sequence of the Garden, the tree, the exposure, and the covering, and the instruction of the verse follows from that recollection.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands twenty-sixth in the chapter, and it opens the series of instructions to the children of Adam that continues through the following verses. The passages on food, on the forbidden things, and on the lawful adornment follow, and the address then turns to the communities and to the messengers. The provision of clothing in front of us is the first of the gifts that the sūrah names.

The reader who has followed the story of the first pair now receives the commands that are addressed to their descendants. The covering of the body, the adornment that goes with it, and the reverence that surpasses both are placed before him, and the sūrah's next verses state the conduct that accompanies them.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse again addresses the children of Adam and warns them not to let Satan deceive them as he caused their parents to leave the Garden, and it states that he and his tribe see them from where they cannot see him, and that the satans have been made the allies of those who disbelieve (7:27). The instruction about clothing is followed by the warning about the enemy, and the two belong to the same passage of guidance.

The pattern of the opening section is repeated: the gift is named, the enemy is named, and the command is given. The reader is told what he has been provided with and what he is to guard against, and the sūrah then proceeds to the practices of the audience that the passage is meant to correct.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:27

> O children of Adam! Do not let Satan deceive you as he tempted your parents out of Paradise and caused their cover to be removed in order to expose their nakedness. Surely he and his soldiers watch you from where you cannot see them. We have made the devils allies of those who disbelieve.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning to the Children of Adam**

The verse addresses the children of Adam with the warning not to let the adversary deceive them as he caused their parents to leave the Garden. The address connects the instruction about clothing with the account of the fall, and it treats the experience of the first pair as a warning that their descendants are expected to use. The Book's other addresses to the children of Adam in this sūrah follow the same method, and the warning is repeated in the verse that follows the passage on conduct.

The verse states that the adversary stripped the parents of their covering in order to expose their nakedness to them, and the description matches the narrative of the previous verses. The warning therefore rests on a case that the audience has just heard in full, and the instruction not to repeat the experience is given to people who know its steps.

**The Enemy Sees and Is Not Seen**

The verse states that the adversary and his tribe see the children of Adam from where they cannot see him, and the commentary tradition explains the difference in terms of the created natures that the sūrah has already named. The adversary was created from fire and the human being from clay, and the subtle nature of the former allows him to observe without being observed. The sūrah's account of the temptation of the first pair illustrates the point, since the whisper reached them without any visible approach.

The Book's treatment of the unseen world includes the warning that its inhabitants are real and active, and the sūrah of the Jinn describes a company of the jinn listening to the recitation and carrying the news to their people. The verse in front of us states the practical consequence of the difference: the human being cannot watch the enemy, and he must therefore rely on the guidance that names the enemy's methods.

**The Allies of the Deniers**

The verse closes with the statement that the satans have been made the allies of those who disbelieve, and the Book uses the language of alliance for the relation between the enemy and those who follow him. The sūrah of the Cow describes the hypocrites going out to the satans and asserting that they are with them, and the sūrah of the Women names the party of the adversary among those who take him as a patron (4:76). The sūrah of the Cattle states that enemies have been made for every prophet from among the satans of humanity and the jinn, who inspire one another with flowery speech in order to deceive (6:112).

The statement in front of us describes the arrangement rather than the inclination of the deniers alone. The Book presents the alliance as something that has been established, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that hostile companions have been assigned to those who turn away from the revelation. The verse places the whole matter within the divine ordering of the world that the sūrah describes.

**Allies and the Sūrah's Warning About Protectors**

The mention of allies follows the command in the third verse of the sūrah not to take protectors besides God, and the two passages together define the choice that the chapter presents. The audience is told that it may not take patrons in place of God, and it is told that the relationship it forms by following the adversary is one of alliance with him. The Book's contrast between the allies of God and the allies of the enemy appears in the sūrah of Yūnus, which states that the friends of God have no fear nor do they grieve (10:62).

The sūrah's later passages describe the leaders of misguidance and their followers in the Fire, and the dialogue between them turns on the alliance that the followers had formed (7:38–39). The verse in front of us states the beginning of that relationship in the world, and the chapter's warnings about following the practices of the ancestors and the protectors are all instances of it.

**The Sin That the Verse Names**

The verse speaks of those who disbelieve rather than of those who commit particular acts, and the Book's use of the term covers the settled rejection of the message. The sūrah's audience is addressed as people who have not yet been classified by the community, and the verse states the condition under which the alliance is formed. The Revelation's call to the children of Adam is therefore presented as an invitation to leave the alliance that the enemy has been given with those who reject the message.

The Book's treatment of disbelief in this sūrah is developed in its narratives of the peoples who refused the prophets, and each of those accounts presents a community that chose the alliance and paid for it. The verse in front of us states the principle, and the narratives supply the evidence.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands twenty-seventh in the chapter, and it follows the instruction about clothing and precedes the passage on the shameful deeds that the audience justified by the practice of their ancestors. The address to the children of Adam continues through several verses, and the warning in front of us supplies the reason for the instructions that follow.

The reader who has been told what God has provided is now told what he is protected against. The enemy's sight and the human being's blindness are stated in a single clause, and the revelation with its descriptions of the enemy's methods is presented as the means by which the blindness is compensated.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the excuse of the disbelievers, that they found their forefathers following the shameful deeds they commit and that God commanded them, and it gives the answer that God never commands what is shameful and asks how they attribute to God what they do not know (7:28). The warning about the enemy in front of us is followed by the warning about the reasoning that the enemy supplies.

The address to the children of Adam is thus continued with the exposure of the arguments that keep a community in its practices. The verse in front of us names the deceiver, and the verse that follows names the deception.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:28

> Whenever they commit a shameful deed, they say, “We found our forefathers doing it and Allah has commanded us to do it.” Say, “No! Allah never commands what is shameful. How can you attribute to Allah what you do not know?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Excuse of the Forefathers**

The verse reports the answer that the audience gives when it is confronted with its shameful practices: that it found its forefathers following them and that God has commanded them. The two claims are joined in a single sentence, and the sūrah's answer addresses both. The Book records the same excuse in several passages, and the sūrah of the Cow, the sūrah of the Table, the sūrah of the Prophets, and the sūrah of Luqmān all report the disbelievers answering the call to what God has sent down with the claim that they follow what they found their fathers upon (2:170; 5:104; 21:52–53; 31:21).

The excuse is offered as a justification rather than as an explanation, and the sūrah treats it as the central obstacle to the audience's acceptance of the message. The practice of the ancestors is treated as carrying the authority of custom and precedent, and the verse in front of us shows the audience placing that authority above the revelation. The Book's answer to the claim appears in the same verses that report it, and the answer is that the Book is a better guide than the practices that were inherited.

**The Shameful Deed in Question**

The practice the verse names is described as a shameful deed, and the term covers the sins that are particularly abominable as well as sexual transgression. The commentary tradition connects the verse with the pre-Islamic practice of circumambulating the sanctuary without clothing, and it treats the passage as one of several in the sūrah that address the rituals of the pilgrimage. The sūrah's instruction to the children of Adam to wear their adornment at every place of worship follows a few verses later, and the two passages belong together.

The Book's lists of the shameful deeds include the same term in the sūrah of the Cattle, where the forbidden things are named in sequence, and in the sūrah of the Night Journey, where the list includes the prohibition of approaching indecency. The verse in front of us reports a practice that the audience defended as sacred, and the answer states that God does not command what is shameful.

**The Claim That God Commanded It**

The second half of the excuse attributes the practice to God, and the sūrah's answer rejects the attribution outright. The sūrah of the Cattle reports the same claim from the audience, that if God had willed they would not have associated partners with Him, and it answers the claim in its own terms. The Book's warnings against fabricating lies about God appear throughout, and the sūrah of the Heights names those who do so among the greatest wrongdoers in its later verses.

The claim in front of us is a version of the reasoning that the sūrah's narrative of the adversary has already illustrated, since the adversary also asserted something about God's intention to support his own case. The Book's answer in both instances is that God has not commanded the thing, and the sūrah's following verse supplies the positive statement of what God has commanded instead.

**The Two Questions in the Answer**

The answer given in the verse contains two parts, and the second is a question about how the audience can attribute to God what it does not know. The question exposes the absence of any evidence for the claim, and it places the burden of proof on those who make it. The Book uses the same form of challenge in its arguments about the idols, and the sūrah of the Cattle asks whether the audience has any knowledge to produce.

The sūrah's treatment of knowledge and its absence runs through the chapter's arguments, and the verse in front of us applies the distinction to the practice of attributing commands to God. The audience claims to know what God has commanded, and the sūrah states that it does not know.

**The Authority of the Ancestors**

The excuse of the ancestors has a social dimension, and the Book's narratives of the prophets show the same excuse being offered by the communities that were destroyed. The people of Noah, ʿĀd, and Thamūd each defended their practice by reference to what their fathers had done, and the sūrah's accounts describe the destruction that followed their refusal. The verse in front of us places the Makkan audience in the same position as those communities.

The Book's answer to the authority of custom rests on the revelation that comes to each community, and the sūrah's third verse has already commanded the audience to follow what has been sent down and not to take protectors besides God. The verse in front of us shows the audience preferring the authority of its fathers to the revelation, and the sūrah's next verses state what the revelation commands.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands twenty-eighth in the chapter, and it follows the warning about the deception of the enemy and precedes the statement of what God has commanded. The passage as a whole addresses the children of Adam through the practices of their society, and the sequence of the verses moves from the account of the fall to the correction of the audience's customs.

The reader who has been told about the enemy's methods is now shown the methods at work in the arguments of his own community. The practices defended in front of us are answered by the commands that the next verses state, and the sūrah's argument proceeds from the exposure of the excuse to the statement of the obligation.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse gives the Prophet the command to say that his Lord has commanded justice, that the people should set their faces toward Him at every place of worship, and that they should call upon Him with sincere devotion, and it adds that just as they were first brought into being they will be brought back (7:29). The false attribution in front of us is answered by a positive statement of what God has commanded.

The two verses together present the exchange that the sūrah conducts with its audience: the excuse is given, the attribution is rejected, and the command is stated. The reader is left with the list of what God has actually required.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:29

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “My Lord has commanded uprightness and dedication ˹to Him alone˺ in worship, calling upon Him with sincere devotion. Just as He first brought you into being, you will be brought to life again.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**What God Has Commanded**

The verse gives the Prophet the declaration of what his Lord has commanded, and the statement answers the false attribution of the previous verse. The first command is justice, and the term covers fairness in judgment, in transactions, and in the treatment of people, as well as the testimony of God's oneness. The commentary tradition connects the word with the sūrah of the Family of Imran's statement that God, the angels, and the possessors of knowledge bear witness that there is no god but He and that He upholds justice (3:18), and it reads the command in front of us as a summary of the religion's requirements.

The Book's commands about justice appear throughout its instructions about trade, testimony, and the treatment of enemies, and the sūrah of the Table commands the community to stand firm for God as witnesses to justice. The verse in front of us places justice at the head of the list, and the sūrah's audience, which lived by the customs of its ancestors and its own preferences, is told what the standard actually is.

**Setting the Face Toward God**

The verse commands the people to set their faces toward God at every place of worship, and the phrase describes the orientation of the whole person toward his Lord. The commentary tradition gives two readings of the command, one of which refers to the direction of prayer and the other to the devotion with which the prayer is performed. The Book's language of setting the face toward God appears in the sūrah of the Cow, the sūrah of the Family of Imran, the sūrah of the Cattle, and the sūrah of the Romans, and in each case it describes a whole orientation rather than a posture.

The command to perform this at every place of worship addresses a practice of the audience that the sūrah has been correcting. The Quraysh had placed restrictions on who could wear clothing or eat food within the sacred precinct, and the sūrah's instruction to the children of Adam to take their adornment at every place of worship stands in the same context. The verse in front of us states that the worship is for God alone and that it is to be conducted at every place devoted to Him.

**Calling Upon Him With Sincere Devotion**

The verse commands the people to call upon God with sincerity of religion, and the phrase describes worship offered without any partner in intention. The Book uses the same expression in several passages, and the sūrah of the Troops tells the Prophet to say that he is commanded to worship God with sincerity, and the sūrah of the Clear Proof describes the command given to the people of the earlier books as the worship of God alone with sincere devotion. The sūrah of Yūnus and the sūrah of the Spider use the expression in the context of the distress of the sea voyage, when the travellers call upon God with sincerity before they return to their old associations.

The verse in front of us makes the same demand of the audience at the sanctuary. Their pilgrimage, their offerings, and their invocations were shared between God and their idols, and the command states that the devotion is to be for God alone. The sūrah's later passages on the offerings and the food rules apply the same principle to the practical arrangements of their religion.

**The Return to the Beginning**

The verse closes with the statement that the people will be brought back just as they were first brought into being, and the clause repeats the theme of the sūrah's account of the creation and the resurrection. The sūrah of the Cattle states that the disbelievers will be told that they have come to their Lord alone as they were created the first time, and the sūrah of Maryam and the sūrah of the Prophets describe the raising of the dead as a second creation. The sūrah of the Heights has already stated that the people will live on the earth, die there, and be brought forth from it (7:25).

The clause supplies the argument for the commands. The One who commands justice and sincerity is the One who brought the people into being and who will bring them back, and the Book's appeals to the origin of human life serve as the ground of its appeals to obedience. The verse in front of us places the commands and the resurrection in the same sentence.

**The Argument and Its Audience**

The sūrah's audience accepted the creation of the world by a single Lord even while it worshipped the idols, and the Book's arguments repeatedly use that acceptance as the starting point for the argument about the resurrection. The sūrah of the Spider states that if the people were asked who created the heavens and the earth they would say it was God, and it draws the conclusion that they should therefore be grateful. This verse uses the same movement, since it places the command to sincere worship beside the statement of the return.

The Book's presentation of the resurrection as easier than the first creation appears in the sūrah of the Romans and the sūrah of the Prophets, and the sūrah's audience is asked to accept the argument on that ground. The passage in front of us states the fact without elaboration, and the sūrah's later passages supply the description of the day when the return takes place.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands twenty-ninth in the chapter, and it follows the rejection of the false attribution and precedes the statement about those who have been guided and those who are destined to stray. The passage as a whole answers the audience's justification of its practices with the statement of what God has commanded, and the verses that follow describe the two responses to the guidance.

The reader is given the command in its positive form: justice, orientation toward God, sincerity in worship, and the certainty of the return. The sūrah then states the division among people that the following of the guidance produces.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that God has guided some while others are destined to stray, and it names the reason: those who strayed took the devils as masters in place of God while thinking that they were guided (7:30). The statement of what God has commanded in front of us is followed by the description of the two responses to it.

The two verses together present the sūrah's account of guidance and misguidance, and the verses that follow describe the clothing, the food, and the conduct of the children of Adam in the same terms. The reader is shown the command, the response, and the practices that follow from each.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:30

> He has guided some, while others are destined to stray. They have taken devils as their masters instead of Allah— thinking they are ˹rightly˺ guided.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Division Among People**

The verse states that God has guided some people while others have been marked out for error, and it places the division at the head of the passage about the practices and beliefs of the children of Adam. The Book states the same division in several passages, and the sūrah of the Cattle says that if God had willed He would have gathered all people to guidance (6:35), while the sūrah of the Bee states that God guides whom He wills and leaves in error whom He wills. The verse in front of us presents the two outcomes as a settled arrangement rather than as a momentary condition.

The commentary tradition treats the statement in the context of the theology of guidance. The discussion concerns whether the guidance and the misguidance are rewards and punishments attached to what people have already chosen or determinations that precede their choices, and the sūrah's own words in the following clause give the account that resolves the passage for the reader.

**Those Who Took the Devils as Masters**

The verse names the reason for the condition of those who went astray: they took the devils as masters in place of God. The language is the same as the command in the sūrah's third verse, which forbids taking protectors besides God, and the passage therefore closes the circle that the chapter's opening opened. The devils in front of us are the satans of the previous verses, and the sūrah's narrative of the first pair has shown the kind of mastery they seek.

The Book's descriptions of those who take the enemy as a patron appear in the sūrah of the Cow, the sūrah of the Women, and the sūrah of the Cattle, and the sūrah of the Jinn describes those who sought refuge with the jinn and increased them in error. The verse in front of us states the choice that produces the condition, and it names the patrons that the audience's practices had taken.

**Thinking Themselves Guided**

The verse closes with the statement that those who went astray thought they were on the right path, and the clause describes the state of those who have exchanged the revelation for their own traditions without noticing the exchange. The Book uses the same description elsewhere, and the sūrah of the Cave states that those whose efforts went astray in the life of the world will think that they are doing well (18:104). The verse in front of us applies the description to the audience's contemporaries.

The attribution of good guidance to oneself is presented in the Book as a consequence of having turned away from the source of guidance. The sūrah of the Troops states that God leaves in error those who do not believe, and the sūrah of the Ant describes the deeds of the deluded as adorned for them. The clause in front of us states the confidence that the wrong path produces in those who walk it, and the sūrah's later narratives show the communities pursuing their customs to destruction.

**The Guidance and the Practices**

The verse's placement between the statement of what God has commanded and the passage on dress and food gives it a practical function. The commands of the previous verse are the guidance, and the condition described in front of us belongs to those who have adopted another standard. The practices of the audience, including the restrictions on clothing and food that the sūrah is about to address, are presented as the fruit of the exchange.

The Book's habit of describing religious error in terms of the practices it produces appears throughout the sūrah, and the passage on the forbidden things that follows the verse states the commands in contrast with the invented prohibitions. The reader who follows the argument from the command to the response to the practice sees the sequence in the sūrah's arrangement.

**The Tradition's Discussion of Guidance**

The commentary tradition divides over the theology of guidance, and its division is reflected in the treatment of this verse. Those who held that human beings determine their own destiny read the guidance as a reward for the choice of the guided and the misguidance as a punishment for the choice of the straying, and they pointed to the clause about taking the devils as masters as the indication of the choice. Those who held that the divine will determines all things read the verse as evidence of the divine control of the outcomes.

The two readings agree that the responsibility for the choice belongs to the people who made it, since the verse states what those who went astray did. The Book's language about guidance throughout the revelation includes both the divine grant and the human response, and the verse in front of us places them together in a single sentence.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands thirtieth in the chapter, and it follows the statement of what God has commanded and precedes the passage on the adornment and the food. The address to the children of Adam continues with the description of the two groups, and the practices of each are described in the verses that follow.

The reader is given the command, the two responses to it, and the condition of those who responded wrongly. The sūrah then turns to the specifics of dress and diet that divided the audience from the guidance it had been offered.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse addresses the children of Adam and commands them to wear their adornment at every place of worship, to eat and drink without excess, and it states that God does not love the wasteful (7:31). The condition of those who thought themselves guided without guidance in front of us gives way to the practical commands that the audience's practice had ignored.

The two verses together move from the general account of guidance to the conduct that follows it. The sūrah's passage on clothing, food, and the practices of the pilgrimage is set inside the framework that the verses before it have established.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:31

> O Children of Adam! Dress properly whenever you are at worship. Eat and drink, but do not waste. Surely He does not like the wasteful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Wear Adornment at Worship**

The verse addresses the children of Adam and commands them to take their adornment at every place of worship, and the command follows the account of the first pair's exposure and the instruction about clothing that answered it. The adornment in question is understood by the commentary tradition as the clothing that covers the body, and the command therefore restates the provision that the previous passage announced. The Book's association of beauty with worship appears in its instructions about the pilgrimage and about the prayer, and the sūrah in front of us states the principle at the point where the audience's practice is being corrected.

The practice that the command corrects is described in the reports about the sanctuary. The Quraysh had established rules about the clothing and the food permitted within the sacred precinct, and those who came to perform the pilgrimage were required to use clothing belonging to the Quraysh or to go without. The verse in front of us ends the arrangement by commanding the people to be clothed at every place of worship, and it treats the covering and the adornment as the ordinary condition of prayer.

**Eating and Drinking Without Excess**

The second command instructs the people to eat and drink, and it places a limit on the conduct with a prohibition of excess. The permission is stated before the limitation, and the Book's instructions about food generally follow the same order, granting what has been made lawful and then naming the exceptions. The commentary tradition connects the command with the dietary restrictions that the audience had imposed on itself during the pilgrimage, and it reads the verse as a restoration of what had been forbidden without warrant.

The Book's treatment of lawful consumption appears throughout the sūrah's later passages about the animals and the offerings, and the sūrah of the Cattle contains a list of what has actually been forbidden. The verse in front of us states the permission in its broadest form, and the prohibition that follows it concerns quantity rather than kind.

**The Prohibition of Excess**

The verse states that God does not love the wasteful, and the commentary tradition gives two senses to the term. The first concerns the eating of what has been made lawful to the point of harm, and the second concerns the waste of what has been provided without any benefit from it. Both readings treat the prohibition as a limit on the use of the good things rather than as a restriction on their availability.

The Book's use of the same word for excess appears in the sūrah of the Cattle's account of the gardens and the fields, where the warning against waste accompanies the permission to eat of the produce. The sūrah of the Children of Israel states that the wasteful have been the brothers of the devils, and the verse in front of us places the same estimate of excess at the head of the passage on food.

**The Middle Way of the Passage**

The verse's combination of permission and limit gives the sūrah's teaching about the created goods its balance. The audience's practice had forbidden what God had not forbidden, and the ascetic impulse in any community tends toward the same error. The verse in front of us affirms the enjoyment of the good things and restrains it in the same sentence, and the commentary tradition cites reports in which the Prophet corrected those who proposed to abandon meat, perfume, marriage, and property in the name of devotion.

The Book's teaching about the goods of the world throughout the sūrah includes instructions about the harvest, the livestock, and the offerings, and the verse in front of us supplies the principle that governs all of them. What God has provided is to be used with gratitude, and what has been forbidden is to be avoided, and excess is excluded between the two.

**Clothing, Food, and the Worship**

The verse joins the two subjects of clothing and food under the heading of the places of worship, and the arrangement reflects the context from which the passage comes. The sūrah is addressed to an audience whose religion consisted largely of practices at a sanctuary, and the two matters that the practices regulated were what to wear and what to eat. The verse in front of us supplies the correct rule for both, and it refers the practices to the worship of God alone.

The Book's presentation of the pilgrimage and the prayer as acts of obedience appears in the sūrah of the Cow and the sūrah of the Pilgrimage, and the verse in front of us places the physical preparation for worship among the things that the Lord has commanded. The adornment is worn for the sake of the One before whom the person stands.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands thirty-first in the chapter, and it follows the account of the two groups and precedes the passage that asks who has forbidden the adornments that God has brought forth. The three verses on clothing, food, and the source of the prohibitions form a unit, and the command in front of us supplies the positive side of the matter.

The reader who has been told about the guidance and the straying is given the practical rule that distinguishes them. The provision is enjoyed within the measure that God has set, and the worship is performed in the clothing that He has given, and the excess that the audience practiced in both directions is excluded.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse gives the Prophet the command to ask who has forbidden the adornments and the lawful provisions that God has brought forth for His servants, and it states that they are for the believers in this world and exclusively theirs on the day of judgment (7:32). The command to wear the adornment and to eat and drink in front of us is followed by the exposure of those who forbid what God has provided.

The two verses together complete the passage's argument. The permission is stated, the limit is stated, and the claim of the audience to forbid what God has not forbidden is answered in the verse that follows.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:32

> Ask, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Who has forbidden the adornments and lawful provisions Allah has brought forth for His servants?” Say, “They are for the enjoyment of the believers in this worldly life, but they will be exclusively theirs on the Day of Judgment. This is how We make Our revelations clear for people of knowledge.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Put to the Audience**

The verse gives the Prophet the question of who has forbidden the adornments and the lawful provisions that God has brought forth for His servants, and the question is a challenge to the audience's authority to legislate in matters of religion. The Book's arguments about the invented prohibitions appear in the sūrah of the Cattle, where the audience's division of the harvests and the herds is described and answered, and in the sūrah of the Table, where the practices of the earlier communities are corrected. The question in front of us returns the matter to its source, since the audience holds its prohibitions with no warrant from God.

The Book's treatment of the lawful and the forbidden as a divine prerogative runs through the revelation, and the sūrah of the Cattle's list of the forbidden things is introduced as the statement of what God has actually prohibited. The verse in front of us states the principle in the form of a question, and the answer that follows names the beneficiaries of what has been provided.

**The Adornments and the Provisions**

The two things named in the question are the adornments that the passage on clothing has discussed and the provisions that the following verses address. The Book's use of adornment covers clothing, ornament, and the good things of the world that beautify life, and the commentary tradition notes that both the covering of the body and the ornamental use of wealth are included. The provisions are the foods and the goods that God has made lawful.

The verse states that these have been brought forth for the servants of God, and the phrase assigns them to the people who worship Him. The audience's practice had restricted them to particular groups, and the sūrah's answer restores them to those for whom they were provided. The Book's teaching about the goods of the world includes the statement that they are a provision for the creatures and that God has measured them, and the verse in front of us applies the teaching to the audience's rules.

**For the Believers in This World, Exclusively Theirs in the Hereafter**

The answer states that the good things are for the believers in the present life and that they will be exclusively theirs on the day of judgment. Two matters are joined in the clause. The first is that the believers share the world's goods with the deniers in the present life, since the provision has been given to all people in their earthly existence. The second is that the goods of the Hereafter belong to the believers alone, since the reward of God will be given to those who followed His guidance.

The commentary tradition explains the arrangement as a statement about the two abodes. The world's enjoyments are common in this life and restricted in the next, and the Book's descriptions of the Hereafter include the garden that is prepared for those who believed and acted rightly. The sūrah's later passages describe the gardens and the fire in the dialogue that occupies its middle section.

**The Form of the Answer**

The answer begins with a command to say, and the Prophet is addressed as the one who delivers the reply. The Book's use of the command to say in its arguments gives the Prophet a defined response to the audience's claims, and the sūrah of the Cattle uses the same form in its answers about the offerings and the crops. The verse in front of us supplies the reply to the question of who forbade the good things, and it states the destination of what has been provided.

The reply also implies the destruction of the audience's system of prohibition. If the good things were brought forth for the servants of God, then the claim to forbid them is a claim to overturn what God has provided, and the Book's warnings about fabricating lies against God address exactly that. The verse in front of us closes the passage on clothing and food with the statement of the divine provision.

**The Book's Teaching about the Goods of the World**

The Book's treatment of the good things of the world includes warnings against their excessive love alongside affirmations of their lawfulness, and the verse in front of us belongs to the side of affirmation. The sūrah of the Cave states that wealth and children are an adornment of the present life while the lasting good deeds are better with God, and the sūrah of the Iron states that people should not forget their share of the world. The verse in front of us states the provision's destination and leaves the manner of its use to the commands that the sūrah has given.

The audience that heard the verse lived in a society of markets and caravans, and the goods of the world were part of its daily concern. The verse affirms them as a provision and warns against the practices that restricted them, and the sūrah then states the things that God has actually forbidden.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands thirty-second in the chapter, and it follows the commands about dress and food and precedes the list of the prohibited things. The three verses together form the sūrah's treatment of the audience's dietary and ritual rules, and the verse in front of us supplies the principle that the next verse states in the form of a list.

The reader who has been told to wear his adornment and to eat without excess is now told that the goods have been provided for him and that no one has the authority to forbid what God has permitted. The sūrah then names what God has forbidden, and the list is short.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse gives the Prophet the statement that his Lord has forbidden only open and secret indecencies, sin, unjust aggression, the association of partners with God, and the attribution to God of what people do not know (7:33). The question about the good things in front of us is followed by the enumeration of the actual prohibitions, and the passage then states the term appointed for every community.

The two verses together set the two lists side by side: what God has provided and what God has forbidden. The reader is given the boundaries in their true extent, and the practices that the audience had invented are left outside them.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:33

> Say, “My Lord has only forbidden open and secret indecencies, sinfulness, unjust aggression, associating ˹others˺ with Allah ˹in worship˺—a practice He has never authorized—and attributing to Allah what you do not know.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The List of the Forbidden Things**

The verse gives the Prophet the statement of what his Lord has actually forbidden, and the list that follows contains five items. The enumeration answers the audience's system of prohibitions, and it presents the divine commands in their true scope. The Book provides similar lists in the sūrah of the Cattle and the sūrah of the Night Journey, where the prohibitions are set out in sequence, and the passage in front of us belongs with them.

The length of the list is part of its message. The audience had surrounded its diet, its pilgrimage, and its livestock with rules that it attributed to God, and the replacement list is short and specific. The sūrah's next verses state that the terms of the communities are fixed and that the messengers are sent with the revelation, and the brief list in front of us is the standard against which the community is measured.

**Open and Secret Indecencies**

The first items are the shameful deeds committed openly and in secret, and the phrase covers both the public act and the hidden intention that precedes it. The Book's treatment of the two categories appears in the sūrah of the Cattle, where the prohibition of open and secret indecency is stated in its own list, and in the sūrah of the Night Journey, where the prohibition of approaching indecency is joined to the prohibition of killing and of taking the orphan's property.

The pairing of the outward act and the inward intention gives the verse its reach. The audience could regulate its public conduct while leaving its intention unexamined, and the verse includes both. The sūrah's teaching about the heart and the outward act appears in its treatment of the two groups that the previous verses described.

**Sin and Unjust Aggression**

The second and third items are sin and unjust aggression, and the commentary tradition explains the first as the transgression of the divine limits and the second as the oppression of people by the misuse of power. The Book's descriptions of aggression appear in its accounts of the communities that used their strength against the weak, and the sūrah's narratives of ʿĀd, Thamūd, and Pharaoh supply the examples. The term also carries the sense of arrogance, and the sūrah's account of the refusal of the adversary uses the same language.

The two items together cover the wrongs done against God's limits and the wrongs done against people. The Book's lists of the major sins generally include both categories, and the sūrah's earlier verses on the weighing of deeds and the questioning of the messengers place the judgment of both in the reckoning.

**Associating Partners With God**

The fourth item is the association of partners with God in worship, and the verse states that no authority has been sent down for it. The Book identifies this sin as the one that is not forgiven if a person dies without repenting, and the sūrah of the Women states the principle twice in its passage on the matter. The sūrah's own argument has been directed at the association from its opening verses, and the narrative of the adversary has shown the first refusal that produced it.

The phrase about the absence of authority returns to the challenge that the verse has made to the audience's claims. The partners are worshipped without any warrant, and the Book's question about what knowledge the audience possesses is answered by the statement that no authority has come down for them. The passage in front of us places the association at the head of the forbidden things in a chapter that has devoted its narratives and arguments to it.

**Attributing to God What Is Not Known**

The fifth item is the attribution to God of what people do not know, and the phrase covers the false claims about His commands that the audience made in the previous verses. The sūrah's answer to the claim that God commanded the shameful deeds has already been given, and the verse in front of us names the practice itself among the forbidden things. The Book's warnings about fabricating lies against God appear in the sūrah of the Cattle, the sūrah of the Table, and the sūrah of the Heights itself, and the sūrah's later verses name those who do it among the greatest wrongdoers.

The prohibition closes the list where the passage began, since the audience's false attributions were the source of the rules that the sūrah has been correcting. The reader is shown the connection between the invented prohibition and the false report about God, and the sūrah's argument against both is complete.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands thirty-third in the chapter, and it follows the question about the good things and precedes the statement about the appointed term of every community. The passage on dress, food, and prohibition is thus closed by the list of what is actually forbidden, and the sūrah then continues with the warning about the term and the messengers.

The reader has been given the permission, the limit, the provision's destination, and the list of the forbidden things. The sūrah's later narratives show the communities that transgressed the list and the messengers who delivered it, and the reckoning that the opening verses described awaits both.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that every community has an appointed term that can neither be delayed nor advanced (7:34). The statement of the forbidden things is followed by the statement of the fixed term, and the passage then turns to the messengers who come to the children of Adam.

The two verses together set the boundaries and the duration in which they apply. The community that has received the revelation has a term in which to follow it, and the verse in front of us states that the term is not subject to negotiation.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:34

> For each community there is an appointed term. When their time arrives, they can neither delay it for a moment, nor could they advance it.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Appointed Term**

The verse states that every community has a term appointed for it, and that when the term arrives it can be neither delayed by a moment nor advanced. The Book repeats the statement in several passages, and the sūrah of Yūnus states that every community has a term and that when it comes there is no delaying it (10:49); the sūrah of the Rocky Tract and the sūrah of the Believers state that no community can outrun its term or delay it (15:5; 23:43); the sūrah of the Sheba, the sūrah of Noah, and the sūrah of the Bee apply the same language to the term of peoples and of individuals (34:30; 71:4; 16:61). The verse in front of us states the principle in the form of a declaration.

The statement follows the list of the forbidden things, and it supplies the framework in which the commands operate. The community that has received the revelation has a fixed span in which to obey, and the span is not extended by the prosperity of the people or shortened by their delay. The Book's warnings about the destroyed communities rest on the same principle, since each community's destruction came at the term that had been set for it.

**Terms for Communities and for Individuals**

The Book applies the term to peoples and to individual human beings, and the two uses belong together. The sūrah of the Heights describes the destruction of the communities that refused their messengers, and each of those destructions is presented as the arrival of a term. The sūrah of the Family of Imran states that no soul dies except by the permission of God at a term appointed, and the sūrah of the Sheba gives the same language for the death of individuals. The verse in front of us states the communal form of the principle, and the sūrah's narratives show its individual application.

The two levels connect for the reader of the sūrah, since the audience lives inside a community with its own term and inside a life with its own span. The Book's addresses to the community frequently refer to the possibility of sudden death and to the arrival of the reckoning without warning, and the verse in front of us supplies the reason for the urgency.

**The Impossibility of Delay and Advance**

The two halves of the verse exclude the possibility of any adjustment to the term, and the Book repeats the formula in its accounts of the destruction of the earlier peoples. The statement rules out both the demand for a postponement and the request for an earlier resolution, and the sūrah of the Prophets reports the audience's demand that the punishment be brought forward. The audience's challenge to the Prophet in Makkah included both kinds of request, and the verse in front of us answers both.

The Book's treatment of the demand for signs and for punishment appears in its accounts of the messengers whose peoples asked when the promise would be fulfilled, and the sūrah of the Heights itself refers to the request for the punishment to be hastened in its later verses. The verse in front of us states the fixed character of the term, and the sūrah's narrative of the destroyed communities shows the arrival that no request could move.

**The Term and the Warning**

The fixed term gives the warning its urgency, and the sūrah's instructions are addressed to people whose time is finite. The Book's calls to repentance are frequently accompanied by the reminder that the term may arrive before the intention is carried out, and the sūrah of the Hypocrites states that God reprieves no one when his term comes. This verse states the arrangement, and the sūrah's remaining verses describe what happens when the term of a community arrives.

The Book's depictions of the last day include the statement that the people will confess the truth when it is too late, and the sūrah's opening verses have already presented the confession of the destroyed communities. The term in front of us is the boundary that separates the time of action from the time of accounting, and the sūrah's reader is placed on the near side of it.

**The Term and the Messengers**

The verse's placement before the passage on the messengers gives the term a particular significance. The messengers are sent to communities that have a term, and their mission operates within the span that the community has been given. The sūrah's accounts of the prophets and their peoples follow this arrangement, since each messenger calls his people while the term is running and each people's destruction follows the expiry of the period of warning.

The Book's stated pattern is that no community is destroyed until a messenger has been sent to it, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that God would not destroy a community until He had sent a messenger to it. The passage in front of us supplies the time frame of the mission, and the passage that follows states the terms on which the messengers deliver their message.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands thirty-fourth in the chapter, and it follows the list of the forbidden things and precedes the address to the children of Adam about the messengers. The passage as a whole moves from the commands to the communities that received them, and the term in front of us supplies the frame within which the commands and the responses to them take place.

The reader has been given the provision, the limits, and now the duration. The sūrah's narratives of the peoples who refused their messengers are the record of what happened when the terms of those peoples arrived, and the audience is addressed as a community whose own term is running.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse addresses the children of Adam and states that when messengers from among them come reciting the revelations of their Lord, those who are mindful and mend their conduct will have no fear and no grief (7:35). The statement of the fixed term is followed by the description of the messengers who come within it, and the promises attached to the response are named.

The two verses together present the mission and its time. The community has a term, the messengers come within it with the revelation, and the response determines whether the term's end brings the promised security or the warning that the following verse describes.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:35

> O children of Adam! When messengers from among yourselves come to you reciting My revelations—whoever shuns evil and mends their ways, there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Messengers From Among the People**

The verse addresses the children of Adam and states that messengers will come to them from among themselves, reciting the revelations of their Lord. The Book's accounts of the messengers present them as human beings drawn from the communities they address, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the argument of the disbelievers that a messenger should have been an angel. The sūrah of the Heights itself will report the same argument in its account of Noah and his people, where the notables object that the messenger is only a man like themselves.

The phrase from among yourselves states the arrangement that the Book consistently follows, and the commentary tradition notes its significance for the reception of the message. The messenger shares the language, the history, and the circumstances of the people he addresses, and the revelation he recites is therefore accessible to them. The sūrah's later accounts of the prophets show each of them addressing his own people in that manner.

**The Recitation of the Revelations**

The verse describes the messengers as reciting the revelations of their Lord, and the recitation is the form in which the message reaches the community. The Book's use of the term for its own text presents it as something read aloud, and the sūrah of the Heights begins with letters that belong to the same presentation. The recitation in front of us is the activity by which the messenger conveys what has been sent down.

The Book's descriptions of the messengers' work include the delivery of the message and the warning that accompanies it, and the sūrah's opening verses have stated the duty of the Prophet in those terms. The verse in front of us states the same work in the context of the children of Adam, and the two passages together define the mission.

**Those Who Are Mindful and Mend Their Ways**

The verse names the response that carries the promise: those who are mindful of God and who mend their conduct. The phrase describes a turning from the wrong path to the right one, and the commentary tradition explains it as repentance from the former conduct combined with obedience to the commands the messenger brings. The Book's use of the phrase appears in its descriptions of the righteous in several sūrahs, and the sūrah of the Heights uses it again in its account of Shuʿayb's people.

The promise attached to the response is the absence of fear and grief. The Book repeats the formula in several passages, and the sūrah of the Cow states it in its account of those who follow the guidance (2:38), the sūrah of the Family of Imran states it of those who have believed and are mindful, and the sūrah of Yūnus states it of the friends of God (10:62). The formula in front of us is one of the Book's standard descriptions of the state of the saved.

**The Fear and the Grief That Are Removed**

The two words name the anxieties that the Book associates with the reckoning and with loss. Fear concerns what is coming, and grief concerns what has gone. The promise is that neither will afflict those who respond to the messengers, and the sūrah's opening verses on the weighing of deeds have already supplied the picture of the day on which the two afflictions would otherwise appear. The formula in front of us is therefore connected with the chapter's own account of the judgment.

The Book's descriptions of the Hereafter include the removal of grief from the people of Paradise, and the sūrah of the Heights will state in its later verses that God has removed rancour from the hearts of the believers. The promise in front of us states the outcome for those who respond while their term is running.

**The Address to the Children of Adam**

The verse's address to the children of Adam continues the sequence of addresses that the sūrah began after the account of the first pair. The audience is therefore placed in the position of the descendants who receive the messengers, and the terms of the promise are stated for them. The Book's practice of addressing the descendants of Adam in instructions about conduct appears throughout this passage, and the call to listen to the messengers is the natural continuation of the earlier commands.

The sūrah's later passages describe the communities that refused the messengers who came to them, and the contrast with the verse in front of us is deliberate. The children of Adam are told what awaits them if they respond, and the narratives that follow show what happened when the response was refusal.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands thirty-fifth in the chapter, and it follows the statement of the appointed term and precedes the account of those who deny the revelations and treat them with disdain. The passage that follows the deliverance of the messengers presents the two responses, and the verse in front of us states the first of them with its promise.

The reader who has been given the commands, the term, and the messengers now receives the statement of the reward. The sūrah then states the condition of those who refuse, and the two descriptions stand side by side in the chapter's arrangement.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that those who deny the revelations and treat them with disdain are the inhabitants of the Fire, where they will remain (7:36). The promise to those who respond in front of us is followed by the description of those who refuse, and the sūrah's account of the two outcomes is complete.

The two verses together present the division that the passage has been preparing. The messengers come with the revelation, one group accepts it and is promised the removal of fear and grief, and the other treats it with contempt and is assigned the Fire.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:36

> But those who receive Our revelations with denial and arrogance will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Those Who Deny and Those Who Disdain**

The verse names two aspects of the same condition: the denial of the revelations and the disdain with which the revelations are treated. The commentary tradition explains that the denial covers the rejection of the message of God's oneness and of the commands that the messengers brought, while the disdain covers the pride that prevents a person from affirming what he knows to be true. The two are joined in the verse because the one leads to the other, and the sūrah's own narrative of the adversary has already shown the refusal that begins with an argument about status.

The Book's descriptions of the deniers include the element of pride in several passages, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that some turn away from the signs out of arrogance as though they had not heard them. The verse in front of us states the two features together, and the following verse adds the fabricated claims that accompany them.

**The Destination of the Deniers**

The verse states that those who deny and disdain are the inhabitants of the Fire and that they will remain there. The Book states the same in several passages, and the sūrah of the Cow states that those who deny the signs are the companions of the Fire and will remain there, while the sūrah of the Table and the sūrah of the Iron repeat the statement. The description in front of us matches the Book's standard formula for the outcome.

The sūrah's arrangement places the verse after the promise to those who are mindful and mend their conduct, and the two outcomes stand in immediate contrast. The reader is given the state of the saved and the state of the condemned in a single movement, and the sūrah's later passages describe the two states in the dialogue between the people of Paradise and the people of the Fire.

**The Meaning of the Abiding**

The Book's statements about the duration of the punishment appear in its descriptions of the two abodes, and the sūrah of the Heights will state in its later verses that the people of the Fire will not enter the Garden until a camel passes through the eye of a needle. The commentary tradition discusses the duration of the punishment and the possibility of its end within the framework of the Book's statements about the divine will, and the verse in front of us states the standard formula without qualification.

The Book's descriptions of the two abodes include the permanence of the reward for the people of Paradise and the terms of the punishment for the condemned, and the sūrah's later passages give both. The verse in front of us states the assignment of the second group, and the chapter's argument about the choices that produce it is already complete.

**The Pride That Produces the Denial**

The verse's mention of disdain identifies the disposition that the Book holds responsible for the refusal of the message, and the sūrah's narratives supply the instances. The people of Noah answer their messenger with the claim that only the weak have followed him; ʿĀd relies on its strength; Thamūd trusts in its dwellings; and the notables of the audience in Makkah refuse to accept a messenger from outside their leading families. The verse in front of us states the general condition of which each of these is an instance.

The Book's treatment of pride as an obstacle to the Garden appears in the sūrah of the Heights itself, where the refusal of the adversary is described as arrogance in a place where it has no standing (7:13). This verse applies the same diagnosis to the human beings who treat the signs with contempt, and the connection between the two passages is deliberate.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands thirty-sixth in the chapter, and it follows the promise to those who respond to the messengers and precedes the account of the greatest wrongdoers. The passage that begins with the commands and the term now states the two responses and their outcomes, and the verse in front of us completes the description of the second response.

The reader has been given the provision, the limits, the term, the messengers, and the promise. The passage in front of us adds the warning that accompanies the promise, and the sūrah then turns to the details of the reckoning that follows the denial.

**The Abiding and the Promise**

The verse's phrase about abiding matches the statement in the following passage about the exclusion of the proud from the Garden, and the two together describe a condition that has no term. The reward promised to those who are mindful and mend their conduct is described in the same Book with the same language of permanence, and the sūrah's arrangement places the two destinations side by side: the Fire for those who denied the signs and treated them with contempt, and the Garden for those who accepted the revelation and acted on it. The reader is given both before the dialogue of the Fire's inhabitants begins.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse asks who does greater wrong than those who fabricate lies against God or deny His signs, and it describes the arrival of their appointed portion, the coming of the messengers who take their souls, the failure of the beings they invoked, and their witness against themselves (7:37). The condition of the deniers in front of us is followed by the account of their end, and the details of that end are set out in the verse that follows.

The two verses together present the denial and its consequence. The verse under discussion assigns the destination, and the verse that follows describes the process by which the person arrives there, from the taking of the soul to the confession that precedes the Fire.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:37

> Who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah or deny His revelations? They will receive what is destined for them, until Our messenger-angels arrive to take their souls, asking them, “Where are those ˹false gods˺ you used to invoke besides Allah?” They will cry, “They have failed us,” and they will confess against themselves that they were indeed disbelievers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Greatest Wrongdoers**

The verse opens with a question about who does greater wrong than the person who fabricates lies against God or denies His signs, and the Book uses the question in several passages. The sūrah of the Cattle puts the same question twice in its accounts of the idolaters and of the prohibition of what God has permitted, the sūrah of Yūnus and the sūrah of Hūd use it of those who invent falsehood, the sūrah of the Cave and the sūrah of the Spider use it of the fabricators generally, and the sūrah of the Ranks uses it of those who invent lies about God. The question in front of us gathers the two offenses that the sūrah's passage on the forbidden things has already named.

The fabrication in question includes the claims the audience made about what God had commanded and forbidden, and the denial includes the rejection of the revelations that the messengers recited. The verse names the combination as the height of wrongdoing, and the sūrah's argument against the invented rules and the false attributions has prepared the reader to recognize the offense.

**The Portion That Reaches Them**

The verse states that those who fabricate and deny will receive their portion of what has been written for them, and the commentary tradition offers several readings of the phrase. The portion may be the share of worldly provision and longevity that has been decreed, the share of good or ill that befalls a person, or the share of the punishment that has been assigned. The readings agree that the phrase describes something measured and delivered in full.

The Book's use of the language of portion appears in its descriptions of the provision that each creature receives, and the sūrah of Hūd states that there is no creature on the earth whose provision is not with God. The verse in front of us applies the language to the end of the deniers, and the arrival of the angels follows the receipt of the portion.

**The Messengers Who Take the Soul**

The verse describes the arrival of the messengers of God who take the deniers away, and the Book identifies them as the angels charged with the collection of souls. The sūrah of the Cattle describes the same messengers and their work, and the sūrah of the Prostration states that the angel of death who has been charged with the people takes them in full. The scene in front of us follows the person to the moment of death, and it places the questioning there.

The question the angels put to the deniers concerns the beings they used to invoke besides God, and the answer they receive is that those beings have failed them. The Book's descriptions of the Hereafter include the same abandonment, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that the partners of the idolaters will say that they were not being worshipped by them. The verse in front of us presents the abandonment at the taking of the soul, and the sūrah's later verses show the same parties addressing one another in the Fire.

**The Witness Against Themselves**

The verse closes with the statement that the deniers bear witness against themselves that they were disbelievers, and the Book uses the language of self-witness in several passages. The sūrah of the Cattle and the sūrah of the Bee report the confessions of the condemned, and the sūrah of Fussilat describes the testimony of the skin and the limbs of the wrongdoers against them. The witness in front of us is given by the persons themselves, and the acknowledgment is complete.

The Book's descriptions of the reckoning include the closing of the mouths and the testimony of the limbs, and the two arrangements serve the same purpose in the narrative. What the verse in front of us establishes is that the deniers recognize their condition at the moment when recognition can no longer change it, and the sūrah's opening verses have already described the same kind of late confession in the case of the destroyed communities.

**The Sūrah's Account of the Denial**

The verse's details gather the elements that the sūrah has presented in its earlier passages. The fabricated claims about God appear in the audience's defense of its practices; the denial of the signs appears in the treatment of the messenger's recitation; and the abandonment by the beings invoked appears in the sūrah's warnings about the protectors taken in place of God. The verse in front of us shows the working out of all three at the moment of death.

The Book's placement of such scenes at the point of death rather than at the resurrection appears in several passages, and the sūrah of the Women describes the condition of those whom the angels take while they are wronging themselves. The verse in front of us belongs to that family, and the sūrah's following verse continues the scene into the Fire itself.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands thirty-seventh in the chapter, and it follows the assignment of the deniers to the Fire and precedes the command to enter it alongside the communities that went before.
