The transition from the verse before it to the verse after it is a transition from the taking of the soul to the entrance into the punishment, and the details in front of us fill the interval.

The reader has been shown the fabrication, the denial, the portion, the taking, the abandonment, and the confession. The sūrah then describes the entry into the Fire and the recriminations among the communities that are gathered there.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the command to enter the Fire among the communities of jinn and humanity that passed away before, and it describes each community cursing its sister, the blaming of the earlier generations by the later, the request that the leaders receive a double punishment, and the answer that the punishment has been multiplied for all (7:38). The confession of the previous verse is followed by the communal scene of the Fire.

The two verses together complete the account of the end of the deniers. The individual is taken and confesses, and the communities are gathered and quarrel, and the sūrah's teaching about responsibility is stated through the exchange that follows.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:38

> Allah will say, “Enter the Fire along with the ˹evil˺ groups of jinn and humans that preceded you.” Whenever a group enters Hell, it will curse the preceding one until they are all gathered inside, the followers will say about their leaders, “Our Lord! They have misled us, so multiply their torment in the Fire.” He will answer, “It has already been multiplied for all, but you do not know.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Entry Alongside the Earlier Communities**

The verse reports the command given to those who fabricated lies and denied the signs to enter the Fire together with the communities of jinn and humanity that passed away before them. The Book's descriptions of the gathering of the condemned include the pairing of human beings with the jinn who misled them, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that the jinn will be gathered with their human associates. The entry in front of us places the communities of the present among all the communities that preceded them, and the sūrah's narratives of the destroyed peoples are completed by the scene.

The command describes the company into which the condemned are gathered, and the company includes every generation that followed the same path. The Book's treatment of the last day includes the gathering of each community with its leaders and its followers, and the sūrah's earlier verses on the questioning of the recipients and the messengers have prepared the ground for the assembly in front of us.

**Each Community Cursing Its Sister**

The verse describes the entry of the communities one after another, with each community cursing the one that preceded it. The commentary tradition offers two readings of the phrase, one of which takes the communities as the successive generations of a single religious community and the other as the successive communities of misguidance as a whole. Both readings describe the same feature of the scene: the condemned turn on one another as each group enters.

The curse in front of us marks the end of the solidarity that the communities had maintained in the world. The Book's descriptions of the Hereafter include the mutual disowning of the leaders and the followers, and the sūrah of the Spider, the sūrah of the Sheba, and the sūrah of the Cow describe the allies who will become enemies on the day. The verse in front of us presents the first stage of that estrangement, and the dialogue that follows shows it in full.

**The Blaming of the Earlier by the Later**

The verse reports that when the communities have all arrived, the last of them will say of the first that they misled them and will ask that the earlier communities receive a double punishment. The arrangement reverses the sequence of the world, since the later generations had followed the earlier ones and now hold them responsible. The sūrah of the Confederates reports the same complaint in other words, where the followers say that they obeyed their leaders and the leaders led them astray (33:67).

The commentary tradition reads the statement as an attempt to shift the burden, and the answer given in the following verse closes the attempt. The Book's treatment of the excuse of following the ancestors appears throughout, and the sūrah's own passage on the practices of the forefathers has already rejected the excuse for the world. The scene in front of us shows the rejection being confirmed at the reckoning.

**The Answer: Doubled for All**

The verse reports the answer given to the request, that the punishment has been doubled for all, and the answer grants the request while denying its purpose. The leaders who misled others carry their own guilt and the guilt of having misled them, and the followers who were misled carry their own guilt for having followed. The Book's statements about the burden of the leaders appear in the sūrah of the Spider, where the leaders promise to bear the sins of the followers and are told that they bear nothing of them, and in the sūrah of the Bee, where the leaders will bear their own burdens and other burdens with them (16:25).

The phrase that the punishment is doubled for all also closes the possibility of comparison between the two groups. Neither the leaders nor the followers escape by the doubling of the other's punishment, and the sūrah's opening verses on the weighing of deeds have already stated that no soul carries another's burden. The answer in front of us applies that principle to the most persistent excuse that the condemned offer.

**The Rancour of the Fire**

The verse's scene of mutual cursing is completed by the sūrah's later statement that God has removed rancour from the hearts of the people of Paradise, and the contrast between the two communities is one of the passage's purposes. The Book's descriptions of the two abodes include greetings of peace among the saved and recriminations among the condemned, and the sūrah of the Confederates states that friends will be enemies on that day except for the mindful (43:67). The verse in front of us supplies the picture of the second community.

The Book's teaching about the social relations of the Hereafter reflects its teaching about the loyalties of the world. The communities that supported one another in their denial are separated by the outcome they share, and the verse in front of us shows the separation beginning with the first entry into the Fire.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands thirty-eighth in the chapter, and it follows the taking of the souls and the confession and precedes the answer that the first will give to the last. The passage as a whole describes the fate of the communities that refused, and the verse in front of us presents the assembly in which the recriminations take place.

The reader who has followed the sūrah's account of the messengers and the peoples now sees the peoples gathered in the Fire and quarrelling. The sūrah then provides the last word of the leaders, and the passage closes with the image of the needle's eye.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the first will say to the last that they were no better than them and that they should taste the punishment for what they earned (7:39). The quarrel in front of us is completed by the reply of the leaders, and the two speeches together present the full exchange of the condemned.

The two verses together state the mutual recrimination of the Fire, and the sūrah then turns to the fate of those who deny the signs and treat them with contempt, whose entry into the Garden is compared with the passage of a camel through the eye of a needle.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:39

> Then the leaders will say to their followers, “You were no better than us! So taste the torment for what you used to commit.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reply of the Earlier to the Later**

The verse reports the answer that the first of the communities give to the last, and the answer rejects the claim that the later generations were led astray without responsibility. The leaders state that the followers were no better than they were, and they call on them to taste the punishment for what they earned. The verse completes the exchange that the previous verse began, and it closes the attempt to transfer the burden from one group to another.

The Book's treatment of the mutual accusations of the Hereafter appears in several passages, and the sūrah of the Confederates presents the followers addressing their leaders with the request that they be given a double punishment (33:68). The reply in front of us is the answer of the accused leaders, and it consists in the observation that the followers made the same choice. The sūrah's teaching about responsibility is stated in the exchange: each group bears what it earned.

**You Were No Better Than Us**

The phrase states the equality of the two groups in the matter that produced their condition. The followers had claimed that they were led astray, and the reply points out that they followed willingly and shared the same denial. The Book's descriptions of the followers in the world include their eagerness to imitate their leaders and their refusal to consider the message on its own terms, and the sūrah's passage on the practices of the forefathers has already described the same willingness.

The observation does not excuse the leaders, since the previous verse has stated that the punishment is multiplied for both. What it does is remove the distinction on which the followers' request depended. Neither group can claim to have been carried into error, and the verse in front of us states the equality in plain words.

**Tasting the Punishment for What Was Earned**

The second half of the reply calls on the followers to taste the punishment for what they earned, and the phrase assigns the punishment to the deeds of those who receive it. The Book's language about earning appears in its accounts of the reward and the punishment, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that no one reaps except what he sows (6:164). The reply in front of us applies the principle to the quarrel of the condemned, and it leaves both groups with the record of their own acts.

The verb of tasting is one of the Book's standing expressions for the experience of punishment, and it appears in its descriptions of the destroyed communities and of the Hereafter alike. The verse in front of us uses it in the dialogue of the Fire, and the sūrah's accounts of the peoples who were destroyed in the world have already used it in the same sense.

**The Rancour and Its End**

The verse closes the exchange between the two groups, and the sūrah does not report any further reply from the followers. The scene is left with the recriminations spoken and the punishment shared, and the passage then turns to those who deny the signs and the gates that will not open for them. The Book's descriptions of the Fire include the quarrelling of its inhabitants as one of the features of their condition, and the verse in front of us supplies the last speech of that quarrel.

The commentary tradition reads the passage as a warning against the excuses that the audience used in the world. The claim to have followed the ancestors and the leaders is answered at the reckoning by the leaders themselves, and the answer removes the ground on which the claim rested. The sūrah's reader who recognizes the excuse in his own community is given its end in the dialogue in front of him.

**The Book's Account of Responsibility**

The Book's statements about responsibility appear throughout the revelation, and the passage in front of us belongs with those that place the burden on the individual. The sūrah has already stated that no soul carries another's burden and that every person reaps what he sows (6:164), and the Book's descriptions of the day include the giving of the record to each person separately. The reply of the leaders in front of us applies that arrangement to the relations between generations and between classes.

The Book's warnings about following others without examination appear in its instructions to the community to use the faculties of hearing, sight, and reason, and the sūrah of the Night Journey states that the hearing, the sight, and the heart will all be questioned. The verse in front of us states the outcome for those who did not use them, and the sūrah's teaching about the revelation as the guide returns in the exchange of the condemned.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands thirty-ninth in the chapter, and it completes the dialogue of the previous verse. The verses that follow turn to those who deny the signs and are too proud to accept them, and the passage describes the gates of the Garden and the comparison with the camel and the needle. The reply in front of us closes the scene of the Fire's recriminations.

The reader has been given the entry of the communities, the cursing, the blaming, the request, the doubling, and the reply. The sūrah then moves from the assembly of the condemned to the final statement of their exclusion from the Garden.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the gates of heaven will not be opened for those who deny the signs and treat them with disdain, and that they will not enter the Garden until a camel passes through the eye of a needle, and it closes with the statement that this is how the wicked are recompensed (7:40). The dialogue of the condemned in front of us is followed by the declaration of their exclusion, and the sūrah's account of their end is complete.

The two verses together state the end of the path that the sūrah's opening verses have described. The community that fabricated and denied is gathered, quarrels, and is excluded, and the reader is left with the image of the needle's eye that the Book uses for the impossibility of the outcome.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:40

> Surely those who receive our revelations with denial and arrogance, the gates of heaven will not be opened for them, nor will they enter Paradise until a camel passes through the eye of a needle. This is how We reward the wicked.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Those Who Deny and Disdain**

The verse returns to the condition that the passage has described, naming those who deny the signs of their Lord and are too proud to accept them. The Book mentions the same combination in several passages, and the sūrah of the Heights itself, the sūrah of the Troops, and the sūrah of Fussilat describe the proud among those who reject the message (7:36; 39:59; 41:15). The verse in front of us places the condition at the head of the sentence that excludes them from the Garden.

The denial and the disdain are joined because the sūrah's narratives have shown them operating together. The communities that refused their messengers did not simply lack information; they regarded the message as beneath them, whether because the messenger was a man like themselves or because the message came to the poor before it came to the great. The verse in front of us states the condition in the terms that the sūrah has used throughout.

**The Gates of Heaven Closed**

The verse states that the gates of heaven will not be opened for those who are proud, and the commentary tradition gives two readings of the phrase. One reading refers to the Hereafter and to the entry into the Garden, which the verse states directly in its following clause. The other reading refers to the ascent of the deeds and prayers of such people, on the basis of the sūrah of Fāṭir's statement that good words ascend to God and that He lifts up the righteous deed (35:10). The two readings are joined in the tradition, since the refusal of the deed's ascent and the refusal of the person's entry belong to the same exclusion.

The image of the gates appears in several of the Book's descriptions of the Hereafter, and the sūrah of the Troops describes the crowds driven to Hell until its gates are opened, and the gardens whose gates are opened for the mindful as they arrive. The verse in front of us states the exclusion of the proud from the opening itself, and the sūrah's later descriptions of the two abodes supply the rest of the picture.

**The Camel and the Needle**

The verse states that the deniers will not enter the Garden until a camel passes through the eye of a needle, and the image is one of the Book's most memorable for an impossibility. The commentary tradition notes that the Arabic word may be read in two ways, one of which denotes the camel and the other a thick cable of the kind used to moor ships, and al-Zamakhsharī preferred the second reading because a cable through a needle's eye gives a more natural image. The two readings leave the force of the comparison intact.

The image appears in the Gospels as well, where the difficulty of a rich man's entry into the kingdom of God is compared to a camel's passage through the eye of a needle. The Book's use of the image in front of us concerns the proud and the deniers rather than the wealthy as such, and the point of the comparison is the same in both cases: the outcome is impossible while the condition remains.

**The Recompense of the Wicked**

The verse closes with the statement that this is how the wicked are recompensed, and the term identifies those who have earned the exclusion. The Book's descriptions of the reward of the wicked appear throughout, and the sūrah of the Heights will state in its following verses that the Fire is their bed and that the flames are their covering. This verse supplies the verdict, and the verses that follow describe the condition.

The Book's practice of closing a description of punishment with the statement that it is the recompense of a particular class appears throughout its accounts of the Hereafter, and the sūrah of the Clatterer and the sūrah of the Inevitable use the same form. The passage in front of us applies the form to the deniers and the proud, and the reader is left with the contrast between their condition and the condition of those who entered the Garden with their Lord's permission.

**The Pride That Bars the Garden**

The exclusion of the proud in front of us recalls the sūrah's account of the first refusal, when the adversary was told that arrogance had no place in the Garden (7:13). The two passages are connected by the same diagnosis, and the sūrah's narrative of the first human pair and its descriptions of the peoples who refused their messengers present the same fault in its individual and communal forms. The verse under discussion states the outcome for those who carry the fault to the end.

The Book's teaching about humility appears in its instructions about prayer, its warnings against boasting, and its statements that the servants of the Most Merciful walk upon the earth with humility. The text in front of us states the other side of the matter, since the pride that the Book condemns is the condition that bars the gate.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fortieth in the chapter, and it follows the dialogue of the condemned and precedes the description of their bed in the Fire and of the gardens prepared for the believers. The passage as a whole closes the sūrah's opening section, which began with the letters and the Book and has moved through the commands, the account of the first pair, and the reckoning.

The reader who began the sūrah with its four letters has now been given the warning that the letters introduced. The Book has been sent down, the command has been stated, the term has been fixed, the messengers have been sent, and the two outcomes have been described. The verse before us states the last of them for the proud, and the chapter's following verses state the reward of the believers.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the Fire will be the bed of the wicked and that flames will be their covering, and it repeats the statement that this is how the wrongdoers are recompensed (7:41). The exclusion from the Garden in front of us is followed by the description of the punishment in which the excluded remain.

The two verses together complete the picture of the end of the deniers. The gates are closed, the impossibility is stated, and the condition that awaits them is named. The sūrah then turns to the believers and their gardens, and the contrast that the passage has been building reaches its statement.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:41

> Hell will be their bed; flames will be their cover. This is how We reward the wrongdoers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Furnishings That Have Been Inverted**

The verse names two objects of rest and turns them into instruments of punishment: a bed beneath the condemned and coverings above them. The Arabic for bed, *mihād*, comes from a root that means to spread out, and the word describes level ground or a carpet prepared for a person to lie on. The Arabic for the coverings, *ghawāshin*, describes what is laid over something and conceals it. A bed and a cover are the ordinary furnishings of sleep, and the verse applies both to the Fire.

The Book uses the image of the spread bed for the ease of the world as well as for the punishment of the Hereafter, and the sūrah of the Clatterer states that the one whose scales are light will find his refuge in a pit. The inversion in front of us is exact, since the two things that make sleep possible are made into the two things that make it impossible. The verse states the reversal without comment, and the reader supplies the rest.

**Layers Above and Below**

The sūrah of the Troops states that the condemned will have layers of fire above and below them, and it closes with the address that calls on the servants to fear their Lord (39:16). The sūrah of the Spider states that on the day the punishment overwhelms them from above them and from beneath their feet, they will be told to reap what they sowed (29:55). The verse in front of us supplies both halves of the arrangement in a single sentence, and the two other passages confirm that the description is a standing image of the Fire's enclosure.

The enclosure leaves no direction of escape. The Book's descriptions of the punishment include the closing of the ways out, and the sūrah of the Cave reports the wrongdoers asking to be released or given relief and being refused. The bed beneath and the covering above are therefore not two separate torments but the statement that the person is held inside the punishment on every side.

**Who the Wrongdoers Are in This Passage**

The verse closes with the declaration that this is how the wrongdoers are recompensed, and the term gathers the offenses that the sūrah has named. The verses immediately before it have described the proud who refuse the signs (7:40), the verse that follows it will name those who believe and act rightly as the opposite group (7:42), and the passage that follows continues with those who turned others from the way of God and sought to make it crooked (7:45) and those who took their religion as amusement (7:51). The formula in front of us is the verdict on all of them.

The Book's use of the term for wrongdoing in this sūrah includes the account of the first pair, who were told that approaching the tree would make them wrongdoers (7:19), and their confession that they had wronged themselves (7:23). The word covers the transgression of a limit, and the passage in front of us applies it to the communities that denied the messengers and to the individuals who follow their example.

**The Formula of Recompense**

The closing formula, that this is how the wrongdoers are recompensed, appears in the sūrah more than once, and its repetition gives the passage its structure. The verse before this one states that the proud are repaid in the same way (7:40), the verse in front of us repeats the formula for the wrongdoers, and the following verse states the reward of the believers without the formula. The arrangement sets the two returns side by side and leaves the reader to mark the difference.

The Book's use of the formula elsewhere carries the same function, and the sūrah of the Clatterer and the sūrah of the Inevitable use matching statements for each of the two groups. The equality of the phrasing means that the Book never presents the punishment as an excess. What the person receives is what the description of the class requires, and the declaration in front of us states the correspondence in the fewest possible words.

**The Contrast in the Book's Depiction of Rest**

The Book describes the rest of the people of the Garden with the same vocabulary it uses here, and the contrast is deliberate. The sūrah of the Overwhelming describes raised couches for the people of the Garden (88:13), the sūrah of the Merciful describes them reclining on furnishings lined with brocade (55:54), and the sūrah of the Heights itself states in its later verses that the people of the Garden will be on couches facing one another. The bed and the covering in front of us belong to the same family of images, and they are aimed in the opposite direction.

The sūrah of the Tidings states that the condemned will find there neither coolness nor drink (78:24), and the sūrah of the Overwhelming states that their food will be a bitter thorn that neither nourishes nor satisfies hunger (88:6–7). The Book builds its descriptions of the Fire out of the absence and the inversion of the goods of the Garden, and the verse in front of us supplies the two most domestic of those goods.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands forty-first in the chapter, and it completes the sequence that began with the exclusion of the proud from the Garden (7:36–40). The passage then turns in the following verse to the believers and their reward, and the two descriptions are set next to each other in the sūrah's account of the two outcomes.

The reader who has followed the sūrah from its account of the weighing of deeds and the questioning of the messengers now sees the first of the two outcomes stated in the present tense of the Hereafter: a bed spread below, coverings above, and the declaration that the arrangement is the recompense of the wrongdoers. The chapter's next verse states what stands on the other side.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that those who believe and do good — and no soul is tasked beyond what it can afford — are the people of the Garden and will remain there (7:42). The furnishings of the condemned in front of us give way to the dwelling of the believers, and the sūrah's account of the two ends of the reckoning is complete.

The two verses together state the division that the passage has been preparing. One group is held inside the fire on every side, and the other is given a garden and a permanence; and the sentence that separates them is the one the chapter has stated from its opening: follow what has been sent down, and do not take protectors besides God.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:42

> As for those who believe and do good—We never require of any soul more than what it can afford—it is they who will be the residents of Paradise. They will be there forever.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Faith and Righteous Action Together**

The verse names the believers and joins their faith to their deeds, and the pairing runs through the Book's descriptions of salvation. The account of the Cow gives the good news to those who believe and do good that they will have gardens beneath which rivers flow (2:25), the sūrah of the Cave states that those who believe and do good will have the gardens of Paradise as a dwelling, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that the messengers were sent as bearers of good news and warners so that people would have no argument against God (6:48). The verse in front of us states the two conditions in the order the Book uses throughout.

The deeds are named alongside the faith because the Book treats the two as inseparable. The Heights' own account states that the believers are those who follow the messenger and the Book, and the sūrah's earlier verses describe the response of those who mend their conduct (7:35). The verse in front of us supplies the outcome for that response, and the mention of the good deeds states what the response looks like in practice.

**No Soul Is Tasked Beyond Its Capacity**

The verse inserts a clause in the middle of the promise: God tasks no soul beyond what it can afford. The statement appears in several passages, and the sūrah of the Cow closes its longest verse with the same formula and asks God not to burden the community beyond its strength (2:286); the sūrah of the Believers states that God never requires of any soul more than it can afford and adds that with Him is a record that speaks the truth (23:62); the sūrah of the Cattle uses the same clause in its list of the commands about fair dealing (6:152); and the sūrah of Divorce states the principle in the rules about maintenance, instructing each person to provide according to his means (65:7).

The clause has consequences in the practical obligations of the religion. The Cow's account states that God intends ease for the community and not hardship (2:185), and the rules of the fast, the prayer, and the alms were adjusted for travellers, the sick, and those without means on that basis. The clause in front of us states the principle from which those adjustments follow, and the Book's mention of it in a verse about the Garden's residents reminds the reader that the deeds required of him are proportionate to what he has.

**Why the Clause Sits Here**

The placement of the clause between the mention of the believer's deeds and the promise of the Garden serves the passage's account of the two groups. The verses before it have described the proud who denied the signs and were excluded from the Garden (7:40), and the reader might measure his own capacity against the attainments of others. The clause states that the measure is the individual's own, and the commentary tradition reads it as a reassurance that what is required of each person is within his reach.

The sūrah of Divorce repeats the same reassurance in a command about spending, and the sūrah of the Cow's closing verse asks God not to impose what the community cannot bear. The Book's statements about the burden of the previous communities appear in the same verse, where the community asks to be spared what was laid on those before it. The clause in front of us states the divine practice in the arrangement of the obligation.

**The People of the Garden and Their Permanence**

The verse states that those who believe and act rightly are the people of the Garden and that they will remain there, and the Book uses the language of permanence for the reward of the believers throughout. The account of the Cow describes the gardens beneath which rivers flow and states that their people will abide there, and the sūrah of the Cave states that the gardens are the dwelling of those who believed and did right. The verse in front of us states the assignment and the duration in the same sentence.

The permanence follows the permanence that the Book attributes to God's promise. The Family of Imran's account states that the reward of the Garden is prepared for the mindful, and the sūrah of the Prophet states that the Garden is the reward of those who are mindful of their Lord. This verse places the believers in the dwelling that the Book describes as lasting, and the contrast with the bed and the covering of the previous verse is exact.

**The Usage of Capacity in the Book's Commands**

The Book applies the principle of capacity to specific obligations, and the applications show what the clause in front of us means in practice. The Cow's rules on the care of children and the maintenance of the divorced are written to the measure of the obligation of the one charged, and the sūrah of Divorce states that the one with generous means should spend from his abundance while the one with limited resources should spend from what God has given him. The verse here states the general rule of the arrangement.

The Book's teaching also states that the reward is not measured by the quantity of the deed except as the deed exceeds the obligation of the one who performs it. The sūrah of the Cattle states that God does not burden a soul beyond its capacity, and the sūrah of the Night Journey specifies the limits of the commands about the orphan's wealth and the measure in trade. The clause in front of us belongs with those passages, since each of them sets the requirement within reach.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands forty-second in the chapter, and it answers the description of the condemned in the verse before it. The passage then continues with the account of the people of the Garden, their gratitude for the guidance they received, and the announcement of what they have been given (7:43). The present verse opens that sequence with the statement of who the people of the Garden are.

The reader who has been told in the sūrah's opening verses to follow what has been sent down now reads the outcome of that following. The faith and the deeds are named, the measure of the requirement is stated, and the dwelling that follows is described as lasting. The chapter's next verse describes what the people of that dwelling say when they enter it.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that God will remove whatever rancour is in the hearts of the people of the Garden, that rivers will flow beneath them, and that they will say that praise belongs to God who guided them to it (7:43). The statement of who the people of the Garden are in front of us is followed by the account of their condition and their words.

The two verses together describe the state of the believers in the Hereafter. They are named by their faith and their deeds, and the requirement laid on them is measured to their capacity; and the verses that follow describe the peace of their hearts, the water beneath them, and the announcement that what they have received is the reward of what they did.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:43

> We will remove whatever bitterness they had in their hearts. Rivers will flow under their feet. And they will say, “Praise be to Allah for guiding us to this. We would have never been guided if Allah had not guided us. The messengers of our Lord had certainly come with the truth.” It will be announced to them, “This is Paradise awarded to you for what you used to do.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Removal of Rancour**

The verse states that whatever bitterness lay in the hearts of the people of the Garden will be taken away, and the Book uses the same language in its other descriptions of the Hereafter. The sūrah of the Rocky Tract states that God will remove whatever bitterness is in the hearts of the people of the Garden and that they will be on couches facing one another (15:47); the sūrah of the Troops closes its account of the believers' entry with the statement that they will be told to enter the Garden with no fear upon them. The change in front of us touches the heart rather than the surroundings, and it is stated before the rivers and the speech that follow it.

The removal of rancour completes the contrast with the condemned that the previous verses have drawn. The sūrah has described the communities cursing one another as they enter the Fire and the leaders answering the followers (7:38–39), and the verse in front of us states that the hearts of the saved hold nothing of that kind. The two passages describe the social condition of the two abodes, and the difference between them begins in the heart.

**Rivers Running Beneath**

The verse places rivers beneath the people of the Garden, and the image of the garden with rivers flowing under it is one of the Book's most frequent descriptions of the reward. The sūrah of the Cow introduces it in its account of the gardens given to those who believe and do good (2:25), and the sūrah of the Family of Imran, the sūrah of the Women, and the sūrah of the Table repeat it in their descriptions of the gardens prepared for the mindful. The verse in front of us uses the same picture at the end of the sūrah's account of the two outcomes.

The water in front of us follows the water that the people of the Fire request and are refused in the sūrah's later verses, where they ask the people of the Garden to pour water upon them or to give them what God has provided (7:50). The two passages belong to the same scene, and the rivers in front of us are the answer that the condemned will not receive.

**The Speech of the People of the Garden**

The verse reports what the people of the Garden say, and their words are an acknowledgement rather than a claim. They praise God for guiding them to this, they state that they would not have been guided if God had not guided them, and they confirm that the messengers of their Lord brought the truth. The Book's descriptions of the saved regularly include their gratitude, and the sūrah of the Troops records their words at the entry into the Garden and their statement that the promise of God has been fulfilled.

The three statements in front of us answer the attitudes that the sūrah has corrected in its audience. The claim to be guided without guidance appeared in the verse that described those who took the devils as masters while thinking themselves on the right path (7:30), and the certainty that the messengers brought the truth answers the demand for proofs that the sūrah's narratives describe. The words in front of us are the confession of the people who followed what was sent down.

**Guidance as a Gift**

The words of the people of the Garden state that their guidance came from God, and the Book presents guidance as a divine grant throughout. The sūrah of the Cow states that the guidance of God is the guidance (2:120), the sūrah of the Family of Imran states that God guides whom He wills to a straight path (3:73), and the sūrah of the Bee states that God leads astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills (16:93). The verse in front of us places the same acknowledgement in the mouths of the saved.

The statement does not remove the responsibility that the sūrah's commands have placed on its audience. The Book joins the divine grant of guidance to the human effort of following it, and the sūrah's opening command to follow what has been sent down stands in the same chapter as the words of the people of the Garden. What the verse in front of us records is the recognition that arrives when the outcome is seen, and the sūrah's reader is given it before he arrives.

**The Announcement of Inheritance**

The verse closes with the announcement made to the people of the Garden that what they have received is theirs for what they used to do. The word used for the garden's bestowal is that of inheritance, and the Book states that the righteous will inherit the Garden in several passages. The sūrah of the Believers states that the believers will inherit the highest part of Paradise, and the sūrah of the Troops records the words of the people of the Garden, who say that they have been made to inherit the land and can dwell in the Garden wherever they wish.

The commentary tradition explains the language of inheritance in two ways. One reading takes it as the statement that the Garden has been assigned to them as a settled possession rather than as a temporary grant. The other reads the inheritance as the dwelling of the people of Paradise, including what had been prepared for others who forfeited it. The two readings agree that the announcement in front of us names a settled and permanent gift.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands forty-third in the chapter, and it follows the statement of who the people of the Garden are and precedes the dialogue between the two groups. The sūrah then reports the call of the people of the Garden to the people of the Fire, the announcement of the curse upon the wrongdoers, and the description of the barrier and the heights. The verse in front of us supplies the first of those speeches.

The reader who has been given the two destinations in the verses before now hears the words of the first group. Their hearts are at peace, the water runs beneath them, and their speech is gratitude and confirmation. The chapter's next verses turn the scene towards the second group, and the two sides of the barrier are placed in view of one another.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the people of the Garden will call out to the people of the Fire, asking whether they have found their Lord's promise to be true, and that a caller will announce the condemnation of the wrongdoers (7:44). The speech of the saved in front of us is followed by an address to the condemned, and the scene then moves to the barrier between the two groups.

The two verses together present the Hereafter as a place of speech. The saved declare the truth of what they were promised, and the condemned are addressed and made to answer; and the verses that follow describe the barrier, the heights above it, and the people who stand there looking at both sides.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:44

> The residents of Paradise will call out to the residents of the Fire, “We have certainly found our Lord’s promise to be true. Have you too found your Lord’s promise to be true?” They will reply, “Yes, we have!” Then a caller will announce to both, “May Allah’s condemnation be upon the wrongdoers,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Call From the Garden**

The verse reports the address of the people of the Garden to the people of the Fire, and the address asks each group's question through the experience of the other: those in the Garden state that they have found their Lord's promise true and ask whether the people of the Fire have found the same. The Book's descriptions of the Hereafter include exchanges between the two groups, and the sūrah of the Heights itself continues the dialogue in the verses that follow with the request for water and the answer that follows it (7:50).

The call establishes that the barrier between the two groups does not prevent speech. The sūrah's following verses describe the barrier and the heights above it, and the people on the heights will address both groups (7:46–49). The verse in front of us begins the sequence with the direct exchange, and the arrangement gives the scene its shape as one in which the truth of the promise is publicly confirmed.

**The Answer Given**

The people of the Fire answer in the affirmative, and the Book records the same acknowledgement at other points of the judgment. The sūrah of the Cattle reports the disbelievers confessing when they are shown their Lord, and the sūrah of the Troops and the sūrah of the Forgiver report the condemned acknowledging the truth once they have entered the punishment or are being driven towards it. The sūrah of the Kingdom records the condemned confessing that they had heard a warner and rejected him.

The acknowledgement in front of us comes after the point at which it could change anything, and the commentary tradition treats all such confessions as confirming the justice of the sentence rather than altering it. The sūrah's opening verses have already described the confession of the destroyed communities in the world, when the punishment came upon them and they said that they had been wrongdoers (7:5). The answer in front of us is the same admission, spoken in the next world and under the same constraint.

**The Herald and the Proclamation**

The verse reports the proclamation of a caller in the midst of the assembly, and the call states that the curse of God is upon the wrongdoers. The commentary tradition identifies the caller as an angel charged with the address, and it notes that the proclamation reaches both groups. The curse in front of us is not the announcement of the punishment itself but the declaration of the ground on which it rests, and the sentence is left hanging at the end of the verse to be completed by the description of the wrongdoers in the next.

The Book's descriptions of the last day include proclamations of this kind, and the sūrah of the Troops reports the address given to the people of the Fire as they arrive. The arrangement in front of us places the announcement between the two groups, so that the curse is heard by those who are addressed by it and by those who are not. The verse ends with the phrase describing the cursed, and the next verse states their conduct.

**The Report of the Prophet at Badr**

The commentary tradition records that the Prophet addressed the dead of his enemies after the battle of Badr in terms similar to the question in front of us, asking whether they had found what their Lord had promised them to be true. The sound report of that exchange includes the objection of the Companions that the dead could not hear, and the Prophet's answer that the dead were not the ones who could not hear the message. The report stands in the Book of Campaigns in al-Bukhārī (3976), where the Prophet’s words and the Companions’ objection are both recorded.

The report connects the question in front of us with the world's own history, since the same principle was demonstrated at a battle within the audience's own lifetime. The Book's descriptions of the punishment of the earlier communities serve the same function, and the sūrah's narratives of the peoples who were destroyed are the extended version of the same warning.

**The Truth of the Promise in the Book**

The Book states repeatedly that the promise of God is true, and the affirmation in front of us stands on that statement. The sūrah of the Women states that God's promise is true and that the gardens beneath which rivers flow are the promise that belongs to the righteous, and the sūrah of Yūnus reports the declaration that the promise of God is true. The sūrah of the Romans closes its account of the signs with the statement that the promise of God is true, and the sūrah of the Troops repeats it in its description of the day.

The two promises that the verse sets beside each other are the reward for the one group and the punishment for the other, and the question in front of us is addressed to the second. The Book presents both as obligations that its Lord will discharge, and the sūrah's reader is given the confirmation of the condemned themselves as evidence for the warning that the chapter has delivered.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands forty-fourth in the chapter, and it follows the account of the people of the Garden and their words. The next verse describes the wrongdoers on whom the curse has been pronounced, and the verses after it describe the barrier, the heights, and the people who stand there. The verse in front of us supplies the exchange that introduces the whole scene.

The reader who has been given the two outcomes in the preceding verses now hears them confirmed by the people who received them. The questions and the answers are placed in the Hereafter, and the chapter's next verses name the conduct that produced the second outcome.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse describes the wrongdoers as those who turned others from the way of God, sought to make it crooked, and disbelieved in the Hereafter (7:45). The curse proclaimed in front of us is given its content in the verse that follows, and the conduct that earned it is stated in three clauses.

The two verses together complete the announcement. The question is asked and answered, the curse is proclaimed, and the offense is named; and the sūrah then turns to the barrier between the two groups and the people who look down from the heights.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:45

> those who hindered ˹others˺ from Allah’s Way, strived to make it ˹appear˺ crooked, and disbelieved in the Hereafter.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conduct Behind the Curse**

The verse states the conduct of those on whom the curse was pronounced, and the description has three parts: turning others from the way of God, seeking to make that way crooked, and denying the Hereafter. The Book provides a nearly identical description in the sūrah of Hūd, where the same three features are attached to those who bar people from the path of God (11:19). The verse in front of us states the offense that the previous verse's proclamation answers, and the three clauses together describe an activity rather than a single act.

The conduct is presented as a campaign rather than as a private opinion. The people described do not merely fail to follow the way; they obstruct others from following it and work to make it appear defective. The sūrah's audience in Makkah knew that campaign from its own experience, since the leading families of the city opposed the message and used their influence over the tribes, the markets, and the pilgrimage to keep people away from it.

**Turning Others From the Way of God**

The first clause describes the obstruction of others, and the Book uses the language of barring for the leaders of the opposition in several passages. The sūrah of the Family of Imran states that a group of the people of the earlier books turns the believers from the way of God and seeks to make it crooked, and the sūrah of the Bee describes the leaders of the disbelievers as those who turn people from the path of God. The verse in front of us states the same activity in the summary of the cursed.

The commentary tradition notes that the clause may describe the turning of the person himself from the path as well as the turning of others, since the Arabic construction allows both readings. The two senses are joined in practice, because the person who abandons the way of God and the person who campaigns against it belong to the same category, and the sūrah's later passages describe both the leaders who mislead and the followers who accept their direction.

**Making the Path Crooked**

The second clause describes the distortion of the way itself, and the Book uses the image of crookedness for false teaching and for the misrepresentation of the revelation. The sūrah of the Family of Imran uses the same expression of those who seek to make the path crooked, the sūrah of Hūd uses it of the people who block the path and seek to make it crooked (11:19), and the sūrah of Abraham states that those who disbelieve and bar others from the way of God have gone far astray. The image in front of us presents the way of God as straight by nature, and the obstruction as an attempt to bend it.

The commentary tradition explains the crookedness as the introduction of alterations into the religion's teaching, whether by inventing practices and attributing them to God, by misquoting the message to those who might accept it, or by presenting the path as harder and less reasonable than it is. The sūrah's audience had its own practice of attributing to God commands that He had not given, and its leaders had an interest in presenting the messenger's teaching as a disruption of the order they controlled. The clause in front of us names the practice in the terms of the road.

**The Denial of the Hereafter**

The third clause states that those who act in this way disbelieve in the Hereafter, and the Book attaches the denial of the last day to the whole conduct of the opposition. The sūrah of the Family of Imran joins the phrase to the description of those who turn from the path, and the sūrah of the Bee states that those who do not believe in the Hereafter have had their deeds made to appear fair to them. The clause in front of us closes the description with the belief that makes the rest of the conduct possible.

The Book's treatment of the denial of the Hereafter frequently connects it to the denial of accountability, and the sūrah's opening verses have already stated the terms of the reckoning in full. The person who does not expect a reckoning has no reason to release the travelers he has barred from the road, and the clause in front of us states that connection in a single phrase. The sūrah's narratives of the communities show the same combination at work, since each of the peoples that rejected its messenger also denied the raising.

**The Audience and the Accusation**

The sūrah's audience included the men who controlled access to the sanctuary and to the tribes, and the description in front of us applies to the conduct of that group. The Book's later passages in this sūrah describe those who turned people from the path of God and threatened those who believed, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the opposition's practice of sitting on every road to warn travelers away from the messenger. The verse in front of us states the offense in the form of a general description.

The Book's treatment of the offense as a wrong against others as well as against God appears in its warnings about those who mislead, and the sūrah of the Bee states that the leaders of misguidance will bear their own burdens and the burdens of those they misled (16:25). The clause in front of us names the activity that produces that double burden, and the passage that follows places the people who performed it on the wrong side of the barrier.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands forty-fifth in the chapter, and it completes the proclamation of the previous verse by naming the conduct that earned the curse. The verses that follow describe the barrier, the heights, and the people who stand on them, and the verse in front of us supplies the description of the group that the people of the heights address from above.

The reader who has been told about the people of the Garden, their words, and the announcement of the curse now receives the account of those who are cursed. The clause about the way of God returns the sūrah to the image with which it began, since the command to follow what has been sent down and the warning against other protectors have already placed the audience on a road.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that a barrier will stand between the two groups, that on the heights of the barrier there will be people who recognize both parties by their marks, and that they will call out to the people of the Garden with the greeting of peace (7:46). The description of the cursed in front of us is followed by the account of the barrier and of those who stand upon it, and the sūrah's name is taken from that place.

The two verses together complete the scene of the Hereafter that the sūrah's account of the two outcomes has been building. The two groups face each other across the barrier, the people of the heights look on both, and the greeting and the warning are given from the elevated station that stands between the two destinations.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:46

> There will be a barrier between Paradise and Hell. And on the heights ˹of that barrier˺ will be people who will recognize ˹the residents of˺ both by their appearance. They will call out to the residents of Paradise, “Peace be upon you!” They will have not yet entered Paradise, but eagerly hope to.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Barrier Between the Two Groups**

The verse names a barrier set between the people of the Garden and the people of the Fire, and the Book describes a similar partition in the sūrah of the Iron, where a wall with a gate is set down between the believers and the hypocrites, with mercy on its inner side and punishment on the outer (57:13). The barrier in front of us separates the two outcomes of the reckoning, and the commentary tradition identifies it with the wall described in that passage.

The two groups in front of us have just exchanged the question and the answer about the truth of the promise (7:44–45), and the barrier is placed between them without preventing the exchange. The Book's descriptions of the Hereafter present the two abodes as adjacent and mutually visible, and the sūrah's later verses continue the dialogue across the divide with the request for water and the refusal (7:50). The barrier in front of us is therefore a boundary of status rather than of distance.

**The Heights and the Name of the Sūrah**

The verse places a group of people on the heights of the barrier, and the chapter takes its name from that place. The Arabic word *aʿrāf* denotes the elevated edge of a thing, and the commentary tradition describes it as a high place above the barrier from which both sides can be seen; some commentators identify it with the bridge that stretches over the Fire and leads to the Garden, and others describe it as a sand dune raised above the two abodes.

The name is connected in the tradition with the ability to recognize, since the same root produces the verb in the verse where the people on the heights know both groups by their marks. Those who occupy the height are therefore described by what they can do there: they identify the people of both sides by their appearance, and the chapter's title records the station rather than a doctrine.

**Recognized by Their Marks**

The verse states that the people of the heights recognize both groups by their marks, and the phrase describes a visible distinction that requires no announcement. The commentary tradition explains the marks as the features that identify the people of the Garden and the people of the Fire, whether the brightness of the faces of the saved or the darkness of the faces of the condemned. The Book's descriptions of the day include the light that runs before the believers and the signs by which the two groups are known.

The recognition in front of us establishes the knowledge of the people on the heights, and the chapter's later verses state what they do with it. They address the people of the Garden with the greeting of peace, and they address some of the people of the Fire with the observation that their numbers and their arrogance have not helped them (7:48). The station on the heights is therefore a place of observation and address rather than of judgment.

**The Greeting of Peace**

The verse reports that the people of the heights call out to the people of the Garden with the greeting of peace, and the greeting is the standard salutation of the saved. The Book reports it among the people of the Garden themselves, where they greet one another with peace, and the sūrah of the Heights will state that the people of the Garden have had rancour removed from their hearts (7:43). The greeting in front of us is given from the barrier, and it is answered by the state of those who receive it.

The people who give the greeting have not entered the Garden at the moment of the scene, and the verse states that they hope to. The commentary tradition draws from this the conclusion that their position is neither that of the saved who have entered nor that of the condemned. They are close enough to greet the first group and far enough to fear the second, and the verse that follows records their prayer when they look at the Fire.

**Who the People of the Heights Are**

The commentary tradition reports several identifications of the people on the heights, and each identification rests on a reading of the verse. One reading holds that they are those whose good and evil deeds are equal, so that the balance does not carry them into the Garden and does not consign them to the Fire; a second holds that they are believers who fell short in their works; and a third holds that they are the martyrs who were killed in the way of God while owing an obligation that was not discharged. The reports differ, and the disagreement concerns who is placed at the barrier rather than whether the barrier exists.

The weighing of the identifications turns on the rest of the passage. The people on the heights greet the people of the Garden and hope to enter, and they pray not to be placed among the wrongdoers when they look at the Fire. The two actions describe a group that belongs to the company of the saved in its hope and shares nothing with the condemned in its fear, and that description is the one the passage supports.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands forty-sixth in the chapter, and the sūrah's name is drawn from the place it names. It follows the proclamation of the curse on the wrongdoers and precedes the prayer of the people on the heights and their address to the dwellers of the Fire. The scene in front of us places a third station between the two that the previous verses have described.

The reader who has followed the sūrah from its opening has been given the Book, the command, the reckoning, the account of the first pair, the warnings against the practices of the audience, and the two ends of the judgment. The verse in front of us adds the station above the barrier, and the remaining verses of the passage complete the scene with the prayer and the address that are made from it.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that when the eyes of the people on the heights turn towards the people of the Fire, they pray that their Lord not place them among the wrongdoing people (7:47). The greeting in front of us is followed by the prayer that the sight of the Fire produces, and the speech of the station between the two outcomes is given both of its parts.

The two verses together complete the description of the people on the heights. They see both sides, they greet the first, and they pray to be spared the company of the second; and the sūrah's following verses report the address they make to the proud among the condemned.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:47

> When their eyes will turn towards the residents of Hell, they will pray, “Our Lord! Do not join us with the wrongdoing people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Eyes That Turn Toward the Fire**

The verse describes the moment at which the gaze of the people on the heights moves from one side of the barrier to the other, and the movement produces a prayer. They have greeted the people of the Garden in the previous verse and stated their hope of entering; when their eyes fall on the company of the condemned, they ask their Lord not to place them among them. The scene records the two directions of a single outlook, and the prayer follows the sight that produces it.

The Book's descriptions of the Hereafter often use the direction of the eyes to mark the state of a person, and the sūrah of the Clatterer describes the faces of the saved as radiant and laughing while the faces of the condemned are covered with dust. The verse in front of us uses the same instrument, and the sight of the Fire turns the hope of the people on the heights into a petition.

**The Prayer Against the Company of the Wrongdoers**

The words of the prayer are brief and precise: do not place us among the wrongdoing people. The Book records the same petition from Moses, who asks his Lord not to count him among the wrongdoing people when he fears that he may share their fate (23:94), and the sūrah of the Family of Imran records the prayer of the believing community for forgiveness and for the protection of their Lord. The verse in front of us gives the people on the heights the same petition in the same words.

The prayer asks to be spared a company rather than a punishment, and the choice of words matches the scene. The people on the heights are looking at the inhabitants of the Fire, and what they ask is that they not be numbered with them. The Book's habit of describing the two outcomes as two companies appears throughout its accounts of the last day, and the sūrah's later verses continue the exchange between the two groups.

**Fear and Hope at the Same Station**

The verse places the two poles of religious feeling in one person, since the people on the heights hope to enter the Garden and pray to be spared the company of the Fire. The Book joins the same two dispositions in its instructions to the community, and the sūrah of the Heights commands the audience to call upon God with hope and fear (7:56), while the sūrah of the Prostration praises those who call upon their Lord in fear and hope (32:16). The verse in front of us presents the two dispositions in action.

The commentary tradition treats the balance of fear and hope as the mark of a sound religious condition, since fear without hope produces despair and hope without fear produces carelessness. The people on the heights in front of us illustrate the balance, and their prayer states the resolution that the sūrah recommends to its audience in its commands about supplication.

**The Wrongdoing That the Prayer Avoids**

The term used in the prayer is the same one the sūrah has applied to the communities that were destroyed, to the proud who were excluded from the Garden (7:40), and to those who turned others from the way of God (7:45). The people on the heights ask their Lord not to place them in that company, and the petition therefore acknowledges that the company exists and that it has a cause. The verse states the request without stating the action that produces the outcome.

The Book's treatment of the term wrongdoing as the description of a settled condition rather than of a single act appears throughout the sūrah, and the confession of the first pair uses the same vocabulary when they say that they have wronged themselves (7:23). The prayer in front of us belongs to the same moral vocabulary, and it places the speaker among those who fear the condition rather than among those who have entered it.

**The Company of the Hereafter**

The prayer against being placed with the wrongdoers reflects the Book's teaching about the company a person keeps in the Hereafter. The sūrah of the Ornament states that close friends will be enemies on that day except for the mindful (43:67), and the sūrah of the Confederates records the followers blaming their leaders and asking for a double share of punishment for them (33:67–68). The two passages describe the same separation that the prayer in front of us seeks to avoid.

The Book's descriptions of the saved include their gathering as brothers on couches facing one another, and the sūrah of the Rocky Tract states that God has removed rancour from their hearts (15:47). The petition in front of us asks for a place among that company, since the opposite of being numbered with the wrongdoers is being numbered with the grateful. The verse states the request, and the sūrah's following verses complete the scene.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands forty-seventh in the chapter, and it supplies the second half of the speech of the people on the heights. The greeting of the previous verse and the prayer in front of us belong to the same station, and the address to the proud among the condemned follows in the next verse. The passage therefore presents the people on the heights as speakers in both directions.

The reader who has been given the two companies in the preceding verses now hears the petition of those who stand between them. Their greeting of the first group and their prayer against the second state the choice that the sūrah's opening command has placed before its audience, and the chapter's remaining verses continue the account of the two ends.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the people on the heights call out to some of the people of the Fire whom they recognize by their appearance, and they tell them that their numbers and their arrogance have not profited them (7:48). The prayer in front of us is followed by the address to the condemned, and the speech of the heights is completed in both directions.

The two verses together present the intermediate station as a position of knowledge and speech. The people on the heights see both companies, greet the one, pray to be spared the other, and address the proud among the second; and the sūrah then states the mercy that the condemned had denied to the weak among the believers.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:48

> Those on the heights will call out to some ˹tyrants in the Fire˺, who they will recognize by their appearance, saying, “Your large numbers and arrogance are of no use ˹today˺!

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address to the Proud**

The verse reports the address of the people on the heights to some of the people of the Fire whom they recognize by their appearance, and the address states that their numbers and their arrogance are of no use on that day. The identification is made from the elevated station, and the address follows the prayer of the previous verse. The people on the heights speak to both sides across the barrier, and the words in front of us are directed at the leaders among the condemned.

The phrasing of the address identifies a group rather than the whole company. The commentary tradition reads the addressees as the notables and chiefs whose rank and following had given them their standing in the world, and the sūrah's narratives of the communities supply the model, since each of them describes the notables of a people dismissing the messenger. The verse in front of us states the reckoning of that group in a single sentence.

**Numbers as a Measure**

The verse names the abundance of the condemned as a thing that no longer helps them, and the Book repeatedly warns its audience against the argument from majority. The sūrah of the Cattle states that if the Prophet were to obey most of those on the earth they would lead him away from the way of God (6:116), and the sūrah of the Table states that good and evil are not equal however much the abundance of the evil amazes the observer (5:100). The verse in front of us takes the argument from numbers and states its outcome.

The same measure appears in the sūrah's own account of the communities. The peoples of Noah, ʿĀd, and Thamūd were the majorities in their own lands, and the sūrah's narratives show what their numbers did for them when the term arrived. The sūrah of the Cow describes a people who left their homes in their thousands for fear of death and were told to die (2:243), and the verse in front of us applies the same verdict to the crowd that stands in the Fire.

**Wealth and Children That Do Not Avail**

The second item in the address is the arrogance that accompanied the abundance, and the Book attaches the two together in its descriptions of the prosperous deniers. The sūrah of Sheba reports the words of those who said that they were greater in wealth and children than the believers and that they would not be punished (34:35), and the sūrah of the Night Journey describes the Israelites being aided with wealth and offspring and made to outnumber their enemies, which did not protect them from the reckoning that followed (17:6). The sūrah of Noah reports the same reliance on wealth and children among the people who followed their leaders (71:21).

The Book's treatment of wealth and offspring as marks of divine favour in the world and as irrelevant to the verdict of the Hereafter appears in several passages, and the sūrah of Sheba states that neither wealth nor children bring a person near to God. The address in front of us names the two grounds of confidence that the great of the world rely upon, and it states that both have been left behind with the world that produced them.

**The Arrogance Named**

The address names arrogance alongside the numbers, and the Book uses the term for the disposition that refuses the command and despises those who accept it. The sūrah of Yūnus describes the chiefs of Pharaoh behaving arrogantly and persisting in their denial, and the sūrah of the Heights itself has already stated that arrogance has no place in the Garden and that the proud who refuse the signs will not enter it until the camel passes through the eye of a needle (7:13; 7:40). The verse in front of us states the same disposition at the reckoning.

The sūrah's treatment of the fault connects the narrative of the adversary with the history of the communities. The first refusal was made on a claim of superior nature, and the sūrah's later accounts of the notables who rejected the messengers repeat the claim with the terms of wealth, lineage, and power. The address in front of us names the common feature of all of them, and it states that the feature has no standing in the place where it is spoken.

**The Marks by Which They Are Known**

The verse states that the people on the heights recognize the condemned by their appearance, and the same statement was made in the previous verse about the recognition of both groups. The Book's descriptions of the day include visible signs that distinguish the saved from the condemned, and the commentary tradition explains the marks as the traces of the two conditions. The people on the heights in front of us identify the notables among the condemned and address them by what they see.

The recognition gives the scene its shape, since the people on the heights speak to a group they can identify without an introduction. The Book's habit of placing recognition at the center of the last-day scenes appears in its descriptions of the believers being known by their light and the condemned by their marks, and the verse in front of us uses the same arrangement to introduce the address.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands forty-eighth in the chapter, and it completes the speech of the people on the heights in the direction of the Fire. The verse that follows reports the question put to the condemned about the humble believers whom they had sworn would never receive mercy, and the answer that is given to the people on the heights themselves. This verse supplies the address that precedes the question.

The reader who has been given the station between the two outcomes now hears it speaking in both directions. The greeting, the prayer, and the reproach to the proud are all spoken from the same height, and the sūrah then states the mercy that the condemned had denied and the command given to those who hoped for it.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse asks whether these are the people concerning whom the condemned swore that God would not extend mercy, and it reports the command given to the people on the heights to enter the Garden with no fear and no grief (7:49). The reproach in front of us is followed by the vindication of the believers who had been despised, and the two verses complete the exchange across the barrier.

The scene thus reverses the judgment of the world. Those who were counted great by their numbers and their arrogance stand in the Fire, and those who were dismissed as too small to be shown mercy are told to enter the Garden; and the sūrah leaves the reader with the two verdicts in the order in which they were delivered.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:49

> Are these ˹humble believers˺ the ones you swore would never be shown Allah’s mercy?” ˹Finally, those on the heights will be told:˺ “Enter Paradise! There will be no fear for you, nor will you grieve.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Put to the Condemned**

The verse reports the question addressed to the people of the Fire about a group of believers whom they had sworn would never be shown mercy, and the question is rhetorical. The people on the heights have just spoken to the proud among the condemned (7:48), and the question in front of us completes the address by naming the specific group that the great ones had dismissed. The scene records the reversal of the verdict that the world had passed.

The wording of the question attributes the oath to the condemned, and the Book's accounts of the notables of the communities supply the kind of oath it was. The great ones of Makkah dismissed the believers as weak, poor, and of no account, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the same dismissal in its account of the notables who asked why the message had not been given to a man of standing in the two towns (6:53). The question in front of us states that the dismissal is answered at the barrier.

**The Believers of Low Standing**

The people whom the question names are the believers whose standing in the world gave the notables their argument, and the Book describes them in several passages. The sūrah of the Cattle states that God tests some people by means of others so that the disbelievers may ask whether these are the ones whom God has favoured among them (6:53), and the sūrah of Hūd reports the notables of Noah's people saying that only the weak had followed him. The verse in front of us places the same believers in the Garden.

The Book's treatment of the lowly believers as the first to accept the messengers appears throughout its narratives, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the charge brought against Noah that only the lowest had followed him. The scene in front of us answers that charge by showing where the two groups have arrived, and the answer is delivered in the form of a question that the condemned cannot answer.

**The Oath and Its Refutation**

The verse states that the condemned had sworn that the believers would never be shown mercy, and the Books' descriptions of the proud include the confidence with which they pronounced on the outcome. The sūrah of the Cave reports the owner of the garden expressing doubt about the Hour and about his own return, and the sūrah of Maryam reports the deniers claiming that they would be given wealth and children in the Hereafter. The oath in front of us belongs to the same confidence, and the verse states that it has been falsified.

The command that follows the question shows the refutation in its plainest form, since the answer to the oath is the instruction to enter the Garden. The Book's practice of answering the claims of the deniers with the announcement of the reward given to the believers appears in its closing descriptions of the two communities, and the verse in front of us uses the practice at the summit of the scene.

**The Command to Enter**

The verse reports the command given to the believers to enter the Garden, and the command supplies the conclusion of the passage. The people on the heights have been described as hoping to enter (7:46), and the command in front of us grants what they hoped for; the verse therefore completes their station by granting the entry. The sūrah's earlier verses have described the announcement made to the people of the Garden that what they have received is theirs for what they did (7:43), and the command in front of us repeats the gift.

The Book's descriptions of the entry into the Garden include the opening of the gates and the greetings of the angels, and the sūrah of the Troops describes the arrival of the mindful at the Garden with its gates opened and the keepers greeting them with the announcement that they have done well. The command in front of us is short, and it places the believers inside the dwelling that the passage has described.

**No Fear and No Grief**

The verse closes with the two promises that the sūrah has already attached to the response of the believers, that no fear will come upon them and that they will not grieve.
