The earlier verse states the same promise in the address to the children of Adam who mend their conduct (7:35), and the Book repeats the formula in its descriptions of the saved in several places, including its statement that those who follow the guidance have nothing to fear or to grieve over (2:38). The promise in front of us repeats the assurance at the point where it is fulfilled.

The two words name the anxieties of the world and of the reckoning, and the Book's use of the formula marks the state of those who have passed beyond both. The sūrah of Yūnus applies the same assurance to the friends of God (10:62), and the sūrah of the Sand Dunes attaches it to those who say that their Lord is God and remain steadfast (46:13). The verse in front of us supplies the promise in the mouth of the command itself.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands forty-ninth in the chapter, and it completes the scene of the barrier, the heights, and the two companies. The verses that follow report the request of the people of the Fire for water and provision and the answer that they are forbidden to the deniers (7:50–51), and the passage then returns to the revelation and its guidance (7:52). The verse in front of us closes the exchange that the chapter's account of the two outcomes has produced.

The reader who has followed the passage from the weighing of deeds now sees the verdict delivered in both directions. The great are told that their numbers and their arrogance have not helped them, and the believers whom they had dismissed are told to enter the Garden; and the sūrah's next verses state what the condemned ask for and what they are refused.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the people of the Fire will call out to the people of the Garden asking for water or for what God has provided them, and that the answer states that God has forbidden both to the disbelievers (7:50). The command to enter the Garden in front of us is followed by the last exchange between the two groups, and the refusal is placed beside the gift.

The two verses together complete the picture of the two conditions. The one group is told to enter and to leave fear and grief behind, and the other asks for a drop of water and is refused; and the sūrah states the reason for the refusal in the verses that follow.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:50

> The residents of the Fire will then cry out to the residents of Paradise, “Aid us with some water or any provision Allah has granted you.” They will reply, “Allah has forbidden both to the disbelievers,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Request Across the Divide**

The verse reports the appeal of the people of the Fire to the people of the Garden, and the appeal asks for water or for anything that God has provided. The request follows the scene of the barrier and the heights, and it confirms that the two groups are close enough to speak. The condemned do not ask for release or for a return to the world, and the request in front of us is limited to a drop of water and a share of provision.

The Book's descriptions of the two abodes place them in sight of one another, and the sūrah's earlier verses have already reported the call made in the other direction (7:44). The verse in front of us completes the exchange with the request that the condemned make, and the answer that follows states the ground of the refusal. The scene is the last conversation the passage records between the two groups.

**What Is Asked and What Is Refused**

The two things requested are water and the provision of the Garden, and the Book's descriptions of the reward supply both in abundance. The sūrah of Muhammad describes gardens with rivers of water that does not change in taste, rivers of milk, rivers of wine, and rivers of purified honey (47:15), and the sūrah of the Cow describes gardens beneath which rivers flow and fruits of every kind (2:25). The verse in front of us names the two things in the fewest words, and the answer denies both to the people who ask.

The refusal is total, and the sūrah's descriptions of the punishment supply the other side of the account. The sūrah of the Inevitable states that the condemned will drink from a boiling spring and that their only food will be bitter thorn, and the sūrah of the Tidings states that they will find neither coolness nor any drink other than boiling water and pus (78:24–25). The verse in front of us states the answer to the request, and the Book's descriptions of the Fire state what takes the place of what was asked.

**The Answer and Its Terms**

The people of the Garden answer that God has forbidden both water and provision to the disbelievers, and the answer is stated as a settled decree rather than as a refusal by the speakers. The verse that follows names the conduct that makes the decree apply, and the two verses together give the scene its legal shape. What is denied in front of us is not the property of the people of the Garden but the mercy of the One who provided it.

The Book's descriptions of the reward and the punishment include the same structure of correspondence, and the sūrah of the Clatterer and the sūrah of the Inevitable present the two outcomes as assigned rather than negotiated. The answer in front of us states the assignment, and the sūrah's reader is left with the contrast between the rivers of the Garden and the drink of the Fire.

**Thirst and Hunger as Torment**

The request for water indicates the nature of the punishment that the Book describes, and the sūrah's earlier verses have stated that the bed of the condemned is fire and that their covering is flame (7:41). The Book refers to thirst and hunger among the torments of the Fire, and the sūrah of the Overwhelming describes the food that neither nourishes nor satisfies hunger (88:6–7). This verse supplies the condemned asking for what would relieve the condition.

The commentary tradition notes that the request is made in spite of the knowledge that it cannot be granted. Al-Zamakhsharī explains it as the product of the confusion and desperation of the condemned, who ask for relief from those who cannot give it because the situation leaves them nothing else to do. The verse states the request without explaining it, and the answer supplies the only information that matters.

**The Nearness of the Two Abodes**

The verse shows the two groups within earshot of each other, and the arrangement is part of the punishment of the second. The Book's descriptions of the Hereafter include the condemned seeing the reward of the saved, and the sūrah of the Heights has already reported the words of the people of the Garden being heard across the barrier (7:44). The refusal in front of us is made more severe by the distance it measures.

The Book uses the visibility of the two outcomes as a warning in the world as well, and the sūrah's opening verses have set the two results of the weighing side by side for the reader. The verse here places the two outcomes within sight of each other, and the request that comes across the divide is the voice of that arrangement.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fiftieth in the chapter, and it begins the last exchange between the two groups in this passage. The verse that follows names the conduct of the disbelievers who took their religion as amusement and were deluded by the life of the world, and the passage then turns to the Book that has been brought to them (7:52). The request in front of us opens the closing sequence of the scene.

The reader who has seen the command given to the believers to enter the Garden now hears the request of the condemned and its refusal. The two outcomes are within sight of each other, and the answer states that the provision of the Garden is not for the group that the next verse describes.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse describes the disbelievers as those who took their religion as amusement and play and were deluded by the life of this world, and it reports the statement that they will be ignored as they ignored the meeting of this day and rejected the revelations (7:51). The refusal in front of us is given its reason in the verse that follows, and the conduct of the condemned is named.

The two verses together state the terms of the exclusion. Water and provision are withheld, and the reason is the amusement that a lifetime was made of and the denial of the day whose arrival is now certain; and the sūrah then turns from the two companies to the Book that has been sent to the audience of the chapter.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:51

> those who took this faith ˹of Islam˺ as mere amusement and play and were deluded by ˹their˺ worldly life.” ˹Allah will say,˺ “Today We will ignore them just as they ignored the coming of this Day of theirs and for rejecting Our revelations.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Religion Taken as Amusement**

The verse describes the condemned as those who took their religion as amusement and play, and the Book attaches the same description to the deniers in several passages. The account of the Cattle instructs the Prophet to leave those who take their religion as play and amusement and are deluded by the life of this world (6:70), and the sūrah of the Ornament and the sūrah of the Heights' own later verses report the same charge. The phrase in front of us describes a settled attitude toward the message rather than a moment of levity.

The Book's descriptions of the audience's response to the revelation include mockery, and the sūrah's narratives of the prophets and their peoples present the messengers being treated as a subject for entertainment. The verse in front of us states the attitude in the terms the Book uses elsewhere for the world itself, and the parallel is the point: what the audience did with the religion was what the Book says the world is.

**The Delusion of the Life of This World**

The second description is that the condemned were deluded by the life of this world, and the Book makes the same statement in its accounts of the deniers. The Cattle's account uses the phrase in the instruction about those who take their faith as play, and the sūrah of Luqmān and the sūrah of Fāṭir warn against the deception of the present life. The verse in front of us names the world as the source of the delusion, and the world's own description in the Book explains how it works.

The Spider's account states that the present life is nothing but play and amusement and that the home of the Hereafter is the real life (29:64), and the sūrah of Iron expands the list to include boasting and competition in wealth and children (57:20). The account of the Cattle states the same in the form of a comparison with the home of the Hereafter (6:32). The verse in front of us applies the description to the people who mistook the amusement for the purpose.

**The Reciprocity of Forgetting**

The verse reports the divine statement that the condemned will be ignored as they ignored the meeting of this day and rejected the revelations, and the Book sets the same correspondence between the deed and the outcome in several passages. The sūrah of Ṭā Hā states that those who neglected the revelations will be neglected on the day (20:126), the sūrah of the Prostration states that those who forgot the meeting of their Lord will be forgotten (32:14), and the sūrah of the Crouching reports the same statement in the address to the condemned (45:34).

The Book's descriptions of the reckoning include several exchanges of this kind, and the principle behind them is the correspondence between the act and its return. The sūrah of the Cow states that the deceivers are themselves deceived (2:9), the sūrah of the Table states that the recompense of an evil is an evil like it (42:40), and the sūrah of the Cow states that those who spend do so for their own souls (2:272). This verse applies the principle to the audience that treated the meeting with its Lord as a thing of no consequence.

**What the Neglect Means**

The commentary tradition discusses what it means for God to neglect the condemned, since the Book states elsewhere that God neither errs nor forgets (20:52). The readings agree that the word does not describe a failure of knowledge on the part of God, since the Book denies that. What it describes is the treatment that the condemned receive: their plea is not answered, mercy is not extended to them, and they are left in the punishment they have entered.

The sūrah of the Hypocrites uses the same language for the relation between the hypocrites and God in this world, where the hypocrites are described as having forgotten God and being forgotten in turn (9:67). The verse here applies the same measure at the reckoning, and the two passages together show that the correspondence is a feature of the Book's account of the relation between conduct and outcome rather than a single verdict on one group.

**The Two Grounds of the Sentence**

The verse names two grounds for the treatment of the condemned: the neglect of the meeting of the day and the rejection of the revelations. The Book joins the two in its warnings throughout, since the denial of the last day and the denial of the message are the two halves of the same rejection. The sūrah's opening verses have stated the reckoning in detail, and the narratives that fill the chapter show the communities refusing the message that warned them of it.

The pair of grounds matches the two subjects of the sūrah's argument. The chapter states the obligations of the religion and the account of the reckoning, and it answers the practices of the audience and their expectation of the world's permanence. The present verse states the condemnation of the group that refused both, and the passage then turns from the two companies to the Book that the audience has received.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fifty-first in the chapter, and it closes the scene of the two companies that began with the exclusion of the proud from the Garden. The verse that follows returns to the revelation and states that a Book has been brought to the audience, explained with knowledge and given as a guide and a mercy for those who believe (7:52). The passage therefore moves from the Hereafter to the Book in the hands of its hearers.

The reader who has been shown the two destinations and the words exchanged between them is now returned to his own situation. The Book that the sūrah's opening verses described is presented again, and the sūrah states that it is in the hands of the people whose letter is being recited.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that a Book has been brought to the audience, explained with knowledge, and that it is a guide and a mercy for people who believe (7:52). The description of the condemned in front of us gives way to the account of the revelation, and the sūrah returns from the Hereafter to the message that the chapter has been presenting throughout.

The two verses together state the choice and the means. The people who took the religion as amusement and rejected the revelations have been shown their end, and the Book that they rejected is named in the next verse as the guide and the mercy that the audience may still accept.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:52

> We have certainly brought them a Book which We explained with knowledge—a guide and mercy for those who believe.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Book Explained With Knowledge**

The verse returns from the account of the two companies to the revelation, and it states that a Book has been brought to the audience and explained with knowledge. The Book's own descriptions of its clarity appear throughout, and the sūrah of the Cow opens with the statement that the Book contains no doubt and is a guide for the mindful (2:2), the sūrah of the Rocky Tract describes the sūrah as verses of the clear Book (15:1), and the sūrah of Fussilat states that its verses have been detailed and made clear. The verse in front of us attaches the explanation to knowledge, which states that the clarification is exact rather than approximate.

The Book's account of its own arrangement includes the statement that God has made the signs clear for people who know, and the sūrah of the Cattle uses the phrase in its descriptions of the revelations (6:97). The verse in front of us states that the Book has been explained by the One whose knowledge covers what it explains, and the sūrah's reader is given the revelation as a text that has been clarified rather than left to guesswork.

**Knowledge and Guidance Together**

The verse names knowledge and guidance in the same sentence, and the combination describes the two services the Book performs. The explanation provides the knowledge of what is required, and the guidance directs the reader to the conduct that the knowledge describes. The Bee's account states that the Book has been sent down to make clear to people what has been sent to them so that they may reflect (16:44), and the sūrah of the Family of Imran states that God has sent down the Book with the truth.

The Book's teaching about knowledge and action as a pair appears in its criticisms of those who know and do not act as well as its praise of those who combine the two. The account of the Ranks criticizes those who say what they do not do, and the sūrah of the Cow describes the people of the earlier books who conceal what they know. The verse in front of us presents the Book as the remedy for both errors, since it explains and it guides.

**A Guide and a Mercy**

The verse describes the Book as a guide and a mercy for the believers, and the Book uses the pair in several passages. The sūrah of the Ant states that the Book is a guide and a mercy for the believers (27:77), the sūrah of the Night Journey states that the Quran is sent down as a healing and a mercy for the believers while it increases the wrongdoers in loss (17:82), and the sūrah of Fussilat states that the Book is a guide and a healing for the believers (41:44). This verse states the two benefits in the order the Book repeats.

The mercy in front of us is attached to the guidance, and the Book presents the guidance as the mercy itself. The sūrah of the Cattle says that God has brought the community a Book that explains everything and a guidance and a mercy (16:89), and the sūrah of the Cow states that the guidance is what the Book exists to provide. The verse here places the two names side by side, and the passage that follows states the response of those who ignore the Book.

**For Those Who Believe**

The verse attaches the benefit of the Book to the believers, and the Book's descriptions of its audience include the same qualification. The sūrah of the Cow states that the Book is a guide for the mindful (2:2), and it states later in its account of the parables that God misleads many by them and guides many, and that He misleads none but the iniquitous (2:26). The present verse states the condition, and the sūrah's reader is given the Book as a gift that produces its fruit in those who receive it.

The Book's teaching about the reception of the revelation uses the image of the land that receives rain, and the sūrah of the Heights itself has already stated that the good land brings forth its produce by the permission of its Lord while the poor land brings forth little (7:58). The two passages belong to the same argument. The Book is brought to all of its hearers, and its guidance and mercy take effect in those who believe.

**The Book That the Audience Has Received**

The verse addresses the audience of the sūrah directly, since the Book in question is the one being recited to them. The sūrah's opening verses named the Book that had been sent down to the Prophet, and the passage in front of us restates that fact after the long account of the two companies. The arrangement reminds the hearer that the warning he has just read comes through a text that is in his hands.

The Book's descriptions of its audience include the charge that they were given the message and turned away from it, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the same accusation against the communities that received messengers before them. The verse under discussion places the audience in the company of those who have received a clarified Book, and the following verses state what awaits those who ignore it.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fifty-second in the chapter, and it begins the passage about the revelation that runs to the description of creation in the following verses. It follows the scene of the two companies and precedes the warning about the fulfillment of the Book's promise (7:53). Here the verse supplies the ground of that warning, since the Book that explains with knowledge is the Book whose promise cannot fail.

The reader who has been shown the Hereafter is returned to the text he is reading. The Book that has been explained and given as a guide and a mercy is presented to the people of the sūrah, and the verses that follow state what happens when its warning arrives.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse asks whether the people are waiting for anything but the fulfillment of the Book's warning, and it reports the words of those who ignored it when the fulfillment arrives, together with their request for intercessors or for a return to the world (7:53). The Book that has been explained in front of us is the same Book whose warning will arrive, and the verse that follows states the regret of those who put off their response to it.

The two verses together place the revelation and its outcome in sequence. The Book has been brought and explained, the guidance and the mercy are available to those who believe, and the day on which its promise is fulfilled will produce the confession of those who ignored it; and the sūrah then turns to the signs of the created order.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:53

> Do they only await the fulfilment ˹of its warning˺? The Day it will be fulfilled, those who ignored it before will say, “The messengers of our Lord certainly came with the truth. Are there any intercessors who can plead on our behalf? Or can we be sent back so we may do ˹good,˺ unlike what we used to do?” They will have certainly ruined themselves, and whatever ˹gods˺ they fabricated will fail them.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Waiting for the Fulfilment**

The verse opens with a question about whether the audience is waiting for anything other than the fulfilment of the Book's warning, and the question treats the delay as the whole of what remains. The warning in question is the promise and the threat that the Book has stated, and the fulfilment is the arrival of the events it describes. The sūrah's preceding verse has described the Book as explained with knowledge and given as a guide and a mercy, and the verse in front of us states that its warning will be completed.

The form of the question implies that there is nothing left to wait for but the event itself. The sūrah's account of the two companies has shown what the warning describes, and the revelation that carries it has been presented as clarified. The verse in front of us turns the hearer's attention to the moment when what the Book has promised becomes the world he is standing in.

**The Confession When It Arrives**

The verse reports the words of those who ignored the Book when the fulfilment arrives, and their first statement is that the messengers of their Lord came with the truth. The Book records the same confession from the condemned in several places, and its accounts of the last day include the acknowledgement of the truth of the message by those who had denied it. The sūrah's earlier verses have already reported the confession of the destroyed communities when the punishment came upon them (7:5), and the statement in front of us belongs to the same sequence.

The confession comes at the point at which it cannot change the outcome, and the Book's accounts of the last day treat such acknowledgements as testimony rather than as repentance. The sūrah of the Troops and the sūrah of the Forgiver report the same words from the inhabitants of the Fire, and the verse in front of us places the confession at the moment of the warning's fulfilment.

**The Search for Intercessors**

The second element of the reported speech is the question about whether any intercessors can plead on the speakers' behalf, and the Book addresses intercession in several passages. The sūrah of the Cow states that no soul will be of help to another on the day, that no intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken, and no help given (2:48), and it states elsewhere that intercession belongs to God and takes place by His permission (2:255). The sūrah of the Cattle states that the intercessors whom the idolaters claimed will not be present (6:94).

The commentary tradition reads the question in front of us as the last resort of people who had attributed intercession to their idols. The audience's religion included the expectation that the beings it associated with God would plead for it, and the sūrah's closing scenes have shown those beings failing their worshippers. The question in front of us is the expression of that failure, and the Book's answer is stated in the passages about the day on which no plea will be accepted.

**The Request to Be Sent Back**

The third element of the speech is the request to be returned so that the speakers may act differently from the way they acted, and the Book records the same request in several passages. The sūrah of the Cattle reports the wish of the condemned that they might be sent back and not repeat what they used to do (6:27–28), the sūrah of the Poets records the same wish, and the sūrah of Fāṭir reports the request of those who will be in the Fire to be brought out of it. The verse in front of us places the request in the mouth of the people who ignored the Book.

The Book's answer to the request appears in the sūrah of the Cattle, where the wish to be returned is followed by the statement that if they were returned they would return to what they had been forbidden, and that they are liars (6:28). The verse in front of us does not carry the answer, and the sūrah's earlier account of the two companies supplies it. What the condemned ask for has been answered before the request is made.

**What the Fabrication Leaves Behind**

The verse closes with the statement that the condemned have ruined themselves and that what they fabricated has failed them. The Book uses the same language for the idols and the invented systems of belief in its accounts of the reckoning, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that the partners of the idolaters will fail them (6:24), the sūrah of Yūnus states that what they fabricated will leave them, and the sūrah of Hūd states that the things they invented will be lost to them. This verse states the outcome of the appeal, and the fabricated gods are named as the failures they have proved to be.

The term for fabrication covers everything the audience had invented in the name of religion, and the sūrah has spent its length exposing the practices that were attributed to God without warrant. The verse here states that the whole structure has collapsed at the moment when the appeal is made, and the sūrah's next verses turn from that collapse to the signs of the created order.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fifty-third in the chapter, and it closes the passage about the Book that began with the statement that it has been explained with knowledge and given as a guide and a mercy (7:52). The verses that follow begin the section on the signs of creation and the command to call upon God (7:54–56), and the passage then proceeds to the account of the prophets and their peoples. The present verse supplies the link between the revelation and the created order.

The reader who has been returned to the Book in his hands now sees its warning fulfilled before him. The messengers are acknowledged, the intercessors are sought and not found, and the request to return is made too late. The sūrah's next verses state the signs by which the same truth is visible from the beginning.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that God is the One who created the heavens and the earth in six days, established Himself on the Throne, made the night and day follow one another, and subjected the sun, the moon, and the stars by His command, and it declares that creation and command belong to Him and that He is blessed, the Lord of all worlds (7:54). The regret of the condemned gives way to the account of the Creator, and the sūrah's argument moves to the signs.

The two verses together complete the movement from the audience to their Lord. The Book explains, its warning arrives, the fabricated structures fail, and the created order is presented as the evidence that the audience could have read at any time; and the verses that follow state the command to call upon the One who made it.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:54

> Indeed your Lord is Allah Who created the heavens and the earth in six Days, then established Himself on the Throne. He makes the day and night overlap in rapid succession. He created the sun, the moon, and the stars—all subjected by His command. The creation and the command belong to Him ˹alone˺. Blessed is Allah—Lord of all worlds!

**Expanded Commentary**

**Six Days and the Throne**

The verse names God as the One who created the heavens and the earth in six days and then established Himself on the Throne, and the pairing appears in several passages of the Book. The sūrah of Iron states that God created the heavens and the earth in six days and then established Himself on the Throne (57:4), the sūrah of the Prostration states that He established Himself on the Throne after creating in six days and conducts every affair from the heavens to the earth (32:4–5), and the sūrah of Jonah and the sūrah of Hūd repeat the arrangement. The verse in front of us states the creation and the rule in a single sentence.

The image of the establishment on the Throne is understood by the tradition as the statement of divine sovereignty rather than of place, since the Book presents God as beyond the limits of bodies. The Cow's account describes the Throne as encompassing the heavens and the earth and states that neither drowsiness nor sleep overtakes its Owner (2:255). The verse in front of us supplies the sovereignty that the following verses apply to the ordering of the world.

**Night and Day in Rapid Succession**

The verse states that the day and the night overlap in rapid succession, and the Book uses the same image of one wrapping around the other. The account of the Troops states that God wraps the night around the day and the day around the night (39:5), and the sūrah of the Family of Imran and the sūrah of the Night Journey cite the alternation of the two as a sign for people of understanding. The verse in front of us places the alternation among the works of the Creator whose command governs the world.

The succession is presented as a search that never pauses, and the commentary tradition notes that the image describes the pursuit of one by the other without interval. The Book's use of the cycle of night and day as evidence for the resurrection appears in several passages, and the sūrah's following verses draw the same argument from the rain and the dead land. This verse states the mechanism that the later verses use as a sign.

**The Sun, the Moon, and the Stars Subjected**

The verse names the sun, the moon, and the stars as created beings subjected by God's command, and the Book mentions the subjection of the heavenly bodies in several places. The Troops' account states that God has subjected the sun and the moon, each running for an appointed term (39:5), and the sūrah of the Cow states that the sun and the moon are made to follow a reckoning. The word used in front of us for the stars describes them as created and directed, and the verse places them under the same command as the rest.

The subjection in front of us is the answer to the audience's veneration of the heavenly bodies. The Cattle's account records Abraham's argument from the rising and setting of the star, the moon, and the sun to the conclusion that he would not worship things that pass away (6:76–78), and the sūrah's audience had its own attachment to the worship of the sky. The verse here names the created lights and states that they are directed rather than divine.

**"His Are the Creation and the Command"**

The verse states that creation and command belong to God alone, and the sentence gathers the two halves of the argument that the passage has been making. The Book's account of God as the Creator of all things appears in the sūrah of the Cattle, where the declaration is followed by the command to worship Him (6:102), and the sūrah of Iron's account of the six days closes with the statement that He knows all things (57:4). The present verse states the claim in the form of a pronouncement.

The two terms cover the making of the world and its governance, and the verse's audience is told that both belong to the One it is being called to worship. The sūrah's opening command forbade the taking of protectors besides God, and the sentence in front of us supplies the reason: nothing else made the world and nothing else directs it. The claim is stated before the passage turns to supplication.

**Blessed Be God, Lord of All Worlds**

The verse closes with the declaration that God is blessed and the Lord of all worlds, and the phrase echoes the opening of the Book. The first sūrah praises God under the same title (1:2), and the sūrah of the Forgiver uses it in its account of the blessings of creation (40:64). The verse under discussion places the title at the end of the description of the Creator, and the declaration gathers the whole list of His acts under one name.

The title states the scope of the lordship, and the sūrah's argument depends on it. The audience is told that the Lord of the worlds is the One who created, ordered, and governs, and the command to call upon Him in the following verse addresses the same Lord. Here the verse supplies the name before it states the command.

**The Days and Their Measure**

The commentary tradition discusses the six days, and the discussion turns on the Book's own statements about the length of a divine day. The account of the Pilgrimage states that a day with the Lord is like a thousand years of what people count (22:47), and the sūrah of the Prostration states that the affairs ascend to Him on a day whose measure is a thousand years (32:5). Some commentators therefore treat the six days as a measure of stages rather than of twenty-four-hour periods, and others hold to the plain reading.

The tradition notes a difference between the Book's account and the account of the earlier scriptures, since the Book attributes no rest to God after the work of creation. The sūrah of the Cow states that neither drowsiness nor sleep overtakes Him (2:255), and the point matters for the practices of the religion, since no day of rest is prescribed. This verse states the completion of the creation without the interval of repose.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fifty-fourth in the chapter, and it opens the passage on the signs of the created order that runs to the description of the rain and the good land (7:57–58). It follows the account of the Book and its warning, and it prepares the command to call upon God that comes next. The verse here supplies the ground on which the supplication rests.

The reader who has been shown the two companies and the fulfilled warning is now directed to the world's construction. The heavens, the earth, the night, the day, and the lights are named as the work of One Lord, and the sūrah's next verses instruct the audience on how to address Him.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse commands the audience to call upon their Lord humbly and in secret and states that He does not love the transgressors (7:55). The description of the Creator in front of us is followed by the command to address Him, and the passage then adds the conditions under which the address is made.

The two verses together move from the works of God to the worship of God. The One who made the heavens and the earth and directs the night, the day, and the lights is the One to whom the next verse directs the supplication; and the verses that follow add the other requirements of supplication and the warning against spreading corruption in the land.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:55

> Call upon your Lord humbly and secretly. Surely He does not like the transgressors.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Call Upon God**

The verse commands the audience to call upon their Lord, and the command follows the description of the Creator in the previous verse. The Book issues the same command in several passages, and the sūrah of the Forgiver states that the Lord has proclaimed that His servants should call upon Him and that He will respond, adding that those who are too proud to worship will enter Hell in humiliation (40:60). The account of the Cow states that God is near and answers the call of the one who calls upon Him (2:186). The verse in front of us states the command in its shortest form and attaches two conditions.

The address is made to the audience of the sūrah, and the Lord named in it is the One whose works the previous verse has just described. The pair of verses joins the evidence to the response that the evidence requires, and the command in front of us supplies the act of worship that follows from the recognition of the Creator.

**Humbly and in Secret**

The two conditions in the verse are humility and concealment, and the Book attaches both to the supplication of the sincere. The Cattle's account describes people in distress calling upon God with humility and in secret, and it reports their abandonment of the practice once they reach dry land (6:63). The sūrah of Maryam describes Zechariah crying out to his Lord privately (19:3), and the sūrah of the Heights itself will state later in the chapter that the remembrance of God is to be kept inward, with humility and reverence and without loudness (7:205).

The concealment in front of us distinguishes the supplication from display, and the Book's criticisms of those who pray to be seen supply the contrast. The sūrah of the Small Kindnesses describes those who only show off in their worship (107:6), and the sūrah of the Cow warns against the alms that are given and then followed by reminders and injury. The verse in front of us states the two conditions that keep the act between the servant and his Lord.

**He Does Not Love the Transgressors**

The verse closes with the statement that God does not love those who transgress the limits, and the term covers excess in supplication as well as the wider sense of transgression. The commentary tradition reads the statement as a warning against the extremes in the act that has just been commanded, whether by raising the voice beyond what the setting requires, by making the petition so elaborate that it turns into display, or by demanding what lies outside the worshipper's proper concern. The Book's instructions about the tone of the remembrance in the later verses of the same chapter belong to the same teaching.

The Prophet's guidance on the subject is reported in the collections, and the tradition records that he corrected those who carried the practice to excess. A report transmitted by al-Tirmidhī states that nothing is more honourable with God than supplication (al-Tirmidhī 3370), which the collector graded as a good narration of a single chain, and the report places the act at the centre of the worshipper's relation with his Lord while leaving the manner of it to the rules the Book and the practice state.

**The Manners of Supplication in the Book**

The Book states the manner of the call in several passages, and the two conditions in front of us are part of a larger set of instructions. The sūrah of the Heights states that the remembrance is to be kept within oneself, with fear and without loudness, in the mornings and the evenings (7:205), and the sūrah of the Night Journey instructs the Prophet not to raise his voice too high in his prayer nor to be silent altogether but to seek a middle way (17:110). The verse in front of us states the two conditions in a single phrase.

The Book's instructions about the call include the address used in it and the confidence with which it is made, and the sūrah of the Cow states that God answers the one who calls on Him and that the callers should respond to Him and believe in Him (2:186). This verse supplies the posture of the call, and the passage that follows states the two other attitudes that accompany it: hope and fear.

**Sincerity Against Display**

The conditions of humility and concealment address the danger of the act becoming a performance, and the Book's teaching about the worship of the heart supplies the alternatives. The sūrah of the Cave commands the Prophet to be with those who call upon their Lord morning and evening seeking His face, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran praises those who stand and sit and lie in prayer while reflecting on the creation. The verse here places the same requirement at the head of the passage on the created order.

The Book's warnings about the display of good deeds appear in the sūrah of the Cow in the parable of the rock with dust upon it that a heavy rain leaves bare (2:264), and the metaphor states that the deed and its appearance diverge. The present verse states the alternative in the conditions of the call.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fifty-fifth in the chapter, and it follows the account of the creation and precedes the command against spreading corruption in the land and the instruction to call upon God with hope and fear (7:56). The three verses form a unit on the worship of the Creator, and the verse in front of us supplies the command that begins it.

The reader who has been given the account of the heavens and the earth is now told how to address their Maker. The call is to be humble and hidden, and the excess that would turn it into a display is excluded by the warning that God does not love those who go beyond the limits, and the next verses state the other conditions of the call and the corruption that the audience is to avoid.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse commands the audience not to spread corruption in the land after it has been set in order and to call upon God with hope and fear, and it states that the mercy of God is near to those who do good (7:56). The instruction about the manner of the call in front of us is followed by the two attitudes that accompany it and by the land's condition, and the passage then turns to the winds and the rain.

The two verses together form the worship that the sūrah asks of its audience. The call is made with humility and concealment, the land is left in the order in which it was set, and the call is offered with hope and fear; and the verses that follow present the rain and the good land as the signs of the One who is called upon.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:56

> Do not spread corruption in the land after it has been set in order. And call upon Him with hope and fear. Indeed, Allah’s mercy is always close to the good-doers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Land Set in Order**

The verse forbids the spreading of corruption in the land after it has been set in order, and the phrase states that the world arrived in a condition of arrangement. The Book describes the created order as measured and balanced, and the sūrah of the Merciful states that God raised the sky and set up the balance so that people would not transgress it (55:7–8). The verse in front of us treats the order of the land as the gift that human conduct is not to undo.

The sūrah's audience lived in a region whose order it took for granted, and the verse states the obligation that the order carries. The sūrah of the Romans describes corruption appearing on land and at sea because of what people's hands have earned, and it states that God will let them taste some of what they have done so that they may return (30:41). The two passages describe the same relation between human conduct and the condition of the world.

**What Corruption in the Land Covers**

The commentary tradition gives the phrase a wide reach, and al-Rāzī treats it as a prohibition against everything that damages human life in its five dimensions: bodies through violence, wealth through fraud and theft, religion through denial and invented practices, lineage through adultery and slander, and reason through intoxication. The Book's prohibitions in other passages match the list, and the sūrah of the Table states that the one who kills a soul without just cause is as though he had killed all of humanity (5:32). The verse in front of us states the general rule of which those prohibitions are the detail.

The phrase is also read as a warning against damage to the physical world itself, and the modern reader of the verse finds the reading natural. The Book's descriptions of the created order include the balance, the measure, and the provisions placed in the earth, and a community that destroys the sources of its own provision has corrupted what it was given. The verse in front of us states the prohibition in the terms of the land and its condition.

**Calling Upon Him With Hope and Fear**

The verse returns to the command of the previous verse and adds the two attitudes that accompany the call, and the combination is one of the Book's standard descriptions of worship. The sūrah of the Prostration praises those who abandon their beds to call upon their Lord with hope and fear and to spend from what they have been given (32:16), and the sūrah of the Prophets describes the supplication of the prophets as a seeking of the reward of their Lord with hope and dread. This verse states the two dispositions in a single phrase.

The two attitudes answer the two errors that the verse is placed to correct. The person who calls with hope alone may neglect the obligations that accompany the call, and the person who calls with fear alone may despair of the mercy that the Book places at the centre of its teaching. The sūrah's command in front of us holds the two together, and the commentary tradition treats the balance as the mark of a sound religious condition.

**The Mercy That Is Near**

The verse closes with the statement that the mercy of God is near to those who do good, and the Book attaches the divine mercy to the practice of good conduct in several passages. The sūrah of the Cow states that the mercy of God is near to those who do right in its account of the signs, and the sūrah of the Heights' account of the people of the Garden includes their entry by the mercy of God. The verse here states the proximity of the mercy as the reason for the two attitudes it has commanded.

The description of mercy as close is a statement about availability rather than about place, and the Book's accounts of the answer to supplication use the same terms. The report transmitted from the Prophet in the collection of Muslim states that when God completed the creation He wrote in a book that lies with Him upon the Throne that His mercy prevails over His wrath (al-Bukhārī 3194; Muslim 2751), and the statement supplies the ground on which the verse's two attitudes can be held together.

**The Command and Its Audience**

The verse addresses the audience of the sūrah in the period before the migration, and the corruption it forbids includes the practices that the chapter has been opposing. The audience's religion involved the division of the harvests, the arbitrary prohibition of food, the killing of the animals it considered sacred, and the burial of infant daughters, and each of these is a corruption in the sense the verse uses. The Book's lists of the forbidden things earlier in the sūrah name the same conduct.

The Book's teaching about the land and its order appears in its instructions about the fair measure, the treatment of the orphan, and the fulfilment of agreements, and the sūrah's passage on the covenant collects those commands into a single list. The present verse states the general prohibition, and the sūrah's later verses supply the details of the conduct that keeps the land in order.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fifty-sixth in the chapter, and it forms a pair with the command to call upon God in the previous verse. The two verses state the two duties of the audience toward their Lord and toward the world they inhabit, and the verse that follows presents the rain and the dead land as the sign of the resurrection (7:57). The verse under discussion supplies the ethical half of the passage.

The reader who has been told how to address his Lord is now told what to do with the world he has been given. The corruption is excluded, the call is made with hope and fear, and the mercy is named as near to those who do good; and the sūrah's next verses present the created order as the evidence for the raising of the dead.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that God sends the winds as bearers of good news before His mercy, that they carry the heavy clouds to a dead land, and that the rain brings forth every kind of fruit, and it states that the dead will be brought forth in the same way (7:57). The instruction about the land in front of us is followed by the account of the rain that revives it, and the passage turns the image of the earth into the proof of the resurrection.

The two verses together place the conduct of the audience and the work of God in the same frame. The land is not to be corrupted, and the rain that revives it is presented as the sign of the raising; and the verse that follows adds the difference between the land that receives the water and the land that does not.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:57

> He is the One Who sends the winds ushering in His mercy. When they bear heavy clouds, We drive them to a lifeless land and then cause rain to fall, producing every type of fruit. Similarly, We will bring the dead to life, so perhaps you will be mindful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Winds Before the Mercy**

The verse names God as the One who sends the winds as bearers of good news before His mercy, and the Book uses the same description in several passages. The Ant's account states that God sends the winds as glad tidings before His mercy (27:63), the sūrah of the Romans states the same (30:46), and the sūrah of the Sorting states that God sends the winds as heralds of good tidings. The verse in front of us places the winds at the front of the rain that follows them, and the phrase before His mercy describes the rain itself as the mercy.

The Book's use of the word mercy for rain appears throughout, and the sūrah of the Romans states that God sends down the rain after people have despaired and spreads His mercy (30:24). The verse in front of us names the sequence of the wind and the cloud and the rain, and the passage that follows draws the argument about the resurrection from the same sequence.

**The Clouds and the Dead Land**

The verse states that when the winds carry the heavy clouds, God drives them to a land that is dead and sends down water upon it, and the Book uses the image of the revived land in its arguments for the raising of the dead. The account of the Romans states that God revives the earth after its death, and that He will revive the dead in the same way. The sūrah of Fāṭir and the sūrah of the Ornament state that God sends down water from the sky and revives the earth with it after its death.

The land in front of us is described as dead, and the commentary tradition treats the image as a deliberate selection of the least promising material. The rain does not restore a garden that was already growing; it brings produce out of ground that had produced nothing, and the Book's argument rests on the difference between the condition before and the condition after. The image returns when the sūrah distinguishes the good land from the poor one.

**Every Kind of Fruit**

The verse states that the rain brings forth every kind of fruit, and the Book's descriptions of the provision of the earth include the variety of the produce as a sign. The Cattle's account lists the produce of the earth, the palm, the olive, and the fruit of every kind in its accounts of the signs, and the sūrah of the Bee describes the rain that brings out crops of every kind. The verse in front of us names the result of the rain as the abundance that the audience ate from every day.

The abundance is presented as the ordinary provision of the world rather than as an exception, and the Book's argument depends on the familiarity of what it describes. The audience observed the sequence of the wind, the cloud, the rain, and the harvest every year, and the verse states that the same power that performs the sequence will perform the raising of the dead.

**Thus We Bring Forth the Dead**

The verse states the conclusion that the image is designed to establish, and the Book repeats it in several passages. The account of the Romans states that God will bring forth the dead in the same manner as He revives the earth, and the sūrah of Fāṭir uses the same comparison. The argument in front of us does not depend on the possibility of the event in the abstract but on the evidence of the world's own cycles.

The audience's objection to the resurrection was an objection about the raising of the scattered body, and the Book answers it with the production of a harvest from soil whose condition was indistinguishable from death. The commentary tradition notes that the argument is presented to people whose livelihood depended on the sequence, since the sūrah's audience lived by the rain and the pasture.

**The Call to Remember**

The verse closes with the statement that these signs are given so that people may remember, and the Book attaches the formula to the created order in several passages. The sūrah of the Cattle closes its account of the signs with the statement that God makes them clear for people who remember, and the sūrah of the Bee uses the same closing formula. This verse states the purpose of the evidence, and the passage that follows states the difference between the land that responds and the land that does not.

The remembrance in front of us is the recognition of the One who sends the winds and the rain, and the Book presents reflection on the created order as the route to that recognition. The sūrah's audience watched the sky for its livelihood, and the verse states that the same observation supplies the evidence for the world's end.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fifty-seventh in the chapter, and it follows the command against corruption in the land and the instruction to call upon God with hope and fear (7:56). The verses that follow use the image of the good land and the poor land to describe the two responses to the revelation (7:58), and the passage then begins the accounts of the prophets and their peoples. The verse here supplies the natural sign that introduces that sequence.

The reader who has been told to call upon his Lord with hope and fear is now shown the rain that answers the call in the world's own order. The winds precede the mercy, the dead land receives the water, and the produce that follows is offered as evidence for the raising of the dead; and the sūrah's next verse distinguishes the land that keeps what it receives from the land that does not.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the good land brings forth its produce by the permission of its Lord while the poor land brings forth little, and it states that the signs are varied for people who give thanks (7:58). The account of the rain in front of us is followed by the account of the two kinds of soil, and the image is then carried into the history of the prophets and their peoples.

The two verses together form a parable about revelation. The rain falls on both kinds of ground, and the difference in the harvest comes from the ground, not from the water; and the sūrah then presents the communities that received the warning as the land that returned little.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:58

> The fertile land produces abundantly by the Will of its Lord, whereas the infertile land hardly produces anything. This is how We vary ˹Our˺ lessons to those who are thankful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Kinds of Ground**

The verse divides the land into two kinds and states the different results of the same rain. The good land brings forth its produce by the permission of its Lord, and the poor land brings forth what it brings forth with difficulty and little of it. The verse follows the account of the rain that revives a dead land (7:57), and it supplies the qualification that the rain alone does not determine the harvest.

The image in front of us turns the natural observation into a statement about reception. The rain in the previous verse was presented as the mercy of God that descends on the earth, and the verse in front of us states that the effect of the descent depends on the condition of what receives it. The sūrah's argument has been built on the signs of the created order, and the two verses together complete the picture of the sign.

**The Parable of the Soil and the Heart**

The commentary tradition reads the two kinds of land as the two responses to the revelation, and the sūrah's own arrangement supports the reading, since the accounts of the prophets and their peoples follow immediately. The rain is the message that descends on every community, and the harvest is the response that each community produces. The difference between the good land and the poor land is therefore the difference between the heart that receives the warning and the heart that does not.

The Book uses the same image of the soil in its parables of the word, and the sūrah of Abraham states that a good word is like a good tree whose root is firm and whose branches reach the sky, while an evil word is like an evil tree uprooted from the surface of the earth (14:24–26). The sūrah of the Thunder states that the water flows in the valleys according to their measure and carries the foam on its surface (13:17). The verse in front of us belongs to the same family of comparisons.

**The Signs Varied for the Thankful**

The verse closes with the statement that God varies the signs for people who give thanks, and the Book uses the phrase in its accounts of the revelation. The sūrah's own earlier verses have stated that the Book is explained with knowledge (7:52), and the sūrah of the Cattle uses the language of varying the signs in its descriptions of the created order. The verse in front of us states that the signs are presented in more than one form so that the reader has more than one route to the same conclusion.

The audience of the sūrah was addressed through the sky, the rain, the soil, its own history, and the recitation of the messenger, and the phrase in front of us accounts for the variety. The commentary tradition notes that the signs are varied for the people who give thanks rather than for all people, since the recognition of the variety requires a disposition toward gratitude. The sūrah's readers are thereby told what the signs are for.

**The Comparison With the Gospel Parable**

The comparison between the two kinds of soil and the two responses to the word appears in the Gospels as well, where the parable of the sower describes seed falling on the path, on rocky ground, among thorns, and on good soil that produces a harvest. The sūrah's version of the comparison is brief and does not describe the intermediate cases, and the difference between the two texts is instructive: the Book's account in front of us divides the world into the land that responds and the land that does not.

The Book's version keeps the attention on the giver of the rain as well as the receiver of it, since the good land is described as producing by the permission of its Lord. The Book's practice of attributing the outcome to God while holding the person responsible appears throughout its parables, and the verse in front of us states both halves in one sentence.

**The Section That Follows**

The verse closes the passage on the created signs and opens the long sequence of the prophets and their peoples that runs through the middle of the chapter. The sūrah's introduction states that the accounts describe the destruction of communities that rejected their messengers, and the comparison in front of us supplies the frame for reading them. Each community received the rain of the warning, and each produced the harvest that its condition allowed.

The Book's arrangement of the two themes side by side is a feature of the sūrah's method. The signs of the created order demonstrate the power of God, and the accounts of the prophets demonstrate the response of the communities to His message; and the verse in front of us supplies the transition between the two sections.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fifty-eighth in the chapter, and it completes the passage on the rain and the land that began with the command to call upon God with hope and fear (7:56). The verse that follows begins the account of Noah and his people (7:59), and the sequence of the prophets continues from there to the account of Moses and the people of Israel. The verse in front of us supplies the image that governs the reading of those accounts.

The reader who has been shown the rain, the dead land, and the two harvests is now prepared for the history that follows. The messengers are sent to communities that have their own soil, and the sūrah's next verses state what the first of those communities did with what it received.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse begins the account of Noah, whom God sent to his people with the call to worship God alone and with the warning of the punishment of a tremendous day (7:59). The parable of the two kinds of land in front of us gives way to the first of the peoples, and the account of the messengers and their communities begins.

The two verses together start the sūrah's history. The signs have been stated, the difference between the two kinds of ground has been drawn, and the first messenger is sent to the first people; and the verses that follow record the answer of the notables and the outcome of the community that refused.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:59

> Indeed, We sent Noah to his people. He said, “O my people! Worship Allah—you have no other god except Him. I truly fear for you the torment of a tremendous Day.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Beginning of the Punishment Accounts**

The verse opens the long sequence of prophets and peoples that occupies the middle of the chapter, and the sequence runs from Noah to Moses and his confrontation with Pharaoh. The sūrah's introduction describes these accounts as the history of communities that rejected the messengers and suffered the consequences, and the Book presents the same series in the sūrah of Hūd, the sūrah of the Poets, and the sūrah of the Moon. The verse in front of us begins the first of them.

The order of the accounts is fixed in the tradition, and each narrative follows the same shape: the messenger is sent to his people, he calls them to the worship of the one God, the notables refuse, the messenger warns, and the community is destroyed. The repetition is deliberate, and the sūrah's use of it makes the history itself the argument. The audience in Makkah met the same message in the same pattern of refusal.

**The Call to Worship the One God**

The verse reports the call of Noah, and it is the same call that the sūrah's later prophets make: worship God, for you have no god besides Him. The sūrah of the Believers reports the same words from Noah (23:23), the sūrah of Hūd reports them with the addition of the warning of a painful day (11:26), and the sūrah of the Heights itself puts the same call in the mouth of Hūd (7:65), Ṣāliḥ (7:73), and Shuʿayb (7:85). The Book states that no messenger was sent before the Prophet without the same revelation of the unity of God (21:25).

The Book's presentation of the prophetic message as one message appears in the sūrah of the Consultation, which states that God has ordained for the community the way He decreed for Noah and revealed to the Prophet (42:13), and in the sūrah of the Sand Dunes, where the Prophet is told to say that he is not the first messenger ever sent (46:9). The verse in front of us supplies the first instance of the message in the sūrah's sequence.

**The Fear of a Tremendous Day**

The verse records the warning that Noah attaches to the call, and the warning names the punishment of a great day. The commentary tradition gives the day two references, one of them the flood in which the people were destroyed in the world and the other the day of the resurrection and the reckoning; the two are joined in the narrative because the first is the proof of the second. The sūrah's later accounts of the peoples describe the punishments that arrived in the world, and the sūrah's opening verses have stated the reckoning of the last day.

The Book's habit of naming the day in the warning appears in the sūrah of Noah, where the messenger calls his people to worship and warns them of the pain of a great day. The verse in front of us gives the same warning in the first of the sūrah's accounts, and the sequence of the narratives that follows shows what the warning meant for the community that disregarded it.

**The Prophet Noah in the Tradition**

The Book reports the story of Noah in several passages, and the accounts differ in their details. The sūrah of Hūd gives the longest of the sequences, the sūrah of the Poets gives a short version with the dialogue of the people, the sūrah of the Moon gives the account of the denial and the flood, and the sūrah of Noah gives the messenger's own appeal and the course of the deluge. This verse supplies the opening line of the sequence in this chapter.

The tradition of the community preserves the genealogy and the circumstances of the prophet, and the accounts of the earlier historians place him among the first of the messengers sent to a people. The narratives of the Book do not depend on those details, and the sūrah's account in front of us keeps to the call, the refusal, and the outcome. What the passage establishes is the pattern that the sūrah's later accounts of ʿĀd, Thamūd, Lot, and Shuʿayb repeat.

**The Argument the Sequence Makes**

The sūrah uses the sequence of the prophets to answer the audience's claim that its religion was that of its ancestors. Each community in the sequence was settled in its customs, and each refused the messenger who called it to the worship of God alone; and the destruction that followed is presented as the outcome of that refusal. The audience in Makkah knew the ruins of the peoples of ʿĀd and Thamūd and the traditions of the peoples of Lot, and the sūrah places its own message in the line of the warnings they had rejected.

The Book's treatment of the same history in the sūrah of the Poets uses the refrain that each people rejected the messengers, and the plural states the point: to refuse one messenger is to refuse the message they all carried. The verse here opens the series in this chapter, and the sūrah's following accounts carry the refrain in their own form.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands fifty-ninth in the chapter, and it follows the passage on the signs and the two kinds of land (7:52–58). The verses that follow report the answer of the notables and the exchanges between Noah and his people, and the narrative ends with the salvation of the messenger and those with him and the drowning of the deniers (7:64). The present verse supplies the opening of the first account.

The reader who has been given the signs of the created order now meets the first of the communities that ignored them. The messenger calls, the notables answer, and the account proceeds to its end; and the sūrah's following narratives repeat the pattern with the peoples that came after Noah.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the notables of the people said that they saw the messenger in manifest error (7:60). The call to the worship of God alone in front of us is followed by the answer of the community's leaders, and the passage then records the messenger's reply.

The two verses together state the pattern that the sūrah's narratives will repeat. The messenger calls, the leaders dismiss him, and the account moves to the exchange that follows; and the sūrah's next verses state the reply of Noah and the terms on which the refusal was answered.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:60

> But the chiefs of his people said, “We surely see that you are clearly misguided.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Notables Who Answer**

The verse reports the answer of the notables among the people of Noah, and the answer charges the messenger with manifest error. The Book uses the term for the leaders of a community in its accounts of several peoples, and it presents them as the group that answers the messengers on behalf of the community. The verse in front of us places that answer at the opening of the sūrah's first narrative, and the pattern repeats with ʿĀd (7:66), Thamūd (7:75), and the people of Shuʿayb.

The term notables identifies the leading men whose standing rested on wealth, lineage, and influence, and the Book's accounts of the communities consistently place the refusal with them. The sūrah of the Believers reports the disbelieving chiefs of Noah's people dismissing him as a man who wished to be superior to them (23:24), and the sūrah of Hūd reports the chiefs of the people saying that they saw nothing in the messenger that made him worthy of their following. The verse in front of us states the answer of that group in the sūrah's own account.

**The Charge of Misguidance**

The notables' answer is the charge that the messenger stands in clear error, and the Book's accounts of the communities show the same charge being answered from the other side. The messenger in the next verse replies that he is not in error and that he is a messenger from the Lord of all worlds (7:61), and the sūrah's later accounts report the same exchange between Hūd and his people (7:66–67) and between Ṣāliḥ and the leaders of Thamūd. The charge and its answer are stated in the same words on both sides.

The Book's treatment of the accusation of error as a return of the accuser's own condition appears in its descriptions of the fate of the communities. The sūrah's record of the destroyed peoples states that the rulers were the ones who went astray, and the sūrah of the Cow reports the disbelievers calling the believers fools while the charge belongs to themselves (2:13). The verse in front of us states the accusation in its first instance, and the sūrah's account of the outcome settles the question.

**The Standing of the Accusers**

The men who brought the charge were the community's leaders, and their standing rested on the arrangements of the world rather than on any knowledge of the message. The sūrah's later narratives describe them counting their numbers, their wealth, and their fortifications, and the sūrah of the Heights states that the numbers and the arrogance of the condemned are of no use on the day (7:48). This verse shows the group whose opinion the community followed, and the sūrah's accounts of the destruction show the outcome of that following.

The Book's treatment of leadership in its narratives includes the leaders' appearance of weight in the community's affairs, and the sūrah of the Confederates reports the followers in the Hereafter saying that they obeyed their leaders and the leaders led them astray (33:67). The verse here supplies the first statement of that leadership in the sūrah, and the narrative that follows shows what it produced.

**Why the Leaders Refuse**

The Book reports the substance of the leaders' objection in other accounts of the same exchange, and the objections are drawn from the messenger's ordinary humanity, his following among the poor, and the disruption he proposes to the community's inherited practice. The sūrah of Hūd reports the chiefs saying that they saw the messenger as a man like themselves and that only the lowest among the people had followed him. The present verse states the conclusion that the leaders draw from those considerations.

The sūrah's later accounts of the communities develop the same objections, and each of them is answered in the course of the narrative. The messenger's humanity is answered by the statement that a messenger must be a man, the following of the poor is answered by the Book's account of the weighing of deeds, and the disruption of the inherited practice is answered by the accounts of what the practice produced.

**The Pattern of the Answer**

The verse's brevity states the pattern that the sūrah will repeat, and the pattern is as much the argument of the passage as the destruction that follows the refusal. The leaders' charge in front of us is met in the next verse by the messenger's denial and his identification of his office, and the sūrah then reports the substance of his call and the response to it. The refusal is presented as a judgment made without examination, and the narrative supplies the examination in the messenger's own words.

The Book's descriptions of the people who dismiss the message without considering it appear in several passages, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that some turn away from the signs as though they had not heard them. The verse under discussion supplies the first instance of the dismissal in the sūrah's history, and the accounts that follow trace its consequences.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixtieth in the chapter, and it follows the call of Noah and precedes his reply. The narrative then continues with the exchange between the messenger and his people, the building of the ark, and the destruction of the deniers in the flood (7:61–64). Here the verse supplies the first response of the community.

The reader who has been given the call to worship the one God now reads the answer of the leaders. The messenger is charged with error, the charge is answered in the next verse, and the account proceeds to the outcome that the sūrah's introduction has promised.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the reply of Noah, that he is not misguided and that he is a messenger from the Lord of all worlds (7:61). The charge brought by the notables in front of us is answered in the messenger's own words, and the passage then states what he was sent to convey.

The two verses together form the exchange that the sūrah's narratives repeat with each people. The messenger is accused, the messenger answers with his office, and the account proceeds to the substance of the message; and the verse that follows states the delivery and the advice that the messenger offers to the community that has rejected him.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:61

> He replied, “O my people! I am not misguided! But I am a messenger from the Lord of all worlds,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Denial of the Charge**

The verse reports the reply of Noah, and the reply begins by rejecting the charge that the notables brought against him. He states that he is not misguided, and the phrase answers the accusation in the terms in which it was made, so that the denial and the charge use the same word. The Book reports the same exchange in its other accounts of the prophets, and the sūrah's later narratives repeat the pattern with Hūd (7:67) and with Shuʿayb.

The reply's first sentence is a denial of a personal fault, and the second states the office that explains the messenger's conduct. The two halves belong together, since the message he brings is the reason his people consider him astray, and his identification of himself as a messenger is the answer to their judgment. The verse in front of us states the answer in the form of an address to the people, and the messenger's care for them is visible in the address itself.

**A Messenger From the Lord of All Worlds**

The verse states the messenger's office in the same words that the Book uses for the Prophet Muhammad and for the other messengers. The sūrah of the Poets reports Noah's words in almost the same terms (26:106–107), the sūrah of Hūd reports them from the same messenger, and the sūrah's own account of the prophet's title appears in its descriptions of the messengers of the peoples. The phrase Lord of all worlds identifies the sender as the Lord of everything rather than as a tribal deity.

The title in front of us answers the audience's understanding of religion as the property of a community. The people of Noah had their own traditions and their own claims about the divine, and the messenger's words place him in the service of the One who is the Lord of all worlds rather than of a particular people. The Book's use of the same title throughout its accounts of the messengers shows that the office was the same in each case.

**The Address to the People**

The reply begins with an address, and the messenger calls the people his own. The Book uses the same opening in the mouths of Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, and Shuʿayb, each of whom addresses his people as my people, and the sūrah's narratives present the messengers as men of the communities they were sent to. The address in front of us is therefore a statement of kinship as much as a form of speech, and the refusal of the people is a refusal of one of their own.

The Book's treatment of the messengers' humanity appears in the objections of the communities and in the answers that the messengers give. The sūrah of the Sand Dunes states that the Prophet is told to say that he is not the first messenger ever sent and that he follows only what is revealed to him (46:9), and the sūrah of the Prophets states that every messenger before him received the same revelation of the unity of God (21:25). The verse in front of us supplies the first of the sūrah's replies to the objection.

**The Structure of the Prophet Narratives**

The verse belongs to a structure that the sūrah's accounts repeat with each people, and the structure consists of the call, the refusal of the notables, the messenger's denial of the charge, the statement of his office, the description of his work, and the warning of the outcome. The fixed order is what makes the accounts a series rather than a collection of stories, and the verse in front of us supplies the third and fourth elements of the first account.

The Book's use of the same sequence in the sūrah of the Poets and the sūrah of Hūd shows that the arrangement is deliberate rather than accidental, and the repetition serves the sūrah's argument.
