Each community heard the same claim, made the same objection, and received the same answer, and the difference between the peoples lies in the outcome of their refusal rather than in the message they were offered.

**What the Verse Says About the Accusation**

The charge of the notables in the previous verse was that the messenger stood in clear error, and the reply in front of us separates the messenger's person from the message he carries. The people had judged the message by the standing of the man who brought it, and the answer states that the man is a messenger and that the message comes from the Lord of all worlds. The sūrah's later narratives show the same judgment being made by the notables of ʿĀd and Thamūd, and each of them receives the same answer.

The Book's treatment of the messengers' lack of worldly standing appears in several passages, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the objection that the message should have come to a man of importance in one of the two towns. The reply in front of us points away from the messenger's standing and toward the sender, and the sūrah's reader is given the criterion by which the messengers are to be judged.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixty-first in the chapter, and it answers the charge of the notables in the previous verse. The two verses that follow describe the work of the messenger and the astonishment of the people at the reminder that came to them through one of their own (7:62–63), and the account then proceeds to the denial and the flood. The verse in front of us supplies the messenger's own statement of his office.

The reader who heard the leaders dismiss the messenger now hears the messenger's answer. The denial of error is followed by the claim of the office, and the claim is the same one that the sūrah's other messengers make; and the verses that follow state what the office requires him to do.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the messenger conveys the messages of his Lord and gives sincere advice to his people and that he knows from God what they do not know (7:62). The statement of the office in front of us is followed by the description of its work, and the passage then records the messenger's appeal to the people's sense of wonder.

The two verses together state the messenger's position in full. He is not astray, he is sent by the Lord of all worlds, and his work among his people is the delivery of the message and the counsel that accompanies it; and the verse that follows states the knowledge that the delivery carries.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:62

> conveying to you my Lord’s messages and giving you ˹sincere˺ advice. And I know from Allah what you do not know.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Delivery of the Lord's Messages**

The verse states the messenger's work in two parts, the conveyance of his Lord's messages and the counsel he offers to his people, and it closes with the knowledge that he has been given. The Book describes the duty of the messengers as the delivery of what they were sent with, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran states that the messenger's duty is only to convey (3:20), while the sūrah of the Table restates the same limit more than once in a single passage. The verse in front of us places the delivery first, and the counsel follows it as the manner of the delivery.

The messages in the plural are the communications sent to the messenger over the course of his mission, and the Book's accounts of the prophets show the same pattern of successive revelation. The sūrah of Noah reports the messenger calling his people by night and by day and repeating his appeal in several forms, and the verse in front of us summarises his work under the two headings that the sūrah's other narratives also use.

**Sincere Advice to the People**

The second part of the messenger's work is counsel, and the Arabic term carries the sense of wishing well to the one advised without any mixture of self-interest. The sūrah's other narratives use the same word, and Hūd describes himself as the sincere adviser of his people (7:68), Ṣāliḥ says the same at the end of his account (7:79), and Shuʿayb repeats it when his people have been destroyed (7:93). The repetition places the term among the sūrah's fixed descriptions of the prophetic office.

The Book contrasts the counsel of the messengers with the concealment practiced by those who know the truth and hide it, and the sūrah of the Cow warns the people of the earlier books against covering what has been revealed. The counsel in front of us is offered to a people who have already dismissed the messenger, and the narrative presents the offer as continuing regardless of the response. The Book's teaching about the persistence of the messengers appears in its accounts of their repeated appeals to communities that refused them.

**What the Messenger Knows**

The verse closes with the statement that the messenger knows from God what the people do not know, and the knowledge refers to what has been revealed to him about the consequences of the people's conduct. The sūrah's narrative continues with the instruction to build the ark and with the news of the flood, and the knowledge in front of us is the content of that instruction. The Book states that the knowledge of the unseen belongs to God alone and that He discloses it only to a messenger whom He has approved (72:26–27).

The contrast in the verse is therefore between the messenger's knowledge of what is coming and the people's confidence in the permanence of their situation. The sūrah's accounts of the other communities repeat the contrast, and the sūrah of the Believers reports the people of Noah treating the messenger as a man possessed who should be endured (23:25). The verse in front of us states the knowledge that the people deny him, and the account that follows shows the arrival of what he knew.

**The Vocabulary of Counsel in the Sūrah**

The word for counsel recurs at the close of each of the sūrah's prophet narratives, and its position gives the series one of its fixed features. Hūd speaks it before his people are destroyed, Ṣāliḥ speaks it as he turns away from his, and Shuʿayb speaks it after the earthquake has taken his community. The repetition establishes that the messengers performed their duty completely and that the outcome belongs to the people who refused.

The Book's use of the term in its instructions to the believing community appears in its commands about advising one another, and the sūrah's narratives present the practice in its original form. The messenger in front of us advises a people who have accused him of error, and his counsel is stated in the same breath as the delivery of the message. The passage therefore pairs the two activities as a single work.

**The Messenger's Knowledge and Its Limits**

The verse's last clause attributes a knowledge to the messenger that his people lack, and the rest of the Book marks the limits of what a messenger knows. The sūrah of the Sand Dunes states that the Prophet is told to say that he does not know what will happen to him or to his people (46:9), and the sūrah of the Spider reports the demand for signs and the answer that signs belong to God and that the messenger is only a clear warner (29:50). The knowledge in front of us is therefore what has been revealed to him for the purpose of the warning.

The sūrah of the Family of Imran states that God does not leave the believers in their condition until He distinguishes the good from the evil, and it states that He does not disclose the unseen to anyone except a messenger whom He approves (3:179; 72:27). The verse in front of us states the arrangement in the messenger's own words, and the narrative that follows shows what the disclosure contained.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixty-second in the chapter, and it completes the answer that Noah gives to the charge of the notables. The verse that follows reports his appeal to the people's sense of wonder at the reminder that has come to them through a man of their own, and the narrative then proceeds to their denial and the flood (7:64). This verse states the work that the messenger performed and the knowledge that supported it.

The reader who heard the leader's charge and the messenger's denial now receives the account of what the messenger did among his people. He delivered the messages, he gave counsel, and he knew what the people did not; and the sūrah's next verse records the appeal he made on the basis of that knowledge.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse asks the people whether they find it astonishing that a reminder should come to them from their Lord through one of their own, warning them so that they may beware and perhaps receive mercy (7:63). The statement of the messenger's knowledge in front of us is followed by the appeal that addresses the people's astonishment, and the narrative then turns to their answer.

The two verses together state the messenger's position and his appeal. He knows what they do not know, and he asks them why the arrival of a warning through a man of their own community should surprise them; and the verses that follow record the refusal that ends the account.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:63

> Do you find it astonishing that a reminder should come to you from your Lord through one of your own, warning you, so you may beware and perhaps be shown mercy?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Astonishment at the Reminder**

The verse records the appeal of Noah to his people's sense of wonder, and the wonder it addresses is that a reminder should reach them through a man of their own community. The Book reports the same astonishment in several passages, and the sūrah of Qāf states that the deniers are astonished that a warner has come to them from among themselves (50:2), while the sūrah of Ṣād reports the same reaction and the accusation that follows it (38:4). The verse in front of us places the astonishment inside the narrative and answers it.

The phrasing of the appeal presents the objection as unreasonable rather than as a difficulty to be resolved. The messenger does not argue for his own fitness; he asks why the community should be surprised at the arrangement. The Book's treatment of the objection elsewhere connects it to the desire for a messenger of higher worldly standing, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the demand that the message should have come to a notable of one of the two towns.

**A Man From Among Yourselves**

The phrase names the feature of the arrangement that the people found hardest to accept, and the Book presents the mission of human messengers as a deliberate choice. The sūrah of the Prophets states that the messengers sent before the Prophet were men to whom revelation came and that they ate food and walked in the markets, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the objection of the notables that the messenger was a man like themselves. The verse in front of us states the same fact in the form of an appeal.

The choice of a messenger from the community serves the delivery of the message, and the Book states that every messenger was sent in the language of his people (14:4). The messenger's share in the life of his community means that the warning arrives without the barrier of a foreign tongue or a foreign custom, and the appeal in front of us treats that as a mercy rather than as a defect. The Book's later passages state the principle that the messengers were drawn from their own peoples.

**The Warning and Its Purpose**

The verse states that the messenger comes to warn, and the Book attaches the same purpose to the sending of the messengers throughout. The sūrah's opening verses described the Book as sent down so that the Prophet might warn by it (7:2), and the sūrah of the Family of Imran states that the messenger's duty is to deliver and to warn. The verse in front of us states the warning as the content of the mission, and the two clauses that follow describe its purpose.

The first purpose is that the people may beware, and the second is that they may receive mercy. The two are stated in sequence, and the Book presents the warning as the route to the mercy rather than as its opposite. The sūrah of the Ant reports the appeal of Ṣāliḥ to his people asking why they seek to hasten the torment rather than the grace that is before them (27:46), and the verse in front of us states the two outcomes in the order in which the messenger offers them.

**The Repetition of the Appeal in the Sūrah**

The appeal in front of us is repeated in the sūrah's later narratives, and Hūd's words to his people use almost the same form (7:69). The repetition is one of the features that ties the accounts together, and it presents the messengers as making the same offer to different communities. The Book's use of the same phrasing in its accounts of the prophets shows that the offer was not tailored to the people who received it.

The sūrah's arrangement makes the repetition part of the argument. The communities that were destroyed had heard the warning in the same terms as the audience in Makkah, and the sūrah's reader is placed in the position of a community that is being offered what the earlier peoples refused. The appeal in front of us supplies the offer in the mouth of the first messenger.

**The Mercy at the End of the Warning**

The verse closes with the possibility of mercy, and the Book attaches mercy to the response of the communities throughout its accounts. The sūrah's earlier verse on the response of the believers states that those who mend their conduct and are mindful have nothing to fear and will not grieve (7:35), and the sūrah's later narratives show what became of the communities that refused. The mercy in front of us is stated as a possibility rather than as a certainty, and the wording leaves the outcome with the people who hear the warning.

The Book's treatment of mercy as the purpose of the sending appears in the sūrah of the Prophets, which states that the Prophet was sent as a mercy to the worlds (21:107), and in the sūrah of the Stories, which describes the mission of Moses as a mercy for the people. This verse states the same purpose in the appeal of the first messenger, and the account that follows shows the community's response.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixty-third in the chapter, and it completes the speech of Noah before the report of the denial. The verse that follows states that the people rejected him and that God saved him and those with him in the ark while drowning the deniers (7:64), and the first of the sūrah's punishment accounts reaches its end. The verse here states the last appeal that the community refused.

The reader who has followed the narrative from the call through the charge and the reply now hears the final invitation. The reminder has come through one of the people, the warning has been given for their benefit, and the mercy has been offered; and the verse that follows records what the community did with all three.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the people rejected the messenger, that God saved him and those with him in the ark, and that He drowned those who denied the signs, describing them as a blind people (7:64). The appeal of the messenger in front of us is followed by the community's refusal and by the outcome that the sūrah's introduction has promised, and the next account begins after that.

The two verses together close the first narrative of the series. The messenger offers the warning and the mercy, the community rejects both, and the destruction follows; and the verse that follows states the rescue of the believers and the blindness of the people who would not see.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:64

> But they rejected him, so We saved him and those with him in the Ark, and drowned those who rejected Our signs. They were certainly a blind people.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Denial and Its Outcome**

The verse states that the people denied the messenger, that God saved him and those who were with him in the ark, and that those who denied the signs were drowned. The account of Noah in this sūrah reaches its end in a single sentence, and the two outcomes are stated without any description of the flood itself. The sūrah of the Moon supplies the missing detail in its account of the event, describing the opening of the gates of the sky, the pouring of the water, and the carrying of the ark on the waves.

The brevity of the verse matches the sūrah's method in its punishment accounts. The narratives are told for the pattern rather than for the scenery, and the details that are supplied concern the exchange between the messenger and his people. The verse in front of us states the conclusion that the sūrah's later accounts will repeat: the messenger and his followers are saved, and the community that denied the signs is destroyed.

**The Rescue of the Messenger and His Company**

The verse states that the messenger was saved along with those who were with him, and the Book presents the rescue of the messenger as a fixed feature of these accounts. The sūrah of the Cattle asks whether any would be destroyed except the wrongdoing people if the punishment came upon them suddenly or openly (6:47), and the sūrah's narratives of ʿĀd, Thamūd, Lot, and Shuʿayb all report the salvation of the messenger and the believers. The verse in front of us supplies the first instance of the pattern in the sūrah.

The company of the saved in the ark is described in the sūrah of Hūd, which reports the entry of the messenger's family and the believers into the vessel and the loss of one of the messenger's sons, who refused to board and was drowned. The sūrah of the Prohibition names the wife of Noah among those who disbelieved, and the two passages together show that the company of the saved was defined by belief rather than by kinship. The verse in front of us states the rescue without naming those who were left out, and the other accounts supply the detail.

**The Ark in the Book's Accounts**

The Book describes the ark in several passages, and the descriptions agree that the vessel was built by the messenger on God's instruction. The sūrah of the Moon states that it was made of planks and nails (54:13), and the sūrah of the Believers reports the instruction given to the messenger to build the vessel under the divine supervision and to take aboard a pair of every kind together with his family and the believers, and it states that those against whom the word had been passed would be left behind. This verse names the vessel and its company and passes on.

The ark in front of us serves the sūrah's argument about the reality of the warning. A community that had heard the call for years and refused it was removed from the world in a single event, and the vessel that carried the believers through the water was the evidence of the messenger's knowledge of what was coming. The Book's later accounts of the punishment of the communities present the same connection between the warning and the event.

**The Blindness of the People**

The verse closes with the statement that the community was a blind people, and the description names the condition that produced the denial. The Book uses blindness for the refusal to see the signs, and the sūrah of the Cattle describes the hearts and eyes that are covered, while the sūrah of the Pilgrimage states that the blind of the heart are the ones who can see with their eyes but not with their insight. The verse here states the condition of the community that watched the water rise.

The term in front of us also completes the contrast that the sūrah's account of the two kinds of land has supplied earlier in the chapter (7:58). The good land brings forth its produce and the poor land does not, and the community that refused the messenger is described as land that could not see what it received. The sūrah's next narratives apply the same diagnosis to the peoples that followed Noah.

**The Pattern Established**

The account in front of us establishes the shape that the sūrah's later narratives repeat, and the elements of the shape are the call to worship the one God, the refusal of the notables, the warning, the messenger's patience, the rescue, and the destruction. The sūrah of Hūd presents the same sequence for several peoples, and the sūrah of the Poets repeats it with the refrain that each people rejected the messengers. The present verse supplies the closing element of the first account.

The Book's use of the repeated sequence serves the audience's own situation. The Makkan listeners were being asked to see themselves in the position of the communities that had received a messenger and refused him, and the sūrah's series of narratives presents the outcome of that position without variation. The verse under discussion states the outcome for the first of those communities, and the next account begins immediately.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixty-fourth in the chapter, and it closes the account of Noah that began with the call in the fifty-ninth verse. The verse that follows opens the account of ʿĀd and their messenger Hūd (7:65), and the series continues in the same form. Here the verse supplies the conclusion of the first narrative and the model for the rest.

The reader who has followed the call, the charge, the reply, and the appeal now reads the outcome. The messenger is saved with his company, the deniers are drowned, and the community is described as blind; and the sūrah's following verses present the next people and its messenger with the same call and the same warning.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that to the people of ʿĀd God sent their brother Hūd, who called them to worship God alone and asked them whether they would not fear Him (7:65). The account of Noah ends and the second of the sūrah's narratives begins, and the call that Hūd makes is the same call that Noah made.

The two verses together carry the series forward. One community has been destroyed for its denial, and the messenger of the next people is introduced with the same message; and the verses that follow record the answer of the leaders of ʿĀd and the response of the messenger to their charge.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:65

> And to the people of ’Âd We sent their brother Hûd. He said, “O my people! Worship Allah—you have no other god except Him. Will you not then fear Him?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The People of ʿĀd and Their Messenger**

The verse begins the second of the sūrah's punishment accounts, and it names the people of ʿĀd and their messenger Hūd. The Book tells the story of this people in several passages, and the accounts run through the sūrah of Hūd (11:50–60), the sūrah of the Poets (26:123–139), the sūrah of the Sand Dunes (46:21–25), and the sūrah of the Moon (54:18–21). The verse in front of us introduces them in the same form that the previous account used for Noah.

The tradition identifies ʿĀd as a people of southern Arabia, and the sūrah of the Dawn names their city as Iram of the pillars and describes its like as not having been created in the lands (89:6–7). The audience of the sūrah knew the traditions of this people, and the Book's account in front of us assumes that knowledge. The narrative states the message and the response rather than the geography.

**The Brother Sent to His People**

The verse describes the messenger as the brother of the people, and the term establishes the kinship that the sūrah's narratives rely upon. The same word is used of Ṣāliḥ among Thamūd and of Shuʿayb among the people of Midian, and the sūrah of Hūd uses it in the opening of the ʿĀd account (11:50). The messengers are drawn from the communities they address, and the Book presents the choice as part of the mercy of the arrangement.

The description of the messenger as a brother also sets up the refusal that follows. The leaders who answer him in the next verse accuse him of folly and falsehood, and the charge is brought against a man of their own people rather than against a stranger. The sūrah's later account of the people of Lot and the people of Shuʿayb uses the same opening, and the frame is fixed across the series.

**The Call to Worship God Alone**

The message of Hūd is the message of Noah, and the verse repeats the words of the earlier account: worship God, for you have no god besides Him. The sūrah of Hūd reports the same call in its own account (11:50), and the sūrah of the Poets opens the ʿĀd narrative with the question of whether the people will fear God (26:123). The verse in front of us states the call in the form that the sūrah's readers have now met three times, and the repetition is the point.

The Book presents the unity of the message as the mark of the messengers, and the sūrah of the Prophets states that every messenger before the Prophet received the same revelation that there is no god but God (21:25). The call in front of us supplies the second instance in the sūrah's series, and the peoples of Ṣāliḥ and Shuʿayb receive it in their turn. What differs between the narratives is the community and its response, not the message.

**The Question About Fear**

The verse closes with the question of whether the people will fear God, and the question adds a note that the earlier account did not have in the same words. The sūrah of the Poets opens the ʿĀd narrative with the same question (26:123), and the sūrah's later verses attach the call to the fear of the punishment that follows the refusal. The question in front of us is therefore a warning in the form of an appeal.

The Book's use of the fear of God as the beginning of right conduct appears in its instructions to the community throughout, and the sūrah's opening verses have placed the fear of the reckoning at the centre of their account of the last things. The question in front of us states the disposition that the people lack, and the narrative that follows shows what its absence produced.

**The Pattern of the Second Account**

The verse repeats the structure of the first account with the names changed, and the repetition is one of the sūrah's deliberate effects. The call is the same, the address to the people is the same, and the answer of the leaders that follows is different only in its wording. The Book's arrangement of the narratives in series makes the history a single argument, and the verse in front of us supplies the first element of the second version.

The tradition transmits the account of ʿĀd in more than one form, and the sūrah of the Sand Dunes reports the messenger's warning to a people dwelling in the sand hills and his reminder of the favours they had received. The verse in front of us states the call in the fewest words, and the later verses of the sūrah's account will add the reminders of what the people had been given.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixty-fifth in the chapter, and it begins the account of ʿĀd immediately after the conclusion of the account of Noah. The verses that follow report the answer of the leaders, the reply of the messenger, and the reminder of the favours that the people had received (7:66–69). This verse supplies the opening of the second narrative in the series.

The reader who has seen the first community destroyed now meets the second with the same message. The messenger is a brother of the people, the call is the worship of the one God, and the question about fear is put in the same form; and the sūrah's next verse records the answer that the leaders of the people gave.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the disbelieving leaders of the people said that they considered the messenger foolish and counted him among the liars (7:66). The call in front of us is followed by the answer of the notables, and the reply of the messenger comes in the verse after that.

The two verses together repeat the exchange of the first account. The messenger calls the people to the worship of God alone and asks whether they will fear Him, and the leaders answer with the two charges of folly and falsehood; and the sūrah's next verses state the messenger's response and the reminder he gives his people.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:66

> The disbelieving chiefs of his people responded, “We surely see you as a fool, and we certainly think you are a liar.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Leaders Who Disbelieved**

The verse reports the answer of the leading men of ʿĀd, and it identifies them as those who disbelieved before it reports what they said. The Book's accounts of the communities consistently place the refusal with the notables, and the sūrah's earlier narrative reported the same group answering Noah in almost the same terms (7:60). The verse in front of us supplies the second instance of the pattern, and the identification of the speakers as the disbelievers states their condition before their words are given.

The term for the leaders indicates the men whose standing in the community gave their opinion its weight, and the Book's narratives present them as the ones who answered for the people. The sūrah of the Believers reports the disbelieving chiefs of Noah's people dismissing the messenger, and the sūrah's later accounts of Thamūd name the arrogant leaders among those who rejected the warning (7:75). The verse in front of us states the composition of the group that refused Hūd.

**The Charge of Folly**

The first charge is that the messenger is foolish, and the term describes a deficiency of understanding rather than a moral fault. The sūrah's earlier account reported the leaders of Noah's people charging him with manifest error (7:60), and the charge in front of us escalates to a defect in the messenger's mind. The Book's narratives show the same escalation in other accounts, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the people of Noah calling their messenger possessed.

The charge is answered in the next verse by the messenger's denial in the same words, and the answer separates his person from his office in the manner of the earlier account. The Book's use of the same exchange in each narrative shows that the accusations were made without substance, and the sūrah's reader is given the messenger's denial before the account proceeds to the reminder of the favours that the people had received.

**The Charge of Falsehood**

The second charge is that the messenger is counted among the liars, and the phrase describes him as one of a known class rather than as a single offender. The Book's accounts of the communities use the same accusation against the messengers, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the people of ʿĀd rejecting their messenger in the same manner. The verse in front of us states the two charges together, and the pairing covers both the messenger's judgment and his honesty.

The charges are presented as the opinion of the leaders rather than as a demonstration, and the phrasing states that they think and consider rather than that they know. The Book's descriptions of the opponents' arguments frequently note the absence of evidence, and the sūrah of the Cattle asks what knowledge the audience possesses for its claims. This verse supplies the form of an accusation that rests on impression, and the messenger's reply states what he actually is.

**The Reversal of the Charge**

The Book's accounts of the communities turn the charges of the leaders against the leaders themselves, and the sūrah's record of the destroyed peoples states that those who were destroyed were the wrongdoers. The sūrah of the Cow reports the disbelievers calling the believers fools while the description belongs to those who made it (2:13), and the sūrah of the Heights' later verses state that those who were taken by the earthquake were the ones who had denied their messenger. The verse here states the accusation, and the narrative supplies the reversal.

The Book's teaching about the judgment of people by their standing rather than by their message appears throughout the sūrah, and the leaders in front of us judge the messenger by what they consider his social place. The sūrah's next verses state the messenger's denial and then his reminder to the people of what they had been given, and the reminder is the evidence that the leaders had not examined.

**The Reply That Follows**

The messenger's answer in the next verse denies the folly and claims the office of a messenger from the Lord of all worlds (7:67), and the reply uses the structure of the answer that Noah gave to the same kind of charge. The sūrah's accounts keep the exchange brief, and the brevity places the weight of the narrative on the reminders and the warnings that follow. The present verse states the accusation that the reply addresses.

The Book's descriptions of the messengers' responses to abuse include patience and persistence, and the sūrah of Hūd reports the messenger continuing his appeal after the leaders had dismissed him. The verse under discussion supplies the occasion for that persistence, and the account that follows shows the messenger turning from the leaders to the people with the reminder of their own history.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixty-sixth in the chapter, and it follows the call of Hūd and precedes his reply. The verses that follow report the reply, the reminder of the favours the people had received, and the challenge that the leaders issue to the messenger (7:67–70). Here the verse supplies the answer of the second community to the second call in the sūrah's series.

The reader who has seen the leaders of Noah's people charge their messenger with error now sees the leaders of ʿĀd charge theirs with folly and falsehood. The pattern is the same, the words are harsher, and the account proceeds in the same direction; and the sūrah's next verses state the reply of the messenger and the terms of the argument that follows.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the reply of Hūd, that there is no folly in him and that he is a messenger from the Lord of all worlds (7:67). The charge in front of us is answered in the messenger's own words, and the account then continues with the description of his work and with the reminder of the favours that his people had received.

Together the two verses mark the point at which the preaching of Hūd meets the reception that Noah's had met. The leaders dismiss the messenger, the messenger states his office, and the argument proceeds to the evidence; and the sūrah's following verses carry the second account to its conclusion in the destruction of the people.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:67

> Hûd replied, “O my people! I am no fool! But I am a messenger from the Lord of all worlds,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Denial of the Charge**

The verse reports the reply of Hūd, and the reply begins with the denial of the charge that the leaders had brought against him in the previous verse. He states that there is no folly in him, and the denial answers the accusation in the terms in which it was made. The sūrah's earlier account contains the same structure, since Noah answers the charge of error with the statement that he is not misguided (7:61). The verse in front of us repeats the exchange with the second messenger.

The denial is stated as a fact about the messenger's person, and the second half of the sentence supplies what he actually is. The two clauses belong together in the way they did in the first account, and the sūrah's arrangement places the messengers' self-description at the point where the community's judgment has been delivered. The Book's narratives use the same answer in each case, and the repetition establishes the office rather than the individual.

**A Messenger From the Lord of All Worlds**

The verse states the messenger's office in the words that the sūrah has used for Noah, and the title of the sender is the same in both accounts. The sūrah of Hūd reports the same identification in its own version of the exchange (11:50), and the sūrah of the Poets presents Hūd among the messengers who were rejected by their people. The phrase Lord of all worlds in front of us states that the mission belongs to the Lord of everything rather than to a tribal patron.

The title carries the argument of the sūrah in miniature. The audience is asked to worship the One whose lordship covers everything, and the messenger presents himself as the servant of that Lord. The Book's use of the same title for the sending of each messenger shows that the communities were addressed by the same authority, and the sūrah's series of narratives makes the point by repetition.

**The Shared Structure of the Two Answers**

The reply in front of us is the reply of Noah with the names changed, and the similarity is one of the sūrah's deliberate effects. The same denial, the same claim, and the same description of the work follow in each account, and the sūrah's later narratives of Ṣāliḥ and Shuʿayb use the same form. The Book's presentation of the messengers as a single company with a single message is the argument that the series carries.

The tradition of the community preserves the distinguishing features of each prophet, and the sūrah's narratives supply them in the reminders that each messenger gives his people. Hūd reminds his people of their establishment in the land and of their increase in stature (7:69), and the reminder in front of us is followed by that history. The reply states the office, and the reminder states the evidence.

**What the Messenger Does Not Claim**

The reply claims nothing for the messenger beyond the office, and the Book states the limits of the messengers' claims in other passages. The sūrah of the Sand Dunes states that the Prophet is told to say that he does not know what will happen to him or to his people and that he follows only what is revealed to him (46:9), and the sūrah of the Spider reports the answer that the messenger is only a clear warner (29:50). The verse in front of us states the claim and stops there.

The restraint in front of us answers the leaders' charge by refusing the terms of it. The leaders had judged the messenger as a man seeking standing in the community, and the reply places him in an office that carries no standing of its own. The Book's accounts of the messengers' poverty and their treatment by their peoples state the same fact in the language of the narratives, and the sūrah's later verses add the reminders that the people had received.

**The Reply in the Sūrah's Series**

The verse belongs to the fixed structure of the sūrah's punishment accounts, and the structure has now appeared twice in the same form. The call, the refusal of the leaders, the charge, and the reply repeat, and the effect of the repetition is to turn the history of the communities into a single account of the response to one message. The verse in front of us supplies the fourth element of the second version.

The Book's arrangement of the series in the sūrah of Hūd and the sūrah of the Poets follows the same order, and the audience of the revelation was familiar with the pattern. The sūrah's use of it in front of us places the Makkan listeners in the line of the communities that had answered the call in the same manner, and the sūrah's later verses state the reminders that the messengers gave and the destruction that followed the refusal.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixty-seventh in the chapter, and it answers the charge of the leaders in the previous verse. The verse that follows states the messenger's work of delivery and counsel (7:68), and the passage then reports the reminder he gives his people of the favours they had received (7:69). This verse supplies the reply at the centre of the second account.

The reader who heard the leaders of ʿĀd dismiss their messenger now hears the messenger's answer. The folly is denied, the office is claimed, and the account proceeds to the description of the work; and the sūrah's next verse states what the messenger does among the people who have rejected him.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the messenger conveys his Lord's messages and that he is the sincere adviser of his people (7:68). The claim of the office in front of us is followed by the description of its exercise, and the account then turns to the reminder of the favours that the people had received.

The two verses together state the messenger's position and his work. He is not foolish but sent, and what he brings is the message of his Lord together with the counsel that belongs to it; and the sūrah's next verses carry the second account towards its conclusion in the destruction of the people.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:68

> conveying to you my Lord’s messages. And I am your sincere advisor.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Delivery of the Message**

The verse states that the messenger conveys the messages of his Lord, and the description repeats the account of Noah's work in the same sūrah (7:62). The Book presents the delivery of the revelation as the substance of the prophetic office, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran states that the messenger's duty is only to convey (3:20), while the sūrah of the Table repeats the same limit twice. The verse in front of us places the delivery before the counsel, and the order states which of the two is the foundation.

The messages in the plural are the successive communications that the messenger received, and the sūrah's narratives present the prophets as transmitters rather than as authors. The Book's accusations against those who conceal the message belong to the same understanding, and the sūrah of the Cow warns the people of the earlier books against covering what has been revealed. The verse in front of us states the work that the messenger performs among a people who have called him a liar.

**The Sincere Adviser**

The second half of the verse describes the messenger as a sincere adviser, and the phrase repeats the term that the sūrah has used for Noah's counsel (7:62) and will use again for Ṣāliḥ (7:79) and Shuʿayb (7:93). The Arabic term for the adviser describes one who wishes the good of the person advised without any mixture of self-interest, and the word for sincerity in front of us adds the sense of purity and unmixed intention. The two terms together state a counsel that seeks nothing from the one who receives it.

The Book's treatment of counsel among the believers appears in its instructions to the community, and the sūrah of the Declining Day states that the people who are not at a loss are those who urge one another to the truth and to perseverance (103:3). The verse in front of us supplies the pattern of that practice at the level of a messenger addressing a whole community, and the sūrah's narratives repeat the pattern with each people.

**Why the Counsel Is Stated After the Charge**

The messenger states his sincerity immediately after his people have accused him of folly and falsehood, and the juxtaposition gives the verse its force. The charge denied his honesty; the statement in front of us asserts it as the purpose of his work. The sūrah's accounts of the communities repeatedly place the false accusation next to the messenger's true description of himself, and the arrangement lets the reader see who is speaking the truth.

The Book's descriptions of the messengers' patience under abuse appear in its accounts of the prophets who continued their warning after being dismissed, and the sūrah of Hūd reports Hūd continuing his appeal to the people after the leaders had rejected him. This verse states the motive that sustained the continuation, and the narrative then turns to the reminder that the messenger gives his people.

**The Counsel and the Community's Answer**

The Book's accounts of the communities show the counsel being refused in the same terms that the leaders used, and the sūrah's record of the destruction states the outcome of the refusal. The sūrah of the Heights will report the messenger turning away from his people at the end of the account with the statement that he conveyed the message and gave the counsel and that the people did not like advisers (7:79; 7:93). The verse here supplies the middle of the sequence, and the closing statement of the messenger comes at the end of the account.

The rejection in front of us is therefore a rejection of the counsel as well as of the message, and the Book treats the two as a single offer. The messenger's work consisted of the warning and the well-wishing that accompanied it, and the community's refusal covered both. The present verse states the full extent of what was offered before the sūrah reports the exchange that follows.

**The Sincerity the Book Admires**

The Book's use of sincerity as a virtue appears in its descriptions of the worship that is offered to God alone, and the sūrah of the Clear Proof describes the command given to the people of the earlier books as the worship of God with sincere devotion. The term in front of us applies the same quality to the counsel that is offered to people, and the verse therefore joins the two directions of the sincere life: toward God and toward one's community.

The sūrah's contemporaries knew the practice of advice in their tribal affairs, and the messenger's claim in front of us uses a term they understood. The Book's later instructions to the community adopt the practice for the religion, and the sūrah of the Declining Day makes the mutual urging of truth and patience the mark of the saved. The verse under discussion supplies the model in the prophetic mission.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixty-eighth in the chapter, and it completes the messenger's reply to the leaders of ʿĀd. The verse that follows reports his reminder to the people of the favours they had received, and the account then proceeds through the challenge of the leaders to the destruction of the community (7:70–72). Here the verse states the work that the messenger performed before the argument reached its end.

The reader who heard the leaders charge the messenger with folly and falsehood now hears him describe his own work. He delivers the messages and he counsels sincerely, and the people's answer to both is recorded in the verses that follow; and the sūrah's next verse states the reminder with which he turned from the leaders to the community.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse asks the people whether they find it astonishing that a reminder should come to them from their Lord through a man of their own, and it reminds them that God made them successors after the people of Noah and increased them in stature, closing with the command to remember the favours of God so that they may succeed (7:69). The statement of the messenger's work in front of us is followed by the reminder that the work contained, and the account then reports the leaders' challenge.

The two verses together state the messenger's approach to his people. He conveys and counsels, and then he reminds them of what they have been given; and the sūrah's following verses state the demand that the leaders made and the destruction that answered their refusal.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:69

> Do you find it astonishing that a reminder should come to you from your Lord through one of your own so he may warn you? Remember that He made you successors after the people of Noah and increased you greatly in stature. So remember Allah’s favours, so you may be successful.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Astonishment Repeated**

The verse opens with the same question that the account of Noah used, asking the people whether they find it astonishing that a reminder should come to them from their Lord through one of their own. The Book reports the astonishment of the deniers in other passages as well, and the sūrah of Qāf states that they are astonished that a warner has come to them from among themselves (50:2), while the sūrah of Ṣād reports the same reaction (38:4). The verse in front of us repeats the appeal that the earlier messenger made, and the repetition carries the series forward.

The question is followed by its purpose clause, that the messenger may warn the people, and the warning is the reminder that the Book attaches to the sending of every prophet. The sūrah's account of Noah used the same form with the mention of the mercy that may follow (7:63), and the verse in front of us substitutes the reminder of the people's own history. The two verses together state the offer and the evidence in the same breath.

**Successors After the People of Noah**

The verse reminds the people that God made them successors after the people of Noah, and the term for succession is the one the sūrah uses in its later account of Thamūd, where the same people are told that they were made successors after ʿĀd (7:74). The Book's use of the term for the peoples that replaced the destroyed communities appears throughout its narratives, and the sūrah's opening section stated that God had placed the audience as successors on the earth (6:165). The verse in front of us applies the description to the people of ʿĀd.

The reminder serves the argument that the sūrah is making. The people of ʿĀd had taken the place of the community that was destroyed for its denial, and the messenger's words in front of us state that the same arrangement has placed them where they stand. The Book's later accounts use the same reminder with the peoples that followed, and the series of the replacements is one of the sūrah's recurring themes.

**The Increase in Stature**

The verse reminds the people of their increase in stature, and the Book attaches the same feature to the people of ʿĀd in its other accounts. The sūrah of the Dawn describes Iram of the pillars as a city whose like was not created in the lands (89:6–7), and the sūrah of Fussilat reports their boasting about their might (41:15). The verse in front of us states the physical advantage as one of the favours for which the people were to be grateful.

The description of the people in the tradition includes their height and the strength of their buildings, and the sūrah's later account of Thamūd mentions the palaces they built on the plains and the homes they carved in the mountains (7:74). The Book's narratives use the achievements of the peoples as evidence against them, since the same strength that allowed the building was the thing they trusted instead of their Lord. This verse states the gift and the reminder together.

**The Command to Remember the Favours**

The verse closes with the command to remember the favours of God so that the people may succeed, and the Book attaches the command to the same contexts in its narratives. The sūrah's later account of Thamūd repeats the command after naming the palaces and the carved homes (7:74), and the sūrah of the Sand Dunes reports the messenger reminding the people of the favours that had been given to them. The verse here states the command in the mouth of Hūd, and the argument then proceeds to the leaders' challenge.

The success named at the end of the verse is the same outcome that the sūrah's opening verses attached to the weighing of deeds (7:8), and the connection places the reminder within the framework the chapter has established. The people are told that the recognition of the favours is the route to the success they seek, and the narrative that follows shows what they chose instead.

**The Favours of ʿĀd in the Book's Wider Account**

The Book gives a fuller account of the position of the people of ʿĀd in the sūrah of the Sand Dunes, where the messenger is reported to have warned a people dwelling in the sand hills and to have reminded them that God had established them in the land as He had not established others, giving them hearing, sight, and hearts (46:26). The passage states the extent of what they had received and then states that their faculties did not profit them when they denied the signs of God. The present verse supplies the shorter form of the same reminder in this sūrah.

The Book's treatment of the peoples that were given power and then destroyed is one of the sūrah's arguments for the audience's own situation. The Makkan community was proud of its standing among the tribes and its control of the sanctuary, and the reminder in front of us states what became of a people whose standing was greater in the things the audience valued. The sūrah's next verses state the demand that the leaders made of the messenger and the end of the community.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands sixty-ninth in the chapter, and it follows the statement of the messenger's work and precedes the challenge of the leaders. The verses that follow report the demand for the punishment to be brought and the messenger's answer, and the account of ʿĀd closes with the destruction of the people (7:70–72). The verse under discussion supplies the reminder that the account of the second people contains.

The reader who has heard the messenger describe his work now reads the reminder with which he addressed the community. The people are successors of a destroyed community, they have been increased in stature, and they are commanded to remember what they were given; and the sūrah's next verses state the answer that the leaders returned.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the people asked whether the messenger had come to make them worship God alone and abandon what their forefathers worshipped, and they demanded that he bring upon them what he threatened if he was truthful (7:70). The reminder of the favours in front of us is followed by the challenge of the leaders, and the account then turns to the messenger's answer and the end of the people.

The two verses together state the exchange that the sūrah's narratives repeat. The messenger reminds the people of what they have been given, and the leaders answer with the appeal to their ancestors and with the demand for proof; and the verses that follow carry the second account to its conclusion.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:70

> They said, “Have you come to us so that we would worship Allah alone and abandon what our forefathers used to worship? Then bring us what you threaten us with, if what you say is true!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Challenge to the Messenger**

The verse reports the answer of the people to the reminder of the previous verse, and the answer states what the messenger's call would require of them: the worship of God alone and the abandonment of what their forefathers worshipped. The objection is stated as a description of the demand rather than as an argument against it, and the phrasing tells the reader what the community valued. The verse in front of us supplies the reason for the refusal in the speakers' own words.

The appeal to the practice of the forefathers is the same one that the sūrah has already addressed in its passage on the shameful deeds (7:28), and the Book reports it from the disbelievers in several passages, including the sūrah of the Cow (2:170), the sūrah of the Table (5:104), and the sūrah of Luqmān (31:21). The verse in front of us places the appeal in the mouth of a community within the sūrah's narratives, and the answer that follows states the ground on which it is rejected.

**The Demand for the Punishment**

The second half of the verse reports the demand that the messenger bring upon the people what he had threatened, if his claim is true, and the demand appears in the sūrah's other narratives. The sūrah of the Sand Dunes reports the people of ʿĀd asking their messenger whether he had come to turn them away from their gods and calling on him to bring the punishment if he was truthful (46:22), and the sūrah of Yūnus and the sūrah of the Prophets report the same challenge from the audience of the Prophet. The verse in front of us states the challenge at the close of the second warning.

The demand treats the warning as a claim to be tested rather than as a warning to be heeded, and the Book's answer to it appears in several passages. The sūrah of the Prophets states that the people would not believe the signs even if they saw them, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the audience's demand for a sign and the answer that the signs belong to God. This verse supplies the challenge that the messenger answers in the next verse.

**The Appeal to the Forefathers**

The worship that the people refuse to abandon is described as what their forefathers worshipped, and the Book treats the appeal to ancestry as a recurring obstacle in its accounts of the communities. The sūrah's earlier passage answered the same appeal with the statement that God never commands what is shameful and with the question of how the people attribute to God what they do not know (7:28). The verse here states the appeal in its simplest form, and the next verse names the objects of the ancestral worship.

The commentary tradition notes that the ancestral practice in the case of ʿĀd included the worship of idols and of natural forces, and the sūrah of Noah names five idols that the people of that messenger worshipped. The present verse states the community's attachment in terms of inheritance rather than of conviction, and the answer that follows addresses the practice rather than the ancestry. The Book's argument in both cases is that a practice does not acquire authority from repetition.

**The Two Halves of the Refusal**

The verse joins two elements that the sūrah's narratives keep together: the refusal to change the inherited practice and the demand for immediate proof. The two belong to the same position, since the community that will not reconsider its customs asks for an event that would make reconsideration unnecessary. The Book's descriptions of the demand for signs connect it to the refusal to accept the message on the terms on which it is offered, and the sūrah's answer states the consequence of the demand.

The Book's treatment of the request for the punishment appears in its accounts of the audience's challenge to the Prophet, where the demand is answered with the statement that the term belongs to God. The verse under discussion places the same demand in the narrative of ʿĀd, and the answer of the messenger in the next verse states what the demand will produce. The community's challenge is thereby turned back on itself, and the narrative that follows states the outcome.

**The Recurring Pattern of the Refusal**

The verse repeats the pattern of the refusal that the sūrah's accounts establish, and the pattern now includes the appeal to the ancestors and the demand for proof. The first account supplied the charge of error, and the second adds the two elements that the sūrah's audience in Makkah used in its own resistance. The Book's arrangement of the narratives in series makes the audience's arguments part of the history of the earlier peoples.

The sūrah's later accounts of Thamūd and of the people of Shuʿayb repeat the refusal in their own terms, and the sūrah of Hūd reports the same arguments in its versions of the narratives. Here the verse supplies the answer of ʿĀd, and the sūrah's reader is shown that the arguments against the message have a history and an outcome.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventieth in the chapter, and it follows the reminder of the favours and precedes the reply of the messenger. The verses that follow state the messenger's answer and the destruction of the people (7:71–72), and the second account then gives way to the third. This verse supplies the community's final answer to the call.

The reader who has heard the reminder of what the people had received now reads their response to the messenger who brought it. The worship of God alone is refused because it would require the abandonment of the ancestral practice, and the proof is demanded in the form of the punishment itself; and the sūrah's next verse states the messenger's answer and the terms on which the demand is met.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the reply of Hūd, that the people were already subject to the wrath and torment of their Lord, that their gods were names invented by them and their forefathers with no authority from God, and that he would wait with them for the outcome (7:71). The challenge in front of us is answered with the statement that the punishment is not a proof to be summoned but a consequence already in motion, and the account then proceeds to its end.

The two verses together state the last exchange between the messenger and his people. The community asks for the punishment as a test of the message, and the messenger states that the terms of the test have long been settled; and the sūrah's following verses record the rescue of the believers and the removal of the people who denied the signs.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:71

> He said, “You will certainly be subjected to your Lord’s torment and wrath. Do you dispute with me regarding the so-called gods which you and your forefathers have made up —a practice Allah has never authorized? Then wait! I too am waiting with you.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Punishment Already Decreed**

The verse reports the answer of Hūd to the demand of his people, and the answer states that the torment and the wrath of their Lord have already fallen due upon them. The messenger does not refuse the challenge; he states that the matter the people wish to hasten is not a proof to be produced but a consequence that has been established by their own conduct. The sūrah's account of the demand is thereby answered in the terms that the Book uses in its other narratives, and the sūrah of Yūnus reports the instruction to tell the audience to consider what would follow if the torment came upon them by night or by day (10:50).

The reply reverses the position that the people had taken. They had asked the messenger to produce the punishment, and the answer states that the punishment has been their portion since they made their choice. The Book's narratives of the communities present the same arrangement in each case, and the sūrah's later accounts of the destruction of the peoples state the arrival of what had been decreed.

**The Gods That Were Invented**

The second element of the answer concerns the objects of the ancestral worship, and the messenger describes them as names that the people and their forefathers had made up. The phrase states that the deities in question have no existence beyond the naming, and the Book uses the same argument in its accounts of the idols. The sūrah of Noah names five of the idols that the people of that messenger worshipped, and the sūrah of the Star states that the names the audience uses for its goddesses are nothing but names that the people and their forefathers invented.

The claim that the practice was never authorized by God appears in the sūrah's earlier passage on the forbidden things, where the audience is told that God has sent down no authority for the partners it associates with Him (7:33). The verse in front of us applies the same statement to the worship of ʿĀd, and the sūrah's narratives repeat it with the other communities. The argument is not that the practice is unfamiliar but that it has no warrant.

**The Appeal to the Ancestors Answered**

The answer in front of us closes the appeal to the forefathers that the previous verse reported, and it turns the appeal into the ground of the charge. The ancestors invented the names, and the descendants inherited the invention; the practice therefore has a human origin and no divine authorization. The sūrah's earlier rejection of the ancestral argument used the same reasoning when it stated that God never commands what is shameful (7:28), and the Book repeats the argument in the sūrah of the Ornament, where the disbelievers say that they found their forefathers on a way and are following in their footsteps (43:22).

The Book's treatment of the argument from inheritance appears in its accounts of the communities that were destroyed, and the sūrah's narratives present each of them relying on the practice of their fathers. The verse in front of us states the answer that the messengers gave, and the sūrah's reader is shown that the argument was made and answered in every case.

**The Waiting**

The verse closes with the messenger's statement that he will wait with his people for the outcome, and the phrase turns the challenge back on the challengers. The Book uses the language of waiting in its accounts of the audience that demanded the punishment, and the sūrah of the Prophets reports the question of when the threat would come to pass and the instruction to the Prophet to say that he is waiting with them (21:38). The verse in front of us places the same exchange in the narrative of ʿĀd.

The waiting in front of us is not a threat of the messenger's own making, and the Book states that the term belongs to God. The sūrah's opening verses on the reckoning have established that nothing is advanced or delayed beyond its term (7:34), and the verse in front of us applies the principle to the community that asks for the punishment to be brought forward. The answer states that the term is running and that the messenger will wait for its arrival with the people who asked.

**The Argument of the Reply**

The reply in front of us gathers the sūrah's arguments against the audience's religion and states them in the messenger's own voice. The idols are names without authority, the ancestral practice has a human origin, the punishment is the consequence of the refusal rather than an object of proof, and the outcome will arrive at its term. The Book's narratives of the communities repeat the same set of arguments, and the sūrah's account of ʿĀd supplies the first full statement of them.

The reply's function in the narrative is to close the exchange and to leave the outcome to the event. The messenger has stated the message, given the counsel, reminded the people of the favours, and answered the challenge; and the account that follows reports what the term brought. This verse supplies the last speech of the messenger before the destruction, and the sūrah's next verse states the rescue and the removal.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventy-first in the chapter, and it answers the challenge of the previous verse. The verse that follows closes the account of ʿĀd with the rescue of the messenger and his companions by mercy and the uprooting of those who denied the signs (7:72), and the third of the sūrah's narratives then begins. The verse here supplies the conclusion of the argument with the second people.

The reader who heard the community demand the punishment now hears the messenger state that the demand has been answered by their own conduct. The gods are names, the practice is inherited, and the term is running; and the sūrah's next verse reports the rescue of the believers and the end of the people who refused.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that God saved the messenger and those with him by mercy and uprooted those who denied the signs, and that they were not believers (7:72). The waiting of the messenger in front of us is followed by the arrival of the term, and the account of ʿĀd reaches its end in the same manner as the account of Noah.

The two verses together complete the second narrative of the series. The argument is closed, the term arrives, the believers are saved, and the people who denied the signs are removed from the earth; and the sūrah's following verses introduce the people of Thamūd and their messenger Ṣāliḥ.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:72

> So We saved him and those with him by Our mercy and uprooted those who denied Our signs. They were not believers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rescue by Mercy**

The verse states that God saved the messenger and those with him by mercy, and the phrase attributes the rescue to the divine mercy rather than to the strength of the saved. The sūrah of Hūd reports the same event in the same terms, stating that when the command came, God rescued Hūd and those who believed with him by a mercy from Him and saved them from a harsh punishment (11:58). The verse in front of us states the rescue in the shortest form, and the account of the second community is closed in the same manner as the first.

The rescue of the messenger and his companions is one of the fixed elements of the sūrah's punishment accounts, and the sequence of the narratives presents it in each case. The sūrah of the Cattle asks whether any would be destroyed except the wrongdoing people if the punishment came upon them suddenly or openly (6:47), and the sūrah's accounts of Noah, ʿĀd, Thamūd, Lot, and Shuʿayb all report the salvation of the believers. The verse in front of us supplies the second instance of the pattern.

**The Uprooting of the Deniers**

The verse states that those who denied the signs were uprooted, and the term describes a cutting off that leaves nothing behind. The Book applies the same image to the peoples that were destroyed, and the sūrah of the Dawn reports the fate of Iram and states that nothing of the city was left. The punishment of ʿĀd is described in several passages: the sūrah of the Moon reports a furious wind sent against them on a day of unrelenting misery (54:19), the sūrah of the Inevitable reports the same wind unleashed for seven nights and eight days so that the people were left lying like hollow palm trunks (69:6–7), and the sūrah of Fussilat reports the wind of punishment that destroyed them in the days of misfortune (41:16).

The descriptions in front of us supply the detail that the sūrah's brief account omits, and the two accounts together show what the uprooting meant. The people who had been increased in stature and had built on the plains were removed by a wind, and the sūrah of the Inevitable's image of the hollow trunks states the completeness of the removal. The verse in front of us states the outcome in a single word.

**The Closing Description of the People**

The verse closes with the statement that the people were not believers, and the description supplies the reason for what happened to them. The sūrah's accounts of the communities repeat the same verdict at the end of each narrative, and the sūrah of Noah's account stated that the people who were drowned were blind (7:64). This verse states the condition that produced the destruction, and the account of the second people ends on the same note as the first.

The phrase places the outcome on the response of the community rather than on any defect in the message it received. The sūrah's narratives have stated the call, the reminder, the argument, and the warning, and the closing sentence in front of us states what the community did with all of them. The sūrah's reader is left with the correspondence between the refusal and the end.

**The Series and Its Argument**

The verse completes the second version of the pattern that the sūrah's accounts follow, and the pattern's repetition is the argument that the chapter is making. Each community received the same message, refused it in its own words, and was removed from the earth; and the audience of the sūrah is placed in the position of the peoples whose histories it is hearing. The Book's arrangement of the narratives in the sūrah of Hūd and the sūrah of the Poets uses the same method, and the sūrah in front of us has now shown the pattern twice.

The Book's use of the history of the earlier peoples as evidence for the present audience appears throughout its addresses, and the sūrah of the Romans tells the audience to travel through the earth and see the end of those who denied. The verse here supplies the second demonstration in the sūrah, and the third is introduced in the following verses with the account of Thamūd.

**The Salvation of the Believers in Every Account**

The verse states that the rescue covered the messenger and those with him, and the Book's narratives consistently extend the salvation to the companions who accepted the message. The sūrah of the Heights will report the rescue of Ṣāliḥ and his followers and the destruction of the people of Thamūd, and the account of Lot reports the rescue of his household except for his wife. The pattern in front of us states that the division at the punishment is the division of belief, and the sūrah's narratives present the two groups in each community.

The Book's teaching about the community of believers as the remnant that is saved appears in its descriptions of the messengers' followers, and the sūrah's accounts name the believing companions of each prophet. The present verse states the second instance of that arrangement, and the sūrah's following narratives carry the same division forward.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventy-second in the chapter, and it closes the account of ʿĀd that began with the call of Hūd (7:65). The verses that follow introduce the people of Thamūd and their messenger Ṣāliḥ (7:73), and the series continues in the same order. The verse under discussion supplies the conclusion of the second narrative and the model for the third.

The reader who has followed the call, the refusal, the argument, and the demand now reads the outcome. The messenger and his companions are saved by mercy, the deniers are removed, and the community is described by its unbelief; and the sūrah's next verses present the third people and its messenger with the same call and the same warning.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that to the people of Thamūd God sent their brother Ṣāliḥ, who called them to worship God alone and produced the she-camel as a sign, warning them not to harm her (7:73). The account of ʿĀd gives way to the account of the third people, and the call that Ṣāliḥ makes is the call that the sūrah's earlier messengers made.

The two verses together carry the series forward. One community has been removed from the earth after refusing its messenger, and the messenger of the next people is introduced with the same message together with a sign; and the verses that follow record the response of the people of Thamūd to both.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:73

> And to the people of Thamûd We sent their brother Ṣâliḥ. He said, “O my people! Worship Allah—you have no other god except Him. A clear proof has come to you from your Lord: this is Allah’s she-camel as a sign to you. So leave her to graze ˹freely˺ on Allah’s land and do not harm her, or else you will be overcome by a painful punishment.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third People and Their Messenger**

The verse begins the third of the sūrah's punishment accounts, and it names the people of Thamūd and their messenger Ṣāliḥ. The Book tells the story of this people in several passages, and the accounts run through the sūrah of Hūd (11:61–68), the sūrah of the Poets (26:141–158), the sūrah of the Moon (54:23–31), and the sūrah of the Rocky Tract, where the inhabitants of al-Ḥijr are described (15:80–83). The verse in front of us introduces them in the same form that the two previous accounts used.

The tradition places the people of Thamūd in the stone country of northwestern Arabia, and the sūrah of the Dawn mentions their carving of the rocks in the valley (89:9). The audience of the sūrah knew their remains, and the Book's narratives rely on that knowledge rather than on descriptions of the sites. The verse in front of us states the message and the sign, and the account that follows states the response.

**The Call to Worship God Alone**

The message of Ṣāliḥ is the message of Noah and Hūd, and the verse repeats the words that the sūrah's two earlier accounts have already given twice: worship God, for you have no god besides Him. The sūrah of Hūd reports the same call in its version of the narrative (11:61), and the sūrah of the Poets opens the Thamūd account with the question of whether the people will fear God (26:142). The verse in front of us supplies the third instance of the call, and the repetition states the point of the series.

The Book presents the unity of the message as the mark of the messengers, and the sūrah of the Prophets states that every messenger before the Prophet received the revelation that there is no god but God (21:25). The call in front of us is placed in the mouth of a messenger of a people in the stone valleys of the north, and the identity of the message with the message of Makkah is the sūrah's argument.

**The Clear Proof**

The verse states that a clear proof has come to the people from their Lord, and the proof is then identified with the she-camel that accompanies the messenger. The Book uses the phrase for the signs given to the prophets in several passages, and the sūrah's accounts of Moses and of the Prophet refer to the clear proofs that were delivered. This verse states that the people of Thamūd were given such a demonstration, and the Book's other accounts of this people name the she-camel as the sign.

The sūrah of the Poets reports the messenger's words about the camel and her turn to drink on an appointed day, together with his warning against touching her with harm (26:155–156), and the sūrah of the Moon states that the she-camel was sent as a test and that the people were told of the water dividing between her and them (54:27–28). The sūrah of the Sun states that the messenger of God warned them about the she-camel and that they denied and hamstrung her (91:13–14). The verse here supplies the first statement of the sign in this sūrah.

**The Order to Let Her Graze**

The verse orders the people to leave the she-camel to graze on the earth of God and forbids them to harm her, and the details in the other accounts state the arrangement more fully. The sūrah of the Poets reports the messenger saying that the camel has a share of the water and that the people have a share on a day known to them, and the sūrah of the Moon states that the water was to be divided between them and that the people were warned not to touch her with harm. The present verse states the command and the warning, and the narrative that follows records what the people did.

The phrase about the earth of God states whose the pasture is, and the Book's argument against the people's claim to their own arrangements runs through the narratives of the sūrah. The camel was to live among them as a sign whose maintenance cost them a share of their water. The verse under discussion states the terms of the test, and the sūrah's account of the outcome shows what the people did with them.

**The Warning of Punishment**

The verse closes with the warning that a painful punishment will seize the people if they harm the she-camel, and the Book repeats the warning in each of its accounts of Thamūd. The sūrah of Hūd reports the messenger saying that a punishment will seize them for a fixed term and that the torment is near, and the sūrah of the Sun states that the people were warned before they killed the camel.
