Here the verse states the warning in the mouth of Ṣāliḥ, and the verses that follow report the response of the community and the arrival of what he warned about.

The Book's treatment of the sign as a trust that the community was to keep appears in the same accounts, and the killing of the camel is presented as the deliberate choice that ended the term. This verse supplies the terms on which the people were tested, and the sūrah's later verses state how they answered.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventy-third in the chapter, and it begins the account of Thamūd immediately after the end of the account of ʿĀd. The verse that follows reports the messenger's reminder of the favours his people had received, and the account then proceeds through the exchange with the leaders to the killing of the camel and the destruction (7:74–78). The verse here supplies the opening of the third narrative in the series.

The reader who has seen two communities destroyed now meets the third with the same message and with an additional proof. The call is the worship of the one God, the sign is the she-camel, and the command is to leave her in peace; and the sūrah's next verse states the reminder with which the messenger addressed his people.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reminds the people that God made them successors after ʿĀd, settled them in the land, and gave them the buildings and the carved dwellings, and it commands them to remember the favours of God and to refrain from spreading corruption (7:74). The sign in front of us is followed by the reminder of what the people had received, and the account then turns to the leaders' answer.

The two verses together state the messenger's approach to the third community. He calls them to the worship of God alone, he presents the sign, and he reminds them of the favours that surrounded them; and the sūrah's following verses record the refusal of the arrogant and the killing of the camel.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:74

> Remember when He made you successors after ’Âd and established you in the land—˹and˺ you built palaces on its plains and carved homes into mountains. So remember Allah’s favours, and do not go about spreading corruption in the land.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reminder of the Succession**

The verse continues the speech of Ṣāliḥ to his people, and it reminds them that God made them successors after the people of ʿĀd. The sūrah has used the same reminder in the mouth of Hūd, who told his people that they had been made successors after the people of Noah (7:69), and the sequence of the two verses states the chain of the replacements. The Book's use of the term for the peoples that took the place of the destroyed communities appears in the sūrah of the Cattle, where the audience is told that God has made them successors in the earth (6:165).

The reminder carries the argument that the sūrah is making. The people of Thamūd occupied the land and the wealth of a community that had been removed for its denial, and the messenger in front of us states the origin of what they possessed. The Book's narratives use the same reminder with the peoples that followed the destroyed communities, and the verse in front of us supplies the third instance in the series.

**The Settlement in the Land**

The verse states that God settled the people in the land, and the settlement is then specified by the kinds of building that it supported. The Book's descriptions of the destroyed peoples' habitation include the details of their construction, and the sūrah of the Rocky Tract states that the people of the stone valley carved dwellings out of the mountains in safety (15:82), while the sūrah of the Poets reports the messenger asking his people how they persist in carving out homes in the mountains with skill (26:149). The verse in front of us names the two kinds of building in a single sentence.

The description of the buildings serves the reminder of the favours, and the Book's accounts of the peoples that were given such advantages state that the advantages did not exempt them from the reckoning. The sūrah of the Dawn describes the people of Iram with their pillars and the people of Thamūd who carved out the rocks in the valley (89:7–9), and the passage then states that the peoples transgressed in the lands and multiplied corruption. The verse in front of us supplies the reminder in the messenger's own words.

**The Acknowledgment of the Dwellings**

The Book treats the remains of these dwellings as a sign that the audience knew, and the sūrah of the Rocky Tract states that the people of the valley were destroyed while their carved homes remained. The tradition records that the Prophet and his companions passed the ruins of Thamūd on the way to the expedition to Tabūk, and that he commanded them not to enter the dwellings of those who had wronged themselves except in a state of weeping, so that what had befallen the people would not befall them (al-Bukhārī 3380; Muslim 2980). The verse in front of us names the dwellings, and the tradition records the encounter with what was left of them.

The mention of the buildings in the verse therefore addresses a people whose monuments stood in the sight of the sūrah's first audience, and the sūrah's reader is directed to look at what the buildings did not protect. The Book's later references to the sites of the destroyed peoples serve the same purpose, and the passage in front of us states the buildings as evidence rather than as ornament.

**The Command to Remember the Boons**

The verse turns from the description of the buildings to the command to remember the boons of God, and the reminder is the purpose of the enumeration. The sūrah's earlier account of ʿĀd used the same command after naming the increase in stature and the inheritance of the land (7:69), and the sūrah of the Hūd's version of Thamūd's story reports the messenger recounting the favours of God among his people. This verse places the command after the list, and the order states that the favours are the evidence.

The Book's habit of connecting gratitude to the right use of what has been given appears throughout its addresses, and the sūrah's opening section has connected the enjoyment of the earth to the accounting that follows. The command in front of us states the response that the favours required, and the narrative that follows records the community's refusal.

**The Prohibition of Corruption**

The verse closes by forbidding the people to behave wickedly on the earth and to work corruption, and the phrase repeats the sūrah's earlier prohibition addressed to the community after the establishment of order (7:56). The Book uses the term for the conduct that spoils the arrangements of life, and the sūrah's account of the people of Shuʿayb will use the same term in the mouth of the messenger who warns against dishonest measures. The verse here states the prohibition after the reminder, and the two together state the messenger's request in full.

The Book's pairing of the command to remember the favours with the prohibition of corruption appears in its addresses to the communities, and the sūrah's series of narratives presents the peoples that disregarded both. The present verse supplies the third instance of the appeal, and the account that follows states the answer that the community gave.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventy-fourth in the chapter, and it follows the presentation of the sign and precedes the answer of the leaders. The verses that follow report the exchange between the arrogant and the believers among the people and the killing of the she-camel (7:75–77), and the account closes with the destruction of the community. The verse under discussion supplies the reminder that the third narrative contains.

The reader who has heard the call and seen the sign now reads the reminder of what the people had been given. The land, the castles, and the carved homes are named, the command to remember is issued, and corruption is forbidden; and the sūrah's next verses state the response of the leaders of Thamūd.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the arrogant leaders asked the believers among the weak whether they knew that Ṣāliḥ had been sent by his Lord, and that the believers answered that they believed in what he had been sent with (7:75). The reminder of the favours in front of us is followed by the account of the division within the community, and the exchange shows which of the two groups the leaders addressed and how the believers answered.

The two verses together state the position of the third community before its destruction. The messenger reminds the people of what they have been given and forbids them to spoil it, and the leaders turn to the believers with a question that reveals their own refusal; and the sūrah's following verses record the killing of the sign and the end of the people.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:75

> The arrogant chiefs of his people asked the lowly who believed among them, “Are you certain that Ṣâliḥ has been sent by his Lord?” They replied, “We certainly believe in what he has been sent with.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Arrogant and the Weak**

The verse reports the exchange within the third community, and it names the two groups on either side of it: the leaders who were arrogant and the believers among the people whom the leaders deemed weak. The Book's accounts of the communities consistently place the refusal with the powerful and the acceptance with the poor, and the sūrah of Sabaʾ states that whenever a warner was sent to a society, its elite said that they rejected what he had been sent with (34:34). The verse in front of us identifies both groups in a single sentence.

The term for the believers in front of us describes them as those whom the leaders counted weak, and the expression states the leaders' judgment rather than a fact about the believers. The sūrah of the Poets reports the people of Noah asking how they could believe in the messenger when only the lowest among them followed him (26:111), and the sūrah of Hūd reports the chiefs of Noah's people making the same argument about the followers who had come to the messenger. The verse in front of us states the social division that the sūrah's narratives repeat.

**The Question Put to the Believers**

The leaders ask the believers whether they know that Ṣāliḥ has been sent by his Lord, and the form of the question states its purpose. The question is not an inquiry after evidence but a challenge to the believers' judgment, and the phrasing places the messenger's standing rather than his message before the people who have accepted him. The sūrah of the Ant reports the same division in its account of Thamūd, stating that the people split into two groups disputing with one another (27:45). The verse in front of us supplies the exchange in the sūrah's own account.

The Book's descriptions of the elite's questions to the believers use the same form in other passages, and the sūrah of the Ornament reports the disbelievers asking whether the message had been sent to the messenger rather than to their own great men. The question in front of us belongs to that family of challenges, and the answer that follows shows that the believers did not accept the terms on which it was put.

**The Believers' Answer**

The believers answer that they believe in that with which the messenger has been sent, and the answer states their faith in the message without engaging the question about the messenger's standing. The sūrah's account of the two groups in the previous narratives shows the same simplicity in the responses of those who accepted the prophets, and the Book's descriptions of the believers' confessions state the content of the faith in similar words. This verse supplies the confession of the third community's believers.

The answer identifies the faith as belief in what was sent rather than in the man who brought it, and the phrasing matches the argument of the sūrah's earlier replies about the office of the messenger. The Book's teaching that the messengers are to be believed because of what they carry appears in its accounts of the communities, and the believers in front of us state the position in the fewest possible words.

**The Reversal of Standing**

The verse's description of the believers as weak is answered by the sūrah's later account of the sign, where the weak are the ones who accepted the clear proof and the powerful are the ones who killed it. The Book's description of the Hereafter uses the same reversal, and the sūrah's passage on the Heights reports its people turning to the arrogant and asking whether the ones they had sworn would receive no mercy were not the very people who had entered the garden (7:49). The verse here states the world's estimate of the two groups, and the sūrah's narrative supplies the divine one.

The Book's accounts of the peoples commonly state the numbers and the standing of the believers as small, and the sūrah of Hūd reports the chiefs dismissing Noah's followers on account of their poverty (11:27). The present verse states the same dismissal in the words of the leaders of Thamūd, and the account that follows shows what the two groups did with the sign that had been given them.

**The Two Groups in the Sūrah's Narratives**

The verse completes the sūrah's picture of the communities by naming the division inside them, and the picture has been building through the two earlier accounts. The leaders answer for the people, the messengers address the leaders and the people together, and a body of believers accepts the message and is saved. The verse under discussion supplies the third instance of the division, and the sūrah's following narratives state the outcome for each group.

The Book's treatment of the believers as a remnant that is rescued appears in its accounts of the destroyed peoples throughout, and the sūrah's accounts of Lot and Shuʿayb will state the division in similar terms. Here the verse supplies the exchange that reveals the two sides, and the account then proceeds to the act that decided the community's fate.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventy-fifth in the chapter, and it follows the reminder of the favours and precedes the leaders' answer to the believers. The verse that follows states the leaders' declaration that they do not believe in what the believers have accepted (7:76), and the account then reports the killing of the she-camel and the destruction of the community (7:77–78). This verse supplies the exchange inside the third community.

The reader who has heard the messenger address his people now sees the people divided. The leaders question the believers about the messenger's commission, the believers answer with the content of their faith, and the leaders' answer follows; and the sūrah's next verses state the refusal in its final form and the deed that followed it.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the arrogant said they did not believe in what the believers believed (7:76). The believers' confession in front of us is followed by the leaders' formal rejection, and the passage then records the hamstringing of the she-camel and the demand that the messenger produce the punishment he had warned about.

The two verses together state the positions of the two groups. The believers accept what has been sent, the leaders reject it in a single sentence, and the account moves to the act that sealed the community's fate; and the sūrah's following verses report the sign's destruction, the demand, and the end of the people.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:76

> The arrogant said, “We surely reject what you believe in.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Leaders' Refusal**

The verse reports the answer of the arrogant leaders of Thamūd, and the answer is a declaration that they do not believe in what the believers have accepted. The sentence matches the confession of the believers in the previous verse and states the opposite of it, and the two verses together set the community's division in the clearest form. The sūrah's earlier accounts reported the leaders' charges against the messengers, and the answer in front of us contains no charge; it states the refusal alone.

The form of the refusal is a statement about belief rather than about argument, and the Book's accounts of the elite's answers frequently take the same form. The sūrah of Sabaʾ reports the elite of each society saying that they reject what the messenger has been sent with (34:34), and the sūrah of the Ornament reports the same people answering that they found their forefathers on a way and are following in their footsteps (43:23). The verse in front of us states the refusal in the shortest words that the sūrah's narratives contain.

**The Two Confessions Set Against Each Other**

The verse sets the leaders' unbelief against the believers' faith in the same words, and the parallel states the nature of the division. The believers had said that they believed in what the messenger had been sent with, and the leaders say that they do not believe in what the believers believe. The two sentences describe the same message from two directions, and the sūrah's reader is shown that the difference lies in the people and not in what was presented to them.

The Book's use of the same pairing appears in its account of the two groups at the opening of the sūrah of the Cow, where the believers are described by what they accept and the disbelievers by their refusal (2:3–6), and in the sūrah of the Cave, where the companions of the garden and the believer state their positions in contrast. The verse in front of us supplies the pairing in the narrative of Thamūd, and the account proceeds to the deed that followed the refusal.

**The Elite's Consistency**

The leaders who speak in front of us appear throughout the sūrah's narratives, and their position is stated in the same terms each time. They are the ones who answer the messengers in the earlier accounts, they ask the believers the question that reveals their own view, and they state their refusal in front of us. The Book's treatment of the elite as the group that answers for the community appears in its accounts of the peoples throughout, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that God has placed in every city the great ones who conspire in it (6:123).

The Book's descriptions of the elite's refusal include the note that their standing in the world was the source of their confidence, and the sūrah's passage on the Heights reports its people asking the arrogant whether the believers they had dismissed were not the ones who had entered the garden (7:49). The verse in front of us supplies the refusal that the sūrah's later passage answers, and the narrative that follows shows what the refusal produced.

**The Refusal and the Sign**

The leaders' declaration comes after the sign has been given, and the Book's accounts of the communities state that the signs do not compel belief. The sūrah of Fussilat reports that the people of Thamūd were shown the guidance and chose blindness over it (41:17), and the sūrah of the Sun states that the messenger warned them about the camel and they denied him and hamstrung her. This verse states the refusal while the sign is still alive among them.

The Book's teaching about the relation between signs and belief appears in several passages, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the audience's demand for a sign and the answer that the signs belong to God. The verse here supplies the declaration of unbelief in the presence of the proof, and the account that follows states what the people did with the sign itself.

**The Community's Answer and Its Consequences**

The refusal in front of us is the community's answer to the message, and the sūrah's narratives state the consequences of the answer in each case. The Book's accounts of the peoples that were destroyed present the destruction as the outcome of the refusal rather than of any defect in the signs, and the sūrah's introduction has stated that the communities that rejected the messengers were destroyed. The present verse supplies the third community's formal rejection, and the account that follows carries the narrative to its end.

The Book's treatment of the refusal as a settled position appears in its accounts of the audiences that asked for the punishment to be brought, and the sūrah's next verse reports the leaders' community carrying out the act that the warning had forbidden. The verse under discussion states the words, and the sūrah's following verses state the deed.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventy-sixth in the chapter, and it answers the believers' confession in the previous verse. The verse that follows reports the hamstringing of the she-camel and the community's demand that the messenger bring the punishment (7:77), and the account closes with the destruction of the people (7:78). Here the verse supplies the leaders' answer in the exchange inside the third community.

The reader who heard the believers state their faith now hears the leaders state their refusal. The two answers are set against each other, and the account proceeds to the act that followed; and the sūrah's next verses report the killing of the sign and the demand that the messenger produce what he had warned about.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the people hamstrung the she-camel and insolently defied the command of their Lord, and that they demanded that the messenger bring upon them what he had threatened if he was among those sent (7:77). The refusal in front of us is followed by the deed, and the account then reports the destruction of the community and the messenger's words as he turned away from them.

The two verses together state the end of the third community's probation. The leaders declare their unbelief, the people destroy the sign that had been given to them, and they demand the punishment; and the sūrah's following verses record the arrival of what they asked for.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:77

> Then they killed the she-camel —defying their Lord’s command—and challenged ˹Ṣâliḥ˺, “Bring us what you threaten us with, if you are ˹truly˺ one of the messengers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Act Against the Sign**

The verse states that the people hamstrung the she-camel, and the deed ends the probation on which the community had been placed. The Book reports the act in each of its accounts of Thamūd: the sūrah of the Sun states that they denied the messenger and hamstrung her, and that their Lord destroyed them for their sin and levelled the city (91:14), the sūrah of the Poets reports that they hamstrung her and became regretful (26:157), and the sūrah of the Moon states that they called their companion and he took the sword and hamstrung her (54:29). The verse in front of us supplies the sūrah's own statement of the deed.

The Book's description of the act includes the conspiracy that preceded it, and the sūrah of the Ant refers to the nine men in the city who were spreading corruption and who plotted against the messenger (27:48). The tradition reports that the community consented to the killing and that the leaders instigated it, and the Book's narratives present the deed as the act of the community rather than of an individual. The verse in front of us states the outcome of the refusal that the previous verses have recorded.

**The Insolent Defiance of the Command**

The verse describes the people as having defiantly transgressed the command of their Lord, and the phrase states the character of the act rather than its details. The Book uses the same description for the peoples that were destroyed, and the sūrah of the Winnowing Winds reports that the people of ʿĀd persisted in defying the command of their Lord and were overtaken by a blast while they looked on (51:44). The verse in front of us supplies the same statement about the people of Thamūd, and the two accounts present the defiance as the common element in the destruction of the peoples.

The Book's treatment of the command in the narratives includes its terms of the sign, and the sūrah's earlier verse stated the command to let the camel graze and the warning against harming her (7:73). The defiance described in front of us is therefore a refusal of an explicit instruction, and the Book's accounts of the communities present the destruction as the consequence of that refusal rather than of an oversight.

**The Demand for the Punishment**

The verse reports the community's demand that the messenger bring upon them what he had threatened if he was among those sent, and the challenge repeats the demand that the people of ʿĀd made of their messenger (7:70). The sūrah of the Sand Dunes reports the same challenge from the people of ʿĀd (46:22), and the sūrah of the Prophets reports the audience of the Prophet asking when the threat would come to pass (21:38). This verse supplies the third instance of the demand in the sūrah's narratives.

The demand treats the warning as a claim to be tested, and the Book's answers to the demand state that the term belongs to God. The sūrah of Yūnus reports the instruction to tell the audience to consider what would follow if the punishment came upon them by night or by day (10:50), and the verse in front of us places the demand immediately after the deed that the warning had forbidden. The sequence states that the community asked for the punishment in the act of earning it.

**The Sign and the Refusal**

The Book's accounts of Thamūd present the killing of the camel as the act that ended the term, and the sūrah of Fussilat states that the people were shown the guidance and chose blindness over it, so that the blast of a disgracing punishment seized them (41:17). The verse here states the deed and the demand in a single sentence, and the two together show the community's position at the end of its probation.

The Book's treatment of the sign as a living proof that the people destroyed appears in the narratives of each community, and the sūrah's account of the sign of Moses before Pharaoh uses the same arrangement. The present verse supplies the sūrah's shortest statement of the act, and the account that follows states what came of it.

**The Community's Last Words**

The verse's second half gives the community's last words to the messenger, and the demand for the punishment is put in the same form that the sūrah's earlier narratives used. The Book's accounts of the peoples that were destroyed end with their defiance stated rather than with any revision of their position, and the verse in front of us supplies the third community's closing speech. The account that follows records the arrival of what they asked for.

The Book's descriptions of the regrets of the peoples appear in its accounts of those who recognised their loss when it was too late, and the sūrah of the Poets states that the people of Thamūd became regretful after the act. The verse under discussion states the demand rather than the regret, and the sūrah's next verse reports what the morning brought.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventy-seventh in the chapter, and it follows the leaders' refusal and precedes the destruction of the community. The verse that follows reports the earthquake that seized the people and the morning on which they lay lifeless in their homes (7:78), and the messenger's words as he turned away come after that (7:79). Here the verse supplies the deed that closed the third community's term.

The reader who has seen the sign given and refused now reads of its destruction. The camel is killed, the command of the Lord is defied, and the punishment is demanded; and the sūrah's next verse states what arrived in answer to the demand.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the earthquake seized the people and that morning found them lying lifeless in their homes (7:78). The deed in front of us is followed by the seizure, and the account of Thamūd reaches its end in the manner of the two accounts before it.

The two verses together complete the third narrative of the series. The sign is destroyed, the defiance is stated, the punishment is demanded, and it arrives; and the sūrah's following verse records the messenger's farewell to the people who would not hear him.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:78

> Then an ˹overwhelming˺ earthquake struck them, and they fell lifeless in their homes.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Seizure**

The verse states that the earthquake seized the people, and the Book names the punishment of Thamūd in several forms across its accounts. The sūrah of Hūd reports that the blast seized them and that morning found them lying lifeless in their homes (11:67), the sūrah of the Inevitable states that they were destroyed by an overwhelming blast (69:5), the sūrah of the Moon states that a single blast was sent against them and left them like the twigs of a fence-builder (54:31), and the sūrah of Fussilat reports the blast of a disgracing punishment seizing them (41:17). The verse in front of us names the earthquake, and the other accounts name the cry and the blast.

The variety of the names in the Book's accounts describes the same event from different sides, and the sūrah's narrative supplies the one that fits its concern. The trembling of the earth states the removal of the ground on which the people had built, and the accounts that describe the blast state the sound that accompanied it. The verse in front of us supplies the sūrah's word for the seizure, and the accounts in the sūrahs of Hūd and the Rocky Tract use the same word for the same people.

**Morning Found Them Lifeless**

The verse states that the people were found in the morning lying lifeless in their homes, and the phrase describes the settlement as it was after the event: the dwellings standing and the people within them without life. The sūrah of Hūd uses the same words for the same people (11:67), and the sūrah of the Spider reports the destruction of the people of Shuʿayb in the same manner. The verse in front of us supplies the sūrah's statement of the end.

The description of the morning in front of us gives the event its setting in the ordinary time of the day, and the Book's accounts of the punishments frequently state the time at which they arrived. The people who had demanded the punishment in the evening were found lifeless in the morning, and the account's brevity states the completeness of the removal without describing the scene. The Book's narratives of the destroyed peoples use the same restraint.

**The Homes That Did Not Shelter**

The verse states that the people lay in their homes, and the Book's accounts of the buildings of Thamūd state that they had carved their dwellings in the mountains and felt secure in them (15:82). This verse places the people inside the structures that their own skill had raised, and the contrast between the security the buildings promised and the death within them is stated by the two passages together. The sūrah of the Poets reports the messenger asking his people how they persist in carving the homes in the mountains with skill (26:149), and the verse in front of us supplies the answer.

The Book's treatment of the works of the destroyed peoples as evidence appears in its addresses that direct the audience to the ruins, and the tradition records that the Prophet and his companions passed the remains of Thamūd on the way to Tabūk and were told not to enter the dwellings of those who had wronged themselves except in a state of weeping (al-Bukhārī 3380; Muslim 2980). The verse here supplies the statement of what happened inside the dwellings, and the tradition records the instruction that the ruins were to be read.

**The Sign and the People**

The verse follows the killing of the she-camel, and the Book's accounts of Thamūd connect the destruction to the act against the sign. The sūrah of the Sun states that they denied the messenger and hamstrung her and that their Lord destroyed them for their sin and levelled the city (91:14), and the sūrah of Fussilat states that they preferred blindness to the guidance that had been shown to them. The present verse states the seizure that followed, and the two verses together state the correspondence between the deed and its consequence.

The Book's accounts of the communities present the punishments as the fitting outcomes of the refusals, and the sūrah's narratives of Noah and ʿĀd used the same correspondence. The verse under discussion supplies the third instance, and the account that follows records the messenger's words over the people who had been removed.

**The Repetition of the End**

The verse completes the third community's story in the same manner as the two before it, and the repetition of the pattern is the sūrah's argument. The people who were given the sign and destroyed it were found lifeless in the places they had built; and the community's standing, its numbers, and its skill did not alter the end. The Book's accounts of the peoples that were destroyed state the same conclusion in each case.

The Book's use of the destruction as evidence for the audience appears in its addresses to the Makkans, and the sūrah's narratives place the audience in the position of the peoples whose histories it has been reading. Here the verse states the third outcome, and the sūrah's next verse records the messenger's farewell and the assessment of his work.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventy-eighth in the chapter, and it follows the killing of the she-camel and precedes the messenger's words. The verse that follows records Ṣāliḥ's turning away from the people and his statement that he had delivered his Lord's message and given sincere counsel (7:79), and the account then gives way to the fourth narrative. This verse supplies the destruction that closed the third account.

The reader who has followed the call, the reminder, the exchange, and the deed now reads the outcome. The earthquake seizes the people, the morning finds them lifeless in the homes they had carved, and the messenger is left to speak over what remains; and the sūrah's next verse states what he said.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the messenger turned away from the people and said that he had conveyed his Lord's message and given them sincere counsel, and that they did not like sincere advisers (7:79). The destruction in front of us is followed by the messenger's farewell, and the account of Thamūd closes with the words that the sūrah's earlier messengers also spoke.

The two verses together end the third narrative of the series. The community is removed, the messenger states that his duty was performed, and the people are described by their refusal of the counsel; and the sūrah's following verses introduce the account of Lot with the same structure.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:79

> So he turned away from them, saying, “O my people! Surely I conveyed to you my Lord’s message and gave you ˹sincere˺ advice, but you do not like ˹sincere˺ advisors.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turning Away**

The verse reports that the messenger turned away from his people and addressed them after their destruction, and the turning away states the end of his work among them. The sūrah's account of Shuʿayb uses the same opening and almost the same words, recording that he turned away from them and said that he had delivered his Lord's messages and given them sincere counsel (7:93). The verse in front of us supplies the third instance of the farewell, and the two passages together establish the form in which the sūrah's messengers take their leave.

The tradition gives the turning away two settings: the messenger's departure from the settlement with the believers before the punishment came, and his return to the ruins afterwards. The verse in front of us does not specify which, and the address that follows fits both, since it states the completed duty rather than any expectation of a response. The Book's narratives of the communities frequently close with the messenger's statement of what he did and what the people did with it.

**The Message Delivered**

The messenger states that he delivered the message of his Lord, and the phrase repeats the description of the prophetic work that the sūrah has already used for Noah (7:62) and for Hūd (7:68). The Book presents the delivery of the revelation as the substance of the office, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran states that the messenger's duty is only to convey (3:20), while the sūrah of the Table repeats the same limit twice. The verse in front of us states the delivery as a completed fact, and the perfect tense marks the end of the commission.

The message in the singular in front of us is the one that the messenger was given, and the sūrah's accounts of the prophets present each of them as the bearer of a single message. The Book's teaching that the messengers were sent with the same religion appears in the sūrah of the Consultation, and the verse in front of us states the third messenger's completion of his charge.

**The Sincere Counsel**

The messenger states that he also gave the people sincere counsel, and the phrase repeats the term that the sūrah has used for the same activity in the earlier narratives (7:62; 7:68). The Book's presentation of the prophetic work includes the two parts, the delivery of the message and the counsel that accompanies it, and the verse in front of us states them together at the close of the account. The messenger's counsel was offered to a people who had already refused the message, and the verse records it as a completed act.

The Book's treatment of counsel among the believers adopts the practice, and the sūrah of the Declining Day states that those who are not at a loss urge one another to the truth and to perseverance (103:3). This verse supplies the prophetic origin of the practice, and the account then states the people's response to it.

**The People Who Did Not Love Counsel**

The verse closes with the messenger's statement that the people did not love sincere advisers, and the phrase describes a disposition rather than a single refusal. The Book's accounts of the communities present the rejection of the messengers as the rejection of what they offered, and the sūrah's earlier accounts stated the leaders' charges and the people's demands. The verse here states the reason for the messenger's grief, and the sūrah's account of Shuʿayb uses the same closing words (7:93).

The Book's descriptions of the people who disliked the counsel include their preference for their own arrangements, and the sūrah of the Ornament reports the disbelievers stating that they found their forefathers on a way and were following in their footsteps (43:23). The present verse states the disposition in the mouth of the messenger, and the account that follows turns to the next people.

**The Address to a Destroyed People**

The messenger's words in front of us are spoken to a community that has already been removed, and the address states the truth about the past rather than appealing for a change. The tradition records that the Prophet addressed the dead of the Quraysh at Badr, asking whether they had found what their Lord promised them to be true, and that he answered ʿUmar's surprise by saying that the dead could hear what he said even if they could not reply (al-Bukhārī 3976). The verse under discussion supplies the earlier instance of an address to a destroyed people, and the tradition records the Prophet's return to the practice.

The Book's narratives of the messengers' closings state the completed work in this form in several accounts, and the sūrah's two other narratives state the rescue of the believers as the outcome of the same work. Here the verse states the farewell of the third messenger, and the account of the fourth people begins after it.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands seventy-ninth in the chapter, and it closes the account of Thamūd that began with the call of Ṣāliḥ (7:73). The verse that follows opens the account of Lot (7:80), and the series continues with the fourth people. This verse supplies the conclusion of the third narrative and the model for the farewells that follow.

The reader who has followed the third community from its call to its destruction now reads the messenger's assessment of his work. The message was delivered, the counsel was given, and the people are described by what they refused; and the sūrah's next verse turns to the people of Lot and the message their messenger brought them.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports Lot's address to his people, asking them whether they commit an indecency that no one in the world had committed before them (7:80). The farewell of the third messenger in front of us is followed by the opening of the fourth account, and the Book's series of the communities continues without a break.

The two verses together carry the series forward. One community has been ended and its messenger has stated the completion of his duty, and the next messenger is introduced with the question that names his people's offence; and the verses that follow record the argument between Lot and his people and the outcome of their refusal.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:80

> And ˹remember˺ when Lot scolded ˹the men of˺ his people, ˹saying,˺ “Do you commit a shameful deed that no man has ever done before?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Account and Its Messenger**

The verse opens the fourth of the sūrah's punishment accounts, and it introduces Lot as the messenger of the people among whom he lived. The Book tells the story of this people in several passages, and the accounts run through the sūrah of Hūd (11:77–83), the sūrah of the Rocky Tract (15:57–77), the sūrah of the Poets (26:160–175), the sūrah of the Ant (27:54–58), the sūrah of the Spider (29:28–35), the sūrah of the Moon (54:33–39), and the sūrah of the Winnowing Winds (51:31–37). The verse in front of us supplies the opening of the sūrah's own version.

The Book presents Lot as a messenger of the people of the cities, and the sūrah of the Poets describes him as the brother of the people he addressed (26:161). The verse in front of us names the people as his own and states his address to them, and the account that follows records the argument, the arrival of the angel messengers, and the destruction of the cities. The narrative is the longest in the sūrah's series before the account of Moses.

**The Kinship With Abraham**

The Book places Lot among the household of Abraham, and the sūrah of the Spider states that Lot believed in him when Abraham announced his emigration to his Lord (29:26). The sūrah of the Prophets states that God delivered Abraham along with Lot to the land that He had blessed for all people (21:71), and the sūrah of Hūd reports the angel messengers visiting Abraham with the news of a son before they went on to Lot's people (11:69–76). The verse in front of us stands in that sequence, and the narrative that follows reports the same messengers arriving in the city.

The Book's accounts of the two men present them as partners in the migration and in the warning, and the sūrah of the Rocky Tract gives the fullest account of the angels' visit to Lot after their visit to Abraham. This verse supplies the opening of the sūrah's account, and the reader who has followed the series from Noah to Thamūd is given the next messenger in the line.

**The Indecency Unprecedented**

The verse states the question that Lot put to his people about the deed they commit, and the question names the deed as one that no one in the world had committed before them. The sūrah of the Spider reports the same words in its account, stating that Lot rebuked his people for a shameful deed that no man had done before them (29:28), and the sūrah of the Ant reports his rebuke of the deed (27:54). The Book's statements about the novelty of the act appear in more than one passage, and the sūrah's version in front of us is the briefest of them.

The Book's identification of the deed follows in the next verse, where Lot states that his people lust after men instead of women and describes them as transgressors (7:81). The sūrah of the Poets reports the same charge in the messenger's address to his people, asking why they lust after men and leaving the wives that their Lord had created for them (26:165–166). The verse here states the question about the deed, and the verse that follows names it.

**The Address to His People**

The verse reports that Lot scolded his people, and the term states the tone of the address. The Book's narratives present the messengers addressing their peoples with the language of counsel and warning, and the sūrah of the Poets records Lot's address with the call to fear God (26:161). The present verse states the rebuke, and the phrase my people within the address identifies the messenger with those he corrects.

The Book's use of the possessive in the messengers' addresses appears in each of the sūrah's narratives, and the messengers are described as the brothers of the peoples they were sent to. The verse under discussion supplies the fourth instance of the form, and the account that follows reports the people's answer and the messengers' response to it.

**The Place of the Account in the Sūrah's Series**

The verse begins the account that turns the sūrah's series from the peoples of Arabia to the cities of the plain, and the narrative introduces features that the earlier accounts did not have. The angel messengers appear, the town is destroyed by an act of God upon the place itself, and the account ends with the punishment raining on the people. The Book's series of destroyed peoples includes the cities of Lot in each of its listings, and the verse in front of us places them fourth in the sūrah's sequence.

The Book's treatment of the offences of the several peoples gives each community its own charge, and the sūrah's narratives state the charges in the messengers' words. The people of Noah were charged with persistence in error, the people of ʿĀd with arrogance and the invention of gods, the people of Thamūd with the killing of the sign and the demand for the punishment, and the people of Lot in front of us with the deed. The series presents the communities as differing in their offences and agreeing in their refusal.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eightieth in the chapter, and it opens the account of Lot immediately after the close of the account of Thamūd. The verses that follow state the charge in full, report the answer of the people, and carry the narrative through the visit of the angels to the destruction of the cities (7:81–84). Here the verse supplies the opening question of the fourth account.

The reader who has seen three communities destroyed now meets the fourth with the charge stated in the mouth of its messenger. The question names the deed and its novelty, and the verses that follow develop the charge and record the answer; and the sūrah's series continues toward the long account of Moses and the people of Israel.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse completes the charge, stating that the people come to men rather than to women and that they are a people who transgress (7:81). The question in front of us is followed by the naming of the deed, and the passage then reports the people's answer and the messenger's prayer against them.

The two verses together state the offence of the fourth community. The messenger asks how the people can commit what no one before them committed, and he then states what the act is and describes those who practice it; and the sūrah's following verses record the refusal, the gathering of the people, and the arrival of the messengers who were sent to destroy the cities.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:81

> You lust after men instead of women! You are certainly transgressors.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Naming of the Deed**

The verse completes the question that Lot put to his people in the previous verse, and it names the deed that the question had left unnamed. The charge states that the men of the city came to men rather than to women, and the Book reports the same accusation in several passages. The sūrah of the Poets gives it in the messenger's address to his people, and the sūrah of the Ant reports it in the form of a question followed by the description of the offenders as a people acting in ignorance (27:55). The verse here supplies the plain statement of the offence.

The Book's treatment of the subject is direct and without euphemism, and the naming of the act in front of us serves the account that follows. The narrative is concerned with the refusal of the messengers and with the destruction of the community, and the offence is stated because it is the ground of that refusal. The two verses together, this one and the one before it, give the charge in full.

**The Wives the Lord Created**

The sūrah of the Poets completes the charge in a way the verse here does not, stating that the people left the wives their Lord had created for them and describing them as a transgressing people (26:166). The pairing in that passage of the act with the created order names what the offenders set aside, and the Book's argument from creation appears in its accounts of the Prophets' appeals to their peoples. The verse here states the preference for men over women; the companion passage states what the preference displaced.

The Book's address to the created order in matters of conduct appears in its commands about marriage and chastity, and the sūrah of the Believers places the guarding of chastity among the marks of the successful (23:5). The offence in this verse is set against that teaching, and the account that follows presents the community's refusal to hear the messenger who carried the teaching.

**The Characterization of the Offenders**

The charge closes with the description of the people as transgressors, and the term places the offence among the violations of the limits God has set. The sūrah of the Ant records the same charge with the description of the offenders as ignorant (27:55), and the sūrah of the Poets describes them as a people who go beyond bounds (26:166). The Book uses the vocabulary of transgression for the communities that refused the messengers, and the description here belongs to that family of terms.

The sūrah's account of the people of Lot places their offence beside the offences of the earlier communities, and the terms used for each people state the character of its refusal. Noah's people were called blind, the people of ʿĀd were called arrogant, the people of Thamūd were called those who had been shown the guidance and preferred blindness, and the people of Lot are called transgressors. The list states the varieties of the refusal rather than a hierarchy among them.

**The Other Charges in the Book's Accounts**

The sūrah of the Spider gives a fuller statement of the people's conduct in Lot's address to them, reporting his rebuke for a shameful deed that no one in the world had done before them and his naming of their lust for men, their abuse of travellers, and their practice of immorality in their gatherings (29:28–29). The book's accounts do not leave the offence as a private matter, and the addition of the treatment of travellers and of public gatherings states its social form. The verse here gives the shortest version of the charge, and the fuller accounts supply the rest.

The sūrah of the Ant adds that the people committed the deed while they could see one another do it, and it describes them as a people acting in ignorance (27:54–55). The Book's presentation of the offence as public and deliberate appears in the accounts of the other communities as well, and its narratives treat the open practice of the offence as part of the refusal of the warning. This verse belongs to that presentation.

**The Prophet's Address and Its Argument**

The sūrah of the Poets reports Lot's opening address to his people with the call to fear God and the statement that he is a trustworthy messenger (26:161–162), and the same passage records his appeal to the created order before the naming of the offence (26:165–166). The Book's messengers argue from what the people can see, and the appeal in that passage places the wives that God created beside the act the people preferred. The argument here is left implicit, and the reader is given the charge and the description of those who commit it.

The Book's address to the peoples in the narratives commonly begins with the call to worship and then names the offence, and the account of Lot follows the same order in the longer passages. The present verse supplies the second half of the charge, and the passage that follows reports the community's answer and the destruction that came upon it.

**The Refusal and Its Consequence**

The Book's accounts of the people of Lot connect the offence to the destruction of the cities, and the sūrah of Hūd and the sūrah of the Rocky Tract describe the punishment in detail. The offence was a public practice that the messenger called the people to abandon, and their refusal was a refusal of the call. The verse under discussion states the content of the call's first charge, and the narrative that follows states the answer of the city and the end of its people.

The Book's treatment of the destruction of the peoples appears throughout the sūrah's series, and the fate of the cities of the plain belongs to the same pattern of warning and refusal that the sūrah has been tracing. The offence named here is the occasion of the warning, and the account that follows states how the warning was answered.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eighty-first in the chapter, and it completes the charge that began in the question of the previous verse. The verse that follows reports the only answer the people gave, the demand that the family of Lot be expelled from the land, and the sūrah's account then proceeds to the salvation of the messenger and the destruction of the cities (7:82–84). Here the verse supplies the naming of the deed.

The reader who heard the messenger's question now reads what the question named, and the account turns to the response of the community. The charge is complete, the offenders are described, and the answer of the city follows.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the people's only answer was the demand that Lot and his household be expelled from the land and the taunt that they were people who wished to remain chaste (7:82). The naming of the deed gives way to the answer of the community, and the account reports how the city received the charge.

The two verses together state the exchange between the messenger and the people of the plain. He names the offence, they answer with expulsion and mockery, and the passage then reports the destruction of the cities.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:82

> But his people’s only response was to say, “Expel them from your land! They are a people who wish to remain chaste!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Response Without an Argument**

The verse reports the only answer that the people of the cities gave to Lot, and the phrasing states that the community had nothing to offer against the charge. The answer does not address the deed or the call; it addresses the presence of the messenger and his household among the people. The Book's accounts of the communities repeatedly present the refusal as a matter of power rather than of argument, and the sūrah's earlier narratives recorded the charges that the leaders of Noah, ʿĀd, and Thamūd brought against their messengers. Here the charge is absent, and the answer is an order to leave.

The Book's description of the response as the people's only reply states the poverty of the position that the community had taken. The deed had been named, the call had been given, and the city could answer neither with a defense of the practice nor with an argument against the message. What remained was the removal of the people who found the practice objectionable.

**The Threat of Expulsion**

The demand that the messenger and his household be driven out of the land belongs to the Book's accounts of the communities' treatment of the prophets, and the sūrah's account of Shuʿayb reports a similar threat in the mouth of the leaders of Midian (7:88). The sūrah of the Ant reports the same words from the people of Lot, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the threat of the people to expel him if he did not desist (26:167). The verse here states the threat in its plainest form, and the three passages together show how the communities dealt with the messengers they would not hear.

The Book's narratives present expulsion as the community's way of protecting its practice, and the sūrah's account of the migration of Abraham and Lot places the two men in the land that God had blessed after they left their people. The Book's treatment of the prophets' removals from their communities appears in several narratives, and the verse here supplies the first instance in the account of the cities.

**The Mockery of Purity**

The answer adds the taunt that the household were a people who wished to keep themselves pure, and the words state the community's view of the conduct it was being called to abandon. The Book reports the same taunt in the sūrah of the Ant (27:56), and the tradition explains the word as the people's description of those who refused to join in their practice. The Book's accounts of the wicked frequently show them naming good by the name of evil, and the taunt here belongs to that habit.

The sūrah of the Cow reports the disbelievers calling the believers fools while the description belonged to themselves (2:13), and the sūrah of the Ant records the people of Lot describing themselves as acting in ignorance. The Book's practice is to let the communities speak and to let the record of their words show their condition, and the taunt here is left in the narrative without comment. The account that follows supplies the judgment.

**The Answer as a Verdict on the Community**

The answer in this verse is the community's verdict on the messenger, and the Book's narratives present such verdicts as the point at which the communities sealed their fate. The people had decided that the presence of the pious among them was the problem to be solved, and the solution they proposed was the removal of the pious. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the destruction of the communities state the outcome of the decisions their leaders made.

The sūrah's later verses report the salvation of the messenger and his household and the rain that came upon the cities, and the two events are presented as the answer to the demand for expulsion. The community that wished to remove the believers from its land was itself removed, and the sūrah's reader is shown the correspondence between the two.

**The Book's Wider Treatment of the Theme**

Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of the conflict between the messengers and their peoples include several threats of expulsion and of violence, and the sūrah of the Cave reports the companions of the cave asking their Lord to grant them mercy and guidance (18:10), while the sūrah of the Prophets reports the disbelievers' plots against the prophets in the same terms. The Book's instruction to the Prophet to endure the opposition of his people appears in the sūrah of the Pen and the sūrah of the Stairways, and the narratives of the earlier messengers supply the pattern.

The sūrah's own account of the people of Makkah includes the recall of the favour of the settlement in the land and the warning of what followed those who drove out the believers, and the sūrah's earlier verses have stated the consequences of the communities' choices. The verse here supplies the answer of the cities of the plain, and the account of their end follows immediately.

**The Silence That Follows**

The verse is the last speech of the people in the account, and the narrative reports no further exchange between Lot and his city. In several places, The Book's accounts of the communities often end with such a sentence, and the removal of the record of further argument states that the discussion was over. The verse here supplies that sentence, and the account proceeds to the act of God.

The Book's treatment of the peoples who had reached the end of their term appears in the narratives of the sūrah's earlier communities, and the destruction that comes after the refusal is presented as the natural end of the choice that the community had made. This verse belongs to that pattern, and the verses that follow state the rescue and the rain.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eighty-second in the chapter, and it follows the naming of the offence and precedes the report of the rescue. The verses that follow state that God saved Lot and his household except his wife and that the rain came upon the cities (7:83–84), and the account of the fourth people then closes. The present verse supplies the community's answer to the charge.

The reader who heard the charge now reads the response of the city, and the account moves to its conclusion. The demand for expulsion is recorded, the mockery is stated, and the rescue and the destruction follow.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that God saved Lot and his household except for his wife, who was among those who stayed behind (7:83). The answer of the city gives way to the act of God, and the account turns to the division within the household itself.

The two verses together state the community's decision and the beginning of its consequence. The people choose expulsion and the messenger is saved with his own, and the passage then reports the rain upon the cities.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:83

> So We saved him and his family except his wife, who was one of the doomed.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rescue of the Household**

The verse states that God saved Lot and his family, and the salvation of the messenger and his people is one of the fixed features of the sūrah's punishment accounts. The accounts of Noah, Hūd, and Ṣāliḥ reported the same rescue, and the sūrah of the Cattle asks whether anyone would be destroyed except the wrongdoing people if the punishment came upon them suddenly or openly (6:47). Here the rescue is stated before the destruction, and the order of the two sentences places the deliverance of the believers first.

The Book's narrative in the sūrah of the Winnowing Winds supplies the detail that the believers were evacuated from the city before the torment, stating that God brought out the believers who were there (51:35). The account in the sūrah of Hūd reports the angels' instruction to Lot to travel with his family in the night and the warning that none of them should look back (11:81). The verse here states the outcome of those instructions in a single sentence.

**The Exception of the Wife**

The verse excepts Lot's wife from the rescue and states that she was among those who were doomed, and the Book names the same exception in each of its accounts of the destruction of the cities. The sūrah of the Rocky Tract and the sūrah of the Poets report the exception with the same wording, and the sūrah of the Spider states that the household of Lot was saved except his wife, who stayed behind with the people (29:32). The verse here supplies the sūrah's statement of the exception, and the sentence places the wife with the community rather than with the saved.

The Book's use of the wife of Lot as an example appears in the sūrah of the Prohibition, where the wives of Noah and of Lot are named as examples set for the disbelievers, each of them married to a servant of God and neither of them availing against anything of what was due to God (66:10). The tradition adds that she followed the religion of her husband outwardly while remaining a disbeliever in her heart, and the exception in this verse is presented as the consequence of that condition.

**The Meaning of the Exception**

The exception states that kinship with a messenger does not shield a person from the consequence of unbelief, and the Book's accounts of the households of the prophets make the same point in several places. The son of Noah is reported in the sūrah of Hūd to have refused the call to board the ark and to have been drowned, and the sūrah of the Prohibition names the two wives as examples. The verse here belongs to that teaching, and the exception is stated in the account of the household of the fourth messenger.

The Book's treatment of individual responsibility appears in the sūrah of the Cattle, where the soul is told that no bearer of a burden will bear the burden of another (6:164), and in the sūrah of the Elevated Places, which states that every soul is held for what it has earned (74:38). The salvation of the household and the loss of one of its members in the same verse place the two teachings side by side. What saves is not the family but the faith.

**The Household and the Believers**

The rescue in this verse covers the household of the messenger, and the sūrah of the Winnowing Winds states that the believers were the ones brought out of the city (51:35). The two statements, taken together, describe the same group under two names: the family of Lot and the believers who had followed him in a city that had rejected the message. The tradition explains the household in the same way, and the verse here shares the term with the accounts of the other messengers, whose followers are named alongside their families.

The Book's accounts of the prophets' households thus present the believing family as part of the community that is saved, and the sūrah of Hūd reports the entry of Noah's family into the ark together with the believers. This verse supplies the pattern for the fourth community, and the exception within the household states the principle more sharply than any of the earlier accounts.

**The Wives of the Prophets in the Book's Teaching**

The sūrah of the Prohibition sets the two wives as examples for those who disbelieve, and the sūrah of the Women records the instruction about the care of orphans and the requirements of justice (4:6; 4:40), while the sūrah of the Prohibition draws its lesson from the two households by contrast with the believing examples of Mary and the wife of Pharaoh. The Book's use of the households of the prophets as examples keeps the teaching at the level of the individual, and the verse here supplies the narrative ground of the first of the two examples.

The exception in this verse is therefore not an incidental detail of the story, and the Book's presentation of the destruction of the cities turns on it. The verse states the outcome for the household, the exception within it, and the place of the excepted person among the doomed, and the narrative then reports the rain upon the cities.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eighty-third in the chapter, and it follows the community's demand for expulsion and precedes the report of the rain. The verse that follows states that a rain was poured upon the cities and commands the reader to consider the end of the wicked (7:84), and the fourth account then closes. The present verse supplies the rescue of the messenger and the exception within his household.

The reader who heard the people demand the departure of the household now reads who actually left and who remained. The believers are saved, the one who stayed behind is lost with the city, and the account proceeds to the destruction.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that a rain was poured upon the cities and directs attention to what became of the guilty (7:84). The rescue of the household gives way to the account of the punishment, and the fourth narrative reaches its end.

The two verses together close the account of the people of Lot. The believers are brought out, the one who stayed behind perishes, and the rain comes down upon the cities; and the sūrah's following verses introduce the people of Midian and their messenger Shuʿayb.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:84

> We poured upon them a rain ˹of brimstone˺. See what was the end of the wicked!

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rain Upon the Cities**

The verse states that a rain was poured upon the cities, and the Book's accounts of the destruction of the people of Lot describe the punishment in several forms. The sūrah of Hūd reports that the cities were turned upside down and that stones of baked clay were rained upon them (11:82), the sūrah of the Rocky Tract reports the same overturned cities and the rain of stones (15:74), and the sūrah of the Poets reports the rain that came upon those who had been warned (26:173). The verse here states the rain in a single word and leaves the detail to the other passages.

The Book's variety in describing the punishment answers to the several angles from which the event can be told. The overturning states the removal of the place, the stones state the instrument, and the rain states the manner in which the destruction came down upon the people. The verse here names the rain, the sūrah of the Ant names the punishment (27:58), the sūrah of the Spider names a torment from the sky (29:34), and the sūrah of the Moon names a torrent of stones (54:34).

**The Identification of the Punishment**

The tradition identifies the rain as a rain of stones, and the sūrah of the Winnowing Winds states that the stones were of clay, marked with the Lord for the transgressors (51:33–34). The Book's description of the stones as marked states the deliberate character of the punishment, and the same account states that the stones were reserved for those who had gone beyond the bounds. The verse here gives the briefest statement of the event, and the fuller accounts supply the identification.

The Book's accounts of the peoples that were destroyed name the instrument of each punishment, and the series in the sūrah's narratives has included the flood, the wind, the earthquake, and now the rain and the overturned cities. The list of the instruments states the reach of the power that executed the sentences, and the verse here supplies its place in that list.

**The Command to Look at the End**

The verse closes with the command to see what the end of the wicked was, and the Book uses the same command at the close of several of its accounts of the destroyed peoples. The sūrah's earlier narratives of ʿĀd and Thamūd ended with the destruction and the description of the people, and the command in this verse turns from the event to the instruction that the reader is to draw from it. The sūrah of the Cattle and the sūrah of the Ant use the same formula, and the Book's habit of attaching the command to the narratives states their purpose.

The command addresses the audience of the revelation and directs it to the visible remains of the punished peoples, and the Book's addresses to the Quraysh call on them to travel through the land and consider the ends of those who denied. This verse supplies the command in the account of the fourth community, and the passage that follows begins the fifth narrative of the series.

**The Cities and Their Site**

The Book's addresses to the audience place the ruins of the cities of the plain on the road that the caravans of Arabia travelled, and the sūrah of the Rocky Tract reports the cities as lying on a road that remains (15:76). The present verse does not locate the cities, and the command to consider their end assumes that the audience knows of them. The tradition records the sites and the judgments that the travellers drew from them, and the Book's references to the remains of the punished peoples serve the same purpose.

The Book's use of the visible remains of the destroyed communities appears throughout its addresses, and the sūrah's narratives of ʿĀd and Thamūd likewise assume the audience's knowledge of their dwelling places. The verse under discussion belongs to that method, and the account of the fourth people is closed with the instruction to look.

**The Fitting of the Punishment to the Offence**

The Book's accounts of the communities present the punishments as corresponding to the offences, and the tradition notes the fitting of the rain to the practice of the people who had gathered in their city. The Book states the correspondence in general terms in its narratives of the sūrah, and the destruction of the cities is reported after the refusal of the warning. Here the verse states the end of the wicked without describing the reasoning, and the sūrah's reader is left with the event and the command.

The Book's treatment of the correspondence between deed and consequence appears in its statements that no soul is wronged, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that God does not wrong people in any way and that the wrong is what the people do to themselves. The verse at hand supplies the outcome of the fourth community, and the series continues with the account of the people of Midian.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eighty-fourth in the chapter, and it closes the account of Lot that began with the question in the eightieth verse. The verse that follows introduces the people of Midian and their messenger Shuʿayb (7:85), and the sūrah's series of the prophets enters its fifth account. This verse supplies the destruction of the fourth community and the command to consider its end.

The reader who has followed the account from the charge to the expulsion now reads the outcome of the cities. The rain is poured upon them, the messenger and his household have been saved, and the account turns to the next people and its messenger.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that God sent to the people of Midian their brother Shuʿayb, who called them to worship God alone and to deal justly in measure and weight and to refrain from spreading corruption in the land (7:85). The conclusion of the account of Lot gives way to the fifth narrative, and the message that Shuʿayb brings includes the call to worship and the rules of honest dealing in trade.

The two verses together carry the series forward. One community has been destroyed by the rain, and the next messenger is introduced with a message addressed to the practice of a people of commerce; and the verses that follow record the answer of Midian to the call.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:85

> And to the people of Midian We sent their brother Shu’aib. He said, “O my people! Worship Allah—you have no other god except Him. A clear proof has already come to you from your Lord. So give just measure and weight, do not defraud people of their property, nor spread corruption in the land after it has been set in order. This is for your own good, if you are ˹truly˺ believers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fifth People and Their Messenger**

The verse opens the account of Midian, and it introduces their messenger Shuʿayb in the same form that the sūrah's earlier narratives used for Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, and Lot. The Book tells the story of this people in the sūrah of Hūd (11:84–95), the sūrah of the Poets (26:176–191), the sūrah of the Spider (29:36–37), and the sūrah of the Heights in front of us. The verse names the messenger as the brother of the people, and the term states the kinship that the sūrah's accounts of the prophets assume.

Midian in the tradition is a town and a region on the trade routes between the Ḥijāz and Syria, and the Book's account of Moses names it as the place of his exile, where he met the daughters of the man who later employed him. The verse here does not describe the place, and the account is concerned with the message and the response.

**The Call to Worship the One God**

The message of Shuʿayb begins with the call that the sūrah's four earlier messengers delivered: worship God, for you have no god besides Him. The sūrah of Hūd reports the same opening in its account of Midian (11:84), and the sūrah of the Spider reports the call together with the prohibition of corruption (29:36). The verse here states the call first, and the commands about trade and measure follow it, so that the message of the fifth messenger is presented as a single whole.

The Book's arrangement places the worship of God at the head of the social commands throughout its addresses, and the sūrah's own earlier verses have treated the two as inseparable. The call in this verse supplies the foundation on which the requirements of honest dealing rest, and the account of the people's refusal concerns both halves of the message.

**The Clear Proof**

The verse states that a clear proof has come to the people from their Lord, and the phrase describes the demonstration that accompanied the messenger. The Book uses the words for the signs given to the prophets, and the sūrah's account of Ṣāliḥ used the same expression for the she-camel that was given as a sign (7:73). The verse here does not identify the proof, and the tradition understands it as the message itself together with the signs that confirmed it.

The Book's accounts of the prophets include the signs given to each of them, and the sūrah's narratives state the signs where the accounts require them. This verse leaves the identification open and turns to the requirements that the message carried, and the reader is directed to the substance of what was asked rather than to the form of the demonstration.

**The Commands About Measure and Weight**

The verse requires just measure and weight and forbids the reduction of the people's goods, and the Book's other accounts of Shuʿayb state the same commands at greater length. The sūrah of Hūd reports the instruction to give full measure and to weigh with justice and the prohibition of defrauding and of corruption (11:85), and the sūrah of the Poets reports the messenger's order to give full measure and cause no loss and to weigh with an accurate scale (26:181–182). The present verse states the requirements in their general form, and the fuller accounts supply the detail.

The Book's treatment of honest dealing appears in its commands to the community about measurement and weight, and the sūrah of the Night Journey states the instruction to give in full when measuring and to weigh with an even balance (17:35). The sūrah of the Defrauders opens with the declaration of woe upon those who deal in short measure (83:1), and the Book's teaching on the subject treats the ordinary transactions of the market as a matter of religious obligation. The verse under discussion supplies the first of the commands in the account of Midian.

**The Prohibition of Corruption**

The verse forbids the spreading of corruption in the land after it has been set in order, and the phrase repeats the prohibition that the sūrah addressed to the audience in the fifty-sixth verse (7:56).
