The command belongs to the sūrah's presentation of the prophets' work as the maintenance of the order that God has established, and the sūrah of the Spider reports the same prohibition in the account of Midian (29:36). Here the verse states the prohibition after the commands about trade, and the two are presented as parts of one obligation.

The Book's use of the term for the corruption that spoils the arrangements of life includes the practices of trade and the conduct of the community, and the sūrah's account of the believers' duty to keep the order whole appears in its earlier passage. The verse at hand adds the fifth community's charge to the series, and the narrative that follows states the response of the leaders.

**The Appeal to the People's Own Good**

The verse closes with the statement that what the messenger has laid down is better for the people if they are believers, and the phrase addresses the community's interest rather than the messenger's authority. The sūrah of Hūd reports the same appeal at the end of the messenger's instruction (11:85), and the Book's addresses repeatedly present its commands as the good of those who keep them. This verse states the appeal in the shortest form, and the account then turns to the second part of the messenger's address.

The Book's teaching about the commands as benefit appears in its statements that the good of the servants lies in their obedience, and the sūrah of the Cow states that the guidance is for those who are mindful. The present verse supplies the conclusion of the first part of the address, and the verses that follow continue with the messenger's warnings about the roads and about the deception of the people.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eighty-fifth in the chapter, and it opens the account of Midian after the close of the account of Lot. The verse that follows continues the address with the prohibition of ambushing travellers on the roads and with the reminder of the people's increase in number (7:86), and the exchange with the leaders comes after that (7:88–90). The verse under discussion supplies the opening of the fifth narrative in the series.

The reader who has followed four communities and their messengers now meets the fifth with the same call and with a message addressed to the conduct of a trading people. The worship of God and the honest measure are stated together, and the account proceeds to the people's answer.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse continues the messenger's address, forbidding the people to lie in wait on the roads to threaten the believers and to turn them from the path of God and to make it crooked, and reminding them that they were few and that God increased their number (7:86). The call to the worship of God and to just dealing gives way to the specific complaint about the treatment of travellers and of the believers among them.

The two verses together state the substance of the message to Midian. The people are called to worship God alone, to deal honestly in the market, to refrain from corruption, and to stop using the roads against those who would accept the message; and the verses that follow record the answer of the community's leaders.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:86

> And do not lie in ambush on every road—threatening and hindering those who believe in Allah from His Path and striving to make it ˹appear˺ crooked. Remember when you were few, then He increased you in number. And consider the fate of the corruptors!

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Roads Held Against the Believers**

The verse continues the address of Shuʿayb with the complaint about the use of the roads, and it describes the people as lying in wait on every path to threaten and turn away those who believe in God. The Book's accounts of the community of Midian present the practice as part of their treatment of anyone who accepted the message, and the sūrah of Hūd reports the leaders of Midian threatening to expel the believers unless they returned to the community's creed (11:88). The verse here supplies the specific form of the pressure that the community applied.

The tradition explains the practice as the waylaying of travellers who were coming to the messenger, and the same passage states that the roads of Midian were the place where the people met the outsiders who might be won over to the message. The Book's narratives of the communities record the several means by which they resisted the messengers, and the practice named in this verse belongs to that record.

**Turning Away From the Path of God**

The verse states the purpose of the ambush, which is to turn people away from the path of God, and the phrase repeats the description that the sūrah has applied to the people who bar others from the way. The sūrah's earlier passage on the people of the Heights reported their prayer against being numbered with those who had hindered people from the path of God and sought to make it crooked (7:45), and the sūrah of Hūd reports the same phrase in its account of the opponents of Shuʿayb (11:19). The verse here states the same offence in the messenger's address to his own people.

The Book's use of the phrase about making the path crooked describes the practice of presenting the religion in a manner that repels rather than attracts, and the sūrah's accounts of the opponents repeatedly state the intent. The verse here supplies the charge in the mouth of the messenger, and the people's resistance is presented as the deliberate obstruction of the road to the message.

**The Reminder of the People's Increase**

The verse interrupts the charge with the reminder that the people were few and that God increased their number, and the reminder belongs to the sūrah's habit of attaching the recollection of favours to its warnings. The sūrah's account of ʿĀd included the reminder that they had been made successors after the people of Noah and increased in stature (7:69), and the account of Thamūd included the reminder of the buildings and the settlements (7:74). This verse supplies the fifth community's version of the reminder, and the phrase states the change in the people's condition within living memory.

The Book's use of the people's own history as evidence appears in its addresses to the communities, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the messenger's reminder to Midian of the favours that surrounded them. The present verse states the increase in number, and the reminder serves the argument that the people owed their standing to the God whose path they were blocking.

**The Command to Consider the Fate of the Corruptors**

The verse closes with the command to consider the fate of those who worked corruption, and the command repeats the refrain that the sūrah has attached to the accounts of the destroyed peoples. The account of ʿĀd closed with the statement of the destruction of the deniers (7:72), the account of Thamūd with the earthquake and the lifeless morning (7:78), and the account of Lot with the rain and the command to consider the end of the wicked (7:84). The verse under discussion supplies the same command within the address of the fifth messenger, and the reference is to the ruins of the earlier peoples.

The Book's addresses to the Quraysh call on them to travel through the land and to see the ends of those who denied, and the sūrah of the Romans and the sūrah of the Creator state the command in the same words (30:9; 35:44). Here the verse places the command in the mouth of Shuʿayb, and the sūrah's reader is directed to the same evidence from within the narrative.

**The Offence of the Fifth Community**

The Book's account of Midian presents a community whose offence is measured in economic and social terms: short measure, defrauded customers, corruption in the land, and roads closed to those who accepted the message. The sūrah of the Poets reports the messenger's warning against the practices of trade that withhold what is due (26:181–183), and the sūrah of Hūd reports his appeal to the community's own memory of his standing among them (11:88). The verse at hand states the road offence as one of the charges, and the account then turns to the leaders' response.

The Book's treatment of the varieties of offence in the sūrah's series states the reach of the message that the communities refused, and each community is charged with the practice that its condition produced. This verse supplies the charge of the fifth community, and the narrative that follows records the threat of expulsion and the destruction that came upon the people.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eighty-sixth in the chapter, and it continues the address that began with the call to the worship of God and just dealing. The verse that follows states the messenger's appeal to the two groups within the people to await the judgment of God between them (7:87), and the leaders' threat comes after that (7:88). The present verse supplies the second part of the message to Midian.

The reader who heard the call to honest measure now reads the charge about the roads and the reminder of the people's own history. The obstruction of the path, the increase in number, and the command to consider the earlier peoples are stated together, and the account proceeds to the messenger's declaration of patience.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the messenger's statement that if one group of the people believes in what he has been sent with while another does not, then the two should wait until God judges between them, and it closes with the declaration that He is the best of judges (7:87). The charges in the address give way to the messenger's willingness to leave the dispute to the judgment of God.

The two verses together state the messenger's position before his people. He names the offences, reminds the community of what it has received, and then commits the outcome to the judgment of God; and the verses that follow record the answer of the leaders of Midian.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:87

> If some of you do believe in what I have been sent with while others do not, then be patient until Allah judges between us. He is the Best of Judges.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Division Within the People**

The verse acknowledges that the people of Midian were divided by the message, with one group accepting what the messenger had been sent with and another refusing it, and the acknowledgement states the condition in which the address was delivered. The sūrah's earlier narratives have recorded the leaders' refusal and the acceptance of the weak among Thamūd (7:75–76), and the account of Midian likewise assumes two groups within the community. The verse here addresses both together and leaves the outcome between them.

The Book's accounts of the communities commonly record the division that the message produced, and the sūrah of the Ant reports the people of Thamūd splitting into two disputing groups (27:45). The verse here supplies the fifth community's version of that division, and the messenger's response to it is the patience that the rest of the verse states.

**The Instruction to Wait for the Judgment**

The verse instructs the two groups to wait until God judges between them, and the instruction places the resolution of the dispute in the hands of God rather than in the arena of the community. The sūrah of Hūd reports the same disposition in its account of Shuʿayb, where the messenger states that the outcome rests with God and that he has committed his affair to Him (11:88), and the sūrah's earlier verse in which the messenger asks God to decide between his people and him in truth (7:89) completes the prayer. The verse here supplies the earlier statement of the same position.

The Book's messengers are presented throughout as those who deliver the message and leave the judgment to God, and the sūrah of the Poets reports Noah asking his Lord to judge between him and his people (26:118). This verse states the same disposition in the mouth of Shuʿayb, and the Book's teaching about the duty of the messenger, which is the delivery of the message rather than the compulsion of the people, appears in its accounts of the prophets' answers to their communities.

**The Confidence in the Judge**

The verse closes with the statement that God is the best of judges, and the declaration states the ground of the messenger's patience. The Book's descriptions of God's judgment appear in its accounts of the reckoning and in its instructions to the community about the settlement of disputes, and the sūrah of the Women and the sūrah of the Table state the requirement of judging with justice. The present verse supplies the messenger's confidence, and the phrase names the attribute that makes the waiting reasonable.

The Book's use of the same declaration at the close of the messengers' prayers appears in the sūrah of the Heights in the next verse of the account, where the messenger asks his Lord to judge between him and his people in truth and calls Him the best of those who decide (7:89). The verse under discussion states the attribute before the prayer, and the two verses together present the messenger's position in full.

**The Patience That Is Asked**

The waiting that the verse asks for is patience rather than passivity, and the Book's use of the term in its addresses to the believers carries the sense of endurance in the work while the outcome is awaited. The sūrah of Hūd reports the messenger's commitment of his affair to God and his call to the people to act according to their position while he acts according to his (11:88; 11:93), and the verse here states the same disposition in shorter form.

The Book's teaching about patience under opposition appears in its instructions to the Prophet and to the believers throughout, and the sūrah's narratives of the messengers present each of them continuing the warning after the refusal. Here the verse supplies the form of that patience in the account of the fifth messenger, and the narrative then turns to the threat of the leaders.

**The Two Groups and Their Futures**

The verse's division of the community into believers and deniers prepares the account of the destruction that follows, and the Book's narratives of the communities consistently state the two outcomes. The sūrah of Hūd reports the salvation of Shuʿayb and those who believed with him and the blast that took the wrongdoers (11:94), and the sūrah of the Spider reports the earthquake that struck the people who rejected him (29:37). The verse at hand supplies the messenger's statement of the division before the account of the outcome.

The Book's treatment of the believers as the group that survives the punishment appears in each of the sūrah's narratives, and the account of Midian follows the pattern of the four communities before it. This verse states the division and the waiting, and the passage that follows supplies the leaders' answer and the end of the community.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eighty-seventh in the chapter, and it concludes the address that began with the call to the worship of God and to just dealing. The verse that follows reports the threat of the arrogant leaders to expel the messenger and the believers from the land unless they return to the community's creed (7:88), and the exchange continues to the messenger's prayer and the people's plaint (7:89). The present verse supplies the messenger's willingness to await the judgment.

The reader who has followed the message to Midian now reads the messenger's disposition toward the division within his people. The two groups are named, the judgment is left to God, and the account proceeds to the reaction of the leaders.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the arrogant leaders of the people threatened to expel Shuʿayb and the believers from the land unless they returned to the community's faith, and that the messenger answered with the question of whether they would do so against his will (7:88). The patience stated here meets the threat of expulsion, and the passage continues with the messenger's declaration of trust in God.

The two verses together state the position of the two sides in Midian. The messenger commits the dispute to the judgment of God, the leaders answer with the threat of expulsion, and the account proceeds to the answer of the messenger and to the destruction of the community.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:88

> The arrogant chiefs of his people threatened, “O Shu’aib! We will certainly expel you and your fellow believers from our land, unless you return to our faith.” He replied, “Even if we hate it?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Threat of the Leaders**

The verse reports the answer of the arrogant leaders of Midian, and the answer takes the form of an ultimatum: the messenger and his fellow believers are to leave the land unless they return to the community's creed. The Book's accounts of the communities record the same instrument of pressure in several places, and the sūrah's account of Lot's people used the demand for expulsion in almost the same words (7:82). The verse here supplies the fifth community's version of the threat, and the leaders are again the ones who speak for the people.

The sūrah's narratives have presented the leaders of each community as the group that answers the messenger, and the account of Midian repeats the pattern with the leaders who were arrogant. The Book's description of the community of Midian in the sūrah of Hūd adds the detail that the leaders considered the messenger weak among them and that only his clan protected him from their violence (11:91), and the verse here states the pressure they were able to apply.

**The Expulsion of the Believers**

The threat extends beyond the messenger to those who believed with him, and the Book's accounts of the communities present the pressure on the believers as part of the refusal of the message. The sūrah's earlier account of the people of Lot included the demand that the household be expelled (7:82), and the sūrah of Hūd reports the leaders of Midian threatening to drive the believers out unless they returned to the creed. The verse here states the same demand in the mouth of the leaders, and the gathering of the believers into the account places them beside their messenger.

The Book's treatment of the believers' endurance under the pressure of their communities appears in its addresses to the community of the Prophet, and the sūrah of the Bee reports those who were compelled to renounce their faith and then emigrated and struggled in the cause of God (16:110). The verse here supplies the earlier instance in the account of Midian, and the account that follows states the messenger's answer.

**The Return to the Community's Creed**

The condition attached to the ultimatum is the return of the believers to the creed of the community, and the term states the inherited religion that the sūrah's narratives have repeatedly presented as the ground of the refusal. The sūrah's earlier account of ʿĀd reported the people's appeal to what their forefathers had worshipped (7:70), and the account of the ancestors' practice recurs in the sūrah of Hūd's version of the Midianites' answer (11:87). This verse states the same condition, and the reply of the messenger addresses it directly.

The Book's treatment of the inherited religion as the obstacle to the message appears in its accounts of the communities throughout, and the sūrah of the Ornament reports the disbelievers stating that they found their forefathers on a way and were following in their footsteps (43:23). The present verse supplies the fifth community's version of the appeal, and the messenger's answer rejects it in the next verse.

**The Messenger's Answer**

The verse closes with the messenger's question of whether the leaders would compel the believers to return even though they detest the creed, and the question states the impossibility of the demand rather than bargaining over its terms. The sūrah of Hūd reports the fuller answer of the messenger, in which he asks his people to consider whether he stands on a clear proof from his Lord and states that he has no desire to act contrary to what he forbids them (11:88). The verse under discussion supplies the first word of that answer, and the following verse expands it.

The Book's accounts of the messengers' answers to compulsion state their refusal to abandon the message and their commitment of the matter to God, and the sūrah of the Cave reports the young men's declaration that they would never call upon any god besides Him (18:14). Here the verse supplies the question that opens the refusal, and the passage that follows states the grounds on which the refusal rests.

**The Leaders and the Community**

The leaders speak in the verse for the community, and the Book's accounts of the communities present the leaders as the group whose decision the people followed. The sūrah's narratives have shown the same arrangement in the accounts of ʿĀd, Thamūd, and Lot's people, and the sūrah of the Confederates reports the followers in the Hereafter blaming their leaders for leading them astray (33:67). The verse at hand supplies the threat of the leaders of Midian, and the account states the outcome of the course they chose.

The Book's teaching about the responsibility of those who answer for others appears in its descriptions of the leaders of the communities and in its statements about the followers who take their religion from them. This verse states the ultimatum, and the narrative proceeds to the answer of the messenger and to the destruction that came upon the people.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eighty-eighth in the chapter, and it answers the messenger's statement of patience in the previous verse. The verse that follows continues the answer with the messenger's declaration that the believers would be fabricating a lie against God if they returned to the creed and with his prayer that God decide between the two parties in truth (7:89), and the leaders' final warning comes after that (7:90). The present verse supplies the confrontation between the messenger and the leaders of Midian.

The reader who heard the messenger commit the dispute to God now reads the leaders' reply. The ultimatum is stated, the messenger's question follows, and the account proceeds to his full answer and to the prayer that closes the exchange.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the messenger's answer in full: that the believers would be inventing a lie about God if they returned to the creed after God had saved them from it, that nothing of the kind would happen unless God willed it, that their Lord encompasses everything in knowledge, that in God they put their trust, and that they asked their Lord to judge between them and their people in truth (7:89). The ultimatum of the leaders gives way to the messenger's declaration of trust, and the passage then reports the final warning of the leaders.

The two verses together state the exchange at the height of the confrontation in Midian. The leaders threaten expulsion and demand the abandonment of the message, and the messenger refuses the terms and places the matter with his Lord; and the account proceeds to the end of the community.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:89

> We would surely be fabricating a lie against Allah if we were to return to your faith after Allah has saved us from it. It does not befit us to return to it unless it is the Will of Allah, our Lord. Our Lord has encompassed everything in ˹His˺ knowledge. In Allah we trust. Our Lord! Judge between us and our people with truth. You are the best of those who judge.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal of the Creed**

The verse gives the messenger's answer to the ultimatum of the leaders, and the answer states that a return to the creed of the community would be the invention of a lie about God. The messenger had been saved from the creed, and the phrase states the change as an act of God rather than as a private decision. The Book's accounts of the messengers present their separation from the religion of their people as the work of God, and the sūrah of Hūd reports the same reasoning in its account of Midian (11:88). The verse here states the refusal in the terms of truth and falsehood.

The Book's treatment of the accusation of inventing lies about God appears in its accounts of the disbelievers who attribute to Him what He has not commanded, and the sūrah's earlier passage on the forbidden things reported the charge of inventing lies against God (7:33). The verse here turns the charge around: the return to the ancestral creed would be the fabrication, and the messenger states the consequence of such a return before the community's leaders.

**The Doing of the Impossible**

The verse states that the return would take place only if God willed it, and the phrase places the entire matter within the divine will. The Book's use of the same clause appears in its statements about the dependence of all things on the will of God, and the sūrah of the Cave reports the young men's words about the will of their Lord (18:24). The verse here states the messenger's submission to the same will, and the clause serves to state the impossibility of the proposed return rather than to keep the question open.

The Book's teaching about the divine will appears in its descriptions of the guidance and the misguidance, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that God guides whom He wills and leaves whom He wills astray. This verse supplies the messenger's statement in the account of Midian, and the mention of the will is followed by the statement of the divine knowledge that encompasses everything.

**The Knowledge That Encompasses Everything**

The verse states that the Lord encompasses everything in knowledge, and the declaration prepares the prayer that follows. The Book's descriptions of the divine knowledge appear in its statements that nothing is hidden from God and that He knows what is in the hearts, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that God's knowledge encompasses all things (6:80). The present verse supplies the messenger's declaration of that knowledge, and the reader is shown the basis on which the dispute is being left to God.

The Book's use of the phrase about encompassing appears in its accounts of the divine attributes, and the sūrah of the Women states that God encompasses all things in knowledge (4:126). The verse under discussion supplies the attestation in the messenger's own words, and the following clause states the trust that the knowledge makes possible.

**The Trust in God**

The verse states that in God the messenger puts his trust, and the declaration is the axis of his answer to the leaders. The Book's instructions to the Prophet to place his reliance on God appear throughout, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the messengers' statement that they rely upon God alone (26:217). Here the verse supplies the same declaration in the mouth of Shuʿayb, and the answer to compulsion is the trust that leaves the outcome with the One who has the power over it.

The Book's treatment of reliance as the disposition of the believers appears in its addresses to the community, and the sūrah of the Gathering states that the believers are those whose hearts are not dismayed by what they encounter and who put their trust in their Lord (3:159). The verse at hand supplies the narrative instance of that disposition, and the account continues with the prayer that closes the messenger's speech.

**The Prayer for a True Judgment**

The verse closes with the messenger's prayer that his Lord judge between him and his people with truth and with the declaration that God is the best of those who judge. The prayer repeats the request of the previous verse, and its position at the end of the answer gives the messenger's speech its conclusion. The Book's accounts of the prophets' prayers against their peoples include the requests of Noah and of other messengers for a judgment between them and the deniers, and the sūrah of the Poets reports one of those prayers (26:118).

The Book's descriptions of God as the best of judges appear in its statements about the reckoning and in the sūrah's own accounts, and the sūrah of the Prophets states that God judges with truth (21:112). This verse supplies the prayer in the account of Midian, and the narrative that follows reports the last word of the leaders and the destruction that came upon the people.

**The Messenger's Answer and the Community's End**

The answer in this verse is the last full statement of the messenger in the account, and the words place the resolution of the dispute beyond the reach of the community's threats. The Book's narratives of the communities follow the same pattern in each case, and the answer to the ultimatum is followed by the punishment. The present verse supplies the conclusion of the exchange, and the leaders' final warning and the earthquake follow in the next verses.

The Book's presentation of the messengers as those who have discharged their duty when they have delivered the message appears in the account's closing address, which reports the messenger's statement that he delivered the messages of his Lord and gave sincere counsel (7:93). The verse under discussion supplies the answer of the messenger at the height of the dispute, and the narrative proceeds to the end of the community.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands eighty-ninth in the chapter, and it answers the ultimatum of the previous verse. The verse that follows reports the leaders' warning to the people that following Shuʿayb would make them losers (7:90), and the destruction of the community follows in the two verses after that (7:91–92). Here the verse supplies the messenger's reply in full to the threat of expulsion.

The reader who heard the leaders demand the abandonment of the message now reads the messenger's refusal in the language of truth, will, knowledge, trust, and prayer. The answer is complete, and the account turns to the last warning that the leaders gave and to the end of the people.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the disbelieving leaders of the people warned the community that those who followed Shuʿayb would be the losers (7:90). The messenger's declaration of trust gives way to the leaders' final attempt to hold the community to its inherited religion, and the account then records the punishment.

The two verses together state the last stage of the confrontation in Midian. The messenger refuses the ultimatum and leaves the judgment to God, the leaders warn the people against the message, and the passage that follows reports the earthquake that took the community.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:90

> The disbelieving chiefs of his people threatened, “If you follow Shu’aib, you will surely be losers!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning of the Leaders**

The verse reports the last warning of the leaders of Midian, and the warning is addressed to the community rather than to the messenger. The leaders tell the people that following Shuʿayb would make them losers, and the sentence attempts to separate the community from the messenger by appealing to the people's interest. The Book's accounts of the communities present the leaders as the group that holds the people to the inherited religion, and the sūrah of Hūd reports the leaders of Midian in the same role (11:87).

The Book's presentation of the elite's warnings to the community appears in its narratives of the several peoples, and the sūrah of the Ornament reports the elite of every society telling the people that they found their forefathers on a way and were following in their footsteps (43:23). The verse here supplies the fifth community's version of the warning, and the account then states the outcome of the course that the leaders urged.

**The Term the Leaders Use**

The leaders describe the followers of the messenger as losers, and the term is turned back on them in the account's next verse, which states that those who denied Shuʿayb were the losers themselves. The Book's narratives repeatedly place the same word in the mouths of the opponents and then apply it to them, and the sūrah of the Troops reports the disbelievers vowing not to believe in the Book and states that the outcome belongs to the wrongdoers (34:31). The verse here states the accusation, and the account supplies the reversal.

The Book's treatment of the accusation of loss appears in its descriptions of those who refuse the message, and the sūrah of the Cow reports the disbelievers calling the believers fools while the description belonged to themselves (2:13). The verse here supplies the same kind of reversal in the account of Midian, and the narrative that follows states which of the two groups was in fact lost.

**The Appeal to the Community's Interest**

The warning rests on the claim that the message would ruin the people, and the claim states the leaders' understanding of the community's interest in terms of its inherited arrangements. The Book's accounts of the communities present the same appeal in each case, and the sūrah of the Confederates reports the followers in the Hereafter acknowledging that their leaders led them astray (33:67). This verse states the warning in the fewest words, and the reader is shown what the community heard from its leaders at the moment when the truth was being decided.

The Book's descriptions of the world's estimate of the believers and their opponents appear in its statements about the ends of the two groups, and the sūrah of the Troops reports the disbelievers describing the believers as those who follow the lowest among them. The present verse supplies the same kind of judgment from the leaders of Midian, and the account that follows reverses it in the manner of the Book's narratives.

**The Message and the Market**

The Book's version of the conflict in Midian places the community's wealth at the centre of the argument, and the leaders warn the people that the message threatens their position. The sūrah of Hūd reports the leaders asking sarcastically whether the messenger's prayer requires them to abandon what their forefathers worshipped and to stop doing what they wished with their property (11:87), and the sūrah's account in this chapter has stated the commands about measure and weight that the message carried. The verse under discussion supplies the conclusion the leaders drew from those commands, and the narrative presents the warning as the last attempt to hold the community together around its practice.

The Book's treatment of wealth as a test appears in its instructions to the community about honest dealing and in its statements that the world's goods are the provision of the world, and the sūrah's series of the communities includes peoples whose ruin was bound up with their prosperity. Here the verse supplies the account of Midian's leaders, and the destruction that follows states the end of the position they defended.

**The Last Word of the Leaders**

The verse is the last speech of the leaders in the account, and the sentence they choose is a warning about loss. The Book's narratives often end the record of a community's leaders with a statement that the reader can measure against the outcome, and the account of the destruction of Midian follows immediately. The verse at hand supplies that last word, and the verses that follow state the earthquake and the description of the people who had denied their messenger.

The Book's use of such closings appears in its accounts of the peoples throughout, and the sūrah of the Troops reports the disbelievers' vows and then states what will be done with what they have earned (34:33). This verse belongs to that method, and the narrative proceeds to the outcome.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands ninetieth in the chapter, and it follows the messenger's prayer and precedes the report of the earthquake. The verse that follows states that the earthquake seized the people and that morning found them lifeless in their homes (7:91), and the sūrah's account then states that those who denied Shuʿayb were as though they had never lived there and that they were the losers (7:92). The present verse supplies the leaders' final warning.

The reader who heard the messengers' refusal now reads the warning that the leaders gave the community. The accusation of loss is stated, the earthquake follows, and the account answers the accusation in its own words.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that an overwhelming earthquake struck the people and that they fell lifeless in their homes (7:91). The leaders' warning to the community gives way to the punishment, and the account of the fifth people approaches its close.

The two verses together state the end of the confrontation in Midian. The leaders warn the people against the message, the punishment arrives, and the following verse states the description of the community that denied its messenger.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:91

> Then an ˹overwhelming˺ earthquake struck them and they fell lifeless in their homes.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Earthquake**

The verse states that an overwhelming earthquake struck the people of Midian, and the account of their destruction uses the same words that the sūrah applied to the people of Thamūd (7:78). The sūrah of the Spider reports the destruction of Midian with the same term, stating that the earthquake seized them and that they fell lifeless in their homes (29:37), and the sūrah of Hūd reports the blast that overtook the wrongdoers in the same manner (11:94). The verse here supplies the sūrah's own statement of the punishment, and the repetition of the wording ties the fifth community's end to the third.

The Book's accounts of the communities name the instruments of the punishments in a variety of forms, and the earthquake appears among them for the peoples of Ṣāliḥ and Shuʿayb. The verse here states the event in a single sentence, and the sūrah's narrative style leaves the description of the terror to the reader's understanding of what an earthquake at that hour would mean.

**The Repetition of the Ending**

The verse's second clause states that the people were found in the morning lying lifeless in their homes, and the phrase repeats the ending of the account of Thamūd word for word. The Book's use of the same formula for two communities states the identity of the outcome for those who refused the messengers, and the sūrah's reader is meant to recognize the repetition. The verse here supplies the second instance of the formula, and the narrative that follows states the description of the people in the terms of a vanished settlement.

The Book's treatment of the destroyed peoples as examples appears in its addresses to the later generations, and the command to consider the fate of the communities recurs throughout the sūrah. This verse supplies the event to which the command refers in the case of Midian, and the verses that follow state the erasure of the community from the record of the living.

**The Account of the Midianites' Destruction in the Tradition**

The tradition adds details to the punishment of Midian, reporting that the people were first afflicted by a terrible heat that gave them no relief in shade or water, and that a cloud was then sent to bring them cool breezes. The people gathered beneath the cloud, and it released upon them fire and flame, after which the earthquake took them. The sūrah of Hūd reports the blast and the destruction of the wrongdoers (11:94), and the sūrah of the Poets reports the punishment of the day of the overshadowing cloud (26:189). The present verse states the earthquake, and the tradition and the parallel passages supply the rest.

The Book's accounts of the peoples include several punishments that arrived in stages, and the sūrah's narratives state the final removal of each community. The verse under discussion supplies the end of Midian, and the account continues with the description of what the people became in the memory of those who came after them.

**The Absence of Rescue**

The verse reports no rescue for the community of Midian, and the account's silence on the subject states that the warning had been fully given before the punishment came. The sūrah of Hūd reports the salvation of Shuʿayb and those who believed with him by a mercy from God before the blast took the wrongdoers (11:94), and the account in this chapter ends the messenger's speech with his statement that he had delivered the message and given counsel (7:93). Here the verse supplies the destruction of those who remained, and the two passages together state the division that the message had produced.

Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the punishments consistently attach the salvation of the believers to the destruction of the deniers, and the sūrah of the Troops states that God saves the messengers and those who believe (10:103). The verse at hand states the second half of that arrangement in the account of Midian, and the narrative proceeds to the description of the people who were lost.

**The Formula and Its Purpose**

The Book's repetition of the same sentence for several communities serves the argument of the sūrah, which presents the history of the destroyed peoples as a single pattern in which the names change and the ending does not. The account of Noah ended with the rescue and the drowning (7:64), the account of ʿĀd with the salvation and the cutting off (7:72), the account of Thamūd with the earthquake and the lifeless morning (7:78), the account of Lot with the rain and the command to look at the end of the wicked (7:84), and the account of Midian with the earthquake in front of us. The series states the constancy of the outcome.

The Book's use of the pattern as a warning to the audience appears in its addresses to the Quraysh and in the sūrah's command to consider the fate of the earlier peoples. This verse supplies the fifth ending in the series, and the following verse states the judgment on the community in the words that the leaders had used against the messenger.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands ninety-first in the chapter, and it follows the leaders' warning to the community and precedes the description of the people who denied their messenger. The verse that follows states that those who rejected Shuʿayb were as though they had never lived in the land and that they were the losers (7:92), and the messenger's farewell comes after that (7:93). The present verse supplies the destruction of the fifth community.

The reader who heard the leaders warn the people against following the messenger now reads what came upon the community. The earthquake strikes, the people are found lifeless in their homes, and the account states in the next verse what became of those who had answered the message with a warning about loss.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that those who rejected Shuʿayb were wiped out as though they had never lived there and that those who rejected Shuʿayb were themselves the losers (7:92). The destruction reported here is followed by the verdict on the community, and the word that the leaders had used against the messenger is applied to them.

The two verses together close the account of the fifth people. The earthquake takes the community, the record states that their dwelling places knew them no more, and the account answers the leaders' warning with the judgment that the Book pronounces on those who denied their messenger.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:92

> Those who rejected Shu’aib were ˹wiped out˺ as if they had never lived there. Those who rejected Shu’aib were the true losers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Erasure of the Community**

The verse states that those who rejected Shuʿayb were as though they had never lived in the land, and the phrase describes the completeness of the removal. The sūrah of Hūd uses the same words in its account of Midian and adds the sentence that Midian was removed as Thamūd had been removed (11:95), and the sūrah of the Spider states that the community was left lifeless in its homes (29:37). The verse here supplies the sūrah's own statement of the erasure, and the repetition of the name of the messenger in the sentence places the offence at the centre of the account.

The Book's descriptions of the destroyed peoples include the vanishing of their settlements from the memory of the living, and the sūrah's earlier accounts stated that the people of ʿĀd were cut off and that the people of Thamūd lay lifeless in their homes. The verse here supplies the fifth community's version of the erasure, and the sentence states that a people with houses, trade, and numbers became as though it had never existed.

**The Repetition of the Name**

The verse repeats the description of the offenders, stating twice that those who rejected Shuʿayb were the ones concerned, and the repetition marks the sentence in the manner of the sūrah's emphatic passages. The Book's narratives use repetition for the sake of emphasis in their verdicts, and the sūrah of the Moon repeats the refrain of the punishment of the peoples throughout its account. The verse here supplies the verdict of the account of Midian, and the two clauses together state the identification of the offenders and the reversal of their expectation.

The Book's use of the messenger's name in the description of the offenders states that the community was defined by its answer to him, and the sūrah's earlier verses have presented the people's identity in terms of the practice they defended. This verse replaces that identity with the description that survives them, and the narrative states what the people became in the record.

**The Reversal of the Warning**

The leaders of Midian had warned the community that following Shuʿayb would make them losers (7:90), and the verse in front of us states that those who rejected him were the losers themselves. The Book's narratives repeatedly place a judgment in the mouths of the opponents and then pronounce it upon them, and the sūrah of the Troops reports the disbelievers' vows and the punishment that follows (34:33). The present verse supplies the reversal in the account of Midian, and the word that the leaders had used against the message is returned to them by the record of the event.

The Book's treatment of the world's estimates and their correction appears in its statements that the losers are those who lose their own souls, and the sūrah of the Troops reports the disbelievers acknowledging their loss on the day of the reckoning. The verse under discussion states the loss of the community of Midian in the world, and the account's repetition of the word states that the calculation the leaders had made was wrong in the direction opposite to the one they feared.

**The Loss of the World and the Loss to Come**

The Book distinguishes between the loss that the punishment of the world brings and the loss that the reckoning brings, and the sūrah's accounts of the communities state the first while their warnings have pointed to the second. The sūrah's narratives of the destruction are addressed to an audience that could see the ruins of the punished peoples, and the loss stated in this verse is the one that the contemporaries of those peoples could measure. Here the verse supplies the visible judgment, and the sūrah's warnings about the Hereafter carry the same word into the final accounting.

The Book's teaching about the two losses appears in its descriptions of those who lose this world and the next, and the sūrah's opening section on the weighing of deeds stated that those whose scales were light would be the ones who lost their souls (7:9). The verse at hand states the loss of the fifth community in the terms of the world, and the reader is left to draw the comparison with the loss that the reckoning states.

**The End of the Fifth Account**

The verse completes the account of Midian's destruction, and the messenger's farewell follows in the next verse. The Book's accounts of the communities conclude with a statement of the outcome before the messenger's last words, and the sūrah's narratives have followed that order in each case. This verse supplies the outcome for the fifth people, and the narrative then gives the messenger's assessment of his work among them.

The Book's treatment of the messengers' closings includes the statement that they delivered the message and gave counsel, and the sūrah's accounts of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, and now Shuʿayb have all included the statement in one form or another. The present verse supplies the destruction that gives the messenger's words their setting, and the account moves to its end.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands ninety-second in the chapter, and it follows the report of the earthquake and precedes the messenger's farewell. The verse that follows reports Shuʿayb's turning away from the people and his statement that he had delivered the messages of his Lord and given sincere counsel, and his question of how he could grieve for a disbelieving people (7:93). The verse under discussion supplies the verdict on the fifth community.

The reader who saw the people fall lifeless in their homes now reads that they were as though they had never lived and that they were the losers. The account of Midian approaches its close, and the sūrah's series of the prophets turns after it to the general lessons of the destroyed towns.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the messenger's turning away from his people with the statement that he had delivered his Lord's messages and given sincere counsel and with the question of how he could grieve for a disbelieving people (7:93). The verdict on the community gives way to the messenger's own words, and the fifth narrative reaches its end.

The two verses together close the account of Midian. The people are erased from the land and named as the losers, and the messenger states that he performed his duty among them; and the verses that follow turn from the narratives of the prophets to the lessons that the sūrah draws from them all.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:93

> He turned away from them, saying, “O my people! Indeed, I have delivered to you the messages of my Lord and gave you ˹sincere˺ advice. How can I then grieve for those who chose to disbelieve?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Farewell of the Fifth Messenger**

The verse reports the turning away of Shuʿayb from his people and his address to them, and the words repeat the farewell of Ṣāliḥ in the same sūrah with a final clause altered. The sūrah's account of the third messenger reported his statement that he had conveyed his Lord's message and given sincere advice and his complaint that the people did not love sincere advisers (7:79), and the verse here reports the same delivery and counsel and adds the question of how the messenger could grieve for a disbelieving people. The Book's two accounts thus close in the same form, and the difference between the endings states the difference in the messengers' situations.

The tradition discusses the setting of the words, as it did with the farewell of Ṣāliḥ: the address may have been delivered when the messenger left the settlement before the punishment, or over the ruins after the event. The verse here does not settle the question, and the words suit both, since they state the completed work and the disposition of the people rather than a further appeal.

**The Delivery and the Counsel**

The verse states that the messenger delivered the messages of his Lord and gave sincere counsel, and the two activities are stated in the same terms as in the farewells of Noah, Hūd, and Ṣāliḥ (7:62; 7:68; 7:79). The Book presents the delivery of the revelation and the counsel offered to the people as the substance of the prophetic office, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran states that the messenger's duty is only to deliver the message (3:20). The verse here supplies the last of the sūrah's five statements of that duty, and the repetition across the narratives states the constancy of the office.

The counsel offered to Midian had included the call to worship, the commands about measure and weight, the prohibition of corruption, and the warnings about the roads and about the fate of the earlier peoples (7:85–86). The verse here states the counsel as a completed act, and the account places it beside the message that the people had refused.

**The Question About Grief**

The verse closes with the messenger's question of how he could grieve for a disbelieving people, and the question states the separation between the messenger and the fate of those who refused the warning. The Book's addresses to the Prophet speak of his grief for a people who would not believe, and the sūrah of the Poets opens with the statement that he would perhaps destroy himself with grief that they did not believe (26:3). This verse supplies the same disposition in the narrative of Shuʿayb, and the question states that the messenger's sorrow has reached its limit with the refusal of the message.

The Book's teaching about the limits of the messengers' responsibility appears in its statements that the messenger's duty is the delivery of the message and not the compulsion of the people, and the sūrah of the Cave states that the Prophet is not a guardian over the people (6:107). The present verse supplies the narrative instance of that teaching, and the question phrased as a question states the resolution of the messenger's position rather than a complaint.

**The Two Farewells Compared**

The farewells of Ṣāliḥ and Shuʿayb differ in their last clauses, and the difference states the conditions of the two peoples. Ṣāliḥ complained that his people did not love sincere advisers, and Shuʿayb asked how he could grieve for the people who chose disbelief. The Book's narratives give each messenger the words that fit the account of his people, and the two closings together state the varieties of the refusal that the sūrah has been tracing.

The Book's accounts of the messengers' reactions to their peoples' refusal include the Prophet's grief at the end of the sūrah of the Cave and the sūrah of the Poets, and the tradition records his anxiety for the people of Makkah. The verse under discussion supplies the earlier instance in the series, and the sūrah's reader is shown the disposition of a messenger towards a community that had passed beyond the reach of the warning.

**The End of the Punishment Narratives**

The verse closes the series of the prophets' accounts that began with Noah in the fifty-ninth verse of the sūrah, and the following verses turn to the general lessons that the narratives carry. The Book's arrangement places the lesson after the history, and the sūrah of Hūd and the sūrah of the Poets use the same order. Here the verse supplies the last word of the last messenger in the series, and the passage that follows states the meaning of the whole sequence.

The Book's use of the histories of the communities as the material of its warnings appears in its addresses throughout, and the sūrah's reader is now given the section that draws the lesson in general terms. The verse at hand supplies the transition from the particular accounts to the general instruction, and the following verse states the way in which God dealt with the societies that received the prophets.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands ninety-third in the chapter, and it closes the account of Midian that began with the call of Shuʿayb (7:85). The verse that follows opens the general section on the societies and on the adversity that accompanied the sending of the prophets (7:94), and that section continues to the hundred-and-second verse. This verse supplies the conclusion of the fifth narrative.

The reader who has followed five communities and their messengers now reads the last of the farewells, and the sūrah turns from the particular histories to their meaning. The duty of the messengers is stated once more, the disposition of the people is named, and the passage that follows begins the summary of the whole series.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that whenever God sent a prophet to a society, He afflicted its people with suffering and adversity so that they might be humbled (7:94). The farewell of the last messenger gives way to the general law of the sending, and the sūrah begins the section that draws out the lessons of the five accounts.

The two verses together carry the sūrah from the history of the communities to its interpretation. Five messengers have delivered their messages and five peoples have been removed, and the verses that follow state the pattern that governed the dealings of God with all of them.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:94

> Whenever We sent a prophet to a society, We afflicted its ˹disbelieving˺ people with suffering and adversity, so perhaps they would be humbled.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Beginning of the General Section**

The verse opens the passage that draws the lesson of the sūrah's five punishment narratives, and the section that begins here runs to the hundred-and-second verse. The Book's arrangement places the general statement after the particular histories, and the sūrah of Hūd and the sūrah of the Poets use the same order. The verse here states the way in which God dealt with the societies that received the messengers, and the reader is given the rule that the narratives have illustrated.

The Book's habit of stating the rule after the examples appears in its addresses to the communities, and the sūrah's narratives have supplied the material for the statement. The verse here supplies the first half of the lesson, and the passage that follows states what became of the peoples who did not respond to the hardship.

**The Sending of the Prophets**

The verse states that God did not send a prophet to a society without an accompanying trial, and the statement places the hardship within the economy of the sending rather than beside it. The Book's accounts of the prophets report the difficulties that attended their missions in several passages, and the sūrah's narratives have shown each messenger meeting the refusal of his people. The verse here states the general form of that experience, and the adversity is presented as part of the message rather than as an unrelated misfortune.

The Book's treatment of the sending of the messengers as a mercy appears in its statements that God would not destroy a society before sending a warner, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that the sending is because the Lord would not destroy a society for wrongs while its people were unaware (6:131). This verse states the trial that accompanied the sending, and the sūrah of the Stories reports the principle that God never destroys a community until He has sent a messenger to its capital (28:59).

**The Nature of the Adversity**

The verse names the adversity as suffering and loss, and the tradition explains the terms as poverty, hunger, illness, and the loss of wealth and children. The Book's accounts of the peoples include the years of drought that came upon the people of Pharaoh and the loss of their produce, and the sūrah's own account of the plagues states the strokes that were sent upon Egypt. The present verse states the general form of the trial, and the sūrah's narratives supply the particular instances.

The Book's treatment of hardship as a warning appears in its addresses to the communities and in its statements about the peoples that were tested before it came to the final punishment, and the sūrah of the Prostration states that God will make the people taste the nearer punishment before the greater one (32:21). The verse under discussion states the milder form of that principle, and the account that follows states what happened when the hardship passed.

**The Purpose of the Humbling**

The verse states that the trial was sent so that the people might be humbled, and the purpose clause states the intended effect of the adversity. The Book's teaching about humility as the response to hardship appears in the sūrah of the Cattle, which asks why the people did not humble themselves when the suffering came and states that their hearts were hardened instead (6:43). Here the verse supplies the same expectation in general form, and the sūrah's reader is shown what the peoples were given the opportunity to do.

The Book's descriptions of the peoples that refused to humble themselves include the sūrah of the Believers, which reports that God seized them with torment and they did not humble themselves before their Lord (23:76). The verse at hand states the purpose of the trial, and the passage that follows states how the peoples of the towns answered the opportunity in the ordinary course of events.

**The Lesson for the Sūrah's Audience**

The verse's statement of the rule is addressed to the sūrah's audience as much as to the peoples of the narratives, and the Makkan listeners had known their own seasons of hardship and of relief. The Book's addresses to the Quraysh recall the famine and the years of scarcity that had come upon them, and the sūrah of the Smoke and the sūrah of the Bee refer to the trials that had touched the city. This verse supplies the general statement, and the passage that follows states the alternative that the peoples had failed to take.

The Book's treatment of the world's comforts as an opportunity for reflection appears in its statements about the signs in the alternation of ease and difficulty, and the sūrah's earlier verses have presented the created order as evidence of the Provider. The present verse supplies the opening of the section, and the verses that follow trace the cycle of adversity, prosperity, and sudden seizure.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands ninety-fourth in the chapter, and it follows the close of the accounts of the five peoples and precedes the statement of the cycle of prosperity and seizure. The verse that follows reports that the adversity was replaced by prosperity until the people forgot and argued that their forefathers had known the same alternation, and that the seizure came upon them suddenly (7:95). The verse under discussion supplies the first statement of the general lesson.

The reader who has followed the histories of the destroyed communities now reads the rule that explains them. The sending of the prophets was accompanied by hardship and loss, the purpose was the humbling of the people, and the passage that follows states what the peoples did with the opportunity.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the adversity was changed into prosperity until the people multiplied and said that their forefathers had been visited by hardship and ease as well, and that God seized them suddenly while they were unaware (7:95). The statement of the purpose of the hardship gives way to the account of how the peoples passed through it, and the section continues with the blessings that the faithful would have received.

The two verses together state the first half of the general lesson. The trial comes, the opportunity to humble oneself is given, and the people who take the easing of the trial as the ordinary course of the world are seized without warning; and the verses that follow state the alternative and the security that the heedless imagined.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:95

> Then We changed their adversity to prosperity until they flourished and argued ˹falsely˺, “Our forefathers ˹too˺ had been visited by adversity and prosperity.” So We seized them by surprise, while they were unaware.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Replacement of Adversity by Prosperity**

The verse states that God replaced the hardship of the peoples with good until they multiplied, and the account describes the recovery of the communities that had been tested. The Book's narratives of the peoples present the alternation of difficulty and ease as the ordinary course of the world, and the sūrah's general section states the cycle in a single sentence. The verse here supplies the second stage of the trial, and the prosperity is presented as another opportunity rather than as a sign of approval.

The Book's accounts of the peoples that were given wealth and children after hardship include the sūrah of the Cattle's statement that God showered the people with everything they desired when they became oblivious to the warnings (6:44). The verse here states the same sequence, and the multiplication of the people is named as the mark of the prosperity that followed the years of loss.

**The Argument of the Forefathers**

The verse reports the argument that the people made when the prosperity came: that their forefathers had been visited by both hardship and ease, so that the alternation was nothing more than the way of the world. The argument draws the conclusion that the religion has no claim upon them, and the Book's narratives place the same reasoning in the mouths of the communities. The verse here states the argument in the words of the people, and the reader is shown how the trial was neutralized in the memory of the community.

The Book's treatment of the appeal to the experience of the ancestors appears in the sūrah's accounts of the communities' refusals, where the practice of the forefathers is offered as the ground for continuing in the inherited religion (7:70; 7:28). This verse supplies a different use of the same appeal, and the argument in this verse turns the plain facts of the people's history into evidence that nothing was being asked of them.

**The Failure of the Warning**

The verse states that the people argued falsely, and the description states that the conclusion they drew from the alternation of their fortunes did not follow from what had happened to them. The Book's accounts of the peoples present the same failure to read the signs in each of the narratives, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that the hearts of the people were hardened when the suffering came (6:43). The present verse supplies the moment at which the warning was set aside, and the passage states that the opportunity for humbling had passed.

The Book's descriptions of the signs that the peoples failed to read include the created order, the messengers, and the events of their own history, and the sūrah's narratives have presented each of those witnesses. The verse under discussion supplies the closing of the opportunity in the general section, and the account of the sudden seizure follows immediately.

**The Seizure by Surprise**

The verse states that the people were seized suddenly while they were unaware, and the phrase states the manner of the punishment in the general section as it did in the particular accounts. The sūrah's narratives of the destroyed communities reported the flood, the wind, the earthquake, and the rain coming upon the peoples at the ordinary hours of their lives, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the punishment that took the community while they slept or while they played. Here the verse states the suddenness in general form, and the passage that follows expands the theme of the security that the peoples imagined.

The Book's treatment of the arrival of the punishment without warning appears in its statements about the Hour and in its accounts of the communities, and the sūrah of the Ornament states that the Hour will come upon the people suddenly while they dispute among themselves (43:66). The verse at hand supplies the general statement of the seizure, and the sūrah's reader is directed to the false confidence that the peoples had built upon their prosperity.

**The Lesson of the Cycle**

The verse presents the cycle of hardship, prosperity, and seizure as the pattern that the peoples of the towns experienced, and the section's purpose is to state the cost of reading the cycle as a matter of course. The Book's teaching about the signs in the alternation of the days appears in its statements that the people are tested by good and evil, and the sūrah of the Prophets states that God tests the people with evil and with good as a trial (21:35). This verse states the general rule, and the verses that follow state what the people of the towns would have received had they believed.

The Book's treatment of prosperity as a test appears in its descriptions of the communities that were given abundance and used it against the messengers, and the sūrah's narratives have supplied the instances. The present verse supplies the general statement of the failure, and the passage that follows draws the contrast with the believing response.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands ninety-fifth in the chapter, and it follows the statement of the purpose of the hardship and precedes the contrast between the believing and the denying towns. The verse that follows states that if the people of the towns had believed and been mindful, the blessings of heaven and earth would have been opened to them, and that they were seized for what they had committed (7:96). The verse under discussion supplies the account of the peoples' response to the cycle of the trial.

The reader who has followed the five histories now reads the general account of how the peoples passed through hardship into prosperity and how they read the passage. The warning is set aside, the seizure comes, and the next verse states what the faithful response would have brought.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that if the people of the towns had believed and observed their duty, God would have opened for them blessings from the heaven and the earth, but that they denied and were seized for what they used to earn (7:96). The account of the peoples' misinterpretation of their fortunes gives way to the statement of the alternative, and the section continues with the questions about the security that the towns imagined.

The two verses together state the loss that the peoples brought upon themselves. The prosperity that followed hardship was read as evidence that nothing was required of them, and the passage that follows states what the same peoples would have received had they drawn the opposite conclusion.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:96

> Had the people of those societies been faithful and mindful ˹of Allah˺, We would have overwhelmed them with blessings from heaven and earth. But they disbelieved, so We seized them for what they used to commit.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conditional Sentence**

The verse states what the people of the towns would have received had they believed and been mindful of God, and the sentence sets the alternative before the reader after the account of what the peoples actually did. The Book's addresses use the same conditional form in several passages, and the sūrah of the Table states that if the people of the earlier books had observed what was revealed to them, they would have eaten from above them and from beneath their feet (5:66). The verse here supplies the statement of what the towns of the narratives forfeited, and the reader is shown the two courses in a single sentence.

The Book's use of the conditional with the communities appears in its promises and its warnings throughout, and the sūrah's earlier verses have stated the conditions on which the settlement in the land and the enjoyment of its goods rested. The verse here supplies the promise attached to faith and mindfulness, and the second half of the verse states the reason for the loss.

**The Blessings of Heaven and Earth**

The verse names the blessings that would have been opened to the people, and the phrase covers the provision that descends from the sky and the provision that grows from the earth. The tradition explains the blessings as rain from above and crops and fruits from below, and the sūrah of the Jinn states that if the people had kept to the right way, God would have given them abundant water to drink (72:16). The verse here states the provision in its general form, and the sūrah of Sabaʾ reports the plenty of a community and the gratitude that was expected of it (34:15).

The Book's treatment of the blessings as conditional on the response of the people appears in its addresses to the communities and in its promises to the believers, and the sūrah of Noah reports the messenger telling his people that if they sought forgiveness, God would send down rain upon them and provide them with wealth and children (71:10–12). This verse states the same arrangement in general form, and the reader is shown what the peoples of the towns could have had.

**The Blessings of Security and Peace**

The tradition extends the meaning of the blessings to security and peace as well as to provision, and the Book's descriptions of the settled communities include the safety of the sanctuaries and the fruit that came to them from every place (28:57). The present verse states the blessings in terms of heaven and earth, and the sūrah's addresses to the Quraysh recall the security and the provision that surrounded their city. The reader is thus directed beyond the crops and the rain to the whole condition of a people whose affairs are set right.

The Book's teaching about the dependence of the community's welfare on its faithfulness appears in its accounts of the settled peoples and in its promises to the believers, and the sūrah of the Bee states that God gives the example of a city that was secure and content and that denied the favours of God, so that God made it taste hunger and fear (16:112). The verse under discussion supplies the general statement of the conditional, and the second half states what the towns of the narratives chose.

**The Reason for the Seizure**

The verse states that the people disbelieved and that they were seized for what they used to commit, and the sentence places the outcome on the conduct of the community rather than on any failure of the provision. The Book's accounts of the communities restate the same reasoning at the close of each narrative, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that the wrongdoers were uprooted for what they had earned (6:45). Here the verse supplies the general statement of the cause, and the sūrah's narratives have supplied the particular instances.

The Book's treatment of the punishment as the fruit of the people's own acts appears in its statements that God does not wrong the servants and that the loss is what the people bring upon themselves, and the sūrah's opening section on the weighing of deeds has stated the same principle in the language of the reckoning. The verse at hand states the cause in the terms of the world, and the passage that follows turns to the false confidence of the towns.

**The Two Courses Before the Towns**

The verse sets the two courses before the reader: the faith and mindfulness that would have opened the blessings, and the denial that brought the seizure. The Book's addresses to the communities commonly state the two alternatives in sequence, and the sūrah's narratives of the five peoples have shown the second course being taken in each case. This verse states the first course as a possibility that remained open until the people chose otherwise, and the passage that follows states the security that they imagined in place of it.

The Book's teaching about the opportunity that the sending of the prophets represented appears in its statements that the messengers came as warners so that the people might have no argument against God, and the sūrah of the Women states that the messengers were sent as bearers of good news and warning so that people would have no excuse before God (4:165). The present verse supplies the general section's statement of what the opportunity offered, and the verses that follow state what the heedless towns thought they had secured.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands ninety-sixth in the chapter, and it follows the statement of the cycle of hardship and prosperity and precedes the series of questions about the security of the towns. The verse that follows asks whether the people of the towns felt secure that the punishment would not come upon them by night while they slept (7:97), and the questions continue to the ninety-ninth verse. The verse under discussion supplies the statement of the alternative that the peoples forfeited.

The reader who has followed the general section from the purpose of the hardship now reads what the towns would have received had they believed. The blessings of heaven and earth are named, the cause of the seizure is stated, and the passage that follows examines the confidence that the peoples placed in their security.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse asks whether the people of the towns felt secure from the coming of the punishment upon them at night while they were asleep (7:97). The conditional statement of what the towns would have received gives way to the question about the confidence with which they lived, and the section continues with the parallel question about the daytime.

The two verses together state the contrast on which the section turns. The blessing that faith and mindfulness would have brought is set beside the false security of the peoples who denied, and the verses that follow develop the questions into the statement of how few are safe from the plan of God.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:97

> Did the people of those societies feel secure that Our punishment would not come upon them by night while they were asleep?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Put to the Towns**

The verse asks whether the people of the towns felt secure from the coming of the punishment upon them at night while they were asleep, and the question is put in the form of a challenge rather than of an inquiry. The Book's addresses use the same interrogative form in its warnings, and the sūrah of the Kingdom asks whether those who live in security consider the One who is in heaven able to make the earth swallow them as it quakes (67:16). The verse here supplies the first of the sūrah's three questions about the security of the towns, and the sequence continues in the two verses that follow.

The question names the night and the state of sleep, and the choice of detail states the condition of the people at the moment when the punishment arrived in several of the sūrah's narratives. The Book's accounts of the destroyed communities present the punishments coming upon the peoples at the ordinary times of their lives, and the sūrah of the Poets states that the punishment came upon the people of Shuʿayb at the time of the overshadowing cloud. The verse here states the night as the setting of the possibility, and the reader is shown how little the ordinary arrangements of life could protect the people.

**The Security That the Peoples Felt**
