The verse speaks of the feeling of security that the people of the towns had, and the term describes the assumption that no harm would come to them. The Book's addresses to the communities present the same assumption in each of the narratives, and the sūrah's accounts of the peoples that were destroyed state that they had gone about their lives in confidence until the punishment took them. The verse here supplies the general statement of that confidence, and the sūrah of the Romans states that many people are content with the life of this world and feel secure in it (10:7).

The Book's treatment of the security of the heedless appears in its descriptions of those who take their ease in the world as though the reckoning were not coming, and the sūrah of the Cave reports the man who was given a garden and who entered it while wronging himself. This verse states the confidence of the towns in general form, and the questions that follow develop it into the statement of how few are safe from the plan of God.

**The Punishment by Night**

The verse states the possibility that the punishment might come at night, and the Book's accounts of the communities include several instances in which the punishment arrived while the people were unaware. The sūrah's narratives of the peoples do not specify the hour in most cases, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the punishment that came upon the people of Shuʿayb in the daytime. The present verse supplies the night as one of the two possibilities, and the verse that follows supplies the other, so that the pair covers the whole of the people's time.

The Book's warnings about the arrival of the Hour and of the punishment while the people are unprepared appear throughout its addresses, and the sūrah of the Ornament states that the Hour will come upon them suddenly while they are disputing (43:66). The verse under discussion states the same warning in the particular form of the night, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the instruction to say that the punishment might come upon the audience by night or by day (6:47).

**The Question as a Warning**

The verse's question functions as a warning to the sūrah's audience, and the Book's addresses use the interrogative form to place the audience in the position of the peoples whose histories are being recalled. The sūrah's narratives have shown the towns of the prophets being seized in the ordinary course of their affairs, and the reader is invited to consider the confidence with which any community goes to sleep. Here the verse supplies the first of the three questions, and the passage that follows states the day, the play, and the plan of God.

The Book's use of the history of the communities as a mirror for the audience appears in its commands to travel through the land and to consider the ends of those who denied, and the sūrah's earlier verses have issued that command. The verse at hand supplies the question that the lesson puts to the sūrah's listeners, and the following verses complete the examination.

**The Reach of the Punishment**

The verse presents the punishment as able to reach the towns at any hour, and the question states the reach of the power that executed the sentence on the peoples. The Book's descriptions of the divine power appear in the same terms throughout, and the sūrah of the Kingdom continues its question with the statement that the One who is in heaven could unleash a storm of stones upon the people (67:17). This verse supplies the statement of the reach of the punishment, and the reader is shown the ground on which the question rests.

The Book's treatment of the impossibility of security from God's power appears in its statements that no one can escape the decree and in its descriptions of the refuge that the people seek in vain. The present verse supplies the night as the first of the possibilities, and the verses that follow state the daytime and the conclusion that no one is safe from the plan of God except those who are ruined.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands ninety-seventh in the chapter, and it follows the statement of what the towns would have received had they believed. The verses that follow ask the parallel question about the daytime while the people are at play (7:98) and state that none feels secure from the plan of God except the losers (7:99), and the section then turns to the meaning of the inheritance of the earth (7:100). The verse under discussion supplies the first of the three questions about the security of the towns.

The reader who has read what the towns forfeited now reads the question about the confidence with which they lived. The night and the sleeping are named, the following verse names the day and the play, and the passage concludes with the statement about the security that no one may claim.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse asks whether the people of the towns felt secure that the punishment would not come upon them in the daytime while they were playing (7:98). The night and the sleep give way to the day and the play, and the two questions together cover the whole span of the people's heedlessness.

The two verses together state the two settings of the danger that the people ignored. Whether the punishment came at night or in the day, the towns that the sūrah has described were taken without the means to resist it; and the verses that follow state the conclusion that the sūrah draws from the pair of questions.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:98

> Or did they feel secure that Our punishment would not come upon them by day while they were at play?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Question**

The verse repeats the question of the previous verse with the setting changed from the night to the daytime, and the two questions together cover the whole cycle of the people's days. The Book's addresses use the paired form to state the impossibility of finding a time when the punishment could not come, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the instruction to say that the punishment might come by night or by day (6:47). The verse here supplies the second half of the pair, and the question is followed by the general statement of the next verse about the plan of God.

The Book's repetition of the question with a change of detail appears in its warnings throughout, and the sūrah's section on the towns uses the device to press the point that the peoples had no refuge in any hour. The verse here states the daytime, and the reader is shown that the same false confidence accompanied the towns through every part of their lives.

**The Day and Its Activity**

The verse names the daytime as the setting in which the people were engaged in their affairs, and the phrase states that the punishment could arrive in the middle of the work by which the towns lived. The Book's accounts of the communities include the destruction that came upon the peoples at the hours of their activity, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the punishment of the people of Shuʿayb at the time of the overshadowing cloud. The verse here supplies the daytime as the second possibility, and the two settings together state the reach of the danger.

The Book's treatment of the day as the time of earning and building appears in its descriptions of the world's affairs, and the sūrah's narratives of the peoples have shown the communities occupied with their wealth, their buildings, and their trade. This verse supplies the ordinary day of the towns, and the reader is shown what was going on among the people at the hour when the question imagines the punishment arriving.

**The Word for Play**

The verse describes the people as playing, and the term states the condition of the towns at their ease rather than a particular amusement. The Book uses the word for the heedlessness of those who take the life of the world as a passing matter, and the sūrah of the Rocky Tract reports the instruction to leave the disbelievers to eat and enjoy and be diverted by hope (15:3). The present verse supplies the same description in the account of the towns, and the sūrah of the Believers and the sūrah of the Ornament use the term in the same sense (23:54; 43:83).

The Book's treatment of play and amusement as the marks of a life that has lost sight of its end appears in its descriptions of the disbelievers and in its warnings to the believers against the distraction of the world, and the sūrah of the Winnowing Winds reports the instruction to leave them indulging until they meet their day (70:42). The verse under discussion states the condition of the towns, and the reader is directed to the contrast between their play and the seriousness of what awaited them.

**The Confidence of the Busy**

The verse's question states that the towns felt no fear while they were occupied with their affairs, and the confidence is presented as the companion of the busy day as it was the companion of the sleeping night. The Book's addresses to the communities present the same assumption in each of the narratives, and the sūrah's accounts of the peoples state that they were taken in the midst of their ordinary occupations. Here the verse supplies the second setting of the confidence, and the verses that follow state the verdict that the section draws from the pair of questions.

The Book's descriptions of the heedless as those who live as though the punishment were far away appear in its statements about the people who ask when the promise will be fulfilled, and the sūrah of the Prophets reports the question of the mockers about the time of the threat (21:38). The verse at hand supplies the picture of the busy day, and the reader is shown the difference between the towns' estimate of their position and the fact of it.

**The Pair of Questions and Their Purpose**

The two questions of this verse and the one before it cover the night and the day, and the pair leaves the people of the towns no hour in which they could claim security. The Book's interrogative addresses frequently use the same completeness of scope, and the sūrah of the Kingdom asks whether the people feel secure from the One who is in heaven causing the earth to swallow them or unleashing a storm of stones (67:16–17). This verse supplies the daytime half of the pair, and the next verse states the conclusion about the security that none may claim before God.

The Book's method in these questions is to leave the answer to the reader's own reflection, and the sūrah's section on the towns proceeds on that method until the statement that none feels secure from the plan of God except the lost. The present verse supplies the second question, and the passage that follows states the verdict in general terms.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands ninety-eighth in the chapter, and it completes the pair of questions about the hours of the towns' danger. The verse that follows states that none feels secure from the plan of God except the people who are lost (7:99), and the section then turns to the lesson of those who inherit the earth after the destroyed peoples (7:100). The verse under discussion supplies the daytime question in the series.

The reader who has considered the night now considers the day with its work and its play. The two questions together state the position of the towns, and the verse that follows states the conclusion that the sūrah draws about the security of anyone before the plan of God.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse asks whether the towns felt secure from the plan of God and states that none feels secure from His plan except the people who are lost (7:99). The question about the daytime gives way to the general question about the plan itself, and the section moves from the particular hours to the total condition of those who live without fear of God.

The two verses together close the interrogation of the towns. The night, the day, and the plan of God have been named in sequence, and the following verse states what the sūrah concludes about those who mistook their security in the world for safety from its Lord.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:99

> Did they feel secure against Allah’s planning? None would feel secure from Allah’s planning except the losers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Question**

The verse asks whether the towns felt secure from the plan of God, and the question turns from the hours in which the punishment might arrive to the plan that stands behind the events. The two previous questions named the night and the day; this one names the arrangement of God by which the peoples were left to their affairs until the term arrived. The sūrah's section on the towns thus reaches its conclusion, and the answer is stated in the same verse: none is safe from the plan of God except those who are lost.

The Book's questions of this kind move from the particular to the general, and the sūrah's section has followed that movement from the setting of the danger to its source. The verse here supplies the conclusion of the interrogation, and the answer states the condition of those who mistake their security in the world for safety from its Lord.

**The Meaning of the Plan**

The term used in the verse for the plan of God describes an arrangement carried out with deliberation, and the Book applies the same vocabulary to God's dealings with the plotters who opposed the messengers. The sūrah of the Family of Imran states that the disbelievers made a plan and that God also planned and that He is the best of planners (3:54), and the sūrah of the Spoils reports the plot of the disbelievers to capture, kill, or expel the Prophet and states that God planned while they were planning (8:30). The verse here uses the term for the arrangement by which the heedless were left in their confidence, and the Book's usage states that the plans of the powerful are not outside the plan of God.

The sūrah of the Thunder states that those before them planned and that God has the ultimate plan and knows what every soul earns (13:42), and the sūrah of the Ant states that they made a plan and that God made a plan while they were unaware (27:50). The verse here supplies the general statement of the same truth in the section on the towns, and the reader is shown that the security of the peoples rested on an arrangement that they had not understood.

**The Answer to the Question**

The verse states that none feels secure from the plan of God except the people who are lost, and the answer identifies the disposition of security with the condition of those who are ruined. The Book's teaching about the fear of God appears in its statements that the knowledge of God produces fear, and the sūrah of the Creator states that only those among His servants who have knowledge fear Him (35:28). This verse supplies the general statement of that teaching, and the sūrah's narratives have shown the peoples of the towns living without the fear that their situation required.

The Book's descriptions of the believers present their worship as the practice of hope and fear together, and the sūrah of the Prostration states that they abandon their beds, invoking their Lord with hope and fear (32:16). The present verse states the fear that the towns lacked, and the sūrah's reader is shown what the opposite disposition looks like in the life of the believers.

**The Word for the Lost**

The verse describes the people who feel secure as the losers, and the term repeats the word that the leaders of Midian used against the followers of the messenger and that the sūrah then applied to the leaders themselves (7:90; 7:92). The Book's narratives return the same word at the decisive points of the accounts, and the sūrah's section on the towns closes with it. The verse under discussion supplies the last instance of the term in the series, and the reader is left with the identification of the truly lost that the narratives have established.

The Book's treatment of loss appears in its statements about those who lose their souls and their families on the day of the reckoning, and the sūrah's opening section on the weighing of deeds stated that those whose scales were light were the ones who lost themselves (7:9). Here the verse states the loss in the general form of the section, and the passage that follows turns to the lesson of the generations that inherited the earth.

**The Fear That Is Not Despair**

The verse's statement that none is safe from the plan of God is addressed to the believers as much as to the heedless, and the Book's teaching joins the fear of God to the hope in His mercy. The sūrah's addresses to the community combine the two dispositions, and the verse here states the fear that keeps the servant from the confidence of the towns. The reader is directed to the middle path between the security of the heedless and the despair that the Book also rejects.

The Book's treatment of the balance between hope and fear appears in its descriptions of the believers' worship and in its commands to call upon God with both dispositions, and the sūrah's earlier verse on supplication required the audience to call upon their Lord with hope and fear (7:56). The verse at hand supplies the general section's statement of the same requirement, and the passage that follows turns to the inheritors of the earth.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands ninety-ninth in the chapter, and it concludes the three questions about the security of the towns. The verse that follows asks whether it has not become clear to those who inherited the earth after the destroyed peoples that God could punish them for their sins and seal their hearts so that they would not hear (7:100), and the section then continues to its end at the hundred-and-second verse. This verse supplies the verdict on the security that the towns imagined.

The reader who has followed the questions of the night, the day, and the plan now reads the answer that the sūrah gives. No one is safe from the plan of God except those who are lost, and the passage that follows turns the lesson toward the generations that came after the destroyed communities.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse asks whether it has not become clear to those who inherited the earth after its former people that God could, if He willed, strike them for their sins and seal their hearts so that they would not hear (7:100). The verdict on the security of the heedless gives way to the question addressed to the inheritors of the land, and the section applies the lesson of the destroyed towns to those who took their place.

The two verses together state the conclusion of the section. The plan of God is not escaped by the confidence of the world, and the peoples who inherited the earth after the destroyed communities are asked whether they have understood what happened to those before them; and the verses that follow state the sealing of the hearts and the record of the towns.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:100

> Is it not clear to those who take over the land after ˹the destruction of˺ its former residents that—if We will—We can punish them ˹too˺ for their sins and seal their hearts so they will not hear ˹the truth˺?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Addressed to the Inheritors**

The verse asks whether it has not become clear to those who inherited the earth after the destruction of its former people that God could, if He willed, strike them for their sins and seal their hearts so that they would not hear. The question is addressed to the generations that took the place of the destroyed communities, and the sūrah's readers in Makkah belonged to that company. The Book's addresses repeatedly place the audience in the position of the inheritors of the punished peoples, and the sūrah of Yūnus states that God made the later generations the successors of the earlier ones to see how they would act (10:14).

The Book's use of the ruins of the destroyed peoples as evidence appears in its commands to travel through the land and to consider the ends of those who denied, and the sūrah's earlier verses have issued the same command. The verse here supplies the question that the evidence puts to the inheritors, and the reader is asked whether the lesson of the ruins has reached the heart.

**The Inheritance of the Land**

The verse describes the audience as those who took over the land after its former residents, and the phrase states the arrangement by which peoples succeed one another on the earth. The sūrah's narratives of the communities included the same reminder in the mouths of the messengers, and the account of Hūd reminded the people of ʿĀd that they had been made successors after the people of Noah (7:69), while the account of Ṣāliḥ reminded Thamūd that they had replaced ʿĀd (7:74). The verse here states the position of the later generations in general form, and the sūrah of the Prophets states that God destroyed many societies of wrongdoers and raised other people after them (21:11).

The Book's descriptions of the succession of the peoples present it as a mercy and a test, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that God made the people successors on the earth and raised some of them above others in ranks so that He might test them in what He had given them (6:165). The verse here supplies the question that the test puts to the inheritors, and the reader is shown that the succession is not a guarantee of permanence.

**The Power to Strike for Sins**

The verse states that God could strike the inheritors for their sins if He willed, and the phrase places the same possibility before the later generations that the narratives have shown being realized in the earlier ones. The Book's accounts of the destroyed peoples state that the punishments came for what the communities had earned, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the destruction of many peoples more firmly established than the ones who followed them (6:6). This verse states the power in general form, and the sūrah's addresses to the Quraysh applied the same warning to the city that surrounded the sanctuary.

The Book's treatment of the equality of the generations before the divine judgment appears in its statements that the earlier and the later peoples are alike in their accountability, and the sūrah's opening section on the weighing of deeds has stated the reckoning in terms that leave no community outside it. The present verse supplies the general statement of the power, and the second half of the verse states the more severe possibility that the punishment might take the form of a sealed heart rather than of an outward destruction.

**The Sealing of the Hearts**

The verse states that God could seal the hearts of the inheritors so that they would not hear, and the phrase describes a punishment in which the people are left in their heedlessness without being removed from the world. The Book uses the same image in several passages, and the sūrah of the Cow states that God has sealed the hearts and the hearing of the disbelievers and covered their sight (2:7), while the sūrah of the Cattle reports the question of what would remain to a people whose hearing, sight, and hearts had been sealed (6:46). The verse under discussion supplies the possibility of that punishment for the inheritors, and the sūrah has already applied the description to the peoples of the towns in the hundred-and-first verse to come.

The tradition treats the sealing of the heart as the consequence of the persistence in denial, and the Book's statements about the sealing follow the descriptions of the refusal rather than preceding them. Here the verse states the possibility after the statement about the sins, and the reader is shown that the loss of the capacity to hear is the end of a course that the people have chosen.

**The Lesson of the Ruins**

The verse's question rests on the visibility of what happened to the earlier peoples, and the sūrah's addresses have assumed that the audience knew the ruins of ʿĀd and Thamūd and the site of the cities of the plain. The Book's commands to consider those remains appear in its addresses to the Quraysh and in the narratives themselves, and the verse here supplies the question that turns the remains into an argument. The reader is directed to the difference between knowing the story and understanding the lesson.

The Book's treatment of the heedless as those who pass by the ruins without understanding appears in its descriptions of the people who travel and do not reflect, and the sūrah of the Romans and the sūrah of the Creator ask whether the people have not travelled through the land to see the ends of those before them (30:9; 35:44). The verse at hand supplies the general section's question to the inheritors, and the verses that follow state the sealing of the hearts and the recounting of the stories.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundredth in the chapter, and it follows the questions about the security of the towns and precedes the statement about the hearts of the disbelievers. The verse that follows states that the stories of the societies have been recounted and that their messengers came with clear proofs and that the people would not believe what they had denied before, and it states that God seals the hearts of the disbelievers (7:101). This verse supplies the application of the section's lesson to the inheritors of the land.

The reader who has followed the section from the purpose of the hardship to the questions about security now reads the question put to the generations that took the place of the destroyed peoples. The power of God, the possibility of the striking, and the sealing of the hearts are stated together, and the passage that follows states the record of the towns and the sealing that the disbelievers brought upon themselves.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the stories of the towns have been recounted to the Prophet, that their messengers came to them with clear proofs, that the people would not believe what they had denied before, and that God seals the hearts of the disbelievers (7:101). The question addressed to the inheritors gives way to the account of the messengers and of the disbelief that preceded their coming, and the section continues to its close.

The two verses together state the sūrah's summary of the histories it has told. The inheritors of the earth are asked what they have learned from the fate of those before them, and the verses that follow state what the messengers brought and how the hearts of the disbelievers were sealed.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:101

> We have narrated to you ˹O Prophet˺ some of the stories of those societies. Surely, their messengers came to them with clear proofs, but still they would not believe in what they had already denied. This is how Allah seals the hearts of the disbelievers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Recounting of the Stories**

The verse states that the stories of the towns have been recounted to the Prophet, and the phrase names the narratives of the five peoples that the sūrah has told. The Book's addresses to the Prophet describe the histories of the earlier communities as material given for his instruction and for the reassurance of the believers, and the sūrah of Hūd states that the stories of the messengers are related to strengthen the heart of the Prophet and that in them has come the truth and an admonition for the believers (11:120). The verse here supplies the same statement for the series that the sūrah has completed.

The Book's use of the earlier histories as evidence appears in its addresses to the audience throughout, and the sūrah of Joseph states that in the stories of the prophets there is a lesson for people of understanding (12:111). The verse here states that the stories have been recounted to the Prophet, and the reader is shown that what is at stake is not the stories themselves but the lesson they carry.

**The Messengers and the Clear Proofs**

The verse states that the messengers came to the towns with clear proofs, and the phrase repeats the description that the sūrah's five narratives applied to the signs and messages of the prophets. The account of Ṣāliḥ named the clear proof that came with him (7:73), and the account of Shuʿayb stated that the proof from the Lord had already come to the people (7:85). The verse here states the general fact for all the towns, and the reader is shown that the refusal of the peoples was not for want of evidence.

The Book's accounts of the messengers consistently attach the proofs to the sending and leave the responsibility with the people who receive them, and the sūrah of the Cattle states that the messengers were sent as bearers of good news and warning so that people would have no argument against God (4:165). This verse supplies the general statement of what the towns received, and the second half of the verse states what they did with it.

**The Disbelief in What Had Been Denied Before**

The verse states that the people would not believe what they had denied before, and the phrase points back to the earlier denial that preceded the coming of the messengers. The tradition reads the phrase in more than one way: some commentators refer it to the denial that the peoples had already expressed when the messengers were first announced, others to the refusal of the communities at the time of the signs, and others to the covenant that the sūrah will mention in its later section (7:172). The present verse states the persistence of the refusal, and the reader is shown a people who were given every opportunity and remained where they had begun.

The Book's treatment of the persistence of the disbelievers appears in its statements that they would not believe even if every sign came to them, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports the instruction to say that even if the angels descended and the dead spoke, the people would not believe unless God willed it. The verse under discussion supplies the general statement for the towns, and the terms state that the refusal was settled before the proofs arrived.

**The Sealing of the Hearts**

The verse closes with the statement that God sets a seal upon the hearts of the disbelievers, and the image describes the condition of those who have passed beyond the reach of the warning. The sūrah's previous verse stated the possibility that God could seal the hearts of the inheritors so that they would not hear (7:100), and the sūrah of the Cow states that God has sealed the hearts and hearing of the disbelievers (2:7). Here the verse supplies the statement of what happened to the peoples of the towns, and the reader is shown the connection between the persistence of the refusal and the loss of the capacity to receive the message.

The Book's descriptions of the sealing follow the same order in each passage, and the statements place the act of God after the settled refusal of the people. The sūrah of the Cattle reports the question of what would remain to a people whose hearing, sight, and hearts had been sealed (6:46), and the verse here states the sealing as the outcome of the town's response to the proofs.

**The Consolation of the Prophet**

The Book's addresses describe the histories of the communities as a consolation for the Prophet in the face of his own people's refusal, and the sūrah of Hūd states the purpose of the stories in those terms (11:120). The tradition notes the same purpose in the sūrah's series, and the account of the messengers' patience under rejection appears in the sūrah of the Cattle (6:34). The verse at hand states that the stories have been told to the Prophet, and the reader is shown that the record of the towns is part of the answer to the rejection that he faced.

The Book's treatment of the Prophet's grief and of the assurance given to him appears in its addresses throughout, and the sūrah of the Cave and the sūrah of the Poets speak of his sorrow for the people who would not believe (18:6; 26:3). This verse supplies the assurance that the pattern of the towns is known and recorded, and the section proceeds to its closing statement about the covenant.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and first in the chapter, and it follows the question addressed to the inheritors of the earth and precedes the statement about the covenant. The verse that follows states that most of the people were not found true to their covenant and that most of them were found rebellious (7:102), and the sūrah's section on the towns then closes. The present verse supplies the summary of the messengers' reception in the towns.

The reader who has followed the histories of the five peoples now reads the general statement of what the messengers brought and what the people did. The proofs came, the denial persisted, and the hearts were sealed; and the passage that follows states the judgment on the covenant that the peoples had failed to keep.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that most of the towns were not found true to their covenant and that most of them were found to be rebellious (7:102). The account of the messengers and the sealed hearts gives way to the statement about the covenant, and the general section that began in the ninety-fourth verse reaches its end.

The two verses together close the lesson of the histories. The proofs have been delivered, the refusal has been recorded, and the covenant has been named; and the sūrah's next verse turns from the towns of the past to the mission of Moses.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:102

> We did not find most of them true to their covenant. Rather, We found most of them truly rebellious.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Finding About the Towns**

The verse states that most of the people of the towns were not found true to their covenant and that most of them were found rebellious, and the statement closes the general section that began with the sending of the prophets. The Book uses the language of finding in its accounts of the peoples, and the sūrah of the Cattle reports that God did not find most of them grateful and that many of them were rebellious. The verse here supplies the verdict on the communities whose histories the section has surveyed, and the reader is given the outcome of the test.

The Book's statements about the majority in the narratives appear in its addresses to the Prophet and in its descriptions of the peoples, and the sūrah's accounts have shown the leaders refusing and the weak accepting in each community. The verse here states the general condition of the towns, and the phrase about the covenant names the obligation that the peoples had undertaken.

**The Covenant and Its Meaning**

The verse speaks of the covenant that the peoples failed to keep, and the Book's use of the term covers the pledges that God has taken from humanity and from the communities. The sūrah will later report the covenant taken from the descendants of Adam when they were brought forth and asked to bear witness that their Lord is their God (7:172), and the Book records covenants taken from the Children of Israel and from the prophets. The verse here states the general failure of the towns, and the tradition notes that the word for covenant covers everything that God has bound His servants to.

The Book's descriptions of the covenant appear in the sūrah of the Cow, which states that God does not disdain to use the parable of a mosquito and then describes those who violate the covenant after it has been affirmed (2:26–27), and in the sūrah of the Thunder, which states that those who honour the covenant never break the pledge (13:20). This verse supplies the general statement of the failure, and the sūrah of the Bee commands the believers to honour their pledges and not to break their oaths (16:91).

**The Breaking of the Covenant**

The Book treats the breaking of the covenant as a violation of the relationship that the servants have with their Lord, and the sūrah of the Thunder describes those who violate it along with those who sever what God has commanded to be joined (13:25). The present verse states that most of the towns were found unfaithful, and the reader is shown that the refusal of the messengers was a breach of an obligation that reached beyond the message itself.

The Book's accounts of the communities present the refusal as a violation of the pledge that the peoples had given, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran reports the covenant taken from the prophets that they would believe in the messenger who came to confirm what they had (3:81). The verse under discussion supplies the general statement for the towns, and the sūrah's reader is shown that the histories of the peoples are histories of the covenant as well as of the messages.

**The Meaning of Rebellion**

The verse describes most of the people as rebellious, and the term names the departure from the obedience that the covenant required. The Book uses the word for the condition of those who leave the command of God, and the sūrah of the Cow applies the description to those who break the covenant of God (2:27). Here the verse supplies the general verdict on the towns, and the two clauses of the sentence state the same condition from the two sides of the agreement.

The Book's treatment of rebellion as a settled condition rather than a single act appears in its descriptions of the peoples that persisted in their ways, and the sūrah's narratives have shown the communities answering the messengers with threats and demands. The verse at hand states the general finding about the towns, and the reader is left with the picture of the peoples whose histories have been recounted.

**The Commentators on the Verse**

The tradition notes that the statement that God found no covenant with most of them is an expression for the failure of the peoples to keep their pledge rather than a declaration that no pledge had been made. Al-Rāzī observes that the infidelity of the people to their covenant was such that it was as though they had made no covenant at all. This verse states the finding in that sense, and the interpretation preserves both the reality of the obligations and the completeness of the failure.

The tradition also identifies the majority in the phrase with the generality of the peoples of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, and Shuʿayb, the peoples whose refusal the sūrah's narratives have recorded. The present verse supplies the conclusion of the section on the towns, and the reader is shown the pattern that the following accounts of Moses will repeat.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and second in the chapter, and it closes the general section that began with the sending of the prophets to the societies (7:94). The verse that follows opens the long account of Moses with the statement that he was sent with the signs to Pharaoh and his chiefs and that they wrongfully rejected them (7:103), and the sūrah's second half begins. The verse under discussion supplies the last statement of the section on the towns.

The reader who has followed the histories of the five peoples and the lessons drawn from them now reads the verdict on the covenant that the peoples failed to keep, and the sūrah turns to the account that will occupy the rest of its narratives. The towns have been surveyed, and the story of Moses begins.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that after the earlier messengers God sent Moses with His signs to Pharaoh and his chiefs and that they wrongfully rejected them, and it commands the reader to consider the end of those who worked corruption (7:103). The closing verdict on the towns gives way to the opening of the account of Moses, and the sūrah enters the longest of its narratives.

The two verses together carry the sūrah from the general lessons to the particular history. The covenant of the peoples has been named and their rebellion stated, and the passage that follows introduces Moses, Pharaoh, and the confrontation that will carry the sūrah's argument to the Children of Israel and their deliverance.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:103

> Then after them We sent Moses with Our signs to Pharaoh and his chiefs, but they wrongfully rejected them. See what was the end of the corruptors!

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening of the Account of Moses**

The verse opens the long narrative that runs through the rest of the sūrah's first half, and it states that Moses was sent with the signs to Pharaoh and his chiefs after the earlier messengers. The Book tells the story of Moses in several passages, and the accounts run through the sūrah of the Cow, the sūrah of Yūnus (10:75–93), the sūrah of the Poets (26:10–68), the sūrah of the Ant (27:7–14), the sūrah of the Stories (28:3–46), the sūrah of the Forgiver (40:23–45), and the sūrah of the Night Journey (17:101–104). The verse here introduces the narrative in the same form that the sūrah used for the five earlier messengers.

The sūrah's arrangement places the account of Moses after the general section on the towns, and the phrase about the sending after them states the sequence of the missions. The Book's narratives present Moses as the messenger sent to the greatest power of his age, and the sūrah's account will show the confrontation between the messenger and the ruler of Egypt in its several stages.

**The Signs Given to Moses**

The verse states that Moses was sent with the signs, and the Book's accounts of his mission name the signs that accompanied him. The sūrah's narrative will report the staff and the hand as the two signs that Moses produced before Pharaoh (7:107–108), and the sūrah of the Stories and the sūrah of the Ant describe the same two signs in their accounts of his commissioning. The verse here states the signs in general form, and the narrative that follows supplies the two that were shown in the contest with the magicians.

The Book's accounts of the messengers attach the signs to the sending and present them as the proofs of the message, and the sūrah's earlier narratives stated the clear proofs that accompanied the prophets to the towns (7:73; 7:85). The verse here supplies the same arrangement for Moses, and the reader is shown that the mission to Pharaoh was accompanied by the evidence that the messenger's claim required.

**Pharaoh and His Chiefs**

The verse names Pharaoh and his chiefs as the recipients of the mission, and the Book's accounts of the Egyptian narrative consistently include the notables who answer with him. The sūrah's earlier narratives presented the notables of the communities as the group that refused the messengers, and the account of Moses follows the same pattern with the leaders of the greatest empire the Book describes. This verse states the two parties, and the reader is shown that the refusal at the highest level is the same refusal that the sūrah has traced in the Arab communities.

The Book's descriptions of Pharaoh present him as a ruler who elevated himself in the land and divided its people into groups, and the sūrah of the Stories states that he exalted himself and oppressed a party among the people, killing their sons and sparing their women (28:4). The present verse supplies the opening of the confrontation with that ruler, and the narrative that follows will state the demand for the release of the Children of Israel.

**The Wrongful Rejection of the Signs**

The verse states that they wrongfully rejected the signs, and the phrase states both the act and its character. The Book's accounts of the communities use the same language for the refusal of the signs, and the sūrah's earlier narratives have reported the denial of Noah's people, the rejection of the people of ʿĀd, and the hamstringing of the she-camel by the people of Thamūd. The verse under discussion supplies the summary of the Egyptian response before the narrative has described it, and the account that follows fills in the stages of the refusal.

The Book's treatment of the rejection of the signs as an act against the self appears in its statements that the wrong is done to the people who commit it, and the sūrah's opening section on the weighing of deeds has stated the same principle for the reckoning. Here the verse states the wrongful character of the rejection, and the reader is shown the correspondence between the act and the outcome that the verse's last clause states.

**The Command to Consider the End**

The verse closes with the command to consider the end of those who worked corruption, and the phrase repeats the refrain that the sūrah has attached to the accounts of the earlier communities. The command appeared at the close of the account of Lot (7:84), in the address of Shuʿayb to Midian (7:86), and in the general section's statement about the seizure of the towns (7:96). The verse at hand places the command at the opening of the Egyptian narrative, and the reader is thus directed to the outcome of the account that is about to be told.

The Book's use of the command to consider the ends of the peoples appears in its addresses throughout, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran states the instruction to travel through the land and see the fate of those who denied (3:137). This verse supplies the command at the head of the longest narrative in the sūrah, and the account of Exodus and the deliverance of the Children of Israel follows.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and third in the chapter, and it follows the close of the general section on the towns and opens the account of Moses. The verse that follows reports the words of Moses to Pharaoh, declaring that he is a messenger from the Lord of all worlds (7:104), and the confrontation unfolds from there. The present verse supplies the opening of the sūrah's second half.

The reader who has followed the five communities and the general lessons now meets the narrative that occupies the centre of the sūrah. The mission of Moses, the signs, the chiefs of Egypt, and the end of the corrupters are stated in a single verse, and the next verse gives the messenger's own words.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the address of Moses to Pharaoh, in which he declares that he is a messenger from the Lord of all worlds (7:104). The general statement of the mission in front of us gives way to the messenger's own words, and the dialogue between Moses and Pharaoh begins.

The two verses together open the Egyptian account. Moses is sent with the signs to Pharaoh and his chiefs, they reject them wrongfully, and the messenger states his commission to the ruler; and the following verses report the demand for the release of the Children of Israel and the signs that Moses produces when Pharaoh asks for the proof.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:104

> And Moses said, “O Pharaoh! I am truly a messenger from the Lord of all worlds,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address to the Ruler**

The verse reports the words of Moses to Pharaoh, and the address opens with the name of the ruler before the introduction of the message. The Book's accounts of the Egyptian mission present the confrontation as a direct address to the holder of power, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the divine instruction to go to the wrongdoing people, the people of Pharaoh, and to ask whether they will not fear God (26:10–11). The verse here supplies the first words of Moses in the sūrah's account, and the reader is shown the messenger standing before the throne with his commission.

The Book's narratives of the prophets present the messengers addressing the holders of power as they address their own peoples, and the sūrah of the Ant reports the call of Moses and Aaron to Pharaoh with the signs of God (10:75). The verse here supplies the same opening in the sūrah of the Heights, and the account that follows reports Pharaoh's answer and the demand for the proof.

**The Title of the Sender**

The verse names the sender as the Lord of all worlds, and the title is the one that the sūrah's earlier messengers used when they identified their office (7:61; 7:67). The Book's accounts of the prophets consistently attach the mission to the Lord of all rather than to a tribal or national deity, and the sūrah of the Poets reports Moses and Aaron instructed to say that they are messengers of the Lord of all worlds (26:16). The verse here supplies the same title in the address to the ruler of Egypt, and the reader is shown that the Lord who sent Moses is not one god among the gods of the Nile.

The Book's use of the title in the Egyptian account states the contest in its true terms, since Pharaoh claimed for himself the attributes of divinity. The sūrah of the Stories reports his declaration that he was the highest lord of the people (28:38), and the sūrah of the Poets reports his question to the chiefs about what they knew of the Lord of Moses (26:23). This verse supplies the messenger's answer before those questions are asked, and the title stands as the claim on which the whole confrontation turns.

**The Background of the Mission**

Elsewhere, The Book's accounts describe the preparation of Moses for the mission, and the tradition places the commissioning at the burning bush, where the voice called to him from the fire and instructed him to go to Pharaoh (20:11–24; 79:16–17). The sūrah of the Stories reports the instruction given to Moses and Aaron to go to Pharaoh and speak to him gently so that he might be mindful or fear (20:43–44), and the sūrah of the Stories reports the flight of Moses from Egypt and his years in Midian before the commissioning. The present verse supplies the words that resulted from that preparation, and the reader is shown the messenger entering the palace of the ruler who had once been his household.

Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of Moses include his upbringing in the house of Pharaoh, and the sūrah of the Stories reports the return of the child to his mother and his growth in the palace (28:8–13). The verse under discussion supplies the confrontation between the man raised in the palace and the ruler who sat on its throne, and the narrative that follows develops the contest between the message and the power of Egypt.

**The Boldness of the Address**

The address in this verse states the message without preface or flattery, and the Book's narratives present the messengers speaking to rulers in the same manner. The sūrah of the Prophet Muhammad's mission includes the command to speak to Pharaoh gently (20:44), and the gentleness required of Moses does not soften the content of the declaration. Here the verse supplies the opening of the address, and the reader is shown the combination of courtesy and firmness that the Book presents in the messengers' dealings with power.

The Book's treatment of the confrontation between the messenger and the tyrant appears in its accounts of the several rulers who opposed the prophets, and the sūrah's narratives present the outcome of each encounter. The verse at hand supplies the first stage of the encounter with Pharaoh, and the dialogue continues in the verses that follow with the statement of what the messenger was sent to say.

**The Messenger and His Commission**

The verse states that Moses speaks as a messenger, and the Book's accounts of the prophets present the messengers' authority as derived entirely from the sender. The sūrah's earlier narratives have shown the prophets refusing the charges against their persons and claiming only their office (7:61; 7:67), and the verse here supplies the same disposition in the address to the ruler of Egypt. The reader is shown that the messenger brings nothing of his own before the throne.

The Book's teaching about the messengers' functions appears in its statements that they are bearers of good news and warning, and the sūrah of the Women states that the messengers were sent so that people would have no argument against God (4:165). This verse supplies the arrival of the messenger at the court, and the verses that follow state the obligation of the messenger to speak the truth and the demand that he was sent to make.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and fourth in the chapter, and it follows the general statement of the mission and precedes the continuation of the address. The verse that follows states that Moses was obliged to say nothing about God except the truth, that he had come with a clear proof from his Lord, and that the Children of Israel should be released to go with him (7:105). The present verse supplies the opening of the dialogue with Pharaoh.

The reader who has met the ruler and his chiefs in the previous verse now hears the messenger's own words. The commission is declared in a single sentence, and the verses that follow state the demand that accompanied it and the answer that Pharaoh gave.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse continues the address with the messenger's statement that he is bound to say nothing of God but the truth and that he has brought a clear proof from his Lord, and it closes with the demand that the Children of Israel be sent with him (7:105). The declaration of the commission gives way to the demand that the mission was made to carry, and the confrontation moves from the claim to the request.

The two verses together state the opening of the Egyptian account. The messenger declares who sent him, states his obligation to the truth, presents the proof, and asks for the release of the people; and the verses that follow report Pharaoh's demand for the sign and the two signs that Moses produced.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:105

> obliged to say nothing about Allah except the truth. Indeed, I have come to you with clear proof from your Lord, so let the children of Israel go with me.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Obligation to Speak the Truth**

The verse states that Moses is obliged to say nothing about God except the truth, and the declaration presents the messenger as bound by his commission. The tradition reads the clause as the answer to an accusation: Pharaoh had replied to the first sentence of the address by calling him a liar, and the messenger states that he would speak nothing of God but the truth. The Book's accounts of the prophets present them as those who speak of God with knowledge, and the sūrah's earlier verse in the account of the Israelites' history states that speaking truthfully of God was a condition of their covenant (7:169). The verse here supplies the messenger's own statement of that obligation.

The Book's teaching about speech concerning God appears in its warnings against saying of Him what one does not know, and the sūrah's passage on the forbidden things names the invention of lies against God among the things that He has prohibited (7:33). The verse here supplies the messenger's declaration of his practice, and the reader is shown the difference between the messenger who speaks with knowledge and the ruler who claims what belongs to God.

**The Clear Proof**

The verse states that the messenger has come with a clear proof from his Lord, and the phrase names the evidence that accompanied the demand that follows. The Book's accounts of Moses report the signs given to him at the commissioning, and the sūrah of the Stories and the sūrah of the Ant describe the staff and the hand among them (28:31–32). The verse here states the proof in general form, and the narrative that follows reports the two signs that the messenger produced when Pharaoh asked for the evidence.

Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the messengers attach the proof to the message, and the sūrah's earlier narratives stated the clear proofs that accompanied the prophets to the towns (7:73; 7:85). This verse supplies the same arrangement for the mission to Egypt, and the reader is shown that the messenger's demand rests on the evidence that his Lord had given him.

**The Demand for the Release of the Children of Israel**

The verse closes with the demand that the Children of Israel be sent with the messenger, and the demand identifies the purpose of the mission to Pharaoh. Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of the Egyptian narrative state the same request in each of its versions, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the instruction to say that the Children of Israel should be released to go with the messengers (26:17), while the sūrah of the Smoke reports the summons to hand over the servants of God (44:18). The present verse supplies the request in the sūrah's own wording, and the narrative that follows reports Pharaoh's answer and the contest of the signs.

The Book's treatment of the Israelites in Egypt includes the account of their oppression, and the sūrah of the Stories states that Pharaoh killed their sons and spared their women and that the people were divided into groups (28:4). The verse under discussion supplies the demand for the release of the oppressed people, and the reader is shown the political dimension of the mission that the sūrah's account of the signs might otherwise obscure.

**The Messenger and the Oppressed**

The demand in this verse places the mission of the messenger on the side of the oppressed people of Egypt, and the Book's narratives of Moses present him as the deliverer of the Children of Israel by the command of God. The sūrah of the Stories reports the prayer of the Israelites under oppression and the birth of Moses as part of the answer to it, and the sūrah of the Forgiver reports a believer among the family of Pharaoh pleading for the life of Moses (40:28). Here the verse supplies the demand that the mission made of the ruler, and the narrative that follows shows the refusal of the demand and the signs that were produced to support it.

In several places, The Book's accounts of the deliverance of the Children of Israel present the exodus as the outcome of the confrontation, and the sūrah's narrative will carry the account through the plagues, the crossing of the sea, and the destruction of Pharaoh. The verse at hand supplies the first stage of that sequence, and the reader is shown the goal toward which the mission was directed from the beginning.

**The Address to Power in the Sūrah**

The verse belongs to the sūrah's presentation of the messengers' dealings with the holders of power, and the account of Moses is the fullest example of the theme in the chapter. The sūrah's earlier narratives dealt with the notables of the communities, and the Egyptian account deals with a ruler whose authority the tradition describes as absolute. This verse supplies the demand made to that authority, and the reader is shown that the message addressed the arrangements of the state as directly as it addressed the beliefs of the people.

The Book's treatment of the confrontation with political power appears in the sūrah's accounts of the prophets who stood before the rulers of their peoples, and the sūrah of the Prophets reports Abraham's confrontation with the ruler who disputed with him about his Lord (2:258). The present verse supplies the instance in the sūrah of the Heights, and the account that follows develops it into the contest of the signs and the deliverance of the people.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and fifth in the chapter, and it completes the address that began with the declaration of the messenger's commission. The verse that follows reports Pharaoh's answer, asking for the sign if the messenger is telling the truth (7:106), and the signs are then produced in the two verses after it (7:107–108). The verse under discussion supplies the demand that the mission was made to carry.

The reader who heard the messenger's declaration now hears the request that accompanied it. The obligation to speak the truth, the proof from the Lord, and the demand for the release of the Children of Israel are stated together, and the account moves to Pharaoh's response and to the contest of the signs.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports Pharaoh's answer, that if the messenger has come with a sign he should bring it forward if he is truthful (7:106). The demand for the release of the Children of Israel gives way to the demand for the proof, and the confrontation enters the phase of the signs.

The two verses together state the opening exchange between Moses and Pharaoh. The messenger declares his obligation to the truth, presents his proof, and asks for the release of the people; and Pharaoh answers by asking for the sign to be produced, which the following verses report.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:106

> Pharaoh said, “If you have come with a sign, then bring it if what you say is true.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Pharaoh's Answer**

The verse reports Pharaoh's answer to the messenger's demand, and the answer does not address the request for the release of the Children of Israel. He asks instead for the sign, and the demand states the terms on which he proposes to judge the message. The Book's accounts of the Egyptian narrative report the same answer, and the sūrah of the Poets reports Pharaoh's demand that the sign be produced if the messenger is truthful (26:31). The verse here supplies the sūrah's version of the reply, and the reader is shown the ruler setting the condition under which he will consider the claim.

The Book's accounts of the communities present the demand for signs as the standard response of the deniers, and the sūrah's narratives have recorded the demands of the peoples of ʿĀd and Thamūd for the punishment and the sign (7:70; 7:73). The verse here supplies the Egyptian instance, and the narrative that follows reports the signs that were given and the answer that they received.

**The Refusal to Answer the Demand**

The answer in this verse leaves the request for the release of the Children of Israel untouched, and the silence states the ruler's position on the matter. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the confrontation with Pharaoh report the demand made several times in the course of the narrative, and the sūrah of the Poets and the sūrah of the Stories record the recurrence of the request after each stage of the contest. The verse here supplies the first of those exchanges, and the reader is shown that the question of the release was to be decided on the field of the signs.

The Book's treatment of the rulers who answered the messengers with demands appears in its accounts of the other peoples, and the sūrah's narratives present the same evasion in the answers of the notables of the communities. This verse states the answer of the Egyptian ruler, and the account that follows shows what the production of the sign accomplished.

**The Sign and the Proof**

The verse uses the word for the sign in the singular, and the two signs that the narrative will report are presented as the proof of the messenger's claim. Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of the commissioning of Moses name the two signs among the confirmations given to him, and the sūrah of the Stories reports the instruction about the staff and the hand as the signs of the mission (28:31–32). The present verse states the demand for the sign in general form, and the narrative supplies the two that Moses produced.

In several places, The Book's accounts of the prophets include the signs given to each messenger, and the sūrah's earlier narratives stated the proofs that accompanied Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, and Shuʿayb. The verse under discussion supplies the Egyptian demand for the same kind of evidence, and the reader is shown that the ruler asked for what the messenger had already been given.

**The Challenge and the Truth**

The verse closes with the condition that the sign be produced if the messenger is speaking the truth, and the phrase turns the proof into a test of the messenger's honesty rather than of the ruler's. Time and again, The Book's accounts of the communities report the same conditional challenges in the mouths of the deniers, and the sūrah of the Prophets reports the demand of the mockers for the threat to be brought forward if the messengers were truthful (21:38). Here the verse supplies the Egyptian version, and the narrative that follows shows the result of the test that the ruler proposed.

The Book's treatment of the demand for signs as evidence of a settled refusal appears in its statements that the signs would not convince those who had already denied, and the sūrah's general section stated that the peoples of the towns would not believe what they had denied before (7:101). The verse at hand supplies the Egyptian instance of the same condition, and the reader is shown what the signs produced in the court of the ruler.

**The Two Signs That Follow**

The two verses that follow report the production of the two signs, and the demand in this verse is answered before Pharaoh's notables have time to respond. Consistently, The Book's accounts of the contest present the signs as the demonstration of the divine commission, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the staff and the hand among the signs of Moses (26:32–33). This verse supplies the setting in which the signs were produced, and the reader is shown the ruler's demand and the answer of the messenger together.

The Book's narratives of the prophets present the signs as the proofs offered once and then left to work upon the witnesses, and the sūrah's account of the Egyptian contest will show the signs dividing the court and the people. The present verse supplies the opening of the contest, and the two verses that follow state the first display.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and sixth in the chapter, and it answers the messenger's address and demand in the verse before it. The two verses that follow report the throwing of the staff and the drawing out of the hand (7:107–108), and the account then moves to the reaction of Pharaoh's chiefs (7:109–110). The verse under discussion supplies the ruler's demand for the proof.

The reader who followed the messenger's declaration now reads the ruler's answer. The demand for the sign is stated, the release of the Children of Israel is passed over, and the account proceeds to the two signs that the messenger produced in the court.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that Moses threw down his staff and that it became a serpent plainly visible (7:107). The demand for the sign gives way to the production of the first of the two signs, and the court of Pharaoh is shown the proof that the ruler had requested.

The two verses together state the exchange in the court of Egypt. The ruler asks for the sign, the messenger produces it, and the following verses report the second sign and the reaction of the chiefs; and the account then moves to the contest with the magicians.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:107

> So Moses threw down his staff and—behold!—it became a real snake.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Throwing of the Staff**

The verse reports that Moses threw down his staff and that it became a serpent plainly visible, and the act is the answer to Pharaoh's demand for the sign. The Book's accounts of the Egyptian narrative report the same event in each version, and the sūrah of the Poets states that the staff became a real snake (26:32), while the sūrah of the Stories and the sūrah of the Ant describe the staff that moved as though it were alive (28:31; 27:10). The verse here supplies the sūrah's account of the first sign, and the reader is shown the proof produced in the court of the ruler.

The Book's narrative sets the sign in the presence of Pharaoh and his notables, and the account that follows reports the reaction of the chiefs and their interpretation of what they had seen (7:109–110). The verse here supplies the first of the two signs, and the second follows immediately.

**The Staff in the Story of Moses**

The Book's accounts give the staff a history before the sign, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the dialogue between God and Moses at the burning bush, in which Moses describes the staff as the tool of his shepherding, on which he leans and with which he beats down branches for his sheep (20:18). The sūrah of the Stories reports the instruction to throw it down and the flight of Moses when he saw it move (28:31), and the sūrah of the Ant reports the same command and the same flight (27:10). The verse here supplies the moment in which the shepherd's staff became the sign of the mission, and the narrative takes the instrument from the daily life of the messenger.

Elsewhere, The Book's accounts report the return of the staff to its former state after the first transformations, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā states that Moses was told to take it and have no fear and that it would be returned to its first condition (20:21). This verse supplies the first transformation, and the reader is shown that the object remained a staff in its ordinary use and became the sign by the command of God.

**The Tradition About the Serpent**

The tradition describes the serpent as supernaturally large and reports that it frightened Pharaoh from his throne, and the accounts give the creature a size beyond the ordinary. The Book's narratives state the fact of the transformation without describing the dimensions, and the sūrah of the Poets and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā likewise leave the description to the imagination of the reader. The present verse supplies the plain statement that the staff became a snake, and the tradition's descriptions belong to the elaboration of the scene rather than to its substance.

The Book's use of the staff as the sign appears again in the contest with the magicians, where the same object devours the illusions of the sorcerers (7:117), and the sūrah of the Poets reports the same event (26:45). The verse under discussion supplies the first appearance of the sign, and the account that follows shows the same staff performing the decisive act of the contest.

**The Sign and Its Witnesses**

The verse states that the serpent was plainly visible, and the phrase places the sign before the eyes of the court. Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of the signs given to the prophets state that they were produced for the witnesses, and the sūrah of the Stories reports the signs as the proofs of the mission. Here the verse supplies the visible character of the demonstration, and the reader is shown that the court was not asked to accept a private claim.

The Book's narratives of the prophets present the signs as public events that divided the witnesses, and the sūrah's account of the Egyptian contest will show the magicians recognizing the difference between the illusion and the sign. The verse at hand supplies the first public display, and the two verses that follow report the second sign and the answer of the chiefs.

**The Flight and Its Reversal**

In several places, The Book's accounts of the commissioning report the fear of Moses when the staff moved, and the sūrah of the Stories states that he ran away without looking back and was called back and reassured (28:31). This verse reports the same act performed deliberately in the court, and the contrast states the difference between the messenger's first encounter with the sign and his bearing before the ruler. The Book's narratives present the courage of the messenger in the contest as the fruit of the divine reassurance, and the reader is shown what the commissioning accomplished in him.

The Book's treatment of the messengers' fear and reassurance appears in its accounts of the several prophets who were strengthened for their missions, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the instruction to Moses and Aaron to go with the signs and not to falter in remembering God (20:42). The present verse supplies the sign as it was produced before Pharaoh, and the account that follows states its effect on the court and the interpretation that the chiefs placed upon it.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and seventh in the chapter, and it answers Pharaoh's demand for the sign in the verse before it. The verse that follows reports the drawing out of the hand and its whiteness (7:108), and the two signs together produce the reaction of the chiefs in the two verses after that (7:109–110). The verse under discussion supplies the first of the two signs of Moses.

The reader who heard the ruler demand the proof now reads the first production of it. The staff is thrown down, the serpent appears, and the second sign follows before the court has answered; and the account then reports how the notables of Egypt explained what they had seen.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that Moses drew his hand out and that it appeared white to the onlookers (7:108). The first sign gives way to the second, and the two together form the demonstration that Pharaoh had demanded and that his chiefs then interpreted as magic.

The two verses together state the production of the signs in the court. The staff becomes a serpent and the hand becomes white, and the passage that follows records the verdict of the notables of Egypt on the display.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:108

> Then he drew his hand ˹out of his collar˺ and it was ˹shining˺ white for all to see.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Sign**

The verse reports that Moses drew his hand out and that it appeared white to the onlookers, and the second sign completes the demonstration that Pharaoh had demanded. The Book's accounts of the mission name the two signs together in several passages, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the hand as the second sign after the staff (26:33), while the sūrah of the Stories reports the instruction to put the hand under the arm and its coming out white and unblemished (28:32). The verse here supplies the sūrah's statement of the second sign, and the two verses together present the evidence in the form that the narrative required.

The Book's accounts of the commissioning in the sūrah of Ṭā Hā name the hand as another sign and describe it as coming out shining and unblemished (20:22), and the same passage reports the reassurance of Moses about both signs. The verse here supplies the production of the second sign before the court, and the narrative does not describe the reaction to it separately from the reaction to the pair.

**The Character of the Sign**

The verse states that the hand was white to the onlookers, and the Book's accounts add that the whiteness was not the whiteness of disease. The sūrah of Ṭā Hā states that the hand came out white without blemish (20:22), and the sūrah of the Stories uses the same description (28:32). The verse here supplies the sign in its visible form, and the reader is shown that the transformation was a sign of light rather than of illness.

The Book's descriptions of the signs given to the prophets include the transformations of ordinary things into visible proofs, and the sūrah's account of the she-camel given to Ṣāliḥ belongs to the same family of signs. This verse supplies the second transformation in the Egyptian narrative, and the reader is shown the pattern of the demonstration that the messenger was given.

**The Pair of Signs**

The two signs of the staff and the hand appear together in the Book's accounts of the mission to Pharaoh, and the sūrah of the Poets states them in consecutive verses (26:32–33) as the sūrah here does. The Book's narratives of Moses name the same pair at the commissioning and in the contest with the magicians, and the two signs accompany the messenger through the stages of his mission. The present verse supplies the second half of the pair, and the account that follows reports the interpretation that the notables of Egypt placed on both.

Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the prophets show the signs serving the message at each stage, and the sūrah's narrative will report the staff again in the contest with the sorcerers (7:117). The verse under discussion supplies the second sign in the court of Pharaoh, and the reader is shown the evidence that the ruler had asked for and that his chiefs then explained as magic.

**The Notables and the Signs**

The two verses that follow report the interpretation of the chiefs, who describe Moses as a skilled magician and accuse him of seeking to drive the people from their land (7:109–110). The pair of signs is thereby set beside the skills of the Egyptian sorcerers, and the narrative prepares the contest that will decide between them. Here the verse supplies the second sign, and the reader is shown how quickly the demonstration was classified by those who were determined to resist it.

Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of the communities show the same classification of the signs in the other narratives, and the sūrah's account of Ṣāliḥ reported the people's demand for a different sign and their killing of the one they had (7:73; 7:77). The verse at hand supplies the Egyptian instance of the rejection, and the narrative that follows states the chiefs' advice to the ruler and the summoning of the sorcerers.

**The Sign and the Witness**

The verse states that the hand was white to those who looked on, and the phrase again places the sign before the public. In several places, The Book's accounts of the signs present them as public demonstrations, and the sūrah's narrative reports the reactions of the court and later of the people. This verse supplies the second public sign, and the reader is shown that the evidence was available to all who were present.

The Book's treatment of the signs as evidence that leaves the witnesses responsible for their response appears in its accounts of the communities, and the sūrah's general section stated that the peoples of the towns would not believe what they had denied before (7:101). The present verse supplies the second sign in the Egyptian account, and the passage that follows reports the response of the notables.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and eighth in the chapter, and it completes the pair of signs that answered Pharaoh's demand. The two verses that follow report the verdict of the chiefs, who called Moses a magician and accused him of wanting to expel the people from the land, and Pharaoh's question about what should be done (7:109–110). The verse under discussion supplies the second of the two signs of Moses.

The reader who saw the staff become a serpent now sees the hand come out white. The two signs stand before the court, and the account turns to the interpretation that the notables of Egypt gave them and to the plan they proposed to the ruler.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the chiefs of Pharaoh's people said that Moses was a skilled magician and that he sought to drive the people from their land, and the verse after it reports Pharaoh's question about what should be done (7:109–110). The second sign gives way to the reaction of the court, and the narrative moves toward the contest with the sorcerers.

The two verses together complete the first stage of the Egyptian account.
