The messenger has declared his commission, demanded the release of the Children of Israel, produced the two signs, and been answered with the charge of magic; and the verses that follow state the plan of the notables and the summoning of the magicians.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:109

> The chiefs of Pharaoh’s people said, “He is indeed a skilled magician,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict of the Chiefs**

The verse reports the verdict of the chiefs of Pharaoh's people, and the verdict classifies the signs that Moses had produced as the work of a skilled magician. The Book's accounts of the Egyptian narrative report the same interpretation in each version, and the sūrah of the Poets reports Pharaoh announcing the same judgment to the chiefs around him (26:34), while the sūrah of Yūnus reports the people saying that the truth that came to them was pure magic (10:76). The verse here supplies the sūrah's account of the court's reaction, and the reader is shown how quickly the two signs were assigned to the familiar category.

The Book's accounts of the communities show the same classification of the signs in the other narratives, and the sūrah's account of Ṣāliḥ reported the people's treatment of the she-camel as an obstacle rather than as a proof (7:73; 7:77). The verse here supplies the Egyptian instance, and the narrative that follows states the political charge that accompanied the verdict.

**The Charge of Magic**

The Book reports the charge of magic against several messengers, and the sūrah of the Poets and the sūrah of the Ant record the accusation in the Egyptian account. The charge served to place the signs within the range of what the magicians of Egypt could do, and the contest that follows was arranged precisely to test that classification. The verse here supplies the first statement of the charge, and the account that follows shows the magicians themselves recognizing the difference between their craft and what Moses had produced.

The Book's treatment of the accusation of magic as a device of the deniers appears in its accounts of the earlier communities, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the people of Noah calling their messenger possessed and the people of ʿĀd calling theirs a liar. This verse supplies the Egyptian version, and the reader is shown the pattern by which each community explained away the proof that had been given to it.

**The Consultation of the Notables**

The verdict in this verse is delivered by the chiefs rather than by the ruler, and the Book's accounts of the Egyptian court present the notables as the group that advises Pharaoh. The sūrah of the Poets reports Pharaoh consulting the chiefs around him (26:34), and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the sorcerers disputing the matter among themselves after the signs were produced (20:62). The present verse supplies the chiefs' judgment in the sūrah's account, and the reader is shown the court divided between the interpretation of the signs and the question of what to do about them.

The Book's treatment of the notables appears in the sūrah's earlier narratives, where the leaders of the communities answer for the people and refuse the messengers. The verse under discussion supplies the Egyptian instance of the same group, and the account that follows reports the plan that they proposed to the ruler.

**The Description of the Magician**

The verse describes the magician as skilled, and the adjective states the chiefs' assessment of the craft that they believed they had recognized. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the Egyptian sorcerers present them as the masters of a recognized art, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports their dispute among themselves about the proper course of action (20:62). Here the verse supplies the court's estimate of the messenger's art, and the narrative that follows sets the estimate against the result of the contest.

The Book's treatment of the world's estimate of the signs appears in its statements that the disbelievers describe the truth as magic when it comes to them, and the sūrah of Yūnus reports the same words from the people of Pharaoh (10:76). The verse at hand supplies the sūrah's statement of the verdict, and the reader is shown the category into which the court placed what it had seen.

**The Signs and the Craft**

Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of the contest present the magicians' craft as a manipulation of appearances, and the sūrah's narrative reports the bewitching of the eyes and the fear that the sorcerers produced (7:116). This verse supplies the first classification of the signs as a craft, and the narrative that follows shows the staff of Moses devouring what the sorcerers had made. The two passages together state the difference that the court could not see until the contest had been decided.

The Book's treatment of magic in its narratives presents the practice as a deception that has no power over the truth, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the instruction to Moses that what the sorcerers had made was only the trick of a magician and would not succeed (20:69). The present verse supplies the charge against the signs, and the account that follows shows the charge answered in the presence of its authors.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and ninth in the chapter, and it follows the production of the two signs and precedes the political charge. The verse that follows reports the chiefs' accusation that Moses sought to drive the people from their land and Pharaoh's question about what should be done (7:110), and the plan for the contest is given after that (7:111–112). The verse under discussion supplies the court's interpretation of the signs.

The reader who saw the staff and the hand now hears the verdict of the notables. The signs are called magic, the political charge follows, and the account proceeds to the summoning of the sorcerers of Egypt.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the continuation of the chiefs' words, that the messenger sought to drive the people from their land, and then reports Pharaoh's question about what he should do (7:110). The classification of the signs gives way to the political charge, and the narrative moves from the interpretation of the demonstration to the plan for answering it.

The two verses together state the reaction of the court to the signs. The chiefs name the craft and the ambition they think they have found, the ruler asks for advice, and the verses that follow state the plan that the notables proposed.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:110

> who seeks to drive you from your land.” ˹So Pharaoh asked,˺ “What do you propose?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Political Charge**

The verse reports the continuation of the notables' words, that the messenger sought to drive the people from their land, and the charge turns the religious claim into a threat to the state. The Book's accounts of the Egyptian narrative report the same accusation in each version, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the chiefs telling Pharaoh that the magician wanted to drive them from their land by his magic (26:35), while the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the sorcerers making the same charge among themselves (20:63). The verse here supplies the sūrah's statement of the accusation, and the reader is shown the court's reading of the messenger's purpose.

The Book's accounts of the communities show the same political framing of the messengers' work, and the sūrah has already reported the demand of Lot's people for the expulsion of his household (7:82) and the threat of the leaders of Midian to expel Shuʿayb and the believers (7:88). The verse here supplies the Egyptian version, and the reader is shown that the charge was the standard response of the powers that the message threatened.

**The Land and Its People**

The charge describes the land as belonging to the people whom the messenger would drive out, and the phrase states the court's understanding of Egypt as the possession of its rulers. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the Egyptian narrative present the land as the object of Pharaoh's domination, and the sūrah of the Stories reports his elevation of himself and his division of the people into groups (28:4). The verse here supplies the accusation in the chiefs' words, and the narrative that follows shows the plan they proposed for holding the land.

The Book's treatment of the rulers who identified their own power with the land appears in its accounts of the peoples, and the sūrah of the Stories reports the declaration of Pharaoh that he was the highest lord of the people (28:38). This verse supplies the reaction of the court to a message that demanded the release of a whole people, and the reader is shown the connection between the demand and the accusation.

**The Accusation and Its Irony**

The accusation states that the messenger sought to expel the people, while the Book's account has shown the Children of Israel being oppressed in the land and the messenger asking for their release. The narrative reports the demand of Moses in the previous verses and the accusation of the chiefs in this one, and the two statements stand beside each other in the account. The present verse supplies the accusation, and the reader is left to measure it against the request that was actually made.

Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of the communities frequently place the accusations of the powerful beside the conduct that the narratives have described, and the sūrah's account of Lot reported the people accusing his household of purity while their own practice was the subject of the charge (7:82). The verse under discussion supplies the Egyptian instance of the same reversal, and the narrative that follows reports the plan that the accusation produced.

**Pharaoh's Question**

The verse closes with Pharaoh's question about what the assembly proposed, and the question places the decision in the hands of the ruler. In several places, The Book's accounts of the Egyptian court present Pharaoh as the one who disposes of the matter, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the same question in his words to the chiefs (26:35). Here the verse supplies the question in the sūrah's account, and the answer that the notables gave follows in the next verses.

The Book's narratives of the rulers who consult their advisers appear in the accounts of the other kingdoms, and the sūrah of the Ant reports the queen of Sheba consulting her nobles about the letter of Solomon (27:29–32). The verse at hand supplies the Egyptian instance of the consultation, and the reader is shown the machinery of the state being set in motion against the messenger.

**The Plan and Its Purpose**

The question in this verse leads to the summoning of the sorcerers, and the Book's accounts of the narrative state that the court decided to answer the signs with the skills of the kingdom. This verse supplies the moment before the plan is stated, and the reader is shown the rulers of the land treating the message as a matter of public order rather than of truth. The account that follows reports the plan in the words of the notables, and the contest is arranged.

The Book's treatment of the rulers who answer the signs with the resources of the state appears in its accounts of the other peoples, and the sūrah's narratives show the means by which each community resisted its messenger. The present verse supplies the Egyptian version, and the passage that follows states the decision that led to the contest at the appointed day.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and tenth in the chapter, and it completes the chiefs' response to the two signs. The verses that follow report their advice to delay the matter and to send for every skilled magician in the land (7:111–112), and the account then moves to the arrival of the sorcerers and their bargain with the ruler (7:113–114). The verse under discussion supplies the political charge and the ruler's question.

The reader who heard the signs called magic now hears the accusation of a plot against the land. The court is set against the messenger, the ruler asks for counsel, and the narrative proceeds to the plan that produced the contest.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the advice of the notables, that the messenger and his brother be delayed while mobilizers are sent to the cities (7:111), and the verse after it completes the plan with the summoning of every skilled magician (7:112). The ruler's question gives way to the answer of his advisers, and the Egyptian contest is prepared.

The two verses together state the reaction of the court to the demand made of it. The messenger has asked for the release of a people, and the court has answered with the charge of a plot against the land and with a plan to test the signs against the skills of the kingdom; and the verses that follow state the contest itself.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:111

> They replied, “Let him and his brother wait and send mobilizers to all cities

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Advice of the Notables**

The verse reports the advice that the chiefs gave Pharaoh, that the messenger and his brother be delayed while messengers are sent to the cities to gather the magicians, and the plan answers the signs with the resources of the kingdom. The Book's accounts of the Egyptian narrative report the same counsel in each version, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the advice in the same words, that the two be delayed and mobilizers dispatched to the cities (26:36). The verse here supplies the sūrah's account of the court's decision, and the reader is shown the means by which the rulers proposed to answer the proof that had been produced.

The Book's accounts of the rulers' councils present the advice of the notables as the instrument of the confrontation, and the sūrah's earlier narratives showed the leaders of the communities answering the messengers on behalf of the peoples. The verse here supplies the Egyptian instance of that leadership, and the account that follows states the gathering of the sorcerers and the bargain they made with the ruler.

**The Delay of the Messenger**

The advice to delay the messenger and his brother is understood by the tradition as a proposal to keep them in the land, either by postponing their departure or by holding them until the contest could be arranged. The Book's account of the demand that Moses had made shows why the delay mattered: the request was for the release of the Children of Israel, and the court's plan kept the question open while it prepared its answer. The verse here supplies the proposal in the words of the notables, and the narrative that follows shows the arrangement of the contest.

The Book's treatment of the rulers who detain the messengers appears in its accounts of the other communities, and the sūrah's account of Shuʿayb reported the threat of expulsion rather than of detention. This verse supplies the Egyptian version of pressure on the messenger, and the reader is shown the court's confidence that the matter could be settled by an exhibition of skills.

**The Sending to the Cities**

The verse states that mobilizers were to be sent to the cities, and the phrase states the reach of the kingdom's resources. The Book's account of Egypt presents the land as organized under the ruler's authority, and the sūrah of the Stories reports the division of the people into groups and the oppression of one of them (28:4). The present verse supplies the order that gathered the experts of the land, and the reader is shown the scale of the preparation that the messenger's two signs had set in motion.

The Book's narratives of the rulers who summon their experts appear in the accounts of the other kingdoms, and the sūrah of the Ant reports the consultation of the nobles of Sheba about the letter of Solomon (27:29–32). The verse under discussion supplies the Egyptian instance of the mobilization, and the account that follows reports the arrival of the sorcerers and the terms on which they agreed to compete.

**The Reputation of the Magicians**

The plan rests on the reputation of the magicians of Egypt, and the tradition describes their skill in the manipulation of staffs, ropes, and snakes. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts present the sorcerers as a recognized profession in the kingdom, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the gathering of the sorcerers for the day of the contest (20:58–60). Here the verse supplies the decision to draw on that profession, and the reader is shown what the court expected from the experts it summoned.

The Book's treatment of the sorcerers' craft as an appearance rather than a reality appears in the account of the contest itself, and the sūrah's narrative reports the bewitching of the eyes and the great display that the sorcerers produced (7:116). The verse at hand supplies the arrangement that brought them to the court, and the narrative that follows states the outcome of the comparison that the court had proposed.

**The Confrontation and Its Terms**

The plan in this verse sets the terms of the Egyptian contest: the proof of the messenger on one side and the assembled skill of the kingdom on the other. Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of the confrontation present the arrangement as the rulers' choice rather than the messenger's, and the sūrah's narrative shows Moses accepting the contest that the court proposed. This verse supplies the court's decision, and the reader is shown the machinery that the state brought against a single staff.

The Book's narratives of the confrontations between the messengers and the powers of their peoples present similar arrangements, and the sūrah's earlier accounts showed the communities demanding signs and punishments from their messengers. The present verse supplies the Egyptian instance of the demand, and the account that follows reports the assembly of the sorcerers and the display that they made before the people.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and eleventh in the chapter, and it answers Pharaoh's question about what should be done. The verse that follows completes the plan with the instruction to bring every learned magician (7:112), and the account then reports the arrival of the sorcerers and their question about the reward (7:113–114). The verse under discussion supplies the advice that set the contest in motion.

The reader who heard the charge against the messenger now reads the plan of the court. The delay is proposed, the cities are to be searched, and the narrative proceeds to the gathering of the magicians and to the bargain that they struck with the ruler.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse completes the counsel of the notables with the instruction to bring every learned magician to the court (7:112). The decision to gather the experts of the land gives way to their arrival in the narrative that follows, and the contest between the messenger and the sorcerers takes shape.

The two verses together state the court's plan for answering the signs. The messenger is to be delayed while the magicians of Egypt are assembled, and the verses that follow report their arrival, their bargain with the ruler, and the day on which the two sides met.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:112

> to bring you every clever magician.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Completion of the Plan**

The verse completes the advice of the notables with the purpose of the mobilization: the cities are to be searched for every skilled magician, who is to be brought to the court. The Book's accounts of the narrative report the same instruction in each version, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the summoning of every learned magician among the people (26:37), while the sūrah of Yūnus reports Pharaoh's order to bring every learned magician to him (10:79). The verse here supplies the last piece of the court's plan, and the reader is shown the full extent of the preparation that the two signs had provoked.

The Book's accounts of the Egyptian contest present the summoning of the sorcerers as the kingdom's answer to the messenger, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the gathering of the sorcerers for the day of the festival (20:59). The verse here supplies the order that produced the assembly, and the narrative that follows states what the sorcerers asked of the ruler when they arrived.

**The Magicians of Egypt**

The Book presents the sorcerers as the masters of a recognized craft, and the tradition describes their skills with staffs, ropes, and illusions. The sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports their dispute among themselves about how to meet the messenger, and the sūrah of the Poets reports their question about the reward that would be theirs if they were victorious (26:41). The verse here supplies the assembly of the craft in the court of the ruler, and the reader is shown the human skill of a whole profession gathered to answer what God had sent.

The Book's treatment of the sorcerers as a body of practitioners appears in the narrative of their conversion, where the sūrah reports that they believed in the Lord of Moses and Aaron after the staff had devoured their work (7:120–122). This verse supplies the gathering that preceded that conversion, and the narrative that follows shows the terms on which the contest was arranged.

**The Terms of the Contest**

The plan in this verse gathers all the skill of Egypt, and the arrangement sets the confrontation between the messenger's sign and the art of the kingdom. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the contest present the messengers accepting the challenge, and the sūrah's narrative reports the exchange between Moses and the sorcerers about who would cast first (7:115). The present verse supplies the summoning, and the reader is shown that the court intended the contest to settle the matter publicly.

The Book's narratives of the signs present the confrontations with the crafts and powers of the peoples as part of the same pattern, and the sūrah's earlier accounts showed the communities demanding signs from their messengers. The verse under discussion supplies the Egyptian instance of the demand, and the narrative that follows states the sorcerers' bargain and the day of the display.

**The Proof and the Profession**

The verse sets the summons to the magicians beside the two signs that Moses had produced, and the comparison is the point of the account. The Book's narratives report the sorcerers' own recognition of the difference between their art and the sign, and the sūrah of the Poets reports their confession after the contest that the feat of Moses could only be from God. Here the verse supplies the gathering of the profession, and the reader is shown what became of the comparison that the court had ordered.

The Book's treatment of the world's expertise as unable to reach the truth appears in its accounts of the sorcerers and in its statements about those who claim knowledge without guidance, and the sūrah of the Stories reports the accusation of magic against the signs and the reply of the truth to it (28:36). The verse at hand supplies the Egyptian instance, and the narrative that follows shows the outcome of the contest arranged by this plan.

**The Court and the Kingdom**

The verse's order travels from the court to the cities, and the phrase states the reach of the ruler's authority over the land. The Book's description of Egypt in the account of Moses presents the kingdom as an organized power, and the sūrah of the Stories reports the oppression of a people within that organization (28:4). This verse supplies the summoning of the experts from the whole country, and the reader is shown the scale on which the confrontation had been arranged.

The Book's treatment of the power of the kings appears in its accounts of the rulers who were given authority and used it against the messengers, and the sūrah's narratives show the ends of those rulers. The present verse supplies the order that gathered the magicians, and the account that follows states the bargain between the sorcerers and the ruler of the land.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and twelfth in the chapter, and it completes the plan of the notables that began in the previous verse. The verses that follow report the arrival of the sorcerers at the court, their question about the reward, and Pharaoh's promise (7:113–114), and the contest itself follows (7:115–120). The verse under discussion supplies the last element of the court's plan.

The reader who heard the advice to delay the messenger now reads the order that gathered the magicians. The cities are to be searched, the experts are to be brought, and the narrative proceeds to the bargain between the sorcerers and the ruler and to the day of the contest.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the magicians came to Pharaoh and asked whether they would receive a reward if they were the ones who prevailed (7:113). The order to gather the experts gives way to their arrival, and the narrative states the terms on which they entered the contest.

The two verses together complete the preparation of the Egyptian stage. The court has decided to answer the signs with the skill of the kingdom, the magicians have been summoned, and the verses that follow state their bargain with the ruler, the choice of the order of casting, and the display that they made before the people.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:113

> The magicians came to Pharaoh, saying, “Shall we receive a ˹suitable˺ reward if we prevail?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Arrival of the Sorcerers**

The verse reports the arrival of the magicians at the court and the question they put to Pharaoh about their reward, and the question states the terms on which they entered the contest. The Book's accounts of the Egyptian narrative report the same exchange, and the sūrah of the Poets states that the magicians asked Pharaoh whether they would have a suitable reward if they prevailed (26:41). The verse here supplies the sūrah's account of the bargain, and the reader is shown the profession treating the confrontation as an engagement of its craft.

The Book's accounts of the contest present the sorcerers as the acknowledged experts of the land, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the appointment that was set for the meeting with Moses (20:58). The verse here supplies their arrival in the sūrah's narrative, and the account that follows states the ruler's answer and the beginning of the display.

**The Question of the Reward**

The verse's question asks about the reward that would follow a victory, and the term states the price the sorcerers set on their skills. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the episode report the same question and the same answer in the parallel passages, and the sūrah of the Poets states the exchange in consecutive verses (26:41–42). The verse here supplies the question in the sūrah's wording, and the reader is shown the world's terms being applied to a contest about the truth.

The Book's treatment of the rewards that people seek from the holders of power appears in its accounts of those who serve the rulers for gain, and the sūrah's narratives contrast the rewards of the world with the reward of God. This verse supplies the sorcerers' question at the beginning of the contest, and the account that follows shows the same sorcerers at the end of it asking for the forgiveness of their Lord (7:120–122).

**The Sorcerers and Their Craft**

The Book presents the sorcerers as the masters of a recognized art, and the tradition describes their skill with staffs, ropes, and illusions. Their question in this verse shows that they understood the contest as a professional engagement, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports their consultation among themselves before the display (20:62). The present verse supplies the terms they sought, and the narrative that follows shows the same men confronted with a sign their craft could not match.

Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of the contest present the sorcerers' conversion as the outcome of the display, and the sūrah of the Poets reports their words after the staff had swallowed their work. The verse under discussion supplies the opening of their part in the narrative, and the reader is shown the distance between the reward they asked for and the faith they later confessed.

**The Profession and the Truth**

The verse places the magicians' bargain beside the signs that Moses had produced, and the account that follows tests the professional skill of the land against the proof from the Lord. The Book's narratives report the sorcerers' own recognition of the difference, and the sūrah of the Poets states their confession that the feat of Moses could only be from God. Here the verse supplies the moment before the test, and the reader is shown the confidence of the men who would be the first to understand what they were facing.

The Book's treatment of the experts who recognize the truth they had opposed appears in several of its narratives, and the sūrah's account of the Egyptian contest is the fullest of them. The verse at hand supplies the arrival and the bargain, and the narrative that follows reports the ruler's promise to the sorcerers and the choice of who would cast first.

**The Court and the Contest**

The verse states that the sorcerers came to Pharaoh, and the phrase places the contest under the authority of the ruler who had ordered it. In several places, The Book's accounts present the whole episode as a public demonstration arranged by the court, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the appointment set for the day of the festival so that the people might gather (20:59). This verse supplies the sorcerers' arrival at the court before the public display, and the reader is shown the plan of the kingdom taking its course.

The Book's treatment of the rulers who stage public contests to protect their authority appears in its accounts of the Egyptian narrative, and the sūrah's narratives show the outcome of the confrontation that they arranged. The present verse supplies the arrival of the experts, and the account that follows states the promise that the ruler made them and the display that they mounted before the people.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and thirteenth in the chapter, and it follows the plan of the notables and precedes the ruler's answer. The verse that follows reports Pharaoh's promise that the sorcerers would be among those brought closest to him (7:114), and the account then moves to the challenge and the display (7:115–116). The verse under discussion supplies the meeting between the sorcerers and the ruler.

The reader who saw the plan to summon the magicians now reads their arrival and their question. The bargain is proposed, the ruler's answer follows, and the narrative proceeds to the public contest and to the sign that decided it.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports Pharaoh's answer, that the sorcerers would receive their reward and would be among those brought near to him (7:114). The question about the reward gives way to the promise of the ruler, and the narrative moves to the day of the display and to the exchange between Moses and the sorcerers about who would cast first.

The two verses together state the arrangement between the court and the experts. The sorcerers ask what they will receive, the ruler promises them a place among his closest circle, and the verses that follow report the display, the sign, and the conversion that neither party had anticipated.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:114

> He replied, “Yes, and you will certainly be among those closest to me.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ruler's Promise**

The verse reports Pharaoh's answer to the sorcerers, promising them the reward they had asked for and adding that they would be among those closest to him. The Book's accounts of the narrative report the same answer, and the sūrah of the Poets states that Pharaoh replied that they would certainly be among those brought near to him (26:42). The verse here supplies the sūrah's account of the promise, and the reader is shown the scale of the reward that the court offered for a victory in the contest.

The Book's accounts of the rulers' promises present the rewards of the world in the terms of proximity to power, and the sūrah's narratives contrast that proximity with the nearness that God grants. The verse here supplies the ruler's offer, and the account that follows shows the sorcerers receiving something other than what they had bargained for.

**The Terms of the Promise**

The verse joins the reward to the privilege of nearness, and the promise places the sorcerers within the inner circle of the ruler if they prevail. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts present the court as the centre of the kingdom's honours, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the same promise in the same words (26:42). The verse here supplies the terms in the ruler's voice, and the reader is shown the ladder of advancement on which the sorcerers were being invited to climb.

The Book's treatment of the honours of the world appears in its descriptions of the courts of the rulers and in its warnings to the believers against seeking the praise of the powerful, and the sūrah's narrative shows the promise made and broken in the single episode. This verse supplies the promise, and the account that follows reports the display that the sorcerers made and the sign that undid it.

**The Nearness of the Kingdom**

The Book's account of the episode leaves the promise of proximity without a description of its content, and the reader is left to imagine the position that the sorcerers were offered. The sūrah's narrative reports the outcome of the contest before any statement about the reward, and the sorcerers' conversion leaves the promise suspended. The present verse supplies the ruler's words, and the account that follows shows what the men who had asked for nearness found instead.

The Book's treatment of the servants who sought nearness to God rather than to the rulers appears in its accounts of the believers who stood against the courts of their peoples, and the sūrah of the Forgiver reports the believer from among the family of Pharaoh who concealed his faith and spoke for Moses (40:28). The verse under discussion supplies the promise of the ruler, and the narrative that follows states the sorcerers' answer in the presence of the sign.

**The Sorcerers and the Reward They Asked For**

The sorcerers had asked what they would receive if they were victorious, and the answer in this verse grants their request. Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of the episode do not report any further negotiation, and the contest proceeds. Here the verse supplies the settlement, and the narrative that follows shows the reward receding as the sign is produced.

The Book's narratives of those who bargain for the rewards of the world present the outcome of their bargains in the light of the truth they encounter, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the sorcerers asking for forgiveness after their conversion and describing the reward of God as better and more lasting (20:73). The verse at hand supplies the promise of the ruler, and the reader is shown the distance between the reward of the court and the reward that the sorcerers came to seek.

**The Court and the Contest**

The verse completes the arrangement of the contest, and the sorcerers are now engaged as the champions of the kingdom. In several places, The Book's accounts present the episode as a public display arranged by the state, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the festival day and the gathering of the people (20:59), while the sūrah of the Poets reports the assembly of the magicians at the appointed time (26:38). This verse supplies the agreement between the court and the experts, and the reader is shown the machinery of the display.

The Book's treatment of the rulers who mobilize their resources against the signs appears in the sūrah's narrative and in the accounts of the other kingdoms, and the sūrah's reader has seen the pattern in the accounts of the earlier communities. The present verse supplies the promise that set the contest in motion, and the verses that follow report the challenge, the display, and the conversion.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and fourteenth in the chapter, and it answers the sorcerers' question about their reward. The verse that follows reports the challenge between Moses and the sorcerers about who would cast first (7:115), and the display and the sign follow in the verses after that (7:116–117). The verse under discussion supplies the agreement between the ruler and the magicians.

The reader who heard the sorcerers bargain for a reward now reads the promise of the ruler. The terms are settled, the contest is arranged, and the narrative proceeds to the display before the people and to the sign that answered it.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports the sorcerers asking Moses whether he would cast first or whether they would be the first to cast (7:115). The promise of the ruler gives way to the challenge between the two sides, and the narrative moves to the display itself.

The two verses together state the agreement that produced the contest. The sorcerers receive their promise, the challenge is issued to the messenger, and the verses that follow report the sorcerers' display, the bewitching of the people's eyes, and the sign of the staff that dissolved their work.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:115

> They asked, “O Moses! Will you cast, or shall we be the first to cast?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Challenge Between the Two Sides**

The verse reports the question that the sorcerers put to Moses about who would cast first, and the question opens the public contest. The Book's accounts of the Egyptian narrative report the same exchange, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā states that the sorcerers offered Moses the choice between casting first and letting them begin (20:65). The verse here supplies the sūrah's account of the challenge, and the reader is shown the formal opening of the display that the court had arranged.

The Book's accounts of the contest present the arrangement of the order as a matter of propriety, and the tradition notes that the sorcerers allowed Moses to choose even though they preferred to begin. The verse here supplies the exchange in the sūrah's wording, and the narrative that follows reports the messenger's answer and the display that the sorcerers made.

**The Offer of the First Cast**

The question offers Moses the choice of beginning, and the offer places the initiative with the messenger rather than claiming it for the sorcerers. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts report the same courtesy in the parallel passage, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā states the offer in the words that the sorcerers used (20:65). The verse here supplies the sūrah's version, and the reader is shown the confidence of the men who were willing to let their opponent determine the order of the contest.

The Book's treatment of the sorcerers' bearing appears in the narrative of their conversion and in the tradition that their courtesy was among the qualities that opened them to the sign. This verse supplies the moment of the offer, and the account that follows shows the same men recognizing the difference between their craft and the sign of God.

**The Messenger's Answer**

The verse is followed by the messenger's instruction that the sorcerers cast first, and the Book's accounts report the same answer. The sūrah of the Poets reports Moses telling them to cast whatever they wished (26:43), and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the same instruction and the appearance of the ropes and staffs moving by the sorcerers' magic (20:66). The present verse supplies the question that the answer addresses, and the narrative that follows states the display that the sorcerers produced when they took the lead.

Repeatedly, The Book's accounts present the messenger's willingness to let the opposing side display its full power before his own sign was produced, and the sūrah's narrative reports the fear that Moses concealed when the illusion appeared (20:67). The verse under discussion supplies the challenge, and the reader is shown the moment before the messenger's staff was thrown.

**The Display and the Day**

In several places, The Book's accounts of the contest place it on a public day, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the appointment for the festival and the gathering of the people (20:59), while the sūrah of the Poets reports the assembly of the sorcerers at the appointed time (26:38). Here the verse supplies the exchange between the two sides at the opening of the display, and the narrative that follows reports what the people saw.

The Book's treatment of the public character of the confrontation appears in its accounts of the signs that were given before whole communities, and the sūrah's narratives show the witnesses divided by what they saw. The verse at hand supplies the challenge before the crowd, and the account that follows states the effect of the sorcerers' display on the people and the answer of the sign.

**The Challenge and the Truth**

The challenge in this verse treats the confrontation as a contest between two performances, and the Book's account shows the two sides operating on entirely different grounds. The sorcerers produced illusions, and the messenger produced a sign that consumed them. This verse supplies the opening of the contest in the terms that the court understood, and the reader is shown the terms being overturned by the outcome.

The Book's treatment of the contrast between appearance and reality in the episode appears in the sūrah of Yūnus, where Moses states that what the sorcerers had produced was magic that God would make useless (10:81). The present verse supplies the challenge, and the narrative that follows states the display, the sign, and the conversion of the sorcerers that neither side had arranged.

**The Order and the Initiative**

The sorcerers' offer gave the messenger the choice of who would begin, and the tradition notes that it came from men confident in their craft. His answer left the first move to them, so that the display was mounted on their initiative and in their own time. The arrangement states the terms of the contest: the illusion had every advantage of the opening, and the sign came only after it had been made. What was answered in the court that day was a case fully argued by the experts of Egypt, and the answer was the act of God.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and fifteenth in the chapter, and it follows the agreement between the court and the magicians and precedes the display. The verse that follows reports the messenger's answer and the sorcerers' bewitching of the people's eyes and the great display that they made (7:116), and the sign of the staff follows after that (7:117). The verse under discussion supplies the opening challenge of the contest.

The reader who followed the bargain with the ruler now reads the exchange that began the display. The choice is offered, the messenger's answer follows, and the narrative proceeds to the illusion that the sorcerers produced and to the sign that dissolved it.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that Moses told the sorcerers to cast, and that when they did they deceived the eyes of the people, struck them with awe, and produced a great display of magic (7:116). The challenge gives way to the contest itself, and the narrative turns to what the people saw.

The two verses together state the opening of the Egyptian display. The sorcerers offer the choice, the messenger lets them begin, and their illusions cover the eyes of the crowd; and the verses that follow report the sign of the staff and the truth that was established by it.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:116

> Moses said, “You first.” So when they did, they deceived the eyes of the people, stunned them, and made a great display of magic.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Messenger's Answer**

The verse reports the messenger's answer to the challenge and the display that followed it, and the answer gives the sorcerers the first cast that they had offered to relinquish. The Book's accounts of the contest report the same answer, and the sūrah of the Poets states that Moses told them to cast whatever they wished (26:43), while the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the instruction and the appearance of the ropes and staffs that moved by the sorcerers' magic (20:66). The verse here supplies the sūrah's account of the answer, and the reader is shown the messenger letting the full power of the opposing side be displayed before his own sign was produced.

The Book's accounts present the choice as the messenger's assurance rather than as a concession, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the fear that Moses concealed when the illusion appeared and the reassurance that followed (20:67–68). The verse here supplies the answer and the display in a single sentence, and the narrative that follows states the sign that answered them.

**The Bewitching of the Eyes**

The verse states that the sorcerers deceived the eyes of the people, and the phrase names the character of the craft that the experts of Egypt practiced. The sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the ropes and the staffs that appeared to move, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the sorcerers casting their ropes and staffs and swearing by the might of Pharaoh that they would prevail (26:44). The verse here supplies the sūrah's statement of the illusion, and the reader is shown what the skill of the kingdom could produce when it was gathered against a single man.

The Book's treatment of magic as a deception of the senses appears in its narratives of the contest and in its statements that what the sorcerers produced was an illusion, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the instruction to Moses that what they had made was only the trick of a magician (20:69). This verse supplies the deception in its fullest public form, and the narrative that follows shows it undone by the sign of the staff.

**The Awe of the People**

The verse states that the display struck the people with awe, and the phrase describes the effect of the illusion on the crowd that had gathered for the day. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts report the gathering of the people at the festival, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the question put to the people whether they would join the assembly (26:39). The present verse supplies the reaction of the crowd to the sorcerers' work, and the reader is shown the moment when the court's arrangement appeared to have succeeded.

The Book's treatment of the impression that the crafts of the world make upon the people appears in its accounts of the crowds that followed the signs of the messengers and the crowds that were swayed by the displays of the powerful, and the sūrah's narrative shows the two effects in sequence. The verse under discussion supplies the awe that the sorcerers produced, and the account that follows states what the sign of God produced in its place.

**The Great Display**

The verse describes the sorcerers' work as a great display of magic, and the phrase states the scale of the illusion that the profession had mounted. Repeatedly, The Book's accounts report the resources that the kingdom had gathered for the contest, and the sūrah of the Poets reports the sorcerers swearing by the might of Pharaoh and casting their ropes and staffs in the same manner (26:44). Here the verse supplies the description of the display, and the reader is shown the full extent of what the messenger's sign was about to answer.

The Book's treatment of the powers of the world in their full display appears in its accounts of the kingdoms that opposed the messengers, and the sūrah's narratives show the ends of those powers. The verse at hand supplies the moment of the greatest display that Egypt could make, and the narrative that follows reports the staff that consumed it.

**The Display and the Sign**

The verse stands immediately before the report of the sign, and the sequence states the whole contest in two steps. The sorcerers produced their illusion, and the messenger was instructed to throw his staff, which devoured what they had made. This verse supplies the first step, and the reader is shown how completely the second answered it.

In several places, The Book's accounts of the episode report the effect on the sorcerers themselves, and the sūrah of the Poets reports their prostration and their confession of faith in the Lord of the worlds (26:46–47). The present verse supplies the display that preceded the sign, and the narrative that follows states the truth that was established and the falsity of what the sorcerers had produced.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and sixteenth in the chapter, and it answers the challenge of the previous verse and precedes the sign of the staff. The verse that follows reports the revelation to Moses to throw down his staff and the staff's devouring of the sorcerers' work (7:117), and the truth is then established and the sorcerers fall prostrate (7:118–120). The verse under discussion supplies the illusion that the sign answered.

The reader who heard the challenge now reads the display that the sorcerers mounted. The eyes of the people are deceived, the crowd is awed, and the great illusion stands before the assembly; and the verses that follow report the sign that dissolved it and the conversion that followed.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that God revealed to Moses the instruction to throw down his staff and that it devoured the sorcerers' illusions (7:117). The display of magic gives way to the sign, and the contest is decided in a single act.

The two verses together state the two halves of the Egyptian contest. The sorcerers produce the greatest display their craft could make, and the messenger is instructed to throw the staff that consumes it; and the verses that follow state the establishment of the truth, the defeat of the court, and the prostration of the sorcerers.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:117

> Then We inspired Moses, “Throw down your staff,” and— behold!—it devoured the objects of their illusion!

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Instruction to Throw**

The verse reports that God inspired Moses with the instruction to throw down his staff, and the command comes after the sorcerers had completed their display. The Book's accounts of the contest report the same instruction, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the revelation to Moses to cast what was in his right hand, stating that it would swallow what the sorcerers had made (20:69), while the sūrah of the Poets reports the throwing of the staff and its devouring of the objects of illusion (26:45). The verse here supplies the sūrah's account of the sign, and the reader is shown the act performed at the command of God rather than at the initiative of the messenger.

The Book's accounts of the episode present the messenger waiting for the instruction before producing the sign, and the tradition notes that the delay allowed the display of the sorcerers to be seen in full before it was undone. The verse here supplies the command and the act, and the reader is shown the moment in which the contest was decided.

**The Devouring of the Illusions**

The verse states that the staff devoured what the sorcerers had produced, and the phrase describes the sign swallowing the objects that the illusion had made to appear alive. The sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the same event and adds the instruction that Moses should not fear, since it was he who would prevail (20:68–69), and the sūrah of the Poets reports the staff devouring the sorcerers' work (26:45). The verse here supplies the sūrah's wording, and the reader is shown the sign reducing the great display to nothing.

Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the signs present the transformations wrought by the messengers as the acts of God, and the sūrah of Yūnus reports the statement of Moses that what the sorcerers had produced was magic that God would make useless and that God would establish the truth by His words (10:81). This verse supplies the deed that confirmed the statement, and the narrative that follows reports the establishment of the truth and the collapse of the illusion.

**The Sign and the Craft**

The verse sets the staff of the messenger beside the ropes and staffs of the sorcerers, and the contest turns on the difference between a sign given by God and a craft practiced by men. Repeatedly, The Book's accounts present the difference as one that the sorcerers themselves recognized, and the sūrah of the Poets reports their prostration and confession after the staff had consumed their work (26:46–47). The present verse supplies the act that produced the recognition, and the reader is shown the professional skill of Egypt undone in a moment.

The Book's treatment of the distinction between the miracles of the prophets and the arts of the magicians appears in its narratives of the several contests, and the sūrah's account of the Egyptian episode is the fullest of them. The verse under discussion supplies the decisive act, and the narrative that follows states its effect on the truth, the court, and the men who had been brought to oppose it.

**The Revelation to Moses**

The verse states that the instruction came by revelation, and the phrase places the sign among the communications that Moses received from his Lord. In several places, The Book's accounts of the mission report the several revelations given to Moses, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the reassurance that followed the display of the sorcerers (20:68). Here the verse supplies the communication that turned the contest, and the reader is shown the dependence of the messenger on what he was given.

The Book's treatment of the prophets' reliance on revelation appears throughout its accounts of the missions, and the sūrah's narratives show each messenger acting by the command that he had received. The verse at hand supplies the instance in the Egyptian contest, and the account that follows states the outcome that the command produced.

**Egypt and the Signs**

The verse's act is the third appearance of the staff in the sūrah's narrative, after the transformation before Pharaoh and the demand for the sign, and the third appearance is the one that decided the matter. Time and again, The Book's accounts of the episode present the staff as the instrument of the mission from the commissioning to the contest, and the sūrah of the Stories and the sūrah of the Ant report the same object in the same role. This verse supplies the decisive use, and the narrative that follows reports the prostration of the sorcerers before the Lord of Moses and Aaron.

The Book's narratives of the signs given to the prophets show the same instrument serving the mission at each stage, and the sūrah's account of Moses carries the staff from the shepherd's life to the court of the ruler. The present verse supplies the moment in which the instrument answered the greatest display that Egypt could make, and the account that follows states the end of the contest.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and seventeenth in the chapter, and it answers the display of the sorcerers in the previous verse. The verse that follows states that the truth was established and that what the sorcerers had done failed (7:118), and the two verses after it report the defeat of the court and the prostration of the magicians (7:119–120). The verse under discussion supplies the sign that decided the contest.

The reader who saw the great display of magic now reads the act that consumed it. The messenger throws the staff at the command of his Lord, the illusions are swallowed, and the narrative proceeds to the establishment of the truth and to the confession of the men who had produced the display.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the truth came to pass and that what the sorcerers had been doing proved false (7:118). The devouring of the illusions gives way to the general statement of the outcome, and the account states what the contest had established.

The two verses together state the decision of the Egyptian contest. The sign consumes the work of the sorcerers, the truth is established, and the passage that follows reports the defeat of the court and the prostration of the magicians before the Lord of the worlds.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:118

> So the truth prevailed and their illusions failed.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Establishment of the Truth**

The verse states that the truth came to pass and that the sorcerers' work proved false, and the sentence states the outcome of the contest in the terms of the two things that had met. The Book's accounts of the episode report the same result, and the sūrah of Yūnus reports the words of Moses that God would make the sorcerers' work useless and establish the truth by His words even to the dismay of the wicked (10:82). The verse here supplies the sūrah's statement of the outcome, and the reader is shown the sign's effect on both sides of the confrontation.

The Book's teaching about the truth and its establishment appears throughout its addresses, and the sūrah of the Night Journey reports the declaration that the truth has come and falsehood has vanished (17:81), while the sūrah of the Spoils and the sūrah of the Ranks state that God will perfect His light despite the wishes of the disbelievers (61:8; 9:32). The verse here supplies the narrative instance of that teaching in the Egyptian contest, and the account that follows states the defeat of the court.

**The Meaning of the Truth's Coming to Pass**

The Book's accounts describe the truth as something that arrives and prevails rather than as a proposition to be debated, and the tradition notes that the term used in this verse can mean both the truth's manifestation and its alighting upon the hearts of those who witnessed it. The sūrah of the Prophets states that God hurls the truth against falsehood so that it crushes it (21:18), and the sūrah of Sabaʾ reports the declaration that the truth has come and falsehood will not return (34:49). The verse here supplies the statement of the event in the narrative, and the reader is shown the two interpretations converging on the conversion of the sorcerers.

The Book's treatment of the signs as the means by which the truth is established appears in its accounts of the prophets, and the sūrah's narrative reports the staff consuming the illusions. This verse supplies the result of that act, and the account that follows reports the prostration of the men who had produced the display.

**The Failure of the Sorcerers' Work**

The verse states that what the sorcerers had been doing proved false, and the phrase covers the craft that they had practiced and the display that they had mounted. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the episode report their own recognition of the failure, and the sūrah of the Poets reports their confession that they believed in the Lord of the worlds after the staff had consumed their work (26:46–47). The present verse supplies the sūrah's statement of the collapse, and the reader is shown the difference between the illusion's power over the eyes and its power over the truth.

The Book's treatment of falsehood as that which vanishes when the truth is presented appears in its descriptions of the foam that the torrent carries away, and the sūrah of the Thunder states that the foam passes while what benefits people remains in the earth (13:17). The verse under discussion supplies the narrative instance of the same image, and the account that follows states the humiliation of the court that had arranged the contest.

**The Court and the Contest**

The verse's statement of the outcome includes the court that had sponsored the display, and the defeat of the sorcerers was the defeat of the arrangement that the notables had proposed. Repeatedly, The Book's accounts report the reaction of Pharaoh and the sorcerers' answer to him after their conversion, and the sūrah of the Poets reports his threat against them and their reply that they would not prefer him to the clear proofs that had come to them (26:49; 20:72). Here the verse supplies the moment in which the plan of the court failed, and the narrative that follows reports how the failure was received.

The Book's treatment of the rulers whose plans are undone by the signs appears in its accounts of the several kingdoms, and the sūrah's narratives show the ends of those who arranged the confrontations. The verse at hand supplies the failure in the Egyptian account, and the reader is shown the distance between the expectations of the court and the outcome of the day.

**The Two Sides After the Contest**

The verse states the outcome for the two sides separately: the truth was established, and the sorcerers' work failed. In several places, The Book's accounts report what followed for each of them, and the sūrah's narrative shows the sorcerers turning to faith while the court turned to threats. This verse supplies the division that the sign produced among the witnesses, and the reader is shown the same sign that confirmed the messenger for one group and exposed the powerlessness of the other.

The Book's teaching about the signs that separate the witnesses appears in its accounts of the communities, and the sūrah's narrative shows the crowd, the magicians, and the ruler each responding to what they had seen. The present verse supplies the establishment of the truth before them all, and the passage that follows states the prostration of the sorcerers and the threats of the ruler.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and eighteenth in the chapter, and it follows the sign of the staff and precedes the report of the defeat. The verse that follows states that the two were defeated at that place and brought low (7:119), and the prostration of the sorcerers follows immediately (7:120). The verse under discussion supplies the statement of what the sign established.

The reader who saw the illusions devoured now reads the verdict on the display. The truth is established, the craft is exposed as false, and the narrative proceeds to the defeat of the court and to the confession of the men who had come to defend it.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse states that the court was defeated at that very place and came away humiliated (7:119). The establishment of the truth gives way to the account of those who had opposed it, and the narrative records the reversal of the position with which the court had entered the contest.

The two verses together state the outcome of the Egyptian contest. The truth prevails and the illusions fail, the rulers who arranged the display are defeated and humiliated, and the verses that follow report the prostration of the magicians and their confession of faith in the Lord of the worlds.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:119

> So Pharaoh and his people were defeated right there and put to shame.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Defeat at the Place**

The verse states that the court was defeated at that very place and came away humiliated, and the phrase places the reversal at the site of the display that had been arranged for its victory. The Book's accounts of the episode report the same outcome, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the prostration of the sorcerers and their confession after the sign (20:70). The verse here supplies the sūrah's statement of the defeat, and the reader is shown the company that had gathered for a demonstration leaving it in shame.

The Book's accounts of the rulers who staged confrontations with the messengers present the same reversal in each case, and the sūrah's narratives show the powers of the peoples undone at the moments of their greatest display. The verse here supplies the Egyptian instance, and the narrative that follows reports the conversion of the men who had been summoned to defend the court.

**The Humiliation of the Court**

The verse states that the court came away humiliated, and the term describes the reduction of those who had been proud. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts of the Egyptian ruler report his elevation of himself in the land, and the sūrah of the Stories states that he exalted himself and divided the people into groups (28:4). The verse here supplies the humiliation that answered the elevation, and the reader is shown the reversal in the terms that the narrative has prepared.

The Book's treatment of the humiliation of the proud appears in its statements that the arrogant will be brought low, and the sūrah's earlier accounts of the communities showed the leaders who had refused the messengers being removed with their peoples. This verse supplies the Egyptian instance of the same ending, and the account that follows states the response of the sorcerers to the defeat of the court they had served.

**The Court and Its Champions**

The verse's defeat belongs to the court that had arranged the contest, and the champions it had hired were the first to abandon its cause. Repeatedly, The Book's accounts report the sorcerers' declaration of faith in the sight of Pharaoh, and the sūrah of the Poets reports his threat against them and their reply (26:49). The present verse supplies the moment of the court's humiliation, and the reader is shown the sequence by which the display arranged for its honour produced its shame.

The Book's treatment of the servants of the powerful who turn to the truth appears in its accounts of the believers who spoke in the courts of the rulers, and the sūrah of the Forgiver reports the believer from the family of Pharaoh who concealed his faith and defended Moses (40:28). The verse under discussion supplies the Egyptian instance, and the narrative that follows reports the prostration that made the court's humiliation complete.

**The Witness of the Assembly**

In several places, The Book's accounts of the contest report that the people had been gathered for the day, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the appointment at the festival and the summoning of the crowd (20:59). Here the verse supplies the defeat before that assembly, and the reader is shown the reversal taking place in the sight of the people whom the court had intended to impress.

The Book's treatment of the public character of the signs appears in its accounts of the other messengers, and the sūrah's narratives show the communities divided by what they witnessed. The verse at hand supplies the public defeat in the Egyptian account, and the account that follows states the confession of the sorcerers before the ruler and the crowd.

**The Defeat and the Truth**

The verse's statement of the defeat belongs to the contest between the sign and the craft, and the humiliation of the court is presented as the consequence of its opposition to the truth rather than of any failure in its preparations. Time and again, The Book's accounts of the episode report the thoroughness of the preparations and the completeness of the failure, and the sūrah's narrative sets the two beside each other. This verse supplies the outcome, and the reader is shown the difference between the resources of the kingdom and the power that the sign represented.

The Book's teaching about the outcome of those who oppose the truth appears in its addresses throughout, and the sūrah's accounts of the communities show the pattern in each of the narratives. The present verse supplies the Egyptian instance, and the narrative that follows reports the conversion of the sorcerers and the anger of the ruler at the faith that followed the defeat.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and nineteenth in the chapter, and it follows the establishment of the truth and the failure of the illusions. The verse that follows reports that the sorcerers fell down in prostration (7:120), and the verses after it report their confession of faith in the Lord of the worlds and the threat of Pharaoh (7:121–123). The verse under discussion supplies the defeat of the court.

The reader who saw the truth established now reads what became of the side that had opposed it. The court is defeated and humiliated at the place of its display, and the narrative proceeds to the prostration of the magicians and to the confession that turned the day's outcome into a different kind of victory.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the magicians fell down in prostration (7:120). The humiliation of the court gives way to the act of the men who had produced the display, and the contest reaches its conclusion in their worship.

The two verses together close the public contest. The court is defeated at the place where it had gathered its experts, and the experts themselves fall down before the Lord of Moses and Aaron; and the verses that follow report their words, the threat of the ruler, and the answer that they gave him.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:120

> And the magicians fell down, prostrating.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prostration of the Magicians**

The verse reports that the magicians fell down in prostration, and the act is the answer of the experts of Egypt to the sign that had consumed their work. The Book's accounts of the episode report the same prostration in each version, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā states that the sorcerers fell down in prostration and declared their faith in the Lord of Aaron and Moses (20:70), while the sūrah of the Poets reports their fall and their confession in consecutive verses (26:46–47). The verse here supplies the sūrah's account of the act, and the reader is shown the profession that had been hired to defend the court turning to the worship of the Lord who had sent the messenger.

The Book's accounts present the prostration as the response of men who recognized what their own craft had been unable to produce, and the tradition notes that the sorcerers knew the difference between the illusions they had made and the sign that had swallowed them. The verse here supplies the moment of recognition, and the narrative that follows reports the words that accompanied the act.

**The Act and Its Meaning**

The verse states that the sorcerers fell down prostrating, and the phrase describes the act of worship in the form that the Book presents as the mark of submission. The sūrah of the Thunder states that all those in the heavens and the earth bow down to God willingly or unwillingly, and the sūrah of the Pilgrimage reports the prostration of all that is in the heavens and on the earth (13:15; 22:18). The verse here supplies the prostration of the men who had been brought to oppose the messenger, and the reader is shown the submission of the craft that had claimed the power of the sign.

The Book's treatment of prostration as the outward form of the inward submission appears in its instructions to the community and in its descriptions of the believers, and the sūrah of the Night Journey reports the falling of the people of knowledge on their faces when the signs are recited to them (17:107). This verse supplies the prostration in the Egyptian narrative, and the account that follows states the confession that accompanied it.

**The Hired Champions and the Truth**

The sorcerers had come to the court for a reward and for a place among those closest to the ruler (7:113–114), and the prostration in this verse places them at the feet of a different Lord. Elsewhere, The Book's accounts report their words about the reward they had sought and their statement that God's reward is better and more lasting, and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports their declaration that they would never prefer Pharaoh to the clear proofs that had come to them (20:72). The present verse supplies the act that sealed the change, and the reader is shown the men who had bargained for the honours of the court surrendering to the Lord of the worlds.

The Book's narratives of those who encounter the truth in the course of serving falsehood present the same reversal in several places, and the sūrah's account of the Egyptian contest is the fullest of them. The verse under discussion supplies the prostration, and the account that follows reports the confession of faith and the threat that the ruler made against the men who had abandoned his cause.

**The Court and the Worshippers**

The prostration in this verse takes place in the presence of Pharaoh and the assembled people, and the Book's accounts present the act as a public declaration. The sūrah of the Poets reports the ruler's angry question about whether they had believed in the messenger without his permission (26:49), and the sūrah of Ṭā Hā reports the same exchange in its account. Here the verse supplies the act that provoked the threat, and the reader is shown the completed humiliation of the court, which had gathered its subjects to witness a victory and now watched its champions worship the Lord of their opponent.

The Book's treatment of the public confessions that cost the confessors their position appears in its accounts of the believers who stood against the power of their peoples, and the sūrah's narrative shows the sorcerers paying the price of their faith in the ruler's presence. The verse at hand supplies the prostration, and the account that follows states the words that the sorcerers spoke and the punishment that the ruler threatened.

**The End of the Contest**

The verse completes the contest that began with the challenge about who would cast first, and the outcome is the prostration of the side that had come to display its skill. Repeatedly, The Book's accounts of the episode present the result as the victory of the sign over the craft and as the entry of a new group of believers into the struggle for the deliverance of the Children of Israel. This verse supplies the act that ends the contest, and the narrative that follows continues the confrontation with the ruler.

The Book's narratives of the Egyptian episode report the sorcerers' faith among the earliest confessions in the account of Moses, and their words are recorded in the sūrahs that tell the story. The present verse supplies the prostration in the sūrah of the Heights, and the reader is shown the first fruits of the sign among the people who had been assembled to resist it.

**Where the Verse Sits**

The verse stands one hundred and twentieth in the chapter, and it follows the humiliation of the court and precedes the confession of faith. The verses that follow report the words of the sorcerers and the threat of Pharaoh with his accusation that they had believed without his permission (7:121–124), and the narrative then continues with the punishment of the sorcerers and the Exodus. The verse under discussion supplies the prostration that ends the public contest.

The reader who saw the court defeated now sees its champions prostrate. The craft of Egypt lies at the feet of the Lord of Moses and Aaron, and the narrative proceeds to the confession of the men who had fallen, to the ruler's anger, and to the deliverance of the Children of Israel that the confrontation had been arranged to prevent.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

The next verse reports that the magicians said they believed in the Lord of all worlds (7:121), and the verse after it identifies that Lord as the God of Moses and Aaron (7:122). The prostration gives way to the words that state the content of the faith, and the account records the confession of the men who had come to defend the court.

The two verses together state the conversion that ended the contest. The sorcerers fall down before the Lord of the sign they had seen, and the verses that follow state the confession of faith, the threat of the ruler, and the answer that the sorcerers gave him in the presence of the court.
